The particular club to which Spargo bent his steps was called the Octoneumenoi. Who evolved this extraordinary combination of Latin and Greek was a dark mystery: there it was, however, on a tiny brass plate you once reached the portals. The portals were gained by devious ways. You turned out of Fleet Street by an alley so narrow that it seemed as if you might suddenly find yourself squeezed between the ancient walls. Then you suddenly dived down another alley and found yourself in a small court, with high walls around you and a smell of printer’s ink in your nose and a whirring of printing presses in your ears. You made another dive into a dark entry, much encumbered by bales of paper, crates of printing material, jars of printing ink; after falling over a few of these you struck an ancient flight of stairs and went up past various landings, always travelling in a state of gloom and fear. After a lot of twisting and turning you came to the very top of the house and found it heavily curtained off. You lifted a curtain and found yourself in a small entresol, somewhat artistically painted﻿—the whole and sole work of an artistic member who came one day with a formidable array of lumber and paint-pots and worked his will on the ancient wood. Then you saw the brass plate and its fearful name, and beneath it the formal legal notice that this club was duly registered and so on, and if you were a member you went in, and if you weren’t a member you tinkled an electric bell and asked to see a member﻿—if you knew one.

===Chinese===
斯帕戈走出《守望报》报馆时心里要去的那处消息灵通人士的巢穴，就藏在舰队街一处院落深处，寻常人的目光与见识均无从企及——这样的院落，世上别处再寻不出第二处。只有特定的一帮人知道它。它自然是个俱乐部，否则也成不了它这副模样。在英国——至少在英国——凑起一个意气相投的俱乐部，实在是人生中最简单的事：招揽若干精选的朋友与熟人围拢身边，自选一个名字注册在案，赁下一幢房子，按财力与品味置办陈设，再循着那宽松好说话的法律条文应付一遍——齐活。只要不越出那条宽松的条文，在自家园子里便可以为所欲为。拥有这样一方自家的乐土，可比在舰队街的酒馆里闲混惬意多了。

斯帕戈此行投奔的这家俱乐部，名叫奥克托努梅诺伊（Octoneumenoi）。这拉丁文与希腊文的奇特杂拌究竟出自谁的手笔，是一桩无人知晓的暗谜：反正它就端端正正刻在一块小铜牌上，只等你寻到门前自会看见。而要寻到那扇门，须得七拐八绕。你从舰队街拐入一条小巷，窄得仿佛随时会把人夹挤在两堵老墙之间。接着你猛地扎下另一条巷子，来到一方小院，四周高墙环绕，鼻端闻着油墨味，耳中灌满印刷机的轰响。你再一头钻进一条黑洞洞的过道，里头堆满了纸捆、印刷器件箱和一罐罐油墨；绊倒几件之后，你摸到一道老旧的楼梯，拾级而上，掠过一层层楼梯口，一路如行幽冥、提心吊胆。辗转再三，你终于到得这幢房子的最顶层，只见一道厚重的帘幕拦断去路。掀开帘子，你已置身一间小小的阁楼厅，四壁绘得颇有几分艺术气息——这全然出自某位艺术家会员的手笔，他某天带着一大堆木料杂件和颜料罐前来，对那些老木头随心所欲地挥洒了一番。然后你便望见那块铜牌和牌上那个吓人的名字，名字底下是一纸规规矩矩的法律通告，声明本俱乐部业经依法注册云云；你若是会员，径直请进；若不是会员，便按一按电铃，求见某位会员——前提是你得认识一位。

===Original===
Spargo was not a member, but he knew many members, and he tinkled the bell, and asked the boy who answered it for Mr. Starkey. Mr. Starkey, a young gentleman with the biceps of a prizefighter and a head of curly hair that would have done credit to Antinous, came forth in due course and shook Spargo by the hand until his teeth rattled.

“Had we known you were coming,” said Mr. Starkey, “we’d have had a brass band on the stairs.”

“I want to come in,” remarked Spargo.

“Sure!” said Mr. Starkey. “That’s what you’ve come for.”

“Well, stand out of the way, then, and let’s get in,” said Spargo. “Look here,” he continued when they had penetrated into a small vestibule, “doesn’t old Crowfoot turn in here about this time every night?”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈不是会员，但认识许多会员。他按了电铃，向应门的侍童说要找斯塔基（Starkey）先生。斯塔基先生应声而出——这位年轻绅士，一副职业拳师般的臂膀，一头鬈发便是安在安提诺乌斯（Antinous）头上也不辱没——他握住斯帕戈的手用力摇撼，摇得对方牙齿格格作响。

“早知道您要光临，”斯塔基先生说，“我们准会在楼梯上安排一支铜管乐队。”

“我要进去。”斯帕戈说。

“那还用说！”斯塔基先生说，“您来不就为的这个嘛。”

“那就劳驾让开道，好叫我进去。”斯帕戈说。“听着，”两人挤进一间小前厅后他接着说，“老克劳富特（Crowfoot）不是每天夜里这个钟点都要来这儿露一面吗？”

===Original===
“Every night as true as the clock, my son Spargo, Crowfoot puts his nose in at precisely eleven, having by that time finished that daily column wherein he informs a section of the populace as to the prospects of their spotting a winner tomorrow,” answered Mr. Starkey. “It’s five minutes to his hour now. Come in and drink till he comes. Want him?”

“A word with him,” answered Spargo. “A mere word﻿—or two.”

He followed Starkey into a room which was so filled with smoke and sound that for a moment it was impossible to either see or hear. But the smoke was gradually making itself into a canopy, and beneath the canopy Spargo made out various groups of men of all ages, sitting around small tables, smoking and drinking, and all talking as if the great object of their lives was to get as many words as possible out of their mouths in the shortest possible time. In the further corner was a small bar; Starkey pulled Spargo up to it.

===Chinese===
“每天夜里跟钟表一样准，我的斯帕戈老弟，克劳富特十一点整准时探头进来，到那个钟点，他那每日专栏恰好收工——在那专栏里，他向一部分民众通报他们明天相中头马的胜算如何。”斯塔基先生答道，“现在离他的钟点还差五分钟。进来喝两杯等他吧。找他有事？”

“跟他说句话，”斯帕戈答道，“就一句——顶多两句。”

他跟着斯塔基走进一间屋子，屋里烟雾与声浪塞得满满当当，一时间既睁不开眼也听不清声。不过烟渐渐聚成一顶顶盖，顶盖底下，斯帕戈认出一群群年纪各异的汉子，围坐在小桌旁，吞云吐雾，举杯畅饮，人人都在高声谈论，仿佛此生的头等大事，就是在最短的时间之内从嘴里吐出尽可能多的字眼。屋子最里角有一座小吧台；斯塔基把斯帕戈拽了过去。

===Original===
“Name it, my son,” commanded Starkey. “Try the Octoneumenoi very extra special. Two of ’em, Dick. Come to beg to be a member, Spargo?”

“I’ll think about being a member of this anteroom of the infernal regions when you start a ventilating fan and provide members with a route-map of the way from Fleet Street,” answered Spargo, taking his glass. “Phew!﻿—what an atmosphere!”

“We’re considering a ventilating fan,” said Starkey. “I’m on the house committee now, and I brought that very matter up at our last meeting. But Templeson, of the *Bulletin*﻿—you know Templeson﻿—he says what we want is a wine-cooler to stand under that sideboard﻿—says no club is proper without a wine-cooler, and that he knows a chap﻿—secondhand dealer, don’t you know﻿—what has a beauty to dispose of in old Sheffield plate. Now, if you were on our house committee, Spargo, old man, would you go in for the wine-cooler or the ventilating fan? You see﻿—”

“There is Crowfoot,” said Spargo. “Shout him over here, Starkey, before anybody else collars him.”

===Chinese===
“点单吧，老弟，”斯塔基发号施令，“尝尝本店奥克托努梅诺伊特制极品。来两杯，迪克。这是来求着入会了吧，斯帕戈？”

“等你们装上通风扇，再给会员配一张从舰队街过来的路线图，我再考虑要不要加入这个冥府前厅。”斯帕戈接过酒杯答道，“嚯！——这叫什么空气！”

“通风扇嘛，我们正在考虑，”斯塔基说，“我如今进了会所事务委员会，上回开会我提的正是这桩事。可《公告报》的坦普尔森（Templeson）——你认识坦普尔森吧——他说咱们要的是一台冰酒柜，就摆在那张餐具柜底下——还说没有冰酒柜就算不上正经俱乐部，又说 他认得一个家伙——做二手买卖的，你懂的——手里有件老谢菲尔德银板的漂亮货要出手。哎，斯帕戈老兄，你要是在我们事务委员会里，你是要冰酒柜还是要通风扇？你瞧——”

“克劳富特来了。”斯帕戈说，“斯塔基，快把他喊过来，别叫别人抢了先。”

===Original===
Through the door by which Spargo had entered a few minutes previously came a man who stood for a moment blinking at the smoke and the lights. He was a tall, elderly man with a figure and bearing of a soldier; a big, sweeping moustache stood well out against a square-cut jaw and beneath a prominent nose; a pair of keen blue eyes looked out from beneath a tousled mass of crinkled hair. He wore neither hat nor cap; his attire was a carelessly put on Norfolk suit of brown tweed; he looked half-unkempt, half-groomed. But knotted at the collar of his flannel shirt were the colours of one of the most famous and exclusive cricket clubs in the world, and everybody knew that in his day their wearer had been a mighty figure in the public eye.

“Hi, Crowfoot!” shouted Starkey above the din and babel. “Crowfoot, Crowfoot! Come over here, there’s a chap dying to see you!”

“Yes, that’s the way to get him, isn’t it?” said Spargo. “Here, I’ll get him myself.”

===Chinese===
从斯帕戈几分钟前进来的那扇门里，走进来一个人，在烟雾和灯光里站定，眨了好一阵眼睛。这是个高个子的上了年纪的男人，身板与气度都带股军人风范；一部浓密上翘的大八字胡，衬着方正的下巴，卧在凸出的鼻子底下；一双锐利的蓝眼睛，从一头蓬乱的鬈发底下望出来。他头上不戴帽子；身上一件棕色粗花呢的诺福克套装，穿得随随便便；整个人半是邋遢，半是齐整。可是在他法兰绒衬衫的领口，却系着一条领带——那是全世界最负盛名、入会最严的板球俱乐部之一的标志色，而人人都知道，佩这颜色的人当年曾是众目所仰的赫赫人物。

“嘿，克劳富特！”斯塔基在鼎沸的人声之上喊道，“克劳富特，克劳富特！过来，这儿有个伙计想你想得要死！”

“可不是，这么一喊他不就来了？”斯帕戈说，“得，我亲自去请。”

===Original===
He went across the room and accosted the old sporting journalist.

“I want a quiet word with you,” he said. “This place is like a pandemonium.”

Crowfoot led the way into a side alcove and ordered a drink.

“Always is, this time,” he said, yawning. “But it’s companionable. What is it, Spargo?”

Spargo took a pull at the glass which he had carried with him. “I should say,” he said, “that you know as much about sporting matters as any man writing about ’em?”

===Chinese===
他穿过屋子，迎上这位老体育记者。

“我想同您清静地讲句话，”他说，“这地方吵得像群魔殿。”

克劳富特领他进了旁边一处壁龛雅座，叫了杯酒。

“这个钟点向来如此，”他打着哈欠说，“不过倒也热络有人气。什么事，斯帕戈？”

斯帕戈端起随身带来的酒杯呷了一口。“要我说，”他道，“论体育界的门道，在写这行的人里头，没谁比您知道得更多了吧？”

===Original===
“Well, I think you might say it with truth,” answered Crowfoot.

“And old sporting matters?” said Spargo.

“Yes, and old sporting matters,” replied the other with a sudden flash of the eye. “Not that they greatly interest the modern generation, you know.”

“Well, there’s something that’s interesting me greatly just now, anyway,” said Spargo. “And I believe it’s got to do with old sporting affairs. And I came to you for information about it, believing you to be the only man I know of that could tell anything.”

“Yes﻿—what is it?” asked Crowfoot.

Spargo drew out an envelope, and took from it the carefully-wrapped-up silver ticket. He took off the wrappings and laid the ticket on Crowfoot’s outstretched palm.

===Chinese===
“嗯，这话由你说来倒也不假。”克劳富特答道。

“那么老一辈的体育旧事呢？”斯帕戈问。

“是啊，老一辈的体育旧事也一样。”对方答道，眼里倏地闪过一丝亮光，“虽说如今这代人对那些不大感兴趣，你知道的。”

“反正，眼下有桩事让我大感兴趣，”斯帕戈说，“而且我信它同体育旧事有关。我来找您讨个底细，因为就我认得的人而言，只有您或许还能说出个所以然。”

“哦——什么事？”克劳富特问。

斯帕戈掏出一只信封，从里头取出那件包得严严实实的银质票牌。他解开包裹，把票牌搁在克劳富特摊开的掌心里。

===Original===
“Can you tell me what that is?” he asked.

Another sudden flash came into the old sportsman’s eyes﻿—he eagerly turned the silver ticket over.

“God bless my soul!” he exclaimed. “Where did you get this?”

“Never mind, just now,” replied Spargo. “You know what it is?”

===Chinese===
“您能告诉我这是什么东西吗？”他问。

老体育家眼里又是一亮——他急切地把银质票牌翻了个面。

“老天爷！”他失声道，“你这是打哪儿弄来的？”

“眼下先别管这个，”斯帕戈答道，“您知道它是什么？”

===Original===
“Certainly I know what it is! But﻿—Gad! I’ve not seen one of these things for Lord knows how many years. It makes me feel something like a young ’un again!” said Crowfoot. “Quite a young ’un!”

“But what is it?” asked Spargo.

Crowfoot turned the ticket over, showing the side on which the heraldic device was almost worn away.

===Chinese===
“我当然知道它是什么！可是——天哪！这类东西我有天晓得多少年没见着了。它叫我觉得自己又像个后生了！”克劳富特说，“十足十的后生！”

“它究竟是什么？”斯帕戈问。

克劳富特把票牌又翻了一面，露出那一面上几乎磨平了的纹章图样。

===Original===
“It’s one of the original silver stand tickets of the old racecourse at Market Milcaster,” answered Crowfoot. “That’s what it is. One of the old original silver stand tickets. There are the arms of Market Milcaster, you see, nearly worn away by much rubbing. There, on the obverse, is the figure of a running horse. Oh, yes, that’s what it is! Bless me!﻿—most interesting.”

“Where’s Market Milcaster?” enquired Spargo. “Don’t know it.”

“Market Milcaster,” replied Crowfoot, still turning the silver ticket over and over, “is what the topographers call a decayed town in Elmshire. It has steadily decayed since the river that led to it got gradually silted up. There used to be a famous race-meeting there in June every year. It’s nearly forty years since that meeting fell through. I went to it often when I was a lad﻿—often!”

“And you say that’s a ticket for the stand?” asked Spargo.

“This is one of fifty silver tickets, or passes, or whatever you like to call ’em, which were given by the race committee to fifty burgesses of the town,” answered Crowfoot. “It was, I remember, considered a great privilege to possess a silver ticket. It admitted its possessor﻿—for life, mind you!﻿—to the stand, the paddocks, the ring, anywhere. It also gave him a place at the annual race-dinner. Where on earth did you get this, Spargo?”

===Chinese===
“这是马基特·米尔卡斯特老赛马场最初那批银质看台票牌里的一枚，”克劳富特答道，“就是这么件东西。老辈原版的银质看台票牌。你瞧，这是马基特·米尔卡斯特的纹章，快叫人摩挲得磨没了。那儿，正面，是一匹奔跑的马。哦没错，就是这么件东西！老天！——真有意思。”

“马基特·米尔卡斯特在哪儿？”斯帕戈问道，“我没听说过。”

“马基特·米尔卡斯特嘛，”克劳富特答道，手里仍把银质票牌翻来翻去，“是地志学家所谓的埃尔姆郡（Elmshire）一座凋敝市镇。自从通往镇上的那条河渐渐淤塞，它便一路衰败下来。那里从前每年六月有一场有名的赛马大会。大会散场至今快四十年了。我小时候常去——常去！”

“您是说，这是看台的票牌？”斯帕戈问。

“这是五十枚银质票牌之一——你也可以叫通行证，随你怎么称呼——当年赛马委员会把它们赠给了镇上五十位市民，”克劳富特答道，“我记得，手里握着一枚银质票牌，向来被看作天大的体面。凭它——终身有效，你记住！——持牌人看台、围场、下注圈，处处进出无阻。它还在每年的赛马晚宴上给持牌人留一个席位。你到底是打哪儿弄来这东西的，斯帕戈？”

===Original===
Spargo took the ticket and carefully re-wrapped it, this time putting it in his purse.

“I’m awfully obliged to you, Crowfoot,” he said. “The fact is, I can’t tell you where I got it just now, but I’ll promise you that I will tell you, and all about it, too, as soon as my tongue’s free to do so.”

“Some mystery, eh?” suggested Crowfoot.

“Considerable,” answered Spargo. “Don’t mention to anyone that I showed it to you. You shall know everything eventually.”

“Oh, all right, my boy, all right!” said Crowfoot. “Odd how things turn up, isn’t it? Now, I’ll wager anything that there aren’t half a dozen of these old things outside Market Milcaster itself. As I said, there were only fifty, and they were all in possession of burgesses. They were so much thought of that they were taken great care of. I’ve been in Market Milcaster myself since the races were given up, and I’ve seen these tickets carefully framed and hung over mantelpieces﻿—oh, yes!”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈收回票牌，仔细重新包好，这一回把它放进了钱袋。

“实在感激不尽，克劳富特，”他说，“实情是，眼下我还不能告诉您它是打哪儿来的；但我答应您，等我的舌头一得自由，我必定原原本本、一五一十全告诉您。”

“里头有文章，嗯？”克劳富特提了一句。

“还不少呢，”斯帕戈答道，“我给您看过它的事，千万别对任何人提起。您迟早会知道全部底细。”

“哦，行，老弟，行！”克劳富特说，“世事就这么怪，冷不丁就冒出来一件，是不是？这么着，拿什么打赌我都干：除了马基特·米尔卡斯特本地，这种老物件外头剩下的不到半打。我说过，统共五十枚，全握在市民们手里。人人都宝贝得紧，自然也保管得仔细。赛马停办以后，我自己去过马基特·米尔卡斯特，亲眼见过这些票牌给仔细装了框，挂在壁炉台上——哦，真的！”

===Original===
Spargo caught at a notion.

“How do you get to Market Milcaster?” he asked.

“Paddington,” replied Crowfoot. “It’s a goodish way.”

“I wonder,” said Spargo, “if there’s any old sporting man there who could remember﻿—things. Anything about this ticket, for instance?”

“Old sporting man!” exclaimed Crowfoot. “Egad!﻿—but no, he must be dead﻿—anyhow, if he isn’t dead, he must be a veritable patriarch. Old Ben Quarterpage, he was an auctioneer in the town, and a rare sportsman.”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈脑中抓住一个念头。

“去马基特·米尔卡斯特怎么走？”他问。

“帕丁顿（Paddington）上车，”克劳富特答道，“路可不算近。”

“不知那儿还有没有哪位老体育界的人，能记得些——旧事，”斯帕戈说，“比方说，关于这枚票牌的事？”

“老体育界的人！”克劳富特失声道，“哎呀！——不过不成，他准死了——退一步说，就算没死，也必定是位货真价实的老寿星了。老本·夸特佩奇（Ben Quarterpage），他从前在镇上做拍卖师，是个顶呱呱的体育行家。”

===Original===
“I may go down there,” said Spargo. “I’ll see if he’s alive.”

“Then, if you do go down,” suggested Crowfoot, “go to the old ‘Yellow Dragon’ in the High Street, a fine old place. Quarterpage’s place of business and his private house were exactly opposite the ‘Dragon.’ But I’m afraid you’ll find him dead﻿—it’s five and twenty years since I was in Market Milcaster, and he was an old bird then. Let’s see, now. If Old Ben Quarterpage is alive, Spargo, he’ll be ninety years of age!”

“Well, I’ve known men of ninety who were spry enough, even in my bit of experience,” said Spargo. “I know one﻿—now﻿—my own grandfather. Well, the best of thanks, Crowfoot, and I’ll tell you all about it some day.”

===Chinese===
“我兴许要跑一趟，”斯帕戈说，“去看看他还健在不在。”

“那你若真去，”克劳富特出主意道，“就下榻高街的老‘黄龙’旅店，一处挺好的老字号。夸特佩奇的铺面和私宅就正对着‘黄龙’。只怕你要扑个空——我上一回在马基特·米尔卡斯特，已是二十五年前的事了，那会儿他就是只老鸟了。来，让我算算。老本·夸特佩奇要是还活着，斯帕戈，他该满九十岁了！”

“这个嘛，就凭我这点阅历，九十岁还硬朗的人我也见识过，”斯帕戈说，“眼前我就认得一位——我自己的祖父。好，多谢多谢，克劳富特，改日我把原委全告诉您。”

===Original===
“Have another drink?” suggested Crowfoot.

But Spargo excused himself. He was going back to the office, he said; he still had something to do. And he got himself away from the Octoneumenoi, in spite of Starkey, who wished to start a general debate on the wisest way of expending the club’s ready money balance, and went back to the *Watchman*, and there he sought the presence of the editor, and in spite of the fact that it was the busiest hour of the night, saw him and remained closeted with him for the extraordinary space of ten minutes. And after that Spargo went home and fell into bed.

But next morning, bright and early, he was on the departure platform at Paddington, suitcase in hand, and ticket in pocket for Market Milcaster, and in the course of that afternoon he found himself in an old-fashioned bedroom looking out on Market Milcaster High Street. And there, right opposite him, he saw an ancient house, old brick, ivy-covered, with an office at its side, over the door of which was the name, *Benjamin Quarterpage*.

===Chinese===
“再来一杯？”克劳富特提议。

可斯帕戈推辞了。他说还要回报馆，还有事没了结。他总算从奥克托努梅诺伊脱了身——斯塔基本想拉他发起一场大辩论，议题是如何花掉俱乐部账上的现款结余才最明智——随后回到《守望报》，求见主编；虽说那正是夜里最忙的时辰，他还是见着了主编，还同他关起门密谈了足足十分钟，长得出奇。此后斯帕戈便回了家，一头栽进被窝。

可是第二天一早，天刚放亮，他已站在帕丁顿站的出发月台上，手提旅行箱，口袋里揣着去马基特·米尔卡斯特的车票；当天下午过半，他已置身一间老式卧室，窗外正对着马基特·米尔卡斯特的高街。就在那儿，正对着他，他望见一幢古宅，老砖砌就，爬满常春藤，宅子侧面带一间办公室，门楣上题着名字：本杰明·夸特佩奇（Benjamin Quarterpage）。

## XXIII Miss Baylis / 第二十三章 贝利斯小姐

===Original===
Next day, a little before noon, Spargo found himself in one of those pretentious yet dismal Bayswater squares, which are almost entirely given up to the trade, calling, or occupation of the lodging and boardinghouse keeper. They are very pretentious, those squares, with their many-storied houses, their stuccoed frontages, and their pilastered and balconied doorways; innocent country folk, coming into them from the neighbouring station of Paddington, take them to be the residences of the dukes and earls who, of course, live nowhere else but in London. They are further encouraged in this belief by the fact that young male persons in evening dress are often seen at the doorways in more or less elegant attitudes. These, of course, are taken by the country folk to be young lords enjoying the air of Bayswater, but others, more knowing, are aware that they are Swiss or German waiters whose linen might be cleaner.

Spargo gauged the character of the house at which he called as soon as the door was opened to him. There was the usual smell of eggs and bacon, of fish and chops; the usual mixed and ancient collection of overcoats, wraps, and sticks in the hall; the usual sort of parlourmaid to answer the bell. And presently, in answer to his enquiries, there was the usual type of landlady confronting him, a more than middle-aged person who desired to look younger, and made attempts in the way of false hair, teeth, and a little rouge, and who wore that somewhat air and smile which in its wearer﻿—under these circumstances﻿—always means that she is considering whether you will be able to cheat her or whether she will be able to see you.

“You wish to see Miss Baylis?” said this person, examining Spargo closely. “Miss Baylis does not often see anybody.”

“I hope,” said Spargo politely, “that Miss Baylis is not an invalid?”

“No, she’s not an invalid,” replied the landlady; “but she’s not as young as she was, and she’s an objection to strangers. Is it anything I can tell her?”

“No,” said Spargo. “But you can, if you please, take her a message from me. Will you kindly give her my card, and tell her that I wish to ask her a question about John Maitland of Market Milcaster, and that I should be much obliged if she would give me a few minutes.”

===Chinese===
第二天，临近正午时分，斯帕戈发现自己置身于贝斯沃特那一类虚有其表而又凄清沉闷的广场之中——这些广场几乎已全然交由出租客房与供膳寄宿的房东那一行去经营。那些广场着实气派：多层高楼，灰泥抹面的门脸，带壁柱与阳台的门廊；天真的乡下人从邻近的帕丁顿车站走进来，只当它们是公爵伯爵们的宅邸——这类人自然是非伦敦不住的。更叫他们笃信不疑的是，门口往往可见一些穿晚礼服的年轻男子，摆出几分或优雅或欠优雅的姿态。乡下人当然把他们当作领略贝斯沃特空气的年轻勋爵，但内行些的人却知道，那是些瑞士或德国侍者，而且他们的亚麻衬衣本可以再干净些。

门一开，斯帕戈便掂量出了这所房子的成色。照例有那么一股鸡蛋培根、鱼和肉排的气味；门厅里照例堆着新旧混杂的大衣、披肩和手杖；应铃的照例是那种寻常式样的客厅女仆。不多时，应他问讯而来的，是照例的那类房东太太——一位早已过了中年的妇人，偏想显得年轻些，在假发、假牙和一点胭脂上下了些功夫，脸上挂着那么一副神情与笑容；而在这种境况下的这一路人，那神情与笑容永远意味着：她正在盘算，究竟是你能骗得了她，还是她能看得穿你。

“您想见贝利斯小姐？”这位妇人说道，一面把斯帕戈细细打量。“贝利斯小姐可是轻易不见人的。”

“我希望，”斯帕戈客气地说，“贝利斯小姐不是个病人吧？”

“不，她不是病人，”房东太太答道，“可她不比从前年轻了，又厌恶生人。有什么话是我能转达的吗？”

“那倒不必，”斯帕戈说，“不过能否劳驾您替我捎句话给她。烦请把我的名片递给她，告诉她我想请教一个关于马基特·米尔卡斯特的约翰·梅特兰的问题，如蒙她赏我几分钟，我将不胜感激。”

===Original===
“Perhaps you will sit down,” said the landlady. She led Spargo into a room which opened out upon a garden; in it two or three old ladies, evidently inmates, were sitting. The landlady left Spargo to sit with them and to amuse himself by watching them knit or sew or read the papers, and he wondered if they always did these things every day, and if they would go on doing them until a day would come when they would do them no more, and he was beginning to feel very dreary when the door opened and a woman entered whom Spargo, after one sharp glance at her, decided to be a person who was undoubtedly out of the common. And as she slowly walked across the room towards him he let his first glance lengthen into a look of steady inspection.

The woman whom Spargo thus narrowly inspected was of very remarkable appearance. She was almost masculine; she stood nearly six feet in height; she was of a masculine gait and tread, and spare, muscular, and athletic. What at once struck Spargo about her face was the strange contrast between her dark eyes and her white hair; the hair, worn in abundant coils round a well-shaped head, was of the most snowy whiteness; the eyes of a real coal-blackness, as were also the eyebrows above them. The features were well-cut and of a striking firmness; the jaw square and determined. And Spargo’s first thought on taking all this in was that Miss Baylis seemed to have been fitted by Nature to be a prison wardress, or the matron of a hospital, or the governess of an unruly girl, and he began to wonder if he would ever manage to extract anything out of those firmly-locked lips.

Miss Baylis, on her part, looked Spargo over as if she was half-minded to order him to instant execution. And Spargo was so impressed by her that he made a profound bow and found a difficulty in finding his tongue.

“Mr. Spargo?” she said in a deep voice which seemed peculiarly suited to her. “Of, I see, the *Watchman*? You wish to speak to me?”

Spargo again bowed in silence. She signed him to the window near which they were standing.

“Open the casement, if you please,” she commanded him. “We will walk in the garden. This is not private.”

===Chinese===
“您请坐吧。”房东太太说。她把斯帕戈领进一间朝着花园开的屋子；屋里坐着两三位老妇人，一望便知是寄宿在此的住客。房东太太把斯帕戈撇在那里与她们同坐，让他瞧着她们织毛线、做针线、读报纸以自遣。他心中暗想，不知她们是否日日如此，不知是否会一直这样做下去，直到有一天再也做不动为止。他正开始觉得百无聊赖之际，门开了，进来一位妇人；斯帕戈对她利落地一瞥，便断定此人绝非等闲之辈。她缓缓穿过屋子朝他走来，他把那最初的一瞥拉长成了持续的端详。

斯帕戈如此细细端详的这位妇人，相貌极为不凡。她几乎带着男子气概：身高近六英尺，步态与脚步都是男子式的，身量清瘦，筋骨结实，一副善运动的体格。她的脸最先令斯帕戈注目的，是黑眼睛与白头发之间那种奇异的对照：那头发在端正的头型上盘成丰厚的发髻，白得雪也似；那双眼睛却是真正的煤玉之黑，眼睛上方的眉毛也一样乌黑。五官轮廓分明，透着惊人的坚毅；下颌方方正正，果决有力。斯帕戈把这一切看在眼里，头一个念头便是：造化分明是照着女狱监、医院护士长或者管教顽劣千金的女家庭教师的模子，造出了贝利斯小姐这样一个人。他随即开始犯嘀咕，自己究竟办不办得成，能从那两片紧闭的嘴唇里掏出点什么来。

贝利斯小姐这边呢，也把斯帕戈从头到脚打量了一遍，那神情仿佛恨不得当场下令把他处决。斯帕戈被她震住了，深深鞠了一躬，一时竟开不了口。

“斯帕戈先生？”她开口说道，嗓音低沉，似乎与她其人其貌相配得出奇。“原来——我看看——是《守望报》的？你想同我说话？”

斯帕戈又不声不响地鞠了一躬。她示意他到两人正站着的近旁那扇窗前。

“劳驾，把窗子打开。”她吩咐道。“我们到花园里走走。这屋里说话不私密。”

===Original===
Spargo obediently obeyed her orders; she swept through the opened window and he followed her. It was not until they had reached the bottom of the garden that she spoke again.

“I understand that you desire to ask me some question about John Maitland, of Market Milcaster?” she said. “Before you put it, I must ask you a question. Do you wish any reply I may give you for publication?”

“Not without your permission,” replied Spargo. “I should not think of publishing anything you may tell me except with your express permission.”

She looked at him gloomily, seemed to gather an impression of his good faith, and nodded her head.

“In that case,” she said, “what do you want to ask?”

“I have lately had reason for making certain enquiries about John Maitland,” answered Spargo. “I suppose you read the newspapers and possibly the *Watchman*, Miss Baylis?”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈恭顺地照办了；她一掠身穿过敞开的窗子，他跟在后面。直到走到花园尽头，她才重新开口。

“我明白了，你想问我一些关于马基特·米尔卡斯特的约翰·梅特兰的事？”她说。“在你开口之前，我得先问你一句：我给出的任何回答，你是否打算拿去发表？”

“没有您的许可，决不会。”斯帕戈答道。“除非得到您的明确允许，否则您对我说的一切，我根本不会考虑拿去发表。”

她阴沉沉地看了他一阵，似乎掂量出了他的一片诚意，点了点头。

“既然如此，”她说，“你想问什么？”

“我近来有缘由，就约翰·梅特兰做了一番查访。”斯帕戈答道，“我想您是读报纸的吧，兴许还读《守望报》，贝利斯小姐？”

===Original===
But Miss Baylis shook her head.

“I read no newspapers,” she said. “I have no interest in the affairs of the world. I have work which occupies all my time: I give my whole devotion to it.”

“Then you have not recently heard of what is known as the Marbury case﻿—a case of a man who was found murdered?” asked Spargo.

“I have not,” she answered. “I am not likely to hear such things.”

Spargo suddenly realized that the power of the Press is not quite as great nor as far-reaching as very young journalists hold it to be, and that there actually are, even in London, people who can live quite cheerfully without a newspaper. He concealed his astonishment and went on.

“Well,” he said, “I believe that the murdered man, known to the police as John Marbury, was, in reality, your brother-in-law, John Maitland. In fact, Miss Baylis, I’m absolutely certain of it!”

===Chinese===
贝利斯小姐却摇了摇头。

“我不读报纸。”她说，“世上之事，我一概没有兴趣。我有工作，占去了我的全部时间：我把全副心力都奉献给它。”

“那么，近来人称马伯里案的那桩案子——就是一个男人遭人谋杀的案子——您也没有听说了？”斯帕戈问。

“没有。”她答道，“这类事也传不进我耳朵里来。”

斯帕戈这才恍然明白：报界的威力，并不像稚气未脱的年轻记者们所想的那样大、那样无远弗届；哪怕在伦敦，也当真有不读一份报纸也能过得欢欢喜喜的人。他掩住讶异，接着往下说。

“是这样，”他说，“我相信那个被害人——警方称之为约翰·马伯里的——其实就是您的妹夫约翰·梅特兰。事实上，贝利斯小姐，我对此确信无疑！”

===Original===
He made this declaration with some emphasis, and looked at his stern companion to see how she was impressed. But Miss Baylis showed no sign of being impressed.

“I can quite believe that, Mr. Spargo,” she said coldly. “It is no surprise to me that John Maitland should come to such an end. He was a thoroughly bad and unprincipled man, who brought the most terrible disgrace on those who were, unfortunately, connected with him. He was likely to die a bad man’s death.”

“I may ask you a few questions about him?” suggested Spargo in his most insinuating manner.

“You may, so long as you do not drag my name into the papers,” she replied. “But pray, how do you know that I have the sad shame of being John Maitland’s sister-in-law?”

“I found that out at Market Milcaster,” said Spargo. “The photographer told me﻿—Cooper.”

“Ah!” she exclaimed.

===Chinese===
他这话说得颇为着力，一面望着他那位冷面的同伴，看她受到怎样的触动。可贝利斯小姐没有露出丝毫被打动的迹象。

“这一点我完全信得过，斯帕戈先生。”她冷冷地说，“约翰·梅特兰落得这般下场，我毫不意外。他是个彻头彻尾、毫无操行的坏人，把可怕的耻辱带给了那些不幸与他沾亲带故的人。他这种人，原就免不了不得好死。”

“我可以就他问您几个问题么？”斯帕戈用他最婉转讨好的腔调试探道。

“可以，只要你不把我的名字拖进报纸里。”她答道，“不过请问，你是怎么知道我竟担着这份伤心的丑名——是约翰·梅特兰的大姨子的？”

“这是我在马基特·米尔卡斯特查访出来的。”斯帕戈说，“是那位照相师告诉我的——库珀。”

“啊！”她应了一声。

===Original===
“The questions I want to ask are very simple,” said Spargo. “But your answers may materially help me. You remember Maitland going to prison, of course?”

Miss Baylis laughed﻿—a laugh of scorn.

“Could I ever forget it?” she exclaimed.

“Did you ever visit him in prison?” asked Spargo.

“Visit him in prison!” she said indignantly. “Visits in prison are to be paid to those who deserve them, who are repentant; not to scoundrels who are hardened in their sin!”

“All right. Did you ever see him after he left prison?”

===Chinese===
“我要问的问题非常简单，”斯帕戈说，“可您的回答或许于我大有助益。您当然记得梅特兰入狱的事？”

贝利斯小姐笑了——那是轻蔑的一笑。

“我能忘得了么！”她叫道。

“您可曾去狱中探视过他？”斯帕戈问。

“去狱中探他！”她愤然道，“狱中的探视，是留给那些尚存悔意之人的；不是留给那些在罪孽里顽冥不化的恶棍的！”

“好罢。他出狱之后，您见过他么？”

===Original===
“I saw him, for he forced himself upon me﻿—I could not help myself. He was in my presence before I was aware that he had even been released.”

“What did he come for?” asked Spargo.

“To ask for his son﻿—who had been in my charge,” she replied.

“That’s a thing I want to know about,” said Spargo. “Do you know what a certain lot of people in Market Milcaster say to this day, Miss Baylis?﻿—they say that you were in at the game with Maitland; that you had a lot of the money placed in your charge; that when Maitland went to prison, you took the child away, first to Brighton, then abroad﻿—disappeared with him﻿—and that you made a home ready for Maitland when he came out. That’s what’s said by some people in Market Milcaster.”

Miss Baylis’s stern lips curled.

“People in Market Milcaster!” she exclaimed. “All the people I ever knew in Market Milcaster had about as many brains between them as that cat on the wall there. As for making a home for John Maitland, I would have seen him die in the gutter, of absolute want, before I would have given him a crust of dry bread!”

===Chinese===
“见过，因为是他硬闯到我跟前来的——我拦不住他。他人已到了我面前，我才知道他原来已经放出来了。”

“他来做什么？”斯帕戈问。

“来要他的儿子——那孩子一直由我照管。”她答道。

“这正是我想弄明白的事。”斯帕戈说，“贝利斯小姐，您可知道马基特·米尔卡斯特有那么一伙人，直到今天还在怎么说么？——他们说，您当初与梅特兰是同伙；说那笔钱有一大笔就托在您手里保管；说梅特兰一入狱，您便把孩子带走了，先到布莱顿，后又出国——带着他消失无踪——还说您备下了一个家，等梅特兰出狱来住。马基特·米尔卡斯特有些人说的就是这些。”

贝利斯小姐那两片严峻的嘴唇撇了撇。

“马基特·米尔卡斯特的人！”她叫道，“我在马基特·米尔卡斯特认得的那些人，把脑筋全加在一起，也抵不过那边墙头上的那只猫。至于给约翰·梅特兰安家——他就是穷死饿死在阴沟里，我也不会给他一口干面包皮！”

===Original===
“You appear to have a terrible dislike of this man,” observed Spargo, astonished at her vehemence.

“I had﻿—and I have,” she answered. “He tricked my sister into a marriage with him when he knew that she would rather have married an honest man who worshipped her; he treated her with quiet, infernal cruelty; he robbed her and me of the small fortunes our father left us.”

“Ah!” said Spargo. “Well, so you say Maitland came to you, when he came out of prison, to ask for his boy. Did he take the boy?”

“No﻿—the boy was dead.”

“Dead, eh? Then I suppose Maitland did not stop long with you?”

Miss Baylis laughed her scornful laugh.

===Chinese===
“您对这个人似乎恨得厉害。”斯帕戈说，她的激烈令他暗暗吃惊。

“从前恨——如今还恨。”她答道，“他明知我妹妹宁愿嫁给一个把她奉若神明的老实人，却使了手段骗她成婚；他待她冷酷歹毒，却不动声色；他把我们父亲留给姐妹俩的那点薄产，从她和我手里夺了个精光。”

“原来如此！”斯帕戈说，“好，那么照您所说，梅特兰出狱之后来找您，是要他的儿子。他把孩子带走了么？”

“没有——那孩子已经死了。”

“死了，嗯？那么梅特兰在您这儿想必没有久留吧？”

贝利斯小姐又发出她那轻蔑的一笑。

===Original===
“I showed him the door!” she said.

“Well, did he tell you that he was going to Australia?” enquired Spargo.

“I should not have listened to anything that he told me, Mr. Spargo,” she answered.

“Then, in short,” said Spargo, “you never heard of him again?”

“I never heard of him again,” she declared passionately, “and I only hope that what you tell me is true, and that Marbury really was Maitland!”

===Chinese===
“我下了逐客令！”她说。

“那么，他可曾告诉过您，说他要去澳大利亚？”斯帕戈问道。

“他跟我说什么我也不会听的，斯帕戈先生。”她答道。

“那么，简而言之，”斯帕戈说，“您从此再没有听到过他的音讯？”

“从此再没有听到过。”她激昂地宣称，“我巴不得你说的是真话，巴不得马伯里当真就是梅特兰！”

## XXIV Mother Gutch / 第二十四章 格奇大妈

===Original===

Spargo, having exhausted the list of questions which he had thought out on his way to Bayswater, was about to take his leave of Miss Baylis, when a new idea suddenly occurred to him, and he turned back to that formidable lady.

“I’ve just thought of something else,” he said. “I told you that I’m certain Marbury was Maitland, and that he came to a sad end﻿—murdered.”

“And I’ve told you,” she replied scornfully, “that in my opinion no end could be too bad for him.”

“Just so﻿—I understand you,” said Spargo. “But I didn’t tell you that he was not only murdered but robbed﻿—robbed of probably a good deal. There’s good reason to believe that he had securities, bank notes, loose diamonds, and other things on him to the value of a large amount. He’d several thousand pounds when he left Coolumbidgee, in New South Wales, where he’d lived quietly for some years.”

Miss Baylis smiled sourly.

“What’s all this to me?” she asked.

===Chinese===

斯帕戈（Spargo）把来贝斯沃特（Bayswater）路上想好的一串问题问了个尽，正要向贝利斯（Baylis）小姐告辞，忽然又生出一个新念头，便回身转向这位令人生畏的老小姐。

“我刚又想起一件事，”他说，“我告诉过您，我确信马伯里（Marbury）就是梅特兰（Maitland），而且他落了个凄惨的下场——被人谋杀了。”

“我也告诉过您了，”她轻蔑地答道，“依我看，他遭什么下场都不算太坏。”

“正是——这我明白，”斯帕戈说，“可我没告诉过您：他不但被谋杀，还被洗劫了——弄不好被劫走了不少。有充分的理由相信，他身上带着证券、钞票、散装的钻石，还有别的值一大笔钱的东西。他离开新南威尔士的库伦比奇（Coolumbidgee）时——他在那儿悄没声儿住了好些年——身上带着好几千镑。”

贝利斯小姐酸溜溜地一笑。

“这跟我有什么相干？”她问。

===Original===

“Possibly nothing. But you see, that money, those securities, may be recovered. And as the boy you speak of is dead, there surely must be somebody who’s entitled to the lot. It’s worth having, Miss Baylis, and there’s strong belief on the part of the police that it will turn up.”

This was a bit of ingenious bluff on the part of Spargo; he watched its effect with keen eyes. But Miss Baylis was adamant, and she looked as scornful as ever.

“I say again what’s all that to me?” she exclaimed.

“Well, but hadn’t the dead boy any relatives on his father’s side?” asked Spargo. “I know you’re his aunt on the mother’s side, and as you’re indifferent perhaps, I can find some on the other side. It’s very easy to find all these things out, you know.”

===Chinese===

“也许不相干。可是您瞧，那笔钱、那些证券，兴许还能追回来。既然您说的那孩子死了，总该有人有权继承这满满一笔吧。这可是笔值得到手的钱财，贝利斯小姐，警方也坚信它终会现身。”

这是斯帕戈机灵的一手唬人；他目光锐利地盯着这番话的效力。可贝利斯小姐毫不动摇，那副轻蔑的神情分毫未改。

“我再说一遍，那跟我有什么相干？”她扬声道。

“那么，那死去的孩子，他父亲那边就没有亲眷么？”斯帕戈问，“我知道您是他母系的姨妈；您既然无所谓，父系那边的人，或许我能找出几个来。您知道，这类事儿查起来容易得很。”

===Original===

Miss Baylis, who had begun to stalk back to the house in gloomy and majestic fashion, and had let Spargo see plainly that this part of the interview was distasteful to her, suddenly paused in her stride and glared at the young journalist.

“Easy to find all these things out?” she repeated.

Spargo caught, or fancied he caught, a note of anxiety in her tone. He was quick to turn his fancy to practical purpose.

“Oh, easy enough!” he said. “I could find out all about Maitland’s family through that boy. Quite, quite easily!”

===Chinese===

贝利斯小姐先前已经阴沉着脸、端足架子往宅子里大步走去，明明白白让斯帕戈看出，访谈的这一节她老大不情愿，这时却蓦地收住脚步，瞪住这年轻记者。

“查起来容易得很？”她重复道。

斯帕戈从她口气里捕捉到——或者自以为捕捉到——一丝焦灼。他立刻把这感觉化作了实际的用处。

“哦，容易得很！”他说，“顺着那孩子，我能把梅特兰的家世查个底儿掉。容易得很，容易得很！”

===Original===

Miss Baylis had stopped now, and stood glaring at him. “How?” she demanded.

“I’ll tell you,” said Spargo with cheerful alacrity. “It is, of course, the easiest thing in the world to trace all about his short life. I suppose I can find the register of his birth at Market Milcaster, and you, of course, will tell me where he died. By the by, when did he die, Miss Baylis?”

But Miss Baylis was going on again to the house.

===Chinese===

贝利斯小姐这时已站定，瞪着他。“怎么个查法？”她质问道。

“我告诉您，”斯帕戈兴冲冲、麻利地答道，“追查他那短短一生的一切，自然是天底下顶容易的事。我想我在马基特·米尔卡斯特（Market Milcaster）就能查到他的出生登记；至于他死在哪儿，您当然会告诉我。顺便问一句，他是什么时候死的，贝利斯小姐？”

不料贝利斯小姐又径自朝宅子里走去。

===Original===

“I shall tell you nothing more,” she said angrily. “I’ve told you too much already, and I believe all you’re here for is to get some news for your paper. But I will, at any rate tell you this﻿—when Maitland went to prison his child would have been defenceless but for me; he’d have had to go to the workhouse but for me; he hadn’t a single relation in the world but me, on either father’s or mother’s side. And even at my age, old woman as I am, I’d rather beg my bread in the street, I’d rather starve and die, than touch a penny piece that had come from John Maitland! That’s all.”

Then without further word, without offering to show Spargo the way out, she marched in at the open window and disappeared. And Spargo, knowing no other way, was about to follow her when he heard a sudden rustling sound in the shadow by which they had stood, and the next moment a queer, cracked, horrible voice, suggesting all sorts of things, said distinctly and yet in a whisper:

“Young man!”

===Chinese===

“我什么也不会再告诉您了，”她怒冲冲地说，“我已经说得太多了；我看您到这儿来，无非是给您那家报馆搜罗点新闻去。不过有一句话我倒愿意奉告——梅特兰下狱的时候，若不是我，他那孩子便孤苦无依；若不是我，他就得进济贫院；普天之下，不论父亲一边还是母亲一边，他除了我再无一个亲人。别看我这样年纪，老太婆一个，我宁可沿街讨饭，宁可饿死，也决不碰一枚从约翰·梅特兰（John Maitland）手里出来的便士！就是这样。”

说罢再无一言，也不招呼斯帕戈一声引他出去，她径自打敞开的窗口跨进去，一晃就不见了。斯帕戈不认得旁的路，正要跟进去，忽听得他们方才站立的那片暗影里响起一阵窸窣，紧跟着一个怪诞、嘶哑破裂、骇人的嗓音——一个叫人联想到种种不祥的嗓音——字字分明、却又是耳语似的说道：

“年轻人！”

===Original===

Spargo turned and stared at the privet hedge behind him. It was thick and bushy, and in its full summer green, but it seemed to him that he saw a nondescript shape behind. “Who’s there?” he demanded. “Somebody listening?”

There was a curious cackle of laughter from behind the hedge; then the cracked, husky voice spoke again.

“Young man, don’t you move or look as if you were talking to anybody. Do you know where the ‘King of Madagascar’ public-house is in this quarter of the town, young man?”

“No!” answered Spargo. “Certainly not!”

“Well, anybody’ll tell you when you get outside, young man,” continued the queer voice of the unseen person. “Go there, and wait at the corner by the ‘King of Madagascar,’ and I’ll come there to you at the end of half an hour. Then I’ll tell you something, young man﻿—I’ll tell you something. Now run away, young man, run away to the ‘King of Madagascar’﻿—I’m coming!”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈回身盯住背后的女贞树篱。树篱密密匝匝，正是盛夏的浓绿，可他仿佛瞧见后头有个说不出名堂的影子。“谁在那儿？”他喝道，“有人偷听么？”

树篱后传来一串古怪的咯咯笑声；随后那嘶哑破裂的嗓音又开口了。

“年轻人，你别动，也别装出在跟人说话的样子。你可知道‘马达加斯加国王’（King of Madagascar）酒馆在这城区的什么地方，年轻人？”

“不知道！”斯帕戈答道，“压根儿不知道！”

“咳，年轻人，你一出这园子，随便碰见谁都能指给你，”那看不见的人的古怪嗓音接着说，“上那儿去，在‘马达加斯加国王’近旁的拐角上等着，半个钟头后我就到那儿找你。到时候我要告诉你一件事，年轻人——我要告诉你一件事。这就去吧，年轻人，快到‘马达加斯加国王’去——我就来！”

===Original===

The voice ended in low, horrible cachinnation which made Spargo feel queer. But he was young enough to be in love with adventure, and he immediately turned on his heel without so much as a glance at the privet hedge, and went across the garden and through the house, and let himself out at the door. And at the next corner of the square he met a policeman and asked him if he knew where the “King of Madagascar” was.

“First to the right, second to the left,” answered the policeman tersely. “You can’t miss it anywhere round there﻿—it’s a landmark.”

And Spargo found the landmark﻿—a great, square-built tavern﻿—easily, and he waited at a corner of it wondering what he was going to see, and intensely curious about the owner of the queer voice, with all its suggestions of he knew not what. And suddenly there came up to him an old woman and leered at him in a fashion that made him suddenly realize how dreadful old age may be.

===Chinese===

那嗓音在一阵低低的、骇人的狂笑里收了场，听得斯帕戈心里发毛。但他到底年轻，正贪恋冒险，当即一转身，连女贞树篱也不再看一眼，穿过花园，穿过宅子，自己开门出去了。到了广场的下一个转角，他遇见一名警察，便问他可知道“马达加斯加国王”在什么地方。

“先右拐，再左拐，”警察简截地答道，“那一带您走到哪儿都错不了——那是个地标。”

斯帕戈果然轻而易举地找到了这个地标——一座方正的大酒馆——便在它的一角等候，心里揣度着自己将会见到什么，对那古怪嗓音的主人好奇到了极点——那嗓音里透着的种种意味，他捉摸不透又忍不住去猜。蓦地，一个老妇人朝他走上前来，朝他递过来那么一种邪睨的眼神，让他猛然醒悟：老境竟能这般可怕。

===Original===

Spargo had never seen such an old woman as this in his life. She was dressed respectably, better than respectably. Her gown was good; her bonnet was smart; her smaller fittings were good. But her face was evil; it showed unmistakable signs of a long devotion to the bottle; the old eyes leered and ogled, the old lips were wicked. Spargo felt a sense of disgust almost amounting to nausea, but he was going to hear what the old harridan had to say and he tried not to look what he felt.

“Well?” he said, almost roughly. “Well?”

“Well, young man, there you are,” said his new acquaintance. “Let us go inside, young man; there’s a quiet little place where a lady can sit and take her drop of gin﻿—I’ll show you. And if you’re good to me, I’ll tell you something about that cat that you were talking to just now. But you’ll give me a little matter to put in my pocket, young man? Old ladies like me have a right to buy little comforts, you know, little comforts.”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈平生没见过这样的老妇人。她穿得体面，比体面还要好些。裙子是上好的，帽子是时新的，零星配饰件件考究。可她那张脸是恶的：分明看得出多年沉溺杯中之物的印记；一双老眼淫邪地瞟来瞟去，两片老唇透着歹毒。斯帕戈只觉一阵厌恶，几乎要泛恶心，但他打定主意要听听这老妖婆讲些什么，便竭力不把心里的感觉摆在脸上。

“怎么样？”他近乎粗鲁地说，“怎么样？”

“嗳，年轻人，你可来了，”这位新相识说，“咱们进去吧，年轻人；里头有间清静的小屋，一位女士可以坐下来喝她那一小口金酒——我领你去。你要是待我好，我就告诉你点儿刚才跟你说话的那个刁婆的底细。不过你得给我一点小意思装装口袋吧，年轻人？像我这样的老太太，有权买些小小的舒服，你说是吧，小小的舒服。”

===Original===

Spargo followed this extraordinary person into a small parlour within; the attendant who came in response to a ring showed no astonishment at her presence; he also seemed to know exactly what she required, which was a certain brand of gin, sweetened, and warm. And Spargo watched her curiously as with shaking hand she pushed up the veil which hid little of her wicked old face, and lifted the glass to her mouth with a zest which was not thirst but pure greed of liquor. Almost instantly he saw a new light steal into her eyes, and she laughed in a voice that grew clearer with every sound she made.

“Ah, young man!” she said with a confidential nudge of the elbow that made Spargo long to get up and fly. “I wanted that! It’s done me good. When I’ve finished that, you’ll pay for another for me﻿—and perhaps another? They’ll do me still more good. And you’ll give me a little matter of money, won’t you, young man?”

“Not till I know what I’m giving it for,” replied Spargo.

“You’ll be giving it because I’m going to tell you that if it’s made worth my while I can tell you, or somebody that sent you, more about Jane Baylis than anybody in the world. I’m not going to tell you that now, young man﻿—I’m sure you don’t carry in your pocket what I shall want for my secret, not you, by the look of you! I’m only going to show you that I have the secret. Eh?”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈跟着这个异乎寻常的人物走进里面一间小雅座；铃声一响，侍者应声而至，对她光临毫无讶异之色，而且对她要什么仿佛一清二楚——某个牌子的金酒，加了糖，温过热。斯帕戈好奇地打量着她：她用一只颤抖的手掀起那层面纱——面纱本也遮不住她那张邪恶老脸的多少——以一种并非口渴、纯属酒馋的贪婪，把酒杯送到嘴边。几乎转眼之间，他就看见一道新的光悄悄溜进她眼里；她笑出声来，嗓音一发比一发清亮。

“啊，年轻人！”她说着，胳膊肘亲热地一搡，搡得斯帕戈直想起身飞逃，“我就缺这一口！它给我提了神。这杯下了肚，你还得再给我付一杯——兴许再一杯？那更能给我提神。你还会给我一点小钱的，是不是，年轻人？”

“先得让我知道这钱为什么给，”斯帕戈答道。

“你之所以要给，是因为我要告诉你：只要这事够我合算，关于简·贝利斯（Jane Baylis），我能告诉你的——或者告诉你那位派你来的人——比世上任何人都要多。我这会儿还不打算说破，年轻人——我敢说你口袋里揣的，还抵不上我这个秘密要的价码，看你这副样子就知道！我这会儿只消让你瞧瞧，我手里有这么个秘密。怎么样？”

===Original===

“Who are you?” asked Spargo.

The woman leered and chuckled. “What are you going to give me, young man?” she asked.

Spargo put his fingers in his pocket and pulled out two half-sovereigns.

“Look here,” he said, showing his companion the coins, “if you can tell me anything of importance you shall have these. But no trifling, now. And no wasting of time. If you have anything to tell, out with it!”

===Chinese===

“你是谁？”斯帕戈问。

老妇人斜着眼笑，咯咯地乐。“你打算给我点儿什么，年轻人？”她问。

斯帕戈把手指伸进口袋，掏出两枚半索维林金币（half-sovereign，每枚值半镑）。

“瞧这儿，”他把金币亮给同伴看，说道，“你要是能告诉我什么要紧的事，这两枚就归你。不过可不许耍花招，也不许耽误工夫。有什么要讲的，快讲！”

===Original===

The woman stretched out a trembling, claw-like hand.

“But let me hold one of those, young man!” she implored. “Let me hold one of the beautiful bits of gold. I shall tell you all the better if I hold one of them. Let me﻿—there’s a good young gentleman.”

Spargo gave her one of the coins, and resigned himself to his fate, whatever it might be.

“You won’t get the other unless you tell something,” he said. “Who are you, anyway?”

===Chinese===

老妇人伸出一只打颤的、爪子似的手。

“可先让我攥上一枚，年轻人！”她央告道，“让我攥上一枚这么漂亮的小金币。攥着一枚，我讲得还要好些。给我吧——真是个好心的年轻绅士。”

斯帕戈给了她一枚，听天由命，管它后事如何。

“你不说出点儿东西，那另一枚可没门儿，”他说，“你究竟是谁？”

===Original===

The woman, who had begun mumbling and chuckling over the half-sovereign, grinned horribly.

“At the boardinghouse yonder, young man, they call me Mother Gutch,” she answered; “but my proper name is Mrs. Sabina Gutch, and once upon a time I was a good-looking young woman. And when my husband died I went to Jane Baylis as housekeeper, and when she retired from that and came to live in that boardinghouse where we live now, she was forced to bring me with her and to keep me. Why had she to do that, young man?”

“Heaven knows!” answered Spargo.

===Chinese===

那老妇人本来正攥着那枚半索维林又是咕咕哝哝、又是咯咯直乐，闻言咧出一个狞笑。

“在那边那家寄宿公寓里，年轻人，她们管我叫格奇大妈（Mother Gutch），”她答道，“可我正式的名姓是萨比娜·格奇太太（Mrs. Sabina Gutch），想当年，我也是个标致的年轻女人呢。我男人死后，我就到简·贝利斯手底下当管家；后来她辞了那份差事，搬进如今我们同住的那家寄宿公寓，她没法子，只好把我一并带上，还得养着我。她为什么非这样不可呢，年轻人？”

“天晓得！”斯帕戈答道。

===Original===

“Because I’ve got a hold on her, young man﻿—I’ve got a secret of hers,” continued Mother Gutch. “She’d be scared to death if she knew I’d been behind that hedge and had heard what she said to you, and she’d be more than scared if she knew that you and I were here, talking. But she’s grown hard and near with me, and she won’t give me a penny to get a drop of anything with, and an old woman like me has a right to her little comforts, and if you’ll buy the secret, young man, I’ll split on her, there and then, when you pay the money.”

“Before I talk about buying any secret,” said Spargo, “you’ll have to prove to me that you’ve a secret to sell that’s worth my buying.”

“And I will prove it!” said Mother Gutch with sudden fierceness. “Touch the bell, and let me have another glass, and then I’ll tell you. Now,” she went on, more quietly﻿—Spargo noticed that the more she drank, the more rational she became, and that her nerves seemed to gain strength and her whole appearance to be improved﻿—“now, you came to her to find out about her brother-in-law, Maitland, that went to prison, didn’t you?”

===Chinese===

“因为我攥着她的把柄，年轻人——我手里有她一桩秘密，”格奇大妈接着说，“她要是知道我方才就在那树篱后头，把她对你说的话听了个全，准得吓个半死；她要是知道你我此刻正坐在这儿说话，那就更吓破了胆。可她对我越来越又刻薄又抠门，一个子儿也不肯给我打口酒喝；像我这样的老太婆，有权图点小小的舒服。你要是肯买这个秘密，年轻人，只消钱一过手，我当场就把她兜底抖搂出来。”

“在我谈买什么秘密之前，”斯帕戈说，“你得先向我证明，你手里这秘密值得我买。”

“我证明给你看！”格奇大妈蓦地凶了起来，“按铃，再给我来一杯，我就告诉你。喏，”她接着往下说，口气平和了些——斯帕戈注意到，她喝得越多，神志越见清明，神经似乎也长了力气，整个人的样子都见好——“喏，你去找她，是要打听她那位坐过牢的妹夫梅特兰的事，是不是？”

===Original===

“Well?” demanded Spargo.

“And about that boy of his?” she continued.

“You heard all that was said,” answered Spargo. “I’m waiting to hear what you have to say.”

But Mother Gutch was resolute in having her own way. She continued her questions:

“And she told you that Maitland came and asked for the boy, and that she told him the boy was dead, didn’t she?” she went on.

===Chinese===

“怎么样？”斯帕戈追问。

“还有他那孩子的事？”她接着问。

“说的话你都听见了，”斯帕戈答道，“我等着听你要说什么。”

可格奇大妈打定主意非照自己的路数来。她自顾自地问下去：

“她告诉你梅特兰来讨过那孩子、她回他说孩子死了，是不是？”她往下说。

===Original===

“Well?” said Spargo despairingly. “She did. What then?”

Mother Gutch took an appreciative pull at her glass and smiled knowingly. “What then?” she chuckled. “All lies, young man, the boy isn’t dead﻿—any more than I am. And my secret is﻿—”

“Well?” demanded Spargo impatiently. “What is it?”

“This!” answered Mother Gutch, digging her companion in the ribs, “I know what she did with him!”

===Chinese===

“怎么样？”斯帕戈绝望地说，“她是这么说的。那又怎样？”

格奇大妈美滋滋地呷了一大口，一副心照不宣的笑。“那又怎样？”她咯咯笑道，“全是谎话，年轻人，那孩子没死——就像我也没死一样。我的秘密就是——”

“怎么样？”斯帕戈不耐烦地催问，“是什么？”

“这个！”格奇大妈答道，一胳膊肘捅进同伴的肋条，“她把那孩子怎么打发了，我知道！”

## XI Mr. Aylmore Is Questioned / 第十一章 艾尔莫尔先生受到盘问

===Original===

It seemed to Spargo as he sat listening to the proceedings at the adjourned inquest next day that the whole story of what was now world-famous as the Middle Temple Murder Case was being reiterated before him for the thousandth time. There was not a detail of the story with which he had not become familiar to fullness. The first proceeding before the coroner had been of a merely formal nature; these were thorough and exhaustive; the representative of the Crown and twelve good men and true of the City of London were there to hear and to find out and to arrive at a conclusion as to how the man known as John Marbury came by his death. And although he knew all about it, Spargo found himself tabulating the evidence in a professional manner, and noting how each successive witness contributed, as it were, a chapter to the story. The story itself ran quite easily, naturally, consecutively—you could make it in sections. And Spargo, sitting merely to listen, made them:

===Chinese===

第二天，斯帕戈坐在延期续审的验尸审讯庭上听着堂上的种种进程，只觉得这桩如今以“中殿谋杀案”之名闻名于世的整个故事，正在他面前被人第一千遍重述。故事里的每一个细节，他都已熟稔到无以复加。先前在验尸官面前的第一堂程序不过是走个形式；这一回却是彻底而详尽；王室代表和伦敦城十二位善良正直的市民齐集于此，要听取、要查明、要就那个名叫约翰·马伯里的人究竟如何丧命得出一个结论。尽管这一切他早已了然于胸，斯帕戈却发觉自己正以职业习惯把证词一条条开列成表，留意着每一个相继出庭的证人如何宛如为这个故事又添上一章。这故事本身讲来顺当、自然、一环扣一环——你尽可以把它分作一节一节。斯帕戈分明只是坐在那里听，却已经在心里把它们分好了：

===Original===

1. The Temple porter and Constable Driscoll proved the finding of the body.
2. The police surgeon testified as to the cause of death—the man had been struck down from behind by a blow, a terrible blow—from some heavy instrument, and had died immediately.
3. The police and the mortuary officials proved that when the body was examined nothing was found in the clothing but the now famous scrap of grey paper.
4. Rathbury proved that by means of the dead man’s new fashionable cloth cap, bought at Fiskie’s well-known shop in the West-End, he traced Marbury to the Anglo-Orient Hotel in the Waterloo District.
5. Mr. and Mrs. Walters gave evidence of the arrival of Marbury at the Anglo-Orient Hotel, and of his doings while he was in and about there.
6. The purser of the S.S. *Wambarino* proved that Marbury sailed from Melbourne to Southampton on that ship, excited no remark, behaved himself like any other well-regulated passenger, and left the *Wambarino* at Southampton early in the morning of what was to be the last day of his life in just the ordinary manner.
7. Mr. Criedir gave evidence of his rencontre with Marbury in the matter of the stamps.
8. Mr. Myerst told of Marbury’s visit to the Safe Deposit, and further proved that the box which he placed there proved, on official examination, to be empty.
9. William Webster retold the story of his encounter with Marbury in one of the vestibules of the House of Commons, and of his witnessing the meeting between him and the gentleman whom he (Webster) now knew to be Mr. Aylmore, a Member of Parliament.

===Chinese===

1. 中殿律师学院的门房和德里斯科尔警员证实了发现尸体的经过。
2. 警局法医就死因作证：死者是被人从背后一记重击打倒的——可怕的一击——出自某种沉重的器具，当场毙命。
3. 警方和停尸间管理人员证实，验看尸体时，死者衣物中除那片如今声名远播的灰纸外，一无所获。
4. 拉思伯里证实，他凭借死者那顶购自西区知名菲斯基帽店的新式时兴呢帽，一路追索，把马伯里追到了滑铁卢区的盎格鲁-东方旅馆。
5. 沃尔特斯先生和沃尔特斯太太就马伯里投宿盎格鲁-东方旅馆的经过，以及他在旅馆里外期间的行止提供了证词。
6. “万巴里诺”号轮船的事务长证实，马伯里搭乘该船自墨尔本航行至南安普顿，一路未惹人注目，举止同任何一位安分守己的乘客无异，并在他生命最后一天的那个清晨，以再寻常不过的方式在南安普顿离船上岸。
7. 克里迪尔先生就自己在邮票一事上与马伯里的那番邂逅作了证。
8. 迈尔斯特先生讲述了马伯里到保险寄存库存放物品的经过，并进一步证实，马伯里存放的那只匣子经官方开验，里面空无一物。
9. 威廉·韦伯斯特重述了他在下议院一处前厅与马伯里相遇的经过，并讲述他亲眼目睹马伯里与一位先生会面——他（韦伯斯特）如今知道，那位先生正是议员艾尔莫尔先生。

===Original===

All this led up to the appearance of Mr. Aylmore, M.P., in the witness-box. And Spargo knew and felt that it was that appearance for which the crowded court was waiting. Thanks to his own vivid and realistic specials in the *Watchman*, everybody there had already become well and thoroughly acquainted with the mass of evidence represented by the nine witnesses who had been in the box before Mr. Aylmore entered it. They were familiar, too, with the facts which Mr. Aylmore had permitted Spargo to print after the interview at the club, which Ronald Breton arranged. Why, then, the extraordinary interest which the Member of Parliament’s appearance aroused? For everybody was extraordinarily interested; from the Coroner downwards to the last man who had managed to squeeze himself into the last available inch of the public gallery, all who were there wanted to hear and see the man who met Marbury under such dramatic circumstances, and who went to his hotel with him, hobnobbed with him, gave him advice, walked out of the hotel with him for a stroll from which Marbury never returned. Spargo knew well why the interest was so keen—everybody knew that Aylmore was the only man who could tell the court anything really pertinent about Marbury; who he was, what he was after; what his life had been.

He looked round the court as the Member of Parliament entered the witness-box—a tall, handsome, perfectly-groomed man, whose beard was only slightly tinged with grey, whose figure was as erect as a well-drilled soldier’s, who carried about him an air of conscious power. Aylmore’s two daughters sat at a little distance away, opposite Spargo, with Ronald Breton in attendance upon them; Spargo had encountered their glance as they entered the court, and they had given him a friendly nod and smile. He had watched them from time to time; it was plain to him that they regarded the whole affair as a novel sort of entertainment; they might have been idlers in some Eastern bazaar, listening to the unfolding of many tales from the professional tale-tellers. Now, as their father entered the box, Spargo looked at them again; he saw nothing more than a little heightening of colour in their cheeks, a little brightening of their eyes.

===Chinese===

这一切都层层铺垫，直引出议员艾尔莫尔先生登上证人席。斯帕戈心里明白，座无虚席的法庭等的就是这一刻。多亏他自己发表在《守望报》上那些生动写实的特稿，在场众人对于艾尔莫尔之前九位证人所代表的那一大堆证据早已了然于胸。对于艾尔莫尔先生在罗纳德·布雷顿安排的俱乐部那次会面之后允许斯帕戈刊出的事实，他们也同样熟悉。那么，这位议员的出现何以激起如此非同寻常的关注？因为每个人兴致都异乎寻常地浓烈；从验尸官往下，直到最后一个把自己塞进旁听席仅剩寸隙的家伙，在场人人都想一闻一见这位在如此富有戏剧性的情形下与马伯里会面的人——此人跟他一起去了旅馆，同他亲密攀谈，给他出主意，又陪他走出旅馆散步，而马伯里这一去便再没有回来。斯帕戈很清楚这份兴趣为何如此炽烈——谁都知道，艾尔莫尔是唯一能就马伯里向法庭讲出任何真正要紧情况的人：他是谁，他要干什么，他过的都是什么样的日子。

这位议员走进证人席时，斯帕戈环视了一下法庭——一个身材高挑、相貌堂堂、修饰得一丝不苟的男人，胡须只微微染了些灰白，身姿挺拔如训练有素的士兵，周身透着一股自觉身负权势的气派。艾尔莫尔的两个女儿坐在不远处，正对着斯帕戈，罗纳德·布雷顿在旁陪伴她们；她们走进法庭时，斯帕戈与她们的目光相遇，她们朝他友好地点头微笑。他不时观察她们；他看得分明，她们把整桩事情当成了一种新鲜的消遣；她们就像东方集市上听职业说书人娓娓道来一个个故事的闲人。这时，她们的父亲走进证人席，斯帕戈又看了她们一眼；他瞧出的，不过是她们脸颊上微微泛起的一点红晕，眼睛里稍稍添的一点亮光。

===Original===

“All that they feel,” he thought, “is a bit of extra excitement at the idea that their father is mixed up in this delightful mystery. Um! Well—now how much is he mixed up?”

And he turned to the witness-box and from that moment never took his eyes off the man who now stood in it. For Spargo had ideas about the witness which he was anxious to develop.

===Chinese===

“她们所感到的，”他心想，“不过是想到父亲卷进了这桩妙不可言的疑案，才多了一分兴奋罢了。嗯！好——那么他卷得有多深呢？”

于是他转向证人席，从那一刻起，眼睛再也没有离开过站在席上的那个人。因为斯帕戈对这位证人已有几分想法，正急于深究下去。

===Original===

The folk who expected something immediately sensational in Mr. Aylmore’s evidence were disappointed. Aylmore, having been sworn, and asked a question or two by the Coroner, requested permission to tell, in his own way, what he knew of the dead man and of this sad affair; and having received that permission, he went on in a calm, unimpassioned manner to repeat precisely what he had told Spargo. It sounded a very plain, ordinary story. He had known Marbury many years ago. He had lost sight of him for—oh, quite twenty years. He had met him accidentally in one of the vestibules of the House of Commons on the evening preceding the murder. Marbury had asked his advice. Having no particular duty, and willing to do an old acquaintance a good turn, he had gone back to the Anglo-Orient Hotel with Marbury, had remained awhile with him in his room, examining his Australian diamonds, and had afterwards gone out with him. He had given him the advice he wanted; they had strolled across Waterloo Bridge; shortly afterwards they had parted. That was all he knew.

The court, the public, Spargo, everybody there, knew all this already. It had been in print, under a big headline, in the *Watchman*. Aylmore had now told it again; having told it, he seemed to consider that his next step was to leave the box and the court, and after a perfunctory question or two from the Coroner and the foreman of the jury he made a motion as if to step down. But Spargo, who had been aware since the beginning of the enquiry of the presence of a certain eminent counsel who represented the Treasury, cocked his eye in that gentleman’s direction, and was not surprised to see him rise in his well-known, apparently indifferent fashion, fix his monocle in his right eye, and glance at the tall figure in the witness-box.

===Chinese===

那些指望艾尔莫尔先生的证词立刻爆出轰动内容的人失望了。艾尔莫尔宣誓之后，回答了验尸官的一两个问题，随即请求允许他以自己的方式讲述他对死者以及对这桩惨案所知的一切；获准之后，他便以平静淡然的口气，一五一十地复述了他对斯帕戈讲过的那些话。听上去是一个再平常不过的故事。他多年前就认识马伯里。后来与他断了联系——哦，足足有二十年。案发前一天的傍晚，他在下议院一处前厅与他偶然相遇。马伯里向他讨主意。他手头没有要紧事，又乐意为老相识帮个忙，便随马伯里回到盎格鲁-东方旅馆，在他房间里盘桓了一阵，看了看他的澳大利亚钻石，随后又同他一起外出。他把对方想要的建议给了他；两人漫步走过滑铁卢桥；不久便分手了。他所知道的就是这些。

法庭、公众、斯帕戈，在场的每一个人，对此早已一清二楚。这些都登过报，顶着大字标题，印在《守望报》上。艾尔莫尔如今又讲了一遍；讲完之后，他似乎觉得下一步就该离开证人席和法庭了；验尸官和陪审团主席敷衍地问了一两个问题后，他便作势要走下席来。可是斯帕戈从审讯一开始就注意到，场中在座有一位代表财政部的显赫律师；此时他朝那位先生的方向瞇眼一望，果然不出所料，只见对方以他那种出了名的、貌似漫不经心的姿态站起身来，把单片眼镜扣进右眼，朝证人席里那个高大的身影瞥了一眼。

===Original===

“The fun is going to begin,” muttered Spargo.

The Treasury representative looked from Aylmore to the Coroner and made a jerky bow; from the Coroner to Aylmore and straightened himself. He looked like a man who is going to ask indifferent questions about the state of the weather, or how Smith’s wife was last time you heard of her, or if stocks are likely to rise or fall. But Spargo had heard this man before, and he knew many signs of his in voice and manner and glance.

===Chinese===

“好戏要开场了。”斯帕戈低声说。

那位财政部代表先从艾尔莫尔望向验尸官，生硬地欠了欠身；又从验尸官望回艾尔莫尔，随即挺直了身子。他那副模样，活像一个将要问些无关痛痒的问题的人——问天气如何啦，问上次听说史密斯太太身子可好啦，问股票看涨还是看跌啦。但斯帕戈从前听过这人发问，深知他声调、举止和眼神里的种种信号。

===Original===

“I want to ask you a few questions, Mr. Aylmore, about your acquaintanceship with the dead man. It was an acquaintanceship of some time ago?” began the suave, seemingly careless voice.

“A considerable time ago,” answered Aylmore.

“How long—roughly speaking?”

“I should say from twenty to twenty-two or three years.”

“Never saw him during that time until you met accidentally in the way you have described to us?”

“Never.”

“Ever heard from him?”

“No.”

===Chinese===

“艾尔莫尔先生，我想就您与死者的交往问您几个问题。那是一段相当久以前的交往吧？”一个圆滑、听着满不在乎的声音开了口。

“相当久以前。”艾尔莫尔答道。

“多久——大体说说？”

“我估计在二十年到二十二三年的样子。”

“在这期间您从未见过他，直到您像刚才对我们讲的那样偶然相遇？”

“从未见过。”

“得到过他的音讯吗？”

“没有。”

===Original===

“Ever heard of him?”

“No.”

“But when you met, you knew each other at once?”

“Well—almost at once.”

“Almost at once. Then, I take it, you were very well known to each other twenty or twenty-two years ago?”

“We were—yes, well known to each other.”

“Close friends?”

“I said we were acquaintances.”

===Chinese===

“听说过他的情况吗？”

“没有。”

“可是你们一见面，就立刻认出了彼此？”

“这个——差不多立刻。”

“差不多立刻。那么据我理解，二十或二十二年前，你们彼此是非常熟识的？”

“我们是——是的，彼此很熟识。”

“是深交吗？”

“我说了，我们不过是相识。”

===Original===

“Acquaintances. What was his name when you knew him at that time?”

“His name? It was—Marbury.”

“Marbury—the same name. Where did you know him?”

“I—oh, here in London.”

“What was he?”

“Do you mean—what was his occupation?”

“What was his occupation?”

“I believe he was concerned in financial matters.”

===Chinese===

“不过是相识。您当年认识他的时候，他叫什么名字？”

“他的名字？叫——马伯里。”

“马伯里——同一个名字。您是在哪儿认识他的？”

“我——哦，就在伦敦。”

“他是干什么的？”

“您的意思是——问他的职业？”

“他的职业是什么？”

“我想他是做金融买卖的。”

===Original===

“Concerned in financial matters. Had you dealings with him?”

“Well, yes—on occasions.”

“What was his business address in London?”

“I can’t remember that.”

“What was his private address?”

“That I never knew.”

“Where did you transact your business with him?”

“Well, we met, now and then.”

===Chinese===

“做金融买卖的。您同他有过交易往来吗？”

“嗯，有过——偶尔有过。”

“他在伦敦的营业地址是哪里？”

“这个我记不得了。”

“他的住址呢？”

“那个我从来不知道。”

“你们在什么地方办理你们的事务？”

“嗯，我们时不时见见面。”

===Original===

“Where? What place, office, resort?”

“I can’t remember particular places. Sometimes—in the City.”

“In the City. Where in the City? Mansion House, or Lombard Street, or St. Paul’s Churchyard, or the Old Bailey, or where?”

“I have recollections of meeting him outside the Stock Exchange.”

“Oh! Was he a member of that institution?”

“Not that I know of.”

“Were you?”

“Certainly not!”

===Chinese===

“在哪儿见的？什么地点、什么办公室，还是什么常去的场所？”

“具体地方我记不得了。有时——在伦敦城。”

“在伦敦城。伦敦城什么地方？市长官邸、朗伯德街、圣保罗教堂园，还是老贝利，还是别的哪儿？”

“我记得曾在证券交易所外面碰见过他。”

“哦！他是那家交易所的成员吗？”

“据我所知不是。”

“您是吗？”

“当然不是！”

===Original===

“What were the dealings that you had with him?”

“Financial dealings—small ones.”

“How long did your acquaintanceship with him last—what period did it extend over?”

“I should say about six months to nine months.”

“No more?”

“Certainly no more.”

“It was quite a slight acquaintanceship, then?”

“Oh, quite!”

===Chinese===

“您同他做的都是些什么交易？”

“金融交易——都是小笔的。”

“您同他的交往持续了多久——前后有多长一段时间？”

“我估计大约六个月到九个月。”

“没有更久？”

“肯定没有更久。”

“那么，这份交情是相当浅的了？”

“哦，相当浅！”

===Original===

“And yet, after losing sight of this merely slight acquaintance for over twenty years, you, on meeting him, take great interest in him?”

“Well, I was willing to do him a good turn, I was interested in what he told me the other evening.”

“I see. Now you will not object to my asking you a personal question or two. You are a public man, and the facts about the lives of public men are more or less public property. You are represented in this work of popular reference as coming to this country in 1902, from Argentina, where you made a considerable fortune. You have told us, however, that you were in London, acquainted with Marbury, about the years, say 1890 to 1892. Did you then leave England soon after knowing Marbury?”

“I did. I left England in 1891 or 1892—I am not sure which.”

“We are wanting to be very sure about this matter, Mr. Aylmore. We want to solve the important question—who is, who was John Marbury, and how did he come by his death? You seem to be the only available person who knows anything about him. What was your business before you left England?”

===Chinese===

“然而，同这样一位不过泛泛之交的人断了音讯二十余年，您一与他重逢，就对他大感兴趣？”

“呃，我乐意帮他个忙，他那天晚上跟我讲的事也让我感兴趣。”

“明白了。那么，我再问您一两个私人问题，您不会见怪吧。您是公众人物，而公众人物的生平事迹多少算得上公共财产。这本人人翻检的通俗名人录里写着，您是1902年从阿根廷来到本国的，并在那里积下了一份可观的家业。可您方才却告诉我们，大约在1890年到1892年间，您人在伦敦，与马伯里相识。这么说，您是在结识马伯里之后不久就离开了英国？”

“是的。我1891年或1892年离开英国——具体哪一年记不准了。”

“艾尔莫尔先生，这件事我们要弄得确凿无疑才行。我们要解答那个重大的问题——约翰·马伯里是谁、曾经是谁，他是怎么死的？您似乎是目前唯一能提供有关他的情况的人。您离开英国之前是做什么营生的？”

===Original===

“I was interested in financial affairs.”

“Like Marbury. Where did you carry on your business?”

“In London, of course.”

“At what address?”

For some moments Aylmore had been growing more and more restive. His brow had flushed; his moustache had begun to twitch. And now he squared his shoulders and faced his questioner defiantly.

“I resent these questions about my private affairs!” he snapped out.

“Possibly. But I must put them. I repeat my last question.”

===Chinese===

“我那时从事的是金融方面的事务。”

“跟马伯里一样。那您在什么地方经营您的事务？”

“自然是在伦敦。”

“在什么地址？”

这时艾尔莫尔已经越来越按捺不住。他的额头涨红了，唇上的胡子开始抽动。此刻他一挺肩膀，毫不示弱地正面对住提问的人。

“我抗议这些关于我私事的问题！”他厉声喝道。

“也许吧。可这些问题我非问不可。我把刚才那个问题再问一遍。”

===Original===

“And I refuse to answer it.”

“Then I ask you another. Where did you live in London at the time you are telling us of, when you knew John Marbury?”

“I refuse to answer that question also!”

The Treasury Counsel sat down and looked at the Coroner.

===Chinese===

“而我拒绝回答。”

“那么我换一个问题。在您方才告诉我们的那段时间里，也就是您认识约翰·马伯里的时候，您住在伦敦什么地方？”

“这个问题我也拒绝回答！”

财政部律师坐了下来，望着验尸官。

## VII Mr. Aylmore / 第七章 艾尔莫尔先生

===Original===
Spargo, keenly observant and watchful, felt, rather than saw, Breton start; he himself preserved an imperturbable equanimity. He gave a mere glance at the photograph to which Mr. Webster was pointing.

“Oh!” he said. “That he?”

“That’s the gentleman, sir,” replied Webster. “Done to the life, that is. No difficulty in recognizing of that, Mr. Spargo.”

“You’re absolutely sure?” demanded Spargo. “There are a lot of men in the House of Commons, you know, who wear beards, and many of the beards are grey.”
===Chinese===
斯帕戈目光敏锐、事事留心，与其说是看见、不如说是感觉到布雷顿猛地一怔；他自己却始终不动声色，沉着如常。他对韦伯斯特（Webster）先生指的那张照片，只扫了一眼。

“哦！”他说。“是他吗？”

“就是这位先生，没错，先生。”韦伯斯特答道。“画得跟真人一个样。认出他来毫不费事，斯帕戈先生。”

“你完全确定？”斯帕戈追问道。“要知道，下议院（House of Commons）里留胡子的人可多着呢，好些人的胡子都是灰白的。”

===Original===
But Webster wagged his head.

“That’s him, sir!” he repeated. “I’m as sure of that as I am that my name’s William Webster. That’s the man I saw talking to him whose picture you’ve got in your paper. Can’t say no more, sir.”

“Very good,” said Spargo. “I’m much obliged to you. I’ll see Mr. Aylmore. Leave me your address in London, Mr. Webster. How long do you remain in town?”

“My address is the Beachcroft Hotel, Bloomsbury, sir, and I shall be there for another week,” answered the farmer. “Hope I’ve been of some use, Mr. Spargo. As I says to my wife﻿—”
===Chinese===
可韦伯斯特直摇头。

“就是他，先生！”他重复道。“这一点我拿得准，就像我叫威廉·韦伯斯特（William Webster）一样拿得准。我瞧见同那人说话的就是他——那人的照片登在你们报上呢。旁的我说不上来了，先生。”

“很好，”斯帕戈说。“多谢您了。我会去见艾尔莫尔先生。请把您在伦敦的地址留下，韦伯斯特先生。您在城里还要待多久？”

“我住在布卢姆斯伯里（Bloomsbury）的比奇克罗夫特旅馆（Beachcroft Hotel），先生，还要再待上一个礼拜。”庄稼人答道。“但愿我这点儿用处帮上了忙，斯帕戈先生。我常跟我家婆娘说——”

===Original===
Spargo cut his visitor short in polite fashion and bowed him out. He turned to Breton, who still stood staring at the album of portraits.

“There!﻿—what did I tell you?” he said. “Didn’t I say I should get some news? There it is.”

Breton nodded his head. He seemed thoughtful.

“Yes,” he agreed. “Yes, I say, Spargo!”

“Well?”
===Chinese===
斯帕戈客客气气地截住了来客的话头，躬身把他送了出去。随后他转向布雷顿，后者还站在那儿，盯着那本人物相册出神。

“瞧见没有！——我怎么跟你说的？”他说。“我不是说过，准能问出点儿消息来吗？这不就有了。”

布雷顿点了点头，像是在琢磨什么。

“是啊，”他表示同意。“我说，斯帕戈！”

“怎么了？”

===Original===
“Mr. Aylmore is my prospective father-in-law, you know.”

“Quite aware of it. Didn’t you introduce me to his daughters﻿—only yesterday?”

“But﻿—how did you know they were his daughters?”

Spargo laughed as he sat down to his desk.
===Chinese===
“你可知道，艾尔莫尔先生是我未来的岳父。”

“这我清楚。昨天不还是你把我引见给他的两位千金的吗？”

“可是——你怎么知道她们是他的女儿？”

斯帕戈在写字台前坐下，笑了。

===Original===
“Instinct﻿—intuition,” he answered. “However, never mind that, just now. Well﻿—I’ve found something out. Marbury﻿—if that is the dead man’s real name, and anyway, it’s all we know him by﻿—was in the company of Mr. Aylmore that night. Good!”

“What are you going to do about it?” asked Breton.

“Do? See Mr. Aylmore, of course.”
===Chinese===
“本能——直觉。”他答道。“不过眼下先不提这个。好了——我摸出一点头绪了。马伯里——姑且当这就是死者的真名，反正我们也只知道他叫这个——那天晚上是同艾尔莫尔先生在一起的。好！”

“那你打算怎么办？”布雷顿问。

“怎么办？当然是去见艾尔莫尔先生。”

===Original===
He was turning over the leaves of a telephone address-book; one hand had already picked up the mouthpiece of the instrument on his desk.

“Look here,” said Breton. “I know where Mr. Aylmore is always to be found at twelve o’clock. At the A. and P.﻿—the Atlantic and Pacific Club, you know, in St. James’s. If you like, I’ll go with you.”

Spargo glanced at the clock and laid down the telephone.

“All right,” he said. “Eleven o’clock, now. I’ve something to do. I’ll meet you outside the A. and P. at exactly noon.”

“I’ll be there,” agreed Breton. He made for the door, and with his hand on it, turned. “What do you expect from﻿—from what we’ve just heard?” he asked.
===Chinese===
他正在翻一本电话地址簿；一只手已经拿起了桌上电话机的听筒。

“是这样，”布雷顿说。“我知道十二点钟在哪儿准能找到艾尔莫尔先生。在A.P.俱乐部——就是圣詹姆斯（St. James’s）的那家大西洋与太平洋俱乐部（Atlantic and Pacific Club），你知道的。你要愿意，我陪你一块儿去。”

斯帕戈瞥了一眼时钟，放下了电话。

“好，”他说。“现在是十一点。我还有点事要办。正午十二点整，我在A.P.俱乐部门口等你。”

“我准到。”布雷顿应道。他朝门口走去，一只手已按在门上，又回过身来。“从——从咱们方才听到的话里，你指望问出什么来？”他问。

===Original===
Spargo shrugged his shoulders.

“Wait﻿—until we hear what Mr. Aylmore has to say,” he answered. “I suppose this man Marbury was some old acquaintance.”

Breton closed the door and went away: left alone, Spargo began to mutter to himself.

“Good God!” he says. “Dainsworth﻿—Painsworth﻿—something of that sort﻿—one of the two. Excellent﻿—that our farmer friend should have so much observation. Ah!﻿—and why should Mr. Stephen Aylmore be recognized as Dainsworth or Painsworth or something of that sort. Now, who is Mr. Stephen Aylmore﻿—beyond being what I know him to be?”
===Chinese===
斯帕戈耸了耸肩。

“等着吧——等听听艾尔莫尔先生自己怎么说。”他答道。“我想这位马伯里，是他的什么旧相识吧。”

布雷顿关上门走了。屋里只剩斯帕戈一人，他开始自言自语地低咕起来。

“天哪！”他咕哝道。“戴恩斯沃思（Dainsworth）——佩恩斯沃思（Painsworth）——差不多是这么个名字——不是这个就是那个。妙极了——亏得咱们这位庄稼人朋友有这么好的眼力。啊！——为什么偏偏是斯蒂芬·艾尔莫尔先生，被人认作戴恩斯沃思或佩恩斯沃思之类的什么人呢。那么，斯蒂芬·艾尔莫尔先生究竟是什么人——除了我已经知道的那些身份以外？”

===Original===
Spargo’s fingers went instinctively to one of a number of books of reference which stood on his desk: they turned with practised swiftness to a page over which his eye ran just as swiftly. He read aloud:

> “**Aylmore, Stephen**, M.P. for Brookminster since 1910. Residences: 23, St. Osythe Court, Kensington: Buena Vista, Great Marlow. Member Atlantic and Pacific and City Venturers’ Clubs. Interested in South American enterprise.”

“Um!” muttered Spargo, putting the book away. “That’s not very illuminating. However, we’ve got one move finished. Now we’ll make another.”

Going over to the album of photographs, Spargo deftly removed that of Mr. Aylmore, put it in an envelope and the envelope in his pocket and, leaving the office, hailed a taxicab, and ordered its driver to take him to the Anglo-Orient Hotel. This was the something-to-do of which he had spoken to Breton: Spargo wanted to do it alone.
===Chinese===
斯帕戈的手指下意识地伸向写字台上摆着的几本参考书中的一本：那双手熟极而流，飞快地翻到某一页，他的目光也同样飞快地在页面上扫过。他朗声念道：

> “艾尔莫尔，斯蒂芬，自1910年起任布鲁克敏斯特（Brookminster）选区下院议员。宅邸：肯辛顿（Kensington）圣奥西思庭院23号；大马洛（Great Marlow）布埃纳维斯塔。大西洋与太平洋俱乐部及城市冒险家俱乐部会员。关注南美实业。”

“嗯！”斯帕戈咕哝一声，把书放到一边。“这说明不了多少问题。不过，咱们一步棋已经走完了。现在来走下一步。”

斯帕戈走到那本相册跟前，麻利地抽出艾尔莫尔先生那张，装进一只信封，又把信封揣进衣袋，随即离开报社，叫了一辆出租汽车，吩咐司机送他去盎格鲁-东方旅馆。这便是他对布雷顿说的那件“要办的事”：斯帕戈要独自去办。

===Original===
Mrs. Walters was in her low-windowed office when Spargo entered the hall; she recognized him at once and motioned him into her parlour.

“I remember you,” said Mrs. Walters; “you came with the detective﻿—Mr. Rathbury.”

“Have you seen him, since?” asked Spargo.

“Not since,” replied Mrs. Walters. “No﻿—and I was wondering if he’d be coming round, because﻿—” She paused there and looked at Spargo with particular enquiry﻿—“You’re a friend of his, aren’t you?” she asked. “I suppose you know as much as he does﻿—about this?”

“He and I,” replied Spargo, with easy confidence, “are working this case together. You can tell me anything you’d tell him.”
===Chinese===
斯帕戈走进门厅时，沃尔特斯太太（Mrs. Walters）正在她那间窗子开得低低的账房里；她一眼就认出了他，招手把他让进自己的客厅。

“我记得您，”沃尔特斯太太说。“您是跟那位侦探——拉思伯里先生——一起来的。”

“打那以后，您还见过他吗？”斯帕戈问。

“没再见过。”沃尔特斯太太答道。“没有——我还正纳闷他会不会再过来呢，因为——”她顿住话头，带着一脸格外探询的神气望住斯帕戈——“您是他的朋友，对吧？”她问。“这件事的内情，我想您跟他知道得一样多吧？”

“他和我，”斯帕戈带着从容自信的口吻答道，“正合力查办这个案子。凡是能对他讲的，您都可以讲给我听。”

===Original===
The landlady rummaged in her pocket and produced an old purse, from an inner compartment of which she brought out a small object wrapped in tissue paper.

“Well,” she said, unwrapping the paper, “we found this in Number 20 this morning﻿—it was lying under the dressing-table. The girl that found it brought it to me, and I thought it was a bit of glass, but Walters, he says as how he shouldn’t be surprised if it’s a diamond. And since we found it, the waiter who took the whisky up to 20, after Mr. Marbury came in with the other gentleman, has told me that when he went into the room the two gentlemen were looking at a paper full of things like this. So there?”

Spargo fingered the shining bit of stone.
===Chinese===
老板娘在衣袋里摸索了一阵，掏出一只旧钱袋，又从钱袋的内层格子里拿出一个薄纸裹着的小物件。

“是这么回事，”她一面拆着纸一面说，“今天早上，我们在20号房间里发现了这个——它就躺在梳妆台底下。发现它的女仆把它交给了我，我还当是块碎玻璃，可是沃尔特斯，他说这玩意儿要真是颗钻石，他一点儿也不奇怪。自打发现这东西以后，那位在马伯里先生同另一位先生进来之后、把威士忌送上20号房间的侍者告诉我，他进屋的时候，两位先生正对着一整张满是这类小东西的纸端详呢。您瞧怎么样？”

斯帕戈捻弄着那颗亮闪闪的小石子。

===Original===
“That’s a diamond﻿—right enough,” he said. “Put it away, Mrs. Walters﻿—I shall see Rathbury presently, and I’ll tell him about it. Now, that other gentleman! You told us you saw him. Could you recognize him﻿—I mean, a photograph of him? Is this the man?”

Spargo knew from the expression of Mrs. Walters’ face that she had no more doubt than Webster had.

“Oh, yes!” she said. “That’s the gentleman who came in with Mr. Marbury﻿—I should have known him in a thousand. Anybody would recognize him from that﻿—perhaps you’d let our hall-porter and the waiter I mentioned just now look at it?”

“I’ll see them separately and see if they’ve ever seen a man who resembles this,” replied Spargo.

The two men recognized the photograph at once, without any prompting, and Spargo, after a word or two with the landlady, rode off to the Atlantic and Pacific Club, and found Ronald Breton awaiting him on the steps. He made no reference to his recent doings, and together they went into the house and asked for Mr. Aylmore.
===Chinese===
“这是颗钻石——错不了。”他说。“把它收好，沃尔特斯太太——我稍后就去看拉思伯里，会把这事告诉他的。好了，说另外那位先生！您说过您见过他。您能认出他来吗——我是说，认出他的照片？是这个人吗？”

斯帕戈从沃尔特斯太太脸上的神情看出来，她和韦伯斯特一样，没有半点怀疑。

“哦，认得！”她说。“就是同马伯里先生一道进来的那位先生——一千个人里头我也认得出他来。谁看了这张照片都能把他认出来——也许您肯让我们的门房和我方才提起的那个侍者也看一看？”

“我会分别见见他们，看看他们是否见过长得像这张照片的人。”斯帕戈答道。

那两个人不用任何提示，一眼就认出了照片上的人。斯帕戈同老板娘又交代了两句，便驱车前往大西洋与太平洋俱乐部，只见罗纳德·布雷顿已在台阶上候着他。他对自己方才的行止只字不提，两人于是一同走进会所，求见艾尔莫尔先生。

===Original===
Spargo looked with more than uncommon interest at the man who presently came to them in the visitors’ room. He was already familiar with Mr. Aylmore’s photograph, but he never remembered seeing him in real life; the Member for Brookminster was one of that rapidly diminishing body of legislators whose members are disposed to work quietly and unobtrusively, doing yeoman service on committees, obeying every behest of the party whips, without forcing themselves into the limelight or seizing every opportunity to air their opinions. Now that Spargo met him in the flesh he proved to be pretty much what the journalist had expected﻿—a rather cold-mannered, self-contained man, who looked as if he had been brought up in a school of rigid repression, and taught not to waste words. He showed no more than the merest of languid interests in Spargo when Breton introduced him, and his face was quite expressionless when Spargo brought to an end his brief explanation﻿—purposely shortened﻿—of his object in calling upon him.

“Yes,” he said indifferently. “Yes, it is quite true that I met Marbury and spent a little time with him on the evening your informant spoke of. I met him, as he told you, in the lobby of the House. I was much surprised to meet him. I had not seen him for﻿—I really don’t know how many years.”
===Chinese===
斯帕戈怀着非同寻常的兴趣，打量着不多时便来到会客室里的这个人。艾尔莫尔先生的照片他早已看熟，可他不记得在现实中见过其人。这位布鲁克敏斯特选区的议员，属于议员中人数日稀的一类——这类人安心于默默做事、不事张扬，在各委员会里尽着得力可靠的实干本分，对党鞭的每一道指令无不俯首遵从，既不硬往聚光灯下钻，也不抓着每个机会发表高见。如今斯帕戈亲眼见到本人，此人同这位记者事先料想的大抵不差——一个举止相当冷淡、沉默自持的人，看上去仿佛是在一所规行矩步、严苛压抑的学校里调教出来的，学得了决不浪费字句的功夫。布雷顿引见的时候，他对斯帕戈至多流露出一丝淡淡的兴味；待斯帕戈把此番登门的来意三言两语——有意删繁就简——讲完，他脸上依旧毫无表情。

“是啊，”他淡漠地说。“是的，你们那位报信人所说的那个晚上，我确实遇见了马伯里，还同他盘桓了一阵。正如他告诉你们的，我是在下议院的前厅遇见他的。遇上他，我很感意外。我有——实在说不清多少年——没见过他了。”

===Original===
He paused and looked at Spargo as if he was wondering what he ought or not to say to a newspaper man. Spargo remained silent, waiting. And presently Mr. Aylmore went on.

“I read your account in the *Watchman* this morning,” he said. “I was wondering, when you called just now, if I would communicate with you or with the police. The fact is﻿—I suppose you want this for your paper, eh?” he continued after a sudden breaking off.

“I shall not print anything that you wish me not to print,” answered Spargo. “If you care to give me any information﻿—”

“Oh, well!” said Mr. Aylmore. “I don’t mind. The fact is, I knew next to nothing. Marbury was a man with whom I had some﻿—well, business relations, of a sort, a great many years ago. It must be twenty years﻿—perhaps more﻿—since I lost sight of him. When he came up to me in the lobby the other night, I had to make an effort of memory to recall him. He wished me, having once met me, to give him some advice, and as there was little doing in the House that night, and as he had once been﻿—almost a friend﻿—I walked to his hotel with him, chatting. He told me that he had only landed from Australia that morning, and what he wanted my advice about, principally, was﻿—diamonds. Australian diamonds.”
===Chinese===
他顿了顿，望着斯帕戈，似乎在踌躇，该不该对报馆的人说这些。斯帕戈不作声，只管等着。片刻之后，艾尔莫尔先生又接着说了下去。

“今天早上，我在《守望报》上读到了你写的报道。”他说。“方才你来拜访的时候，我还在盘算，是该找你接洽，还是该找警方。实情是——你要的是供报纸用的材料，对吧？”他突然收住话头，随即又这样说了下去。

“凡是你不愿我见报的，我一概不登。”斯帕戈答道。“您若是愿意提供什么情况——”

“哦，那也好！”艾尔莫尔先生说。“我无所谓。实情是，我几乎一无所知。马伯里这个人，许多年以前，我同他有过一些——呃，算是生意上的往来吧。算来总有二十年了——兴许还不止——我们断了音讯。前几天夜里他在下议院前厅里走到我跟前时，我还得费一番劲儿回想，才认出他来。他念在跟我有过一面之缘，希望我给他出出主意；那天晚上议院里没什么正经事，而他从前也算是——差不多是个朋友——我便一路陪他走回旅馆，边走边谈。他告诉我，他那天早上才从澳大利亚上岸；他想向我请教的，主要是一件东西——钻石。澳大利亚钻石。”

===Original===
“I was unaware,” remarked Spargo, “that diamonds were ever found in Australia.”

Mr. Aylmore smiled﻿—a little cynically.

“Perhaps so,” he said. “But diamonds have been found in Australia from time to time, ever since Australia was known to Europeans, and in the opinion of experts, they will eventually be found there in quantity. Anyhow, Marbury had got hold of some Australian diamonds, and he showed them to me at his hotel﻿—a number of them. We examined them in his room.”

“What did he do with them﻿—afterwards?” asked Spargo.
===Chinese===
“我还不知道，”斯帕戈说，“澳大利亚也出过钻石。”

艾尔莫尔先生微微一笑——笑意里带着几分讥诮。

“也许吧，”他说。“不过，自打澳大利亚为欧洲人所知以来，那里就陆续有钻石出土，而且依专家们的高见，那里终有一天会发现大批钻石。无论如何，马伯里弄到了一些澳大利亚钻石，还在他下榻的旅馆里拿给我看了——有好些颗呢。我们在他房间里细细看了一回。”

“后来他把这些钻石怎么处置了？”斯帕戈问。

===Original===
“He put them in his waistcoat pocket﻿—in a very small wash-leather bag, from which he had taken them. There were, in all, sixteen or twenty stones﻿—not more, and they were all small. I advised him to see some expert﻿—I mentioned Streeter’s to him. Now, I can tell you how he got hold of Mr. Breton’s address.”

The two young men pricked up their ears. Spargo unconsciously tightened his hold on the pencil with which he was making notes.

“He got it from me,” continued Mr. Aylmore. “The handwriting on the scrap of paper is mine, hurriedly scrawled. He wanted legal advice. As I knew very little about lawyers, I told him that if he called on Mr. Breton, Mr. Breton would be able to tell him of a first-class, sharp solicitor. I wrote down Mr. Breton’s address for him, on a scrap of paper which he tore off a letter that he took from his pocket. By the by, I observe that when his body was found there was nothing on it in the shape of papers or money. I am quite sure that when I left him he had a lot of gold on him, those diamonds, and a breast-pocket full of letters.”

“Where did you leave him, sir?” asked Spargo. “You left the hotel together, I believe?”
===Chinese===
“他把它们放回了马甲口袋——装在一只很小的油皮袋里，就是他取出钻石时用的那只。总共十六颗，至多二十颗——不会再多了，颗颗都小。我劝他去请教一位真正的行家——我向他提了斯特里特珠宝店（Streeter’s）。对了，我还可以告诉你们，他是怎么弄到布雷顿先生的地址的。”

两个年轻人都竖起了耳朵。斯帕戈不知不觉间，把记笔记用的那支铅笔攥得更紧了。

“地址是我给的。”艾尔莫尔先生继续说道。“那张小纸片上的笔迹就是我的，写得仓促潦草。他想得到法律方面的指点。我对律师这一行所知甚少，便告诉他，若是去拜访布雷顿先生，布雷顿先生便能给他介绍一位一流干练的事务律师。我替他写下布雷顿先生的地址，用的是他从口袋里取出一封信、当场撕下来的一角纸。顺便提一句，我注意到，人们发现他尸体的时候，他身上没有任何文件，也没有钱。我可以肯定，我离开他的时候，他身上带着一大笔金币，带着那些钻石，胸前的口袋里还塞满了信件。”

“您是在哪儿同他分手的，先生？”斯帕戈问。“你们是一起离开旅馆的，我想没说错吧？”

===Original===
“Yes. We strolled along when we left it. Having once met, we had much to talk of, and it was a fine night. We walked across Waterloo Bridge and very shortly afterwards he left me. And that is really all I know. My own impression﻿—” He paused for a moment and Spargo waited silently.

“My own impression﻿—though I confess it may seem to have no very solid grounds﻿—is that Marbury was decoyed to where he was found, and was robbed and murdered by some person who knew he had valuables on him. There is the fact that he was robbed, at any rate.”

“I’ve had a notion,” said Breton, diffidently. “Mayn’t be worth much, but I’ve had it, all the same. Some fellow-passenger of Marbury’s may have tracked him all day﻿—Middle Temple Lane’s pretty lonely at night, you know.”
===Chinese===
“没错。离开旅馆后，我们信步同行。久别重逢，要谈的话很多，夜色又好。我们走过滑铁卢桥，没过多久，他便同我分手了。我所知道的，实在就只有这些。我个人的印象是——”他停了片刻，斯帕戈静静地等着。

“我个人的印象——尽管我承认，这看法似乎并没有什么扎实的根据——是马伯里被人诱到了他遇害的那个地方，遭一个知道他身带着贵重财物的人抢劫、杀害了。无论如何，他遭了抢劫，这是事实。”

“我倒有过一个念头，”布雷顿怯生生地说。“未必值当什么，可我终究是这么想的。马伯里的某位同船旅伴，也许一整天都在盯着他的行踪——您知道，中殿巷一到夜里是挺冷清的。”

===Original===
No one made any comment upon this suggestion, and on Spargo looking at Mr. Aylmore, the Member of Parliament rose and glanced at the door.

“Well, that’s all I can tell you, Mr. Spargo,” he said. “You see, it’s not much, after all. Of course, there’ll be an inquest on Marbury, and I shall have to retell it. But you’re welcome to print what I’ve told you.”

Spargo left Breton with his future father-in-law and went away towards New Scotland Yard. He and Rathbury had promised to share news﻿—now he had some to communicate.
===Chinese===
对这一揣测，谁也没有作声。斯帕戈朝艾尔莫尔先生望去，这位议员便站起身来，朝门口望了一眼。

“好吧，我能奉告的就这么多了，斯帕戈先生。”他说。“你也看到了，到底不算多。当然，马伯里的案子要开验尸审讯庭，这些话我还得再讲一遍。不过，我告诉你的这些，尽管拿去登报好了。”

斯帕戈把布雷顿留在他未来的岳父身边，自己朝新苏格兰场去了。他和拉思伯里有言在先，要互通消息——如今，他可有消息可通报了。

## XXVII Mr. Elphick’s Chambers / 第二十七章 埃尔菲克的寓所

===Original===
Spargo went round again to the Temple that night at nine o’clock, asking himself over and over again two questions﻿—the first, how much does Elphick know? the second, how much shall I tell him?

The old house in the Temple to which he repaired and in which many a generation of old fogies had lived since the days of Queen Anne, was full of stairs and passages, and as Spargo had forgotten to get the exact number of the set of chambers he wanted, he was obliged to wander about in what was a deserted building. So wandering, he suddenly heard steps, firm, decisive steps coming up a staircase which he himself had just climbed. He looked over the banisters down into the hollow beneath. And there, marching up resolutely, was the figure of a tall, veiled woman, and Spargo suddenly realized, with a sharp quickening of his pulses, that for the second time that day he was beneath one roof with Miss Baylis.

Spargo’s mind acted quickly. Knowing what he now knew, from his extraordinary dealings with Mother Gutch, he had no doubt whatever that Miss Baylis had come to see Mr. Elphick﻿—come, of course, to tell Mr. Elphick that he, Spargo, had visited her that morning, and that he was on the track of the Maitland secret history. He had never thought of it before, for he had been busily engaged since the departure of Mother Gutch; but, naturally, Miss Baylis and Mr. Elphick would keep in communication with each other. At any rate, here she was, and her destination was, surely, Elphick’s chambers. And the question for him, Spargo, was﻿—what to do?

===Chinese===
当天晚上九点钟，斯帕戈再度来到中殿律师学院，一路上反反复复问自己两个问题——第一，埃尔菲克究竟知道多少？第二，我又该向他透露多少？

他寻去的这座中殿里的老宅，自安妮女王时代以来，一代又一代的老古板在里头住过，楼梯纵横，过道曲折。斯帕戈忘了先问清楚他要找的那套寓所的确切门牌，只好在一座空无一人的楼里四处逡巡。正转悠间，他忽然听见脚步声——沉着而果断的脚步声，正顺着他自己方才爬上来的那道楼梯上来。他凭栏下望，俯瞰楼梯井的深处。只见一个高挑女人的身影正毅然拾级而上，脸上罩着面纱。斯帕戈猛然意识到，这一天之内，他已是第二次同贝利斯小姐共处一个屋顶之下——这念头令他脉搏陡然一紧。

斯帕戈的脑子转得飞快。自从与格奇大妈打过那番非同寻常的交道，他如今既已知晓内情，便丝毫不怀疑：贝利斯小姐是来见埃尔菲克先生的——当然，是来告诉埃尔菲克先生，他斯帕戈今早去找过她，而且已经盯上了梅特兰那段秘史的踪迹。他先前从未想到这一层，因为打发送走格奇大妈之后他就一直忙个不停；可是自然而然，贝利斯小姐和埃尔菲克先生两人必定互通音讯。无论如何，她眼下就在这里，而她的目的地，必是埃尔菲克的寓所无疑。于是摆在他斯帕戈面前的问题便是——怎么办？

===Original===
What Spargo did was to remain in absolute silence, motionless, tense, where he was on the stair, and to trust to the chance that the woman did not look up.
