But Miss Baylis neither looked up nor down: she reached a landing, turned along a corridor with decision, and marched forward. A moment later Spargo heard a sharp double knock on a door: a moment after that he heard a door heavily shut; he knew then that Miss Baylis had sought and gained admittance﻿—somewhere.

To find out precisely where that somewhere was drew Spargo down to the landing which Miss Baylis had just left. There was no one about﻿—he had not, in fact, seen a soul since he entered the building. Accordingly he went along the corridor into which he had seen Miss Baylis turn. He knew that all the doors in that house were double ones, and that the outer oak in each was solid and substantial enough to be sound proof. Yet, as men will under such circumstances, he walked softly; he said to himself, smiling at the thought, that he would be sure to start if somebody suddenly opened a door on him. But no hand opened any door, and at last he came to the end of the corridor and found himself confronting a small board on which was painted in white letters on a black ground, Mr. Elphick’s Chambers.

Having satisfied himself as to his exact whereabouts, Spargo drew back as quietly as he had come. There was a window halfway along the corridor from which, he had noticed as he came along, one could catch a glimpse of the Embankment and the Thames; to this he withdrew, and leaning on the sill looked out and considered matters. Should he go and﻿—if he could gain admittance﻿—beard these two conspirators? Should he wait until the woman came out and let her see that he was on the track? Should he hide again until she went, and then see Elphick alone?

===Chinese===
斯帕戈的做法，是站在楼梯上原地不动，纹丝不动，屏息凝神，只赌这女人不抬头。可贝利斯小姐既不抬头，也不四顾：她上了一处楼梯平台，果断地拐进一条走廊，径直向前。片刻之后，斯帕戈听见一记干脆的、连叩两声的敲门声；又过片刻，他听见一扇门沉沉关上。他于是明白，贝利斯小姐已寻到门路，获准进了某处——某扇门内。

要查明那“某处”究竟在哪儿，就得下到贝利斯小姐方才离开的那个平台去。四下无人——事实上，自打进楼以来，他连一个人影也没见过。于是他顺着方才望见贝利斯小姐拐入的那条走廊走去。他知道这宅子里所有的门都是双层的，外层橡木门厚实坚固，足以隔音。然而人在这种情形下总是如此，他放轻了脚步；他暗自失笑地想，若是有人冷不丁朝他这边开一扇门，他保准会吓一跳。可是没有任何一只手推开任何一扇门，他终于走到走廊尽头，发现自己面前是一块小木牌，黑底上漆着白字：埃尔菲克先生寓所。

确知了自己的确切位置之后，斯帕戈便如来时一样悄然退了回去。走廊半腰有一扇窗，他来时便已留意到，从那里可以望见泰晤士河堤与泰晤士河的一角；他退到那窗前，倚着窗台向外眺望，盘算着眼前的局面。他该不该径直上去——倘若能获准进门——当面会一会这两个同谋？该不该等那女人出来，让她瞧瞧他已追到了踪迹？还是该重新躲起来，等她走了，再见埃尔菲克一个人？

===Original===
In the end Spargo did none of these things immediately. He let things slide for the moment. He lighted a cigarette and stared at the river and the brown sails, and the buildings across on the Surrey side. Ten minutes went by﻿—twenty minutes﻿—nothing happened. Then, as half-past nine struck from all the neighbouring clocks, Spargo flung away a second cigarette, marched straight down the corridor and knocked boldly at Mr. Elphick’s door.

Greatly to Spargo’s surprise, the door was opened before there was any necessity to knock again. And there, calmly confronting him, a benevolent, yet somewhat deprecating expression on his spectacled and placid face, stood Mr. Elphick, a smoking cap on his head, a tasseled smoking jacket over his dress shirt, and a short pipe in his hand.

Spargo was taken aback: Mr. Elphick apparently was not. He held the door well open, and motioned the journalist to enter.

===Chinese===
到头来，这几桩事斯帕戈一件也没有立刻去做。他暂且听其自然。他点起一支香烟，望着河面、褐色的船帆，以及对岸萨里那一侧的房舍。十分钟过去了——二十分钟过去了——毫无动静。直到四邻的钟一齐敲响九点半，斯帕戈才抛掉第二支烟，径直沿走廊走过去，毫不迟疑地叩响了埃尔菲克先生的房门。

大出斯帕戈意料的是，他还来不及叩第二遍，门就开了。埃尔菲克先生就站在那里，神色安然地迎着他：那张戴眼镜的安详面孔上，是一副慈祥中略带告罪的神情；头上顶着居家便帽，礼服衬衫外面罩一件缀穗的吸烟服，手里握着一支短烟斗。

斯帕戈吃了一惊：埃尔菲克先生显然没有。他把门大大敞开，示意这位记者进去。

===Original===
“Come in, Mr. Spargo,” he said. “I was expecting you. Walk forward into my sitting-room.”

Spargo, much astonished at this reception, passed through an anteroom into a handsomely furnished apartment full of books and pictures. In spite of the fact that it was still very little past midsummer there was a cheery fire in the grate, and on a table set near a roomy armchair was set such creature comforts as a spirit-case, a syphon, a tumbler, and a novel﻿—from which things Spargo argued that Mr. Elphick had been taking his ease since his dinner. But in another armchair on the opposite side of the hearth was the forbidding figure of Miss Baylis, blacker, gloomier, more mysterious than ever. She neither spoke nor moved when Spargo entered: she did not even look at him. And Spargo stood staring at her until Mr. Elphick, having closed his doors, touched him on the elbow, and motioned him courteously to a seat.

“Yes, I was expecting you, Mr. Spargo,” he said, as he resumed his own chair. “I have been expecting you at any time, ever since you took up your investigation of the Marbury affair, in some of the earlier stages of which you saw me, you will remember, at the mortuary. But since Miss Baylis told me, twenty minutes ago, that you had been to her this morning I felt sure that it would not be more than a few hours before you would come to me.”

===Chinese===
“请进，斯帕戈先生，”他说。“我正等着您呢。一直往里走，到我的起居室来。”

对这样的接待，斯帕戈大为错愕。他穿过一间前厅，走进一间陈设讲究的屋子，四壁尽是书籍与图画。虽说仲夏刚过不久，壁炉里却烧着一炉欢腾的火；宽大的扶手椅近旁摆着一张桌子，桌上备着酒箱、苏打水瓶、平底大玻璃杯和一本小说——由这些物件，斯帕戈推断埃尔菲克先生晚饭之后一直在这里安逸消遣。可是在壁炉另一侧的另一张扶手椅里，坐着贝利斯小姐那令人生畏的身影，比先前更黑、更阴沉、更神秘莫测。斯帕戈进屋时，她一言不发，一动不动，甚至连看也不看他一眼。斯帕戈站在那里直盯着她，直到埃尔菲克先生关好房门，碰了碰他的手肘，客气地请他入座。

“是啊，我正等着您，斯帕戈先生。”他坐回自己的椅子里，说道。“自打您着手调查马伯里一案，我就料着您随时会上门——您总还记得，那案子早先有几个阶段，您在停尸房见过我。不过，既然贝利斯小姐二十分钟前告诉我，您今天上午去过她那儿，我就敢断定，不出几个钟头，您必定会来找我。”

===Original===
“Why, Mr. Elphick, should you suppose that I should come to you at all?” asked Spargo, now in full possession of his wits.

“Because I felt sure that you would leave no stone unturned, no corner unexplored,” replied Mr. Elphick. “The curiosity of the modern pressman is insatiable.”

Spargo stiffened.

“I have no curiosity, Mr. Elphick,” he said. “I am charged by my paper to investigate the circumstances of the death of the man who was found in Middle Temple Lane, and, if possible, to track his murderer, and﻿—”

===Chinese===
“哦，埃尔菲克先生，您凭什么认定我一定会来找您呢？”斯帕戈问道，此刻他已完全定下神来。

“因为我料定，您必定会把每块石头都翻个遍，每个角落都搜个够。”埃尔菲克先生答道。“现代报人的好奇心是填不满的。”

斯帕戈绷起了脸。

“我没有什么好奇心，埃尔菲克先生，”他说。“敝报委托我调查那具在中殿巷被发现的尸体的死亡情形，并且，倘若可能，追查杀害他的凶手，还有——”

===Original===
Mr. Elphick laughed slightly and waved his hand.

“My good young gentleman!” he said. “You exaggerate your own importance. I don’t approve of modern journalism nor of its methods. In your own case you have got hold of some absurd notion that the man John Marbury was in reality one John Maitland, once of Market Milcaster, and you have been trying to frighten Miss Baylis here into﻿—”

Spargo suddenly rose from his chair. There was a certain temper in him which, when once roused, led him to straight hitting, and it was roused now. He looked the old barrister full in the face.

“Mr. Elphick,” he said, “you are evidently unaware of all that I know. So I will tell you what I will do. I will go back to my office, and I will write down what I do know, and give the true and absolute proofs of what I know, and, if you will trouble yourself to read the *Watchman* tomorrow morning, then you, too, will know.”

===Chinese===
埃尔菲克先生轻笑一声，摆了摆手。

“我可爱的年轻人！”他说。“您把自己看得太重了。我不赞成现代的新闻业，也不赞成它那一套手法。就说您吧，您抱住了某个荒唐的念头，认定那个叫约翰·马伯里的死者，其实就是从前马基特·米尔卡斯特的约翰·梅特兰，于是就一直想吓唬这儿的贝利斯小姐，好叫她——”

斯帕戈霍地从椅子上站了起来。他身上有一种脾性，一旦被激起来，便叫他说打就打、直来直去，此刻它被激起来了。他直视着这位老出庭律师的脸。

“埃尔菲克先生，”他说，“您显然不知道我知道的全部。那我就告诉您，我要怎么做。我要回报社去，把我所知道的一五一十写下来，附上真实而确凿无疑的证据。明天早上您若有兴致费神读一读《守望报》，那您也就都知道了。”

===Original===
“Dear me﻿—dear me!” said Mr. Elphick, banteringly. “We are so used to ultra-sensational stories from the *Watchman* that﻿—but I am a curious and inquisitive old man, my good young sir, so perhaps you will tell me in a word what it is you do know, eh?”

Spargo reflected for a second. Then he bent forward across the table and looked the old barrister straight in the face.

“Yes,” he said quietly. “I will tell you what I know beyond doubt. I know that the man murdered under the name of John Marbury was, without doubt, John Maitland, of Market Milcaster, and that Ronald Breton is his son, whom you took from that woman!”

===Chinese===
“哎呀——哎呀！”埃尔菲克先生打趣道。“《守望报》那种耸人听闻的报道我们见得多了，所以——不过我这老头子好奇心重，爱刨根问底，我可爱的年轻先生，您何不用三言两语告诉我，您究竟知道些什么，嗯？”

斯帕戈沉吟了一秒。随后他隔着桌子俯身向前，直勾勾地逼视着这位老出庭律师。

“好，”他平静地说。“我就告诉您我确凿无疑知道的事。我知道，那个顶着约翰·马伯里的名字被害的人，毫无疑问就是马基特·米尔卡斯特的约翰·梅特兰；我还知道，罗纳德·布雷顿是他的儿子——那个您从这女人手里带走的孩子！”

===Original===
If Spargo had desired a complete revenge for the cavalier fashion in which Mr. Elphick had treated it he could not have been afforded a more ample one than that offered to him by the old barrister’s reception of this news. Mr. Elphick’s face not only fell, but changed; his expression of almost sneering contempt was transformed to one clearly resembling abject terror; he dropped his pipe, fell back in his chair, recovered himself, gripped the chair’s arms, and stared at Spargo as if the young man had suddenly announced to him that in another minute he must be led to instant execution. And Spargo, quick to see his advantage, followed it up.

“That is what I know, Mr. Elphick, and if I choose, all the world shall know it tomorrow morning!” he said firmly. “Ronald Breton is the son of the murdered man, and Ronald Breton is engaged to be married to the daughter of the man charged with the murder. Do you hear that? It is not matter of suspicion, or of idea, or of conjecture, it is fact﻿—fact!”

Mr. Elphick slowly turned his face to Miss Baylis. He gasped out a few words.

“You﻿—did﻿—not﻿—tell﻿—me﻿—this!”

===Chinese===
倘若说斯帕戈有意为埃尔菲克先生方才那副怠慢轻慢的态度讨一个彻底的报复，那么这位老出庭律师闻听此讯的反应，给他的满足再丰厚不过了。埃尔菲克先生的脸不但沉了下来，简直变了样；那副近乎讥诮轻蔑的表情，转眼化作一副明白无误、近乎卑怯的惊惶之色；烟斗脱了手，人朝椅背上一倒，又挣扎着坐直，死死抓住扶手，直勾勾瞪着斯帕戈，仿佛这年轻人忽然向他宣布，再过一分钟他就要被押去立即处决似的。斯帕戈眼疾手快，一见自己占了上风，便乘胜追击。

“这就是我知道的，埃尔菲克先生，只要我愿意，明天早上全世界都会知道！”他斩钉截铁地说。“罗纳德·布雷顿是被害人的儿子，而罗纳德·布雷顿又与被控行凶之人的女儿订了婚。您听清楚了吗？这不是怀疑，不是臆想，不是猜测，是事实——事实！”

埃尔菲克先生缓缓把脸转向贝利斯小姐。他喘着气吐出几个字。

“你——没有——告诉——我——这个！”

===Original===
Then Spargo, turning to the woman, saw that she, too, was white to the lips and as frightened as the man.

“I﻿—didn’t know!” she muttered. “He didn’t tell me. He only told me this morning what﻿—what I’ve told you.”

Spargo picked up his hat.

“Good night, Mr. Elphick,” he said.

===Chinese===
斯帕戈转向那女人，只见她同样面无血色，连嘴唇都白了，惊惶得与那男人一般无二。

“我——原先不知道！”她喃喃道。“他没有告诉我。他只是今天早上才对我说了那些——就是我方才告诉您的话。”

斯帕戈拿起帽子。

“晚安，埃尔菲克先生。”他说。

===Original===
But before he could reach the door the old barrister had leapt from his chair and seized him with trembling hands. Spargo turned and looked at him. He knew then that for some reason or other he had given Mr. Septimus Elphick a thoroughly bad fright.

“Well?” he growled.

“My dear young gentleman!” implored Mr. Elphick. “Don’t go! I’ll﻿—I’ll do anything for you if you won’t go away to print that. I’ll﻿—I’ll give you a thousand pounds!”

Spargo shook him off.

===Chinese===
可他还没走到门口，老出庭律师已经从椅子里跳起来，用一双颤抖的手抓住了他。斯帕戈回过头看着他。他这时才明白，不知怎的，他着实把塞普蒂默斯·埃尔菲克先生吓得不轻。

“怎么？”他瓮声瓮气地问。

“我亲爱的年轻先生！”埃尔菲克先生哀求道。“别走！只要您不去把那东西登出来，我——我什么都依您。我——我给您一千镑！”

斯帕戈挣脱了他。

===Original===
“That’s enough!” he snarled. “Now, I am off! What, you’d try to bribe me?”

Mr. Elphick wrung his hands.

“I didn’t mean that﻿—indeed I didn’t!” he almost wailed. “I﻿—I don’t know what I meant. Stay, young gentleman, stay a little, and let us﻿—let us talk. Let me have a word with you﻿—as many words as you please. I implore you!”

Spargo made a fine pretence of hesitation.

===Chinese===
“够了！”他厉声道。“行了，我这就走！什么，您还想贿赂我？”

埃尔菲克先生直搓双手。

“我不是那个意思——真的不是！”他几乎要哭出来了。“我——我不知道自己说的是什么。留下吧，年轻先生，稍留一留，让我们——让我们谈谈。容我跟您说句话——您愿意说多少句都行。我求您了！”

斯帕戈拿出一副煞有介事的犹豫姿态。

===Original===
“If I stay,” he said, at last, “it will only be on the strict condition that you answer﻿—and answer truly﻿—whatever questions I like to ask you. Otherwise﻿—”

He made another move to the door, and again Mr. Elphick laid beseeching hands on him.

“Stay!” he said. “I’ll answer anything you like!”

===Chinese===
“如果我留下，”他终于开口，“那只有一个不容含糊的条件：我高兴问什么，您就得回答——并且如实回答。要不然——”

他朝门口又挪了一步，埃尔菲克先生又把哀求的双手搭了上来。

“留下吧！”他说。“您问什么我都答！”

## XVII Mr. Quarterpage Harks Back / 第十七章 夸特佩奇先生追忆往事

===Original===
If Spargo had upset the old gentlemen’s bowl of punch﻿—the second of the evening﻿—or had dropped an infernal machine in their midst, he could scarcely have produced a more startling effect than that wrought upon them by his sudden production of the silver ticket. Their babble of conversation died out; one of them dropped his pipe; another took his cigar out of his mouth as if he had suddenly discovered that he was sucking a stick of poison; all lifted astonished faces to the interrupter, staring from him to the shining object exhibited in his outstretched palm, from it back to him. And at last Mr. Quarterpage, to whom Spargo had more particularly addressed himself, spoke, pointing with great empressement to the ticket.

“Young gentleman!” he said, in accents that seemed to Spargo to tremble a little, “young gentleman, where did you get that?”

“You know what it is, then?” asked Spargo, willing to dally a little with the matter. “You recognize it?”

“Know it! Recognize it!” exclaimed Mr. Quarterpage. “Yes, and so does every gentleman present. And it is just because I see you are a stranger to this town that I ask you where you got it. Not, I think, young gentleman, in this town.”

===Chinese===
倘若斯帕戈打翻了老先生们的潘趣酒钵——今晚已是第二钵——或是往他们中间丢下一枚炸弹，只怕也造不出比他骤然亮出那枚银质票牌更惊人的效果。满屋的絮语顿时止息；有一位失手掉了烟斗；另一位把雪茄从嘴边拿开，仿佛猛然发现自己嘬着的竟是一根毒棍；人人抬起惊愕的脸，望着这位闯进来搅局的后生，目光从他身上移到他摊开的掌心里那件亮闪闪的物件上，又从那物件移回他脸上。末了，斯帕戈特意向他问话的那位夸特佩奇先生终于开了口，郑重其事地指着那枚票牌。

“年轻绅士！”他说，那声调在斯帕戈听来微微发颤，“年轻绅士，您这东西是从哪儿弄来的？”

“这么说，您认得它是什么？”斯帕戈问道，有心把这桩事稍吊一吊胃口，“您认得它？”

“认得！认得！”夸特佩奇先生失声道，“何止认得，在座每一位绅士全认得。恰恰因为瞧出您是外乡人，我才要问您这东西从何而来。依我看，年轻绅士，它总不是在本镇到手的吧。

===Original===
“No,” replied Spargo. “Certainly not in this town. How should I get it in this town if I’m a stranger?”

“Quite true, quite true!” murmured Mr. Quarterpage. “I cannot conceive how any person in the town who is in possession of one of those﻿—what shall we call them﻿—heirlooms?﻿—yes, heirlooms of antiquity, could possibly be base enough to part with it. Therefore, I ask again﻿—Where did you get that, young gentleman?”

“Before I tell you that,” answered Spargo, who, in answer to a silent sign from the fat man had drawn a chair amongst them, “perhaps you will tell me exactly what this is? I see it to be a bit of old, polished, much worn silver, having on the obverse the arms or heraldic bearings of somebody or something; on the reverse the figure of a running horse. But﻿—what is it?”

The five old men all glanced at each other and made simultaneous grunts. Then Mr. Quarterpage spoke.

===Chinese===
“不是，”斯帕戈答道，“当然不是在本镇。我一个外乡人，又怎会在本镇弄到它？”

“正是，正是！”夸特佩奇先生喃喃道，“我实在想不明白，镇上哪个人手里握着这样一件——该怎么称呼它们呢——传家宝？——对，古老公传的传家宝——竟会下作到肯把它脱手。因此我再问一遍——年轻绅士，您这东西究竟从哪儿弄来的？”

“先不忙说这个，”斯帕戈答道——方才那位胖绅士默默向他递了个眼色，他便拖过一把椅子，坐进了众人中间——“也许您肯先告诉我，这究竟是个什么物件？依我看，这是一片老旧的白银，磨得锃亮，也磨损得厉害；正面是某位人物或某个机构的纹章纹记，背面是一匹奔马的图形。可是——它到底是什么？”

五位老先生面面相觑，同时哼出声来。随后夸特佩奇先生开了口。

===Original===
“It is one of the original fifty burgess tickets of Market Milcaster, young sir, which gave its holder special and greatly valued privileges in respect to attendance at our once famous race-meeting, now unfortunately a thing of the past,” he added. “Fifty﻿—aye, forty!﻿—years ago, to be in possession of one of those tickets was﻿—was﻿—”

“A grand thing!” said one of the old gentlemen.

“Mr. Lummis is right,” said Mr. Quarterpage. “It was a grand thing﻿—a very grand thing. Those tickets, sir, were treasured﻿—are treasured. And yet you, a stranger, show us one! You got it, sir﻿—”

Spargo saw that it was now necessary to cut matters short.

===Chinese===
“这是马基特·米尔卡斯特最初那五十枚市民票牌中的一枚，年轻先生，”他接着说，“凭它在当年我们那场名重一时的赛马会上，持有人享有种种特别的、人人艳羡的特权——可惜赛马会如今已成陈迹。五十年前——哦不，四十年前！——手里能握上这么一枚票牌，那可是——可是——”

“天大的体面！”其中一位老先生说。

“拉米斯先生说得对，”夸特佩奇先生说，“是天大的体面——天大的体面。这些票牌，先生，从前被人珍藏着——如今仍被人珍藏着。可您呢，一个外乡人，竟拿出一枚来给我们开眼！您弄到它，先生——”

斯帕戈看出，这会儿不得不长话短说了。

===Original===
“I found this ticket﻿—under mysterious circumstances﻿—in London,” he answered. “I want to trace it. I want to know who its original owner was. That is why I have come to Market Milcaster.”

Mr. Quarterpage slowly looked round the circle of faces.

“Wonderful!” he said. “Wonderful! He found this ticket﻿—one of our famous fifty﻿—in London, and under mysterious circumstances. He wants to trace it﻿—he wants to know to whom it belonged! That is why he has come to Market Milcaster. Most extraordinary! Gentlemen, I appeal to you if this is not the most extraordinary event that has happened in Market Milcaster for﻿—I don’t know how many years?”

There was a general murmur of assent, and Spargo found everybody looking at him as if he had just announced that he had come to buy the whole town.

===Chinese===
“我是在伦敦——在一种神秘的境况之下——发现这枚票牌的，”他答道，“我想追查它的来历。我想知道它最初的主人是谁。这正是我到马基特·米尔卡斯特来的缘由。”

夸特佩奇先生缓缓地环视一圈众人的脸。

“妙啊！”他说，“妙啊！他在伦敦——在神秘的境况之下——发现了这枚票牌，我们赫赫有名的五十枚之一。他要追查它的来历——他要知道它原先归谁所有！这就是他来马基特·米尔卡斯特的缘由。真是奇哉怪也！诸位先生，我倒要请诸位评评理，这难道不是马基特·米尔卡斯特不知多少年来头一桩奇事么？”

四下响起一片附和的低语。斯帕戈发觉人人都望着自己，仿佛他刚才宣布的是要出钱把整座镇子买下来。

===Original===
“But﻿—why?” he asked, showing great surprise. “Why?”

“Why?” exclaimed Mr. Quarterpage. “Why? He asks﻿—why? Because, young gentleman, it is the greatest surprise to me, and to these friends of mine, too, every man jack of ’em, to hear that any one of our fifty tickets ever passed out of the possession of any of the fifty families to whom they belonged! And unless I am vastly, greatly, most unexplainably mistaken, young sir, you are not a member of any Market Milcaster family.”

“No, I’m not,” admitted Spargo. And he was going to add that until the previous evening he had never even heard of Market Milcaster, but he wisely refrained. “No, I’m certainly not,” he added.

Mr. Quarterpage waved his long pipe.

===Chinese===
“可是——为什么呢？”他问道，满脸惊讶，“为什么？”

“为什么？”夸特佩奇先生失声道，“为什么？他居然问——为什么？就因为，年轻绅士，听说我们这五十枚票牌里竟有一枚，流出它所属的那五十户人家的手，这在我看来——在我这几位朋友看来也一样，一位不落——真是奇闻一桩！而且，年轻先生，除非我大错特错、错得再没法解释，您可不是马基特·米尔卡斯特哪一户人家的成员。”

“是，我不是，”斯帕戈承认道。他本想再添一句：直到头天晚上，他连马基特·米尔卡斯特这个名字都从未听说过——但他识相地忍住了。“不，我肯定不是。”他又补了一句。

夸特佩奇先生挥了挥他那支长烟斗。

===Original===
“I believe,” he said, “I believe that if the evening were not drawing to a close﻿—it is already within a few minutes of our departure, young gentleman﻿—I believe, I say, that if I had time, I could, from memory, give the names of the fifty families who held those tickets when the race-meeting came to an end. I believe I could!”

“I’m sure you could!” asserted the little man in the loud suit. “Never was such a memory as yours, never!”

“Especially for anything relating to the old racing matters,” said the fat man. “Mr. Quarterpage is a walking encyclopaedia.”

“My memory is good,” said Mr. Quarterpage. “It’s the greatest blessing I have in my declining years. Yes, I am sure I could do that, with a little thought. And what’s more, nearly every one of those fifty families is still in the town, or if not in the town, close by it, or if not close by it, I know where they are. Therefore, I cannot make out how this young gentleman﻿—from London, did you say, sir?”

===Chinese===
“我相信，”他说，“我相信，要不是天色已近尾声——离我们散场只剩几分钟了，年轻绅士——我相信，我是说，倘若有工夫，我能凭记忆把赛马会收场时握着那些票牌的五十户人家一一报名道姓。我相信我办得到！”

“那还用说，您准办得到！”那位衣裳穿得花哨的小个子男人一口咬定，“天底下就没见过像您这样的记性，没有！”

“尤其是当年赛马会上那些事，更是记得分毫不差，”胖绅士说，“夸特佩奇先生就是一部活的百科全书。”

“我这记性是不坏，”夸特佩奇先生说，“这是我风烛残年顶大的一桩福气。是了，稍一思量，我准能报出来。不单如此，那五十户人家几乎如今都还在镇上；就算不在镇上，也在左近；就算不在左近，他们的下落我也一清二楚。所以我就闹不明白了，这位年轻绅士——您方才说是打伦敦来的，先生？”

===Original===
“From London,” answered Spargo.

“This young gentleman from London comes to be in possession of one of our tickets,” continued Mr. Quarterpage. “It is﻿—wonderful! But I tell you what, young gentleman from London, if you will do me the honour to breakfast with me in the morning, sir, I will show you my racing books and papers and we will speedily discover who the original holder of that ticket was. My name, sir, is Quarterpage﻿—Benjamin Quarterpage﻿—and I reside at the ivy-covered house exactly opposite this inn, and my breakfast hour is nine o’clock sharp, and I shall bid you heartily welcome!”

Spargo made his best bow.

“Sir,” he said, “I am greatly obliged by your kind invitation, and I shall consider it an honour to wait upon you to the moment.”

===Chinese===
“打伦敦来的。”斯帕戈答道。

“这位打伦敦来的年轻绅士，竟把我们的一枚票牌弄到了手，”夸特佩奇先生接着说，“这真是——奇哉怪也！不过这么着吧，伦敦来的年轻绅士，您若肯赏光，明儿早晨来陪我用顿早餐，先生，我就把我的赛马书册文件拿给您过目，咱们很快便能查明那枚票牌原先归谁。鄙人姓夸特佩奇，先生——本杰明·夸特佩奇——就住在这家客栈正正对面那幢爬满常春藤的宅子里。我家早餐九点整开饭，届时必竭诚恭迎！”

斯帕戈深深鞠了一躬。

“先生，”他说，“承蒙盛情相邀，不胜感激。届时定当分秒不差登门拜望，引以为荣。”

===Original===
Accordingly, at five minutes to nine next morning, Spargo found himself in an old-fashioned parlour, looking out upon a delightful garden, gay with summer flowers, and being introduced by Mr. Quarterpage, Senior, to Mr. Quarterpage, Junior﻿—a pleasant gentleman of sixty, always referred to by his father as something quite juvenile﻿—and to Miss Quarterpage, a young-old lady of something a little less elderly than her brother, and to a breakfast table bounteously spread with all the choice fare of the season. Mr. Quarterpage, Senior, was as fresh and rosy as a cherub; it was a revelation to Spargo to encounter so old a man who was still in possession of such life and spirits, and of such a vigorous and healthy appetite.

Naturally, the talk over the breakfast table ran on Spargo’s possession of the old silver ticket, upon which subject it was evident Mr. Quarterpage was still exercising his intellect. And Spargo, who had judged it well to enlighten his host as to who he was, and had exhibited a letter with which the editor of the *Watchman* had furnished him, told how in the exercise of his journalistic duties he had discovered the ticket in the lining of an old box. But he made no mention of the Marbury matter, being anxious to see first whither Mr. Quarterpage’s revelations would lead him.

===Chinese===
于是第二天早晨八点五十五分，斯帕戈已置身于一间老式客厅，窗外一座赏心悦目的花园，夏日繁花开得正盛。老夸特佩奇先生领他一一见过：先是小夸特佩奇先生——一位六十岁上下、和蔼可亲的绅士，在他父亲嘴里始终被说成是个年轻小伙子；再是夸特佩奇小姐——一位比乃兄稍稍年轻些的、半老不小的“年轻老太太”；最后是那张早餐桌——满桌时鲜美馔，铺陈得丰盛极了。老夸特佩奇先生面色红润精神焕发，活脱脱一个小天使；这般高龄的人竟还有这样的生气与兴致，还有这样健旺的好胃口，直叫斯帕戈大开眼界。

早餐桌上，话题自然而然落在斯帕戈那枚老银质票牌上，看得出这个题目仍在夸特佩奇先生脑子里盘桓。斯帕戈觉得理应让主人知道自己的身份，便出示了《守望报》主编替他备好的一封信，又讲了讲自己如何在履行记者职务时，从一只旧盒子的夹层里发现了这枚票牌。至于马伯里那桩案子，他只字不提——他急于先看看夸特佩奇先生的讲述会把话头引向何处。

===Original===
“You have no idea, Mr. Spargo,” said the old gentleman, when, breakfast over, he and Spargo were closeted together in a little library in which were abundant evidences of the host’s taste in sporting matters; “you have no idea of the value which was attached to the possession of one of those silver tickets. There is mine, as you see, securely framed and just as securely fastened to the wall. Those fifty silver tickets, my dear sir, were made when our old race-meeting was initiated, in the year 1781. They were made in the town by a local silversmith, whose great-great-grandson still carries on the business. The fifty were distributed amongst the fifty leading burgesses of the town to be kept in their families forever﻿—nobody ever anticipated in those days that our race-meeting would ever be discontinued. The ticket carried great privileges. It made its holder, and all members of his family, male and female, free of the stands, rings, and paddocks. It gave the holder himself and his eldest son, if of age, the right to a seat at our grand race banquet﻿—at which, I may tell you, Mr. Spargo, Royalty itself has been present in the good old days. Consequently, as you see, to be the holder of a silver ticket was to be somebody.”

“And when the race-meeting fell through?” asked Spargo. “What then?”

===Chinese===
“您是万万想不到的，斯帕戈先生，”早餐过后，他把斯帕戈让进一间小小的书房单独密谈，书房里到处可见主人在体育上的雅好——“您万万想不到，当年握上这样一枚银质票牌是何等贵重的东西。喏，我那枚您瞧见了，装在框里，牢牢钉在墙上。这五十枚银质票牌，亲爱的先生，是我们老赛马会创办那一年——1781年——打造的，出自本镇一位银匠之手，那位银匠的玄孙如今还开着这家铺子呢。五十枚分发给镇上五十户领头的市民人家，嘱他们永传家中——那年月谁也料不到我们的赛马会竟会有停办的一天。这票牌附带着莫大的特权：持牌人连同他一家老小、男男女女，看台、围场、下注圈一概通行无阻；持牌人本人和他的长子——只消已届成年——在我们盛大的赛马宴会上各有一席之地——我不妨告诉您，斯帕戈先生，在那美好的旧日里，连王室贵人都光临过那宴会。所以说您瞧，手里握着一枚银质票牌，那人就够得上是个人物了。”

“那后来赛马会停办了呢？”斯帕戈问，“那又如何？”

===Original===
“Then, of course, the families who held the tickets looked upon them as heirlooms, to be taken great care of,” replied Mr. Quarterpage. “They were dealt with as I dealt with mine﻿—framed on velvet, and hung up﻿—or locked away: I am sure that anybody who had one took the greatest care of it. Now, I said last night, over there at the ‘Dragon,’ that I could repeat the names of all the families who held these tickets. So I can. But here”﻿—the old gentleman drew out a drawer and produced from it a parchment-bound book which he handled with great reverence﻿—“here is a little volume of my own handwriting﻿—memoranda relating to Market Milcaster Races﻿—in which is a list of the original holders, together with another list showing who held the tickets when the races were given up. I make bold to say, Mr. Spargo, that by going through the second list, I could trace every ticket﻿—except the one you have in your purse.”

“Every one?” said Spargo, in some surprise.

===Chinese===
“那自然，持牌的人家便把它们当传家宝看待，精心保管，”夸特佩奇先生答道，“处置的法子跟我那枚一个样——衬丝绒装框，挂起来——再不然就锁藏起来：我敢保，谁手里有一枚，谁就把它当心肝宝贝供着。昨晚我在那边的‘龙旅店’说过，这些票牌都在谁家手里，我一家家报得出名字。如今也报得出。不过这儿——”老先生拉开一只抽屉，从中取出一本羊皮封面的簿子，小心翼翼、满怀敬意地捧在手里——“这本小册子是我亲笔写的——记的是马基特·米尔卡斯特赛马会的种种备忘——里头有一份最初持有人的名单，还附一份名单，载明赛马停办时票牌各在谁手。我斗胆说一句，斯帕戈先生，只要把第二份名单从头到尾过一遍，每一枚票牌我都能追出下落——唯独您钱袋里那一枚除外。”

“每一枚都追得出？”斯帕戈不无惊讶地问。

===Original===
“Every one! For as I told you,” continued Mr. Quarterpage, “the families are either in the town (we’re a conservative people here in Market Milcaster and we don’t move far afield) or they’re just outside the town, or they’re not far away. I can’t conceive how the ticket you have﻿—and it’s genuine enough﻿—could ever get out of possession of one of these families, and﻿—”

“Perhaps,” suggested Spargo, “it never has been out of possession. I told you it was found in the lining of a box﻿—that box belonged to a dead man.”

“A dead man!” exclaimed Mr. Quarterpage. “A dead man! Who could﻿—ah! Perhaps﻿—perhaps I have an idea. Yes!﻿—an idea. I remember something now that I had never thought of.”

===Chinese===
“每一枚！因为正像我方才说的，”夸特佩奇先生接着说，“这些人家要么还在镇上（我们马基特·米尔卡斯特人守旧恋土，不远走他乡），要么就在镇子边上，再不然也离得不远。我想不明白您手里这枚——它可是货真价实的——怎么会流出这些人家的手，还有——”

“也许，”斯帕戈提点道，“它压根就没流出过谁的手。我说过，它是在一只盒子的夹层里发现的——那只盒子属于一个死人。”

“一个死人！”夸特佩奇先生失声道，“一个死人！那能是谁——啊！也许——也许我想起什么来了。对！——想起一桩事。我记起一桩从前从未想到过的事了。”

===Original===
The old gentleman unfastened the clasp of his parchment-bound book, and turned over its pages until he came to one whereon was a list of names. He pointed this out to Spargo.

“There is the list of holders of the silver tickets at the time the race-meetings came to an end,” he said. “If you were acquainted with this town you would know that those are the names of our best-known inhabitants﻿—all, of course, burgesses. There’s mine, you see﻿—Quarterpage. There’s Lummis, there’s Kaye, there’s Skene, there’s Templeby﻿—the gentlemen you saw last night. All good old town names. They all are﻿—on this list. I know every family mentioned. The holders of that time are many of them dead; but their successors have the tickets. Yes﻿—and now that I think of it, there’s only one man who held a ticket when this list was made about whom I don’t know anything﻿—at least, anything recent. The ticket, Mr. Spargo, which you’ve found must have been his. But I thought﻿—I thought somebody else had it!”

“And this man, sir? Who was he?” asked Spargo, intuitively conscious that he was coming to news. “Is his name there?”

===Chinese===
老先生解开羊皮簿子上的搭扣，一页页翻过去，翻到写着一份名单的那一页，指给斯帕戈看。

“这就是赛马会收场那阵子银质票牌持有人的名单，”他说，“您若熟识本镇，一看便知这些全是镇上顶有名望的居民——当然，个个都是市民。喏，这是我——夸特佩奇。这是拉米斯，这是凯伊，这是斯基恩，这是坦普尔比——都是您昨晚见过的那几位先生。全是镇上的老姓氏。这名单上全是。名单里的人家我无一不识。当年那些持牌人大半已经故去，票牌便传到了后人手里。是了——这么一想，立这份名单的时候，持牌人里头只有一位，他的事我全然不知——至少近些年的情形一概不知。斯帕戈先生，您发现的这枚票牌，必定就是他那一枚。可我原先还只道——只道它另在别人手里呢！”

“那么这个人呢，先生？他是谁？”斯帕戈问，凭直觉感到就要听到要紧的消息了，“名单上有他的名字？”

===Original===
The old man ran the tip of his finger down the list of names.

“There it is!” he said. “John Maitland.”

Spargo bent over the fine writing.

===Chinese===
老人用指尖顺着那一列名字往下划。

“在这儿！”他说，“约翰·梅特兰。”

斯帕戈俯身端详那笔娟秀的字迹。

===Original===
“Yes, John Maitland,” he observed. “And who was John Maitland?”

Mr. Quarterpage shook his head. He turned to another of the many drawers in an ancient bureau, and began to search amongst a mass of old newspapers, carefully sorted into small bundles and tied up.

“If you had lived in Market Milcaster one-and-twenty years ago, Mr. Spargo,” he said, “you would have known who John Maitland was. For some time, sir, he was the best-known man in the place﻿—aye, and in this corner of the world. But﻿—aye, here it is﻿—the newspaper of October 5th, 1891. Now, Mr. Spargo, you’ll find in this old newspaper who John Maitland was, and all about him. Now, I’ll tell you what to do. I’ve just got to go into my office for an hour to talk the day’s business over with my son﻿—you take this newspaper out into the garden there with one of these cigars, and read what’ll you find in it, and when you’ve read that we’ll have some more talk.”

Spargo carried the old newspaper into the sunlit garden.

===Chinese===
“不错，约翰·梅特兰，”他说，“约翰·梅特兰是谁？”

夸特佩奇先生摇了摇头。他转向那张古旧写字台众多抽屉中的另一只，开始在一大堆旧报纸里翻找——那些报纸仔细分成小捆，一捆捆扎系得整整齐齐。

“斯帕戈先生，您若是二十一年前住在马基特·米尔卡斯特，”他说，“就知道约翰·梅特兰是谁了。有一阵子，先生，他是此地名声最响的人物——不，是这一方天地里名声最响的。不过——瞧，在这儿——1891年10月5日的报纸。喏，斯帕戈先生，约翰·梅特兰是谁、他是怎么一号人物，这张旧报纸上写得一清二楚。来，我给您出个主意。我正得去办公室待上一个钟头，跟我儿子把今天的生意理一理——您呢，拿上这份报纸，带一支这里的雪茄，到那边花园里去，把它登的东西细细读一遍；读完了，咱们再接着谈。”

斯帕戈拿着那张旧报纸，走进洒满阳光的花园。

## XXXV Myerst Explains / 第三十五章 迈尔斯特的解释

===Original===
It had been apparent to Spargo, from the moment of his entering the cottage, that the two old men were suffering badly from shock and fright: Cardlestone still sat in his corner shivering and trembling; he looked incapable of explaining anything; Elphick was scarcely more fitted to speak. And when Breton issued his peremptory invitation to his guardian to tell the truth, Spargo intervened.

“Far better leave him alone, Breton,” he said in a low voice. “Don’t you see the old chap’s done up? They’re both done up. We don’t know what they’ve gone through with this fellow before we came, and it’s certain they’ve had no sleep. Leave it all till later﻿—after all, we’ve found them and we’ve found him.” He jerked his thumb over his shoulder in Myerst’s direction, and Breton involuntarily followed the movement. He caught the prisoner’s eye, and Myerst laughed.

“I daresay you two young men think yourselves very clever,” he said sneeringly. “Don’t you, now?”

“We’ve been clever enough to catch you, anyway,” retorted Breton. “And now we’ve got you we’ll keep you till the police can relieve us of you.”

===Chinese===
从跨进小屋的那一刻起，斯帕戈就看得分明，两位老人受的惊吓实在不轻：卡德尔斯顿仍旧缩在角落里瑟瑟发抖，看样子什么也说不出来；埃尔菲克也不比他强得了多少。因此，当布雷顿疾言厉色地要求他的监护人说出真相时，斯帕戈便插了话。

“还是别逼他的好，布雷顿。”他低声说道，“你没瞧见这老家伙已经撑不住了吗？他们俩都撑不住了。咱们不知道来之前这两个老人遭了这家伙什么罪，可有一件事是明摆着的——他们一夜没合眼。全都留到以后再说吧——不管怎样，人我们找到了，他也抓到了。”他朝身后迈尔斯特的方向竖了竖拇指，布雷顿不由自主地随着这个动作望了过去。他的目光与那俘虏的视线撞个正着，迈尔斯特笑了。

“我敢说，你们两个年轻人自以为聪明得很。”他冷笑着说，“是不是啊？”

“无论如何，我们总算聪明到把你逮住了。”布雷顿回敬道，“既然抓到了你，我们就看住你，直到警察来把你接走。”

===Original===
“Oh!” said Myerst, with another sneering laugh. “And on what charge do you propose to hand me over to the police? It strikes me you’ll have some difficulty in formulating one, Mr. Breton.”

“Well see about that later,” said Breton. “You’ve extorted money by menaces from these gentlemen, at any rate.”

“Have I? How do you know they didn’t entrust me with these cheques as their agent?” exclaimed Myerst. “Answer me that! Or, rather, let them answer if they dare. Here you, Cardlestone, you Elphick﻿—didn’t you give me these cheques as your agent? Speak up now, and quick!”

Spargo, watching the two old men, saw them both quiver at the sound of Myerst’s voice; Cardlestone indeed, began to whimper softly.

===Chinese===
“哦！”迈尔斯特又冷笑一声，“那么，你们打算以什么罪名把我移交警察？依我看，要定出个罪名来，你们只怕有些为难吧，布雷顿先生。”

“那个以后再理论。”布雷顿道，“至少，你靠威吓从这两位先生手里敲诈了钱，这一条是跑不掉的。”

“我敲诈了吗？你们怎么知道不是他们两位委托我做代理人，才把这些支票交到我手里的？”迈尔斯特嚷了起来，“回答我呀！不，还是让他们自己来回答吧——要是他们敢的话。喂，卡德尔斯顿，还有你，埃尔菲克——你们难道不是把我当作代理人，才把这些支票交给我的吗？说话呀，快说！”

斯帕戈注视着两位老人，只见他们一听到迈尔斯特的声音便都浑身一颤；卡德尔斯顿更是低声呜咽起来。

===Original===
“Look here, Breton,” he said, whispering, “this scoundrel’s got some hold on these two old chaps﻿—they’re frightened to death of him. Leave them alone: it would be best for them if they could get some rest. Hold your tongue, you!” he added aloud, turning to Myerst. “When we want you to speak we’ll tell you.”

But Myerst laughed again.

“All very high and mighty, Mr. Spargo of the *Watchman*!” he sneered. “You’re another of the cocksure lot. And you’re very clever, but not clever enough. Now, look here! Supposing﻿—”

Spargo turned his back on him. He went over to old Cardlestone and felt his hands. And he turned to Breton with a look of concern.

===Chinese===
“听我说，布雷顿。”他压低嗓音道，“这恶棍手里抓着两位老家伙的什么把柄——他们怕他怕得要死。别逼他们了：他们要能歇一歇才最好。你给我闭嘴！”他转向迈尔斯特，扬声补了一句，“我们要你开口的时候，自然会告诉你。”

可迈尔斯特又笑了。

“好大的威风，《守望报》的斯帕戈先生！”他讥讽道，“你也是那号自命不凡的角色。你是很聪明，可还不够聪明。听好了！假如——”

斯帕戈转过身去，不再理他。他走到老卡德尔斯顿身边，摸了摸他的手，随即面带忧色地转向布雷顿。

===Original===
“I say!” he exclaimed. “He’s more than frightened﻿—he’s ill! What’s to be done?”

“I asked the police to bring a doctor along with them,” answered Breton. “In the meantime, let’s put him to bed﻿—there are beds in that inner room. We’ll get him to bed and give him something hot to drink﻿—that’s all I can think of for the present.”

Between them they managed to get Cardlestone to his bed, and Spargo, with a happy thought, boiled water on the rusty stove and put hot bottles to his feet. When that was done they persuaded Elphick to lie down in the inner room. Presently both old men fell asleep, and then Breton and Spargo suddenly realized that they themselves were hungry and wet and weary.

“There ought to be food in the cupboard,” said Breton, beginning to rummage. “They’ve generally had a good stock of tinned things. Here we are, Spargo﻿—these are tongues and sardines. Make some hot coffee while I open one of these tins.”

===Chinese===
“我说！”他失声道，“这不止是吓着了——他是病了！怎么办？”

“我已经请警察来时带个医生一道。”布雷顿答道，“眼下先让他躺下吧——里间有床。先安顿他睡下，再给他弄点热乎的喝——眼下我只想得出这些。”

两人合力把卡德尔斯顿安顿到床上，斯帕戈灵机一动，在锈迹斑斑的炉子上烧了水，给他脚边搁了热水瓶。安顿完毕，他们又劝埃尔菲克到里间躺下。不多时，两位老人都睡着了，这时布雷顿和斯帕戈才猛然意识到，他们自己也又饿、又湿、又累。

“碗柜里应该有吃的。”布雷顿说着，翻找起来，“他们平时总存着不少罐头。有了，斯帕戈——这儿是牛舌罐头和沙丁鱼。趁我开罐头的工夫，你煮点热咖啡。”

===Original===
The prisoner watched the preparations for a rough and ready breakfast with eyes that eventually began to glisten.

“I may remind you that I’m hungry, too,” he said as Spargo set the coffee on the table. “And you’ve no right to starve me, even if you’ve the physical ability to keep me tied up. Give me something to eat, if you please.”

“You shan’t starve,” said Breton, carelessly. He cut an ample supply of bread and meat, filled a cup with coffee and placed cup and plate before Myerst. “Untie his right arm, Spargo,” he continued. “I think we can give him that liberty. We’ve got his revolver, anyhow.”

For a while the three men ate and drank in silence. At last Myerst pushed his plate away. He looked scrutinizingly at his two captors. “Look here!” he said. “You think you know a lot about all this affair, Spargo, but there’s only one person who knows all about it. That’s me!”

===Chinese===
那俘虏望着两人张罗这顿将就的早饭，眼睛渐渐亮了起来。

“我提醒二位，我也饿了。”斯帕戈把咖啡端上桌时，他开口道，“就算你们有力气把我捆着，也没权利饿死我。请给我点吃的吧。”

“饿不着你。”布雷顿漫不经心地说。他切了满满一盘面包和肉，斟上一杯咖啡，把杯盘放到迈尔斯特面前。“给他解开右臂，斯帕戈。”他接着说，“这点自由我想可以给他。反正他的左轮手枪在我们手里。”

三个人默默吃了一阵。最后，迈尔斯特把盘子推开，细细打量着抓住他的这两个年轻人。“听着！”他说，“斯帕戈，你以为自己对这件事知道得不少了，可是只有一个人知道全部底细。那就是我！”

===Original===
“We’re taking that for granted,” said Spargo. “We guessed as much when we found you here. You’ll have ample opportunity for explanation, you know, later on.”

“I’ll explain now, if you care to hear,” said Myerst with another of his cynical laughs. “And if I do, I’ll tell you the truth. I know you’ve got an idea in your heads that isn’t favourable to me, but you’re utterly wrong, whatever you may think. Look here!﻿—I’ll make you a fair offer. There are some cigars in my case there﻿—give me one, and mix me a drink of that whisky﻿—a good ’un﻿—and I’ll tell you what I know about this matter. Come on!﻿—anything’s better than sitting here doing nothing.”

The two young men looked at each other. Then Breton nodded. “Let him talk if he likes,” he said. “We’re not bound to believe him. And we may hear something that’s true. Give him his cigar and his drink.”

Myerst took a stiff pull at the contents of the tumbler which Spargo presently set before him. He laughed as he inhaled the first fumes of his cigar.

===Chinese===
“这一点我们姑且认了。”斯帕戈说，“在这儿撞见你的时候，我们就猜到几分了。往后有的是机会让你解释，这你放心。”

“要是二位愿意听，我现在就可以解释。”迈尔斯特又发出一声他那种玩世不恭的笑，“而且只要我开口，说的就是实话。我知道你们俩脑子里对我的看法不妙，可不管你们怎么想，都全错了。这样吧！——我给你们个公道的提议。我那只匣子里有几支雪茄——给我一支，再给我调一杯威士忌——要调得地道些——我就把这件事的底细讲给你们听。来吧！——总比干坐在这儿强。”

两个年轻人对视一眼，布雷顿点了点头。“他想说就让他说。”他说，“我们又不是非信他不可。说不定还能听到几句真话。把雪茄和酒给他吧。”

斯帕戈很快把大酒杯放到迈尔斯特面前，他仰头灌了一大口；抽上雪茄、吸进第一口烟气时，他笑了。

===Original===
“As it happens, you’ll hear nothing but the truth,” he observed. “Now that things are as they are, there’s no reason why I shouldn’t tell the truth. The fact is, I’ve nothing to fear. You can’t give me in charge, for it so happens that I’ve got a power of attorney from these two old chaps inside there to act for them in regard to the money they entrusted me with. It’s in an inside pocket of that letter-case, and if you look at it, Breton, you’ll see it’s in order. I’m not even going to dare you to interfere with or destroy it﻿—you’re a barrister, and you’ll respect the law. But that’s a fact﻿—and if anybody’s got a case against anybody, I have against you two for assault and illegal detention. But I’m not a vindictive man, and﻿—”

Breton took up Myerst’s letter-case and examined its contents. And presently he turned to Spargo.

“He’s right!” he whispered. “This is quite in order.” He turned to Myerst. “All the same,” he said, addressing him, “we shan’t release you, because we believe you’re concerned in the murder of John Marbury. We’re justified in holding you on that account.”

===Chinese===
“说来也巧，你们听到的将全是实话。”他说，“事到如今，我没有理由不说实话。事实上，我无所畏惧。你们没法把我送官究办，因为偏偏凑巧，里头那两位老家伙给我签过一份授权委托书，委托我代办他们交我经手的那些钱款。就放在那只文件夹的内层口袋里，布雷顿，你去看，手续一应俱全。我甚至不必警告你们不得动它、不得毁它——你是出庭律师，自然尊重法律。可这就是实情——真要说谁对谁有官司，倒是我可以告你们两个，罪名是人身侵害和非法拘禁。不过我这人不记仇，所以——”

布雷顿拿起迈尔斯特的文件夹，检查了里面的东西，随即转向斯帕戈。

“他说得没错！”他低声道，“手续确实齐全。”他又转向迈尔斯特。“不过，”他对着他说，“我们不会放你，因为我们相信你与约翰·马伯里被害一案有牵连。就此拘着你，我们是站得住脚的。”

===Original===
“All right, my young friend,” said Myerst. “Have your own stupid way. But I said I’d tell you the plain truth. Well, the plain truth is that I know no more of the absolute murder of your father than I know of what is going on in Timbuktu at this moment! I do not know who killed John Maitland. That’s a fact! It may have been the old man in there who’s already at his own last gasp, or it mayn’t. I tell you I don’t know﻿—though, like you, Spargo, I’ve tried hard to find out. That’s the truth﻿—I do not know.”

“You expect us to believe that?” exclaimed Breton incredulously.

“Believe it or not, as you like﻿—it’s the truth,” answered Myerst. “Now, look here﻿—I said nobody knew as much of this affair as I know, and that’s true also. And here’s the truth of what I know. The old man in that room, whom you know as Nicholas Cardlestone, is in reality Chamberlayne, the stockbroker, of Market Milcaster, whose name was so freely mentioned when your father was tried there. That’s another fact!”

===Chinese===
“行啊，我的年轻朋友。”迈尔斯特说，“你们就由着性子犯傻吧。可我说过要告诉你们不折不扣的实话。那么，不折不扣的实话就是：你父亲究竟死于谁手，我知道的不比此刻廷巴克图城里正发生的事多！我不知道是谁杀了约翰·梅特兰。这是事实！可能是里头那位已经一口气喘到头的老人，也可能不是。我告诉你，我不知道——虽然跟你一样，斯帕戈，我也下过苦功去查。这就是实话——我不知道。”

“你要我们相信这话？”布雷顿喊了起来，满心的不信。

“信不信由你们——反正这是实话。”迈尔斯特答道，“听好了——我说过这件事没人知道得有我多，这也是真的。下面就是我所知的一切真相。那间屋里的老人，你们知道他叫尼古拉斯·卡德尔斯顿，其实就是马基特·米尔卡斯特的股票经纪人张伯莱恩——你父亲当年在那儿受审的时候，这个名字可没少被人挂在嘴上。这又是一桩事实！”

===Original===
“How,” asked Breton, sternly, “can you prove it? How do you know it?”

“Because,” replied Myerst, with a cunning grin, “I helped to carry out his mock death and burial﻿—I was a solicitor in those days, and my name was﻿—something else. There were three of us at it: Chamberlayne’s nephew; a doctor of no reputation; and myself. We carried it out very cleverly, and Chamberlayne gave us five thousand pounds apiece for our trouble. It was not the first time that I had helped him and been well paid for my help. The first time was in connection with the Cloudhampton Hearth and Home Mutual Benefit Society affair﻿—Aylmore, or Ainsworth, was as innocent as a child in that!﻿—Chamberlayne was the man at the back. But, unfortunately, Chamberlayne didn’t profit﻿—he lost all he got by it, pretty quick. That was why be transferred his abilities to Market Milcaster.”

“You can prove all this, I suppose?” remarked Spargo.

===Chinese===
“你有什么凭据，”布雷顿厉声问道，“能证明这一点？你又是从何知道的？”

“因为，”迈尔斯特答道，脸上挂着狡黠的笑，“帮他操办那场假死假葬的，正是我——那年头我还是个事务律师，名字嘛——叫别的什么。办这件事的一共三个人：张伯莱恩的侄子；一个名声不怎么样的医生；还有我。我们办得干净利落，张伯莱恩给了我们每人五千镑作辛苦费。我帮他忙并为此拿到丰厚的报酬，这也不是头一回了。头一回是为克劳德汉普顿炉边家园互助会那桩案子——在那件事里，艾尔莫尔，或者说艾恩斯沃斯，清白得跟个孩子一样！——幕后主使正是张伯莱恩。可惜啊，张伯莱恩没捞着好处——从那件事上到手的钱，没多久就赔了个精光。所以他才把他的本事挪到马基特·米尔卡斯特去施展。”

“这些你都能拿出凭据来，我猜？”斯帕戈说道。

===Original===
“Every word﻿—every letter! But about the Market Milcaster affair: Your father, Breton, was right in what he said about Chamberlayne having all the money that was got from the bank. He had﻿—and he engineered that mock death and funeral so that he could disappear, and he paid us who helped him generously, as I’ve told you. The thing couldn’t have been better done. When it was done, the nephew disappeared; the doctor disappeared; Chamberlayne disappeared. I had bad luck﻿—to tell you the truth, I was struck off the rolls for a technical offence. So I changed my name and became Mr. Myerst, and eventually what I am now. And it was not until three years ago that I found Chamberlayne. I found him in this way: After I became secretary to the Safe Deposit Company, I took chambers in the Temple, above Cardlestone’s. And I speedily found out who he was. Instead of going abroad, the old fox﻿—though he was a comparatively young ’un, then!﻿—had shaved off his beard, settled down in the Temple and given himself up to his two hobbies, collecting curiosities and stamps. There he’d lived quietly all these years, and nobody had ever recognized or suspected him. Indeed, I don’t see how they could; he lived such a quiet, secluded life, with his collections, his old port, and his little whims and fads. But﻿—I knew him!”

“And you doubtless profited by your recognition,” suggested Breton.

“I certainly did. He was glad to pay me a nice sum every quarter to hold my tongue,” replied Myerst, “and I was glad to take it and, naturally, I gained a considerable knowledge of him. He had only one friend﻿—Mr. Elphick, in there. Now, I’ll tell you about him.”

===Chinese===
“字字有据——信信有凭！至于马基特·米尔卡斯特那桩案子：布雷顿，你父亲说张伯莱恩独吞了从银行弄出来的全部钱款，他说得一点不错。钱确实都在他手里——他策划那场假死假葬，就是为了脱身遁走，而且如我方才所说，给我们这些帮忙的人的报酬也够慷慨。那件事办得再漂亮不过了。事成之后，侄子消失了；医生消失了；张伯莱恩也消失了。只有我时运不济——不瞒你们说，我因为一桩技术性的过错被从律师名册上除了名。于是我改了名字，成了迈尔斯特先生，一步步混成了如今这副模样。而直到三年前，我才找到张伯莱恩。我是这么找到他的：我当了保险寄存库公司的秘书之后，在中殿律师学院里租下一套寓所，就在卡德尔斯顿的楼上。我不久便查明了他的真实身份。这只老狐狸——虽说那时候他还算个年轻的！——根本没出国，而是刮掉了胡子，在中殿安顿下来，一头扎进他的两大癖好：收藏古玩和邮票。这些年他就安安静静住在那里，从来没有人认出他，也没有人起过疑心。说实在的，我看也没人能认出来；他过得那样清静避世，守着他的收藏、他的陈年波特酒，还有他那些小小的心血来潮和怪脾气。可是——我认得他！”

“而你认出他之后，想必也借此捞了不少好处。”布雷顿接口道。

“那当然。他乐得每季度付我一笔可观的钱，买我守口如瓶。”迈尔斯特答道，“我也乐得收下，而且自然而然，我对他的底细也就知道得相当多了。他只有一个朋友——就是里头那位埃尔菲克先生。现在，我跟你们讲讲此人。”

===Original===
“Only if you are going to speak respectfully of him,” said Breton sternly.

“I’ve no reason to do otherwise. Elphick is the man who ought to have married your mother. When things turned out as they did, Elphick took you and brought you up as he has done, so that you should never know of your father’s disgrace. Elphick never knew until last night that Cardlestone is Chamberlayne. Even the biggest scoundrels have friends﻿—Elphick’s very fond of Cardlestone. He﻿—”

Spargo turned sharply on Myerst.

“You say Elphick didn’t know until last night!” he exclaimed. “Why, then, this running away? What were they running from?”

===Chinese===
“先说好，你得口下留德，好好说他。”布雷顿厉声道。

“我本来也没有理由说他别的。埃尔菲克，就是本该娶你母亲的那个人。后来事态既已那样发展，埃尔菲克便收养了你，把你抚养成人，为的是让你永远不知道你父亲的耻辱。直到昨天晚上，埃尔菲克才知道卡德尔斯顿就是张伯莱恩。再大的恶棍也有朋友——埃尔菲克对卡德尔斯顿感情很深。他——”

斯帕戈猛地转向迈尔斯特。

“你说埃尔菲克昨晚才知道！”他失声道，“那么，这一路的逃亡又是为什么？他们躲的究竟是什么？”

===Original===
“I have no more notion than you have, Spargo,” replied Myerst. “I tell you one or other of them knows something that I don’t. Elphick, I gather, took fright from you, and went to Cardlestone﻿—then they both vanished. It may be that Cardlestone did kill Maitland﻿—I don’t know. But I’ll tell you what I know about the actual murder﻿—for I do know a good deal about it, though, as I say, I don’t know who killed Maitland. Now, first, you know all that about Maitland’s having papers and valuables and gold on him? Very well﻿—I’ve got all that. The whole lot is locked up﻿—safely﻿—and I’m willing to hand it over to you, Breton, when we go back to town, and the necessary proof is given﻿—as it will be﻿—that you’re Maitland’s son.”

Myerst paused to see the effect of this announcement, and laughed when he saw the blank astonishment which stole over his hearers’ faces.

===Chinese===
“我跟你们一样摸不着头脑，斯帕戈。”迈尔斯特答道，“我告诉你们，那两位中间总有一位知道些我不知道的事。我料想，是埃尔菲克被你吓着了，去找卡德尔斯顿——随后两人便一同失踪了。也可能是卡德尔斯顿真的杀了梅特兰——我不知道。不过，关于凶案本身，我可以把知道的都告诉你们——这方面我的确知道不少，虽然如我所说，我不知道是谁杀了梅特兰。首先，梅特兰身上带着文件、贵重物品和金币的那些事，你们都清楚了吧？很好——那些东西全在我手里。整批东西都锁起来了——锁得稳稳当当——等我们回城，办妥必要的手续——手续是会办妥的——证明你确实是梅特兰的儿子，布雷顿，我就把那些东西交到你手上。”

迈尔斯特顿了顿，看看这番话引起什么反应；待到看见听者脸上悄然浮起的错愕之色，他笑了。

===Original===
“And still more,” he continued, “I’ve got all the contents of that leather box which Maitland deposited with me﻿—that’s safely locked up, too, and at your disposal. I took possession of that the day after the murder. Then, for purposes of my own, I went to Scotland Yard, as Spargo there is aware. You see, I was playing a game﻿—and it required some ingenuity.”

“A game!” exclaimed Breton. “Good heavens﻿—what game?”

“I never knew until I had possession of all these things that Marbury was Maitland of Market Milcaster,” answered Myerst. “When I did know then I began to put things together and to pursue my own line, independent of everybody. I tell you I had all Maitland’s papers and possessions, by that time﻿—except one thing. That packet of Australian stamps. And﻿—I found out that those stamps were in the hands of﻿—Cardlestone!”

===Chinese===
“还不止呢。”他接着说，“梅特兰存放在我这里的那只皮盒，里面的东西也全在我手里——同样锁得稳稳当当，听凭你们处置。凶案第二天，我就把它取了过来。然后，出于我自己的盘算，我去了苏格兰场——那边那位斯帕戈是知道的。你们瞧，我一直在下一盘棋——这盘棋下起来，可得动些脑筋。”

“一盘棋！”布雷顿喊了起来，“天哪——什么棋？”

“直到把这些东西全部弄到手，我才知道马伯里就是马基特·米尔卡斯特的梅特兰。”迈尔斯特答道，“一旦知道，我便开始把各条线索拼到一起，按自己的路子追查下去，不依靠任何人。我告诉你们，那时候梅特兰的文件和财物已尽在我手——只差一样东西。那包澳大利亚邮票。而——我查出来了，那些邮票落在了——卡德尔斯顿手里！”

## XXVIII Of Proved Identity / 第二十八章 身份确证

===Original===

Spargo sat down again in the chair which he had just left, and looked at the two people upon whom his startling announcement had produced such a curious effect. And he recognized as he looked at them that, while they were both frightened, they were frightened in different ways. Miss Baylis had already recovered her composure; she now sat sombre and stern as ever, returning Spargo’s look with something of indifferent defiance; he thought he could see that in her mind a certain fear was battling with a certain amount of wonder that he had discovered the secret. It seemed to him that so far as she was concerned the secret had come to an end; it was as if she said in so many words that now the secret was out he might do his worst.

But upon Mr. Septimus Elphick the effect was very different. He was still trembling from excitement; he groaned as he sank into his chair and the hand with which he poured out a glass of spirits shook; the glass rattled against his teeth when he raised it to his lips. The half-contemptuous fashion of his reception of Spargo had now wholly disappeared; he was a man who had received a shock, and a bad one. And Spargo, watching him keenly, said to himself: This man knows a great deal more than, a great deal beyond, the mere fact that Marbury was Maitland, and that Ronald Breton is in reality Maitland’s son; he knows something which he never wanted anybody to know, which he firmly believed it impossible anybody ever could know. It was as if he had buried something deep, deep down in the lowest depths, and was as astounded as he was frightened to find that it had been at last flung up to the broad light of day.

===Chinese===

斯帕戈重新在方才离开的那把椅子上坐下，打量着这两个人——他那石破天惊的一句宣告，在他们身上激起了何等古怪的反应。他一面看着，一面看出门道：两人虽都受了惊吓，惊法却各不相同。贝利斯小姐已经恢复了平静；此刻她照旧阴沉而严厉地坐着，回视斯帕戈的目光里带着几分满不在乎的挑衅。他觉得可以看出，她心里某种恐惧正与几分惊异交战——惊的是他竟探出了这个秘密。在他看来，就她而论，这秘密已然到了头；那副神情简直等于明说：如今秘密既已败露，他爱怎么摆布就怎么摆布吧。

可这番话落在塞普蒂默斯·埃尔菲克先生身上，效果却大不相同。他仍因激动而浑身发抖；跌坐进椅子时他呻吟出声，斟烈酒的那只手抖个不停；杯子举到唇边，磕得牙齿咯咯作响。方才接待斯帕戈时那副半带轻慢的做派，此刻已荡然无存；这是一个挨了打击的人，而且挨得不轻。斯帕戈敏锐地注视着他，心里暗自断言：此人所知的，远远超出马伯里就是梅特兰、罗纳德·布雷顿实为梅特兰之子这一层事实；他还知道一件他从来不想让任何人知道的事，一件他坚信任何人永远也不可能知道的事。那情形，就像他把某样东西埋进了极深极深的渊底，如今却发现它终于被翻抛到光天化日之下，又是骇异，又是惶恐。

===Original===

“I shall wait,” suddenly said Spargo, “until you are composed, Mr. Elphick. I have no wish to distress you. But I see, of course, that the truths which I have told you are of a sort that cause you considerable﻿—shall we say fear?”

Elphick took another stiff pull at his liquor. His hand had grown steadier, and the colour was coming back to his face.

“If you will let me explain,” he said. “If you will hear what was done for the boy’s sake﻿—eh?”

“That,” answered Spargo, “is precisely what I wish. I can tell you this﻿—I am the last man in the world to wish harm of any sort to Mr. Breton.”

Miss Baylis relieved her feelings with a scornful sniff. “He says that!” she exclaimed, addressing the ceiling. “He says that, knowing that he means to tell the world in his rag of a paper that Ronald Breton, on whom every care has been lavished, is the son of a scoundrel, an ex-convict, a﻿—”

===Chinese===

“我可以等，”斯帕戈忽然说道，“等到您平静下来，埃尔菲克先生。我无意让您难过。不过我自然看得出来，我方才告诉您的这些实情，是叫您相当——我们就说是恐惧吧？”

埃尔菲克又就着酒瓶狠狠灌了一大口。他的手稳了些，脸上也渐渐回了血色。

“您若容我解释，”他说，“您若肯听一听当年为了那孩子是怎么做的——嗯？”

“这，”斯帕戈答道，“恰恰是我求之不得的。我可以这么说——世上再没有一个人，比我更不愿让布雷顿先生受到半点伤害。”

贝利斯小姐轻蔑地哼了一声，泄了泄心头的火气。“他说得出这话！”她冲着天花板嚷道。“他说得出这话，分明知道自己是打算在他那张破烂小报上告诉全世界：受尽呵护栽培的罗纳德·布雷顿，竟是恶棍的儿子、坐过牢的罪犯、一个——”

===Original===

Elphick lifted his hand.

“Hush﻿—hush!” he said imploringly. “Mr. Spargo means well, I am sure﻿—I am convinced. If Mr. Spargo will hear me﻿—”

But before Spargo could reply, a loud insistent knocking came at the outer door. Elphick started nervously, but presently he moved across the room, walking as if he had received a blow, and opened the door. A boy’s voice penetrated into the sitting-room.

“If you please, sir, is Mr. Spargo, of the *Watchman*, here? He left this address in case he was wanted.”

Spargo recognized the voice as that of one of the office messenger boys, and jumping up, went to the door.

===Chinese===

埃尔菲克抬起手来。

“嘘——嘘！”他哀告道。“斯帕戈先生是一番好意，我敢肯定——我深信不疑。只要斯帕戈先生肯听我说——”

不等斯帕戈答话，外门骤然响起一阵急促而不容分说的敲门声。埃尔菲克吓得一惊，但随即还是穿过房间去开了门——那走相，活像挨了一记闷棍。一个少年的声音传进客厅里来。

“请问，先生，《守望报》的斯帕戈先生是在这儿吗？他留下了这个地址，以防有人要找他。”

斯帕戈听出那是报馆一个听差童子的声音，便一跃而起，走向门口。

===Original===

“What is it, Rawlins?” he asked.

“Will you please come back to the office, sir, at once? There’s Mr. Rathbury there and says he must see you instantly.”

“All right,” answered Spargo. “I’m coming just now.”

He motioned the lad away, and turned to Elphick.

“I shall have to go,” he said. “I may be kept. Now, Mr. Elphick, can I come to see you tomorrow morning?”

“Yes, yes, tomorrow morning!” replied Elphick eagerly. “Tomorrow morning, certainly. At eleven﻿—eleven o’clock. That will do?”

===Chinese===

“什么事，罗林斯？”他问。

“请您马上回报馆来一趟好吗，先生？拉思伯里先生在那儿，说必须立刻见您。”

“好，”斯帕戈答道，“我这就来。”

他挥手打发那孩子走了，转身向着埃尔菲克。

“我不得不走了，”他说，“说不定一时脱不开身。那么，埃尔菲克先生，明天早晨我能来见您吗？”

“是的，是的，明天早晨！”埃尔菲克急切地答道。“明天早晨，一定。十一点——十一点钟。行不行？”

===Original===

“I shall be here at eleven,” said Spargo. “Eleven sharp.”

He was moving away when Elphick caught him by the sleeve.

“A word﻿—just a word!” he said. “You﻿—you have not told the﻿—the boy﻿—Ronald﻿—of what you know? You haven’t?”

“I haven’t,” replied Spargo.

Elphick tightened his grip on Spargo’s sleeve. He looked into his face beseechingly.

“Promise me﻿—promise me, Mr. Spargo, that you won’t tell him until you have seen me in the morning!” he implored. “I beg you to promise me this.”

===Chinese===

“我十一点到，”斯帕戈说，“十一点整。”

他正要走开，埃尔菲克一把抓住了他的衣袖。

“说句话——就一句！”他说。“你——你没有把你知道的事告诉那——那孩子——罗纳德——吧？没有吧？”

“没有，”斯帕戈答道。

埃尔菲克把斯帕戈的袖子攥得更紧了。他哀求地望着他的脸。

“答应我——答应我，斯帕戈先生，明早见过我之前，千万别告诉他！”他恳求道。“我求您答应我这一点。”

===Original===

Spargo hesitated, considering matters.

“Very well﻿—I promise,” he said.

“And you won’t print it?” continued Elphick, still clinging to him. “Say you won’t print it tonight?”

“I shall not print it tonight,” answered Spargo. “That’s certain.”

Elphick released his grip on the young man’s arm.

“Come﻿—at eleven tomorrow morning,” he said, and drew back and closed the door.

===Chinese===

斯帕戈迟疑了一下，把事情掂量了一番。

“好吧——我答应你，”他说。

“也不登报？”埃尔菲克仍抓着他不放，追问道。“你说，今晚不登报？”

“今晚我不登出去，”斯帕戈答道，“这是肯定的。”

埃尔菲克松开了抓着这年轻人手臂的手。

“来吧——明天早晨十一点，”他说着，退回去关上了门。

===Original===

Spargo ran quickly to the office and hurried up to his own room. And there, calmly seated in an easy-chair, smoking a cigar, and reading an evening newspaper, was Rathbury, unconcerned and outwardly as imperturbable as ever. He greeted Spargo with a careless nod and a smile.

“Well,” he said, “how’s things?”

Spargo, half-breathless, dropped into his desk-chair.

“You didn’t come here to tell me that,” he said.

Rathbury laughed.

===Chinese===

斯帕戈一路快跑回报馆，径直奔上自己的房间。只见拉思伯里正安然坐在安乐椅里，抽着雪茄，读一份晚报，一副满不在乎的样子，表面上一如既往地镇定自若。他朝斯帕戈漫不经意地点点头，笑了笑，算是打招呼。

“喂，”他说，“事情怎么样？”

斯帕戈气喘吁吁地跌坐进自己的办公椅。

“你可不是专程来跟我说这个的，”他说。

拉思伯里笑了。

===Original===

“No,” he said, throwing the newspaper aside, “I didn’t. I came to tell you my latest. You’re at full liberty to stick it into your paper tonight: it may just as well be known.”

“Well?” said Spargo.

Rathbury took his cigar out of his lips and yawned.

“Aylmore’s identified,” he said lazily.

Spargo sat up, sharply.

===Chinese===

“不是，”他把报纸往旁边一扔，“不是为这个。我是来告诉你我的最新消息。你尽管今晚就把它塞进你的报里去：这事索性传开也无妨。”

“哦？”斯帕戈说。

拉思伯里从唇边取下雪茄，打了个哈欠。

“艾尔莫尔被认出来了，”他懒洋洋地说。

斯帕戈猛地坐直了身子。

===Original===

“Identified!”

“Identified, my son. Beyond doubt.”

“But as whom﻿—as what?” exclaimed Spargo.

Rathbury laughed.

“He’s an old lag﻿—an ex-convict. Served his time partly at Dartmoor. That, of course, is where he met Maitland or Marbury. D’ye see? Clear as noontide now, Spargo.”

Spargo sat drumming his fingers on the desk before him. His eyes were fixed on a map of London that hung on the opposite wall; his ears heard the throbbing of the printing-machines far below. But what he really saw was the faces of the two girls; what he really heard was the voices of two girls﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“认出来了？”

“认出来了，老弟。铁板钉钉。”

“可认出他是什么人——是什么？”斯帕戈失声道。

拉思伯里笑了。

“他是个老官司——蹲过大狱的。刑期有一部分就是在达特穆尔服的。不用说，他就是在那儿认识梅特兰、也就是马伯里的。明白了吗？现在清楚得像正午的日头了，斯帕戈。”

斯帕戈坐在那里，手指在面前的书桌上敲个不停。他的两眼盯着对面墙上挂的一幅伦敦地图；两耳听着楼下深处印刷机沉闷的轰鸣。可他真正看见的，是那两个姑娘的脸；真正听见的，是两个姑娘的声音……

===Original===

“Clear as noontide﻿—as noontide,” repeated Rathbury with great cheerfulness.

Spargo came back to the earth of plain and brutal fact.

“What’s clear as noontide?” he asked sharply.

“What? Why, the whole thing! Motive﻿—everything,” answered Rathbury. “Don’t you see, Maitland and Aylmore (his real name is Ainsworth, by the by) meet at Dartmoor, probably, or, rather, certainly, just before Aylmore’s release. Aylmore goes abroad, makes money, in time comes back, starts new career, gets into Parliament, becomes big man. In time, Maitland, who, after his time, has also gone abroad, also comes back. The two meet. Maitland probably tries to blackmail Aylmore or threatens to let folk know that the flourishing Mr. Aylmore, M.P., is an ex-convict. Result﻿—Aylmore lures him to the Temple and quiets him. Pooh!﻿—the whole thing’s clear as noontide, as I say. As﻿—noontide!”

Spargo drummed his fingers again.

===Chinese===

“清楚得像正午的日头——正午的日头，”拉思伯里兴高采烈地重复道。

斯帕戈这才回过神来，落回那直白而残酷的现实的地面。

“什么清楚得像正午的日头？”他没好气地问。

“什么？嗐，整件事呗！动机——样样俱全，”拉思伯里答道。“你没看出来吗？梅特兰和艾尔莫尔（顺便说一句，他的真姓是艾恩斯沃斯）是在达特穆尔碰的头，多半是——不，干脆说一定是在艾尔莫尔出狱前不久。艾尔莫尔出国，发了财，过些年回来，另起一番事业，进了议会，成了大人物。也是过了些年，刑满后同样出了国的梅特兰也回来了。两人碰上了。梅特兰多半是想敲艾尔莫尔的竹杠，或是威胁着要把风风光光的议员艾尔莫尔先生是个坐过牢的这件事抖给世人。结果——艾尔莫尔把他诱到中殿律师学院，让他永远闭了嘴。呸！——整件事清楚得像正午的日头，我说的一点不错。正——午——日头！”

斯帕戈又敲起手指来。

===Original===

“How?” he asked quietly. “How came Aylmore to be identified?”

“My work,” said Rathbury proudly. “My work, my son. You see, I thought a lot. And especially after we’d found out that Marbury was Maitland.”

“You mean after I’d found out,” remarked Spargo.

Rathbury waved his cigar.

“Well, well, it’s all the same,” he said. “You help me, and I help you, eh? Well, as I say, I thought a considerable lot. I thought﻿—now, where did Maitland, or Marbury, know or meet Aylmore twenty or twenty-two years ago? Not in London, because we knew Maitland never was in London﻿—at any rate, before his trial, and we haven’t the least proof that he was in London after. And why won’t Aylmore tell? Clearly because it must have been in some undesirable place. And then, all of a sudden, it flashed on me in a moment of﻿—what do you writing fellows call those moments, Spargo?”

“Inspiration, I should think,” said Spargo. “Direct inspiration.”

===Chinese===

“怎么？”他不动声色地问。“艾尔莫尔是怎么被认出来的？”

“我的功劳，”拉思伯里得意地说。“我的功劳，老弟。你瞧，我琢磨了又琢磨，尤其是在我们发现马伯里就是梅特兰之后。”

“你是说，在我发现之后，”斯帕戈说。

拉思伯里挥了挥雪茄。

“得了，得了，横竖一回事，”他说。“你帮我，我帮你，嗯？好好，我方才说了，我着实琢磨了一大通。我想——梅特兰，或者说马伯里，二十或二十二年前是在哪儿认识、在哪儿碰上艾尔莫尔的？总不会在伦敦，因为我们知道梅特兰压根儿没到过伦敦——至少受审之前没到过，而受审之后，我们也没有丝毫证据证明他来过。那艾尔莫尔为什么死活不肯说？显然因为那必是个不体面的地方。就在这时，我脑子里忽地一亮，来了个——你们这些舞文弄墨的家伙管那种时刻叫什么来着，斯帕戈？”

“灵感吧，我想，”斯帕戈说。“直截了当的灵感。”

===Original===

“That’s it. In a moment of direct inspiration, it flashed on me﻿—why, twenty years ago, Maitland was in Dartmoor﻿—they must have met there! And so, we got some old warders who’d been there at that time to come to town, and we gave ’em opportunities to see Aylmore and to study him. Of course, he’s twenty years older, and he’s grown a beard, but they began to recall him, and then one man remembered that if he was the man they thought he’d a certain birthmark. And﻿—he has!”

“Does Aylmore know that he’s been identified?” asked Spargo.

Rathbury pitched his cigar into the fireplace and laughed.

“Know!” he said scornfully. “Know? He’s admitted it. What was the use of standing out against proof like that. He admitted it tonight in my presence. Oh, he knows all right!”

“And what did he say?”

===Chinese===

“正是。就在那直截了当的灵光一闪之间，我想明白了——嗐，二十年前，梅特兰正在达特穆尔——他们准是在那儿碰上的！于是我们请了几位当年在那儿当差的老狱卒进城，安排他们见艾尔莫尔、端详他。当然，他老了二十岁，又蓄了络腮胡子，可他们渐渐把他回想起来了，接着有一位记起，若真是他们想的那个人，他身上该有块胎记。结果——他真有！”

“艾尔莫尔知道自己被认出来了吗？”斯帕戈问。

拉思伯里把雪茄扔进壁炉，笑了起来。

“知道！”他不屑地说。“知道？他都招了。那样的铁证，抵赖还有什么用。今晚他当着我的面承认了。哼，他清楚得很！”

“那他怎么说？”

===Original===

Rathbury laughed contemptuously.

“Say? Oh, not much. Pretty much what he said about this affair﻿—that when he was convicted the time before he was an innocent man. He’s certainly a good hand at playing the innocent game.”

“And of what was he convicted?”

“Oh, of course, we know all about it﻿—now. As soon as we found out who he really was, we had all the particulars turned up. Aylmore, or Ainsworth (Stephen Ainsworth his name really is) was a man who ran a sort of what they call a Mutual Benefit Society in a town right away up in the North﻿—Cloudhampton﻿—some thirty years ago. He was nominally secretary, but it was really his own affair. It was patronized by the working classes﻿—Cloudhampton’s a purely artisan population﻿—and they stuck a lot of their brass, as they call it, in it. Then suddenly it came to smash, and there was nothing. He﻿—Ainsworth, or Aylmore﻿—pleaded that he was robbed and duped by another man, but the court didn’t believe him, and he got seven years. Plain story you see, Spargo, when it all comes out, eh?”

“All stories are quite plain﻿—when they come out,” observed Spargo. “And he kept silence now, I suppose, because he didn’t want his daughters to know about his past?”

===Chinese===

拉思伯里轻蔑地笑了。

“说？嗐，没说几句。跟他对这案子的那套说法差不多——说是上一回定罪的时候，他是个清白无辜的人。装无辜这门功夫，他倒真是一把好手。”

“那回他定的是什么罪？”

“哦，这个嘛，如今我们当然一清二楚。一查明他的真实身份，我们便把全部底细都翻了出来。艾尔莫尔，或者说艾恩斯沃斯（他的真名其实是斯蒂芬·艾恩斯沃斯），三十年前在北边很远的一座镇子——克劳德汉普顿——办了个他们所谓的互助会之类的玩意儿。他名义上是秘书，其实全是他一个人的买卖。捧场的主顾全是劳动阶级——克劳德汉普顿住的纯粹是工匠人口——他们把大把的钱，用他们自己的话说‘铜子儿’，都投了进去。后来那玩意儿忽然间垮了个干净，一个子儿都不剩。他——艾恩斯沃思，也就是艾尔莫尔——辩称自己是让另一个男人抢劫蒙骗了，可法庭不信，判了他七年。你瞧，事情一摊开，故事再明白不过了，斯帕戈，嗯？”

“所有的故事摊开了都再明白不过，”斯帕戈说。“我猜，他这回守口如瓶，是不想让他的女儿们知道他的过去吧？”

===Original===

“Just so,” agreed Rathbury. “And I don’t know that I blame him. He thought, of course, that he’d go scot-free over this Marbury affair. But he made his mistake in the initial stages, my boy﻿—oh, yes!”

Spargo got up from his desk and walked around his room for a few minutes, Rathbury meanwhile finding and lighting another cigar. At last Spargo came back and clapped a hand on the detective’s shoulder.

“Look here, Rathbury!” he said. “It’s very evident that you’re now going on the lines that Aylmore did murder Marbury. Eh?”

Rathbury looked up. His face showed astonishment.

“After evidence like that!” he exclaimed. “Why, of course. There’s the motive, my son, the motive!”

===Chinese===

“正是，”拉思伯里深表赞同。“换了我，也不见得会怪他。他自然以为，这桩马伯里的案子他能安然脱身。可惜头一步就走错了棋，老伙计——哦，没错！”

斯帕戈从桌前起身，在房间里踱了几分钟，拉思伯里则摸出另一支雪茄点上。末了，斯帕戈走回来，一只手拍在警探肩上。

“听着，拉思伯里！”他说。“很显然，你们如今是循着艾尔莫尔确实杀了马伯里这条思路走了。嗯？”

拉思伯里抬起头来，满脸惊讶。

“有那么些证据摆在眼前！”他叫道。“嗐，那还用说。动机在那儿摆着呢，老弟，动机！”

===Original===

Spargo laughed.

“Rathbury!” he said. “Aylmore no more murdered Marbury than you did!”

The detective got up and put on his hat.

“Oh!” he said. “Perhaps you know who did, then?”

“I shall know in a few days,” answered Spargo.

===Chinese===

斯帕戈笑了。

“拉思伯里！”他说。“艾尔莫尔压根儿没有杀马伯里，就跟你没有杀他一样！”

警探起身戴上帽子。

“哦！”他说。“那么，也许你知道是谁干的了？”

“过几天我就会知道，”斯帕戈答道。

===Original===

Rathbury stared wonderingly at him. Then he suddenly walked to the door. “Good night!” he said gruffly.

“Good night, Rathbury,” replied Spargo and sat down at his desk.

But that night Spargo wrote nothing for the *Watchman*. All he wrote was a short telegram addressed to Aylmore’s daughters. There were only three words on it﻿—Have no fear.

===Chinese===

拉思伯里愕然盯着他看了半晌。随后他突然走向门口。“晚安！”他粗声粗气地说。

“晚安，拉思伯里，”斯帕戈答道，随即在桌前坐下。

但那一夜，斯帕戈什么也没给《守望报》写。他只写了一封致艾尔莫尔女儿们的简短电报，电文只有三个词——不必害怕。

## XXX Revelation / 第三十章 真相大白

===Original===

There was as much bright sunshine that morning in Middle Temple Lane as ever manages to get into it, and some of it was shining in the entry into which Spargo and Breton presently hurried. Full of haste as he was Breton paused at the foot of the stair. He looked down at the floor and at the wall at its side.

“Wasn’t it there?” he said in a low voice, pointing at the place he looked at. “Wasn’t it there, Spargo, just there, that Marbury, or, rather, Maitland, was found?”

“It was just there,” answered Spargo.

“You saw him?”

“I saw him.”

===Chinese===

那天早晨，中殿巷里照进了这条巷子往常总算能挤进来的那许多明亮阳光，其中几缕正落进斯帕戈和布雷顿此刻匆匆赶入的门厅里。布雷顿尽管心急火燎，还是在楼梯脚下停住了。他低头看看地面，又看看楼梯一侧的墙壁。

“不就是那儿吗？”他压低声音说，手指着自己注视的地方，“斯帕戈，不就是那儿，就在那儿，人们发现了马伯里——或者说梅特兰——的地方吗？”

“正是那儿。”斯帕戈答道。

“你看见他了？”

“看见了。”

===Original===

“Soon﻿—afterwards?”

“Immediately after he was found. You know all that, Breton. Why do you ask now?”

Breton, who was still staring at the place on which he had fixed his eyes on walking into the entry, shook his head.

“Don’t know,” he answered. “I﻿—but come on﻿—let’s see if old Cardlestone can tell us anything.”

===Chinese===

“事后不久——就看见了？”

“尸体一被发现我就看见了。这些你全知道，布雷顿。现在为什么又问起来？”

布雷顿仍旧死死盯着他一进门厅便落眼的那块地方，摇了摇头。

“不知道。”他答道，“我——算了，走吧——去看看老卡德尔斯顿能不能告诉我们点什么。”

===Original===

There was another charwoman, armed with pails and buckets, outside Cardlestone’s door, into which she was just fitting a key. It was evident to Spargo that she knew Breton, for she smiled at him as she opened the door.

“I don’t think Mr. Cardlestone’ll be in, sir,” she said. “He’s generally gone out to breakfast at this time﻿—him and Mr. Elphick goes together.”

“Just see,” said Breton. “I want to see him if he is in.” The charwoman entered the chambers and immediately screamed.

“Quite so,” remarked Spargo. “That’s what I expected to hear. Cardlestone, you see, Breton, is also﻿—off!”

===Chinese===

卡德尔斯顿的门外还有一个打杂女工，提桶拎盆地正往锁孔里插钥匙。斯帕戈看得出她认识布雷顿，因为她开门时朝他笑了笑。

“我看卡德尔斯顿先生不在家，先生。”她说，“这个时辰他一般都出去吃早饭了——他跟埃尔菲克先生总是一道去。”

“还是去看一眼。”布雷顿说，“他要是在，我想见他。”女工走进寓所，随即发出一声尖叫。

“果然如此。”斯帕戈说道，“我料到的就是这句话。你瞧，布雷顿，卡德尔斯顿也——跑了！”

===Original===

Breton made no reply. He rushed after the charwoman, with Spargo in close attendance.

“Good God﻿—another!” groaned Breton.

If the confusion in Elphick’s rooms had been bad, that in Cardlestone’s chambers was worse. Here again all the features of the previous scene were repeated﻿—drawers had been torn open, papers thrown about; the hearth was choked with light ashes; everything was at sixes and sevens. An open door leading into an inner room showed that Cardlestone, like Elphick, had hastily packed a bag; like Elphick had changed his clothes, and had thrown his discarded garments anywhere, into any corner. Spargo began to realize what had taken place﻿—Elphick, having made his own preparations for flight, had come to Cardlestone, and had expedited him, and they had fled together. But﻿—why?

===Chinese===

布雷顿没有答话。他跟着女工冲了进去，斯帕戈紧随其后。

“天哪——又是一桩！”布雷顿呻吟道。

如果说埃尔菲克寓所里的狼藉已经够糟，卡德尔斯顿这边的就更有过之。先前那一幕的一切特征在这里重演了一遍——抽屉被硬扯开，文件扔得四处都是；壁炉里塞满了轻飘飘的纸灰；一切都乱得七颠八倒。一扇通向里间的敞开的门表明，卡德尔斯顿同埃尔菲克一样，匆忙收拾了一只提包；也同埃尔菲克一样换掉了衣裳，把换下的衣服随手乱丢，丢在随便哪个角落。斯帕戈开始明白出了什么事——埃尔菲克把自己出逃的准备料理停当之后，来找卡德尔斯顿，催他快走，两人一同逃走了。可是——为什么？

===Original===

The charwoman sat down in the nearest chair and began to moan and sob; Breton strode forward, across the heaps of papers and miscellaneous objects tossed aside in that hurried search and clearing up, into the inner room. And Spargo, looking about him, suddenly caught sight of something lying on the floor at which he made a sharp clutch. He had just secured it and hurried it into his pocket when Breton came back.

“I don’t know what all this means, Spargo,” he said, almost wearily. “I suppose you do. Look here,” he went on, turning to the charwoman, “stop that row﻿—that’ll do no good, you know. I suppose Mr. Cardlestone’s gone away in a hurry. You’d better﻿—what had she better do, Spargo?”

“Leave things exactly as they are, lock up the chambers, and as you’re a friend of Mr. Cardlestone’s give you the key,” answered Spargo, with a significant glance. “Do that, now, and let’s go﻿—I’ve something to do.”

===Chinese===

打杂女工一屁股坐进身边最近的一把椅子里，开始又哭又号；布雷顿大步跨过那一堆堆在那阵慌乱翻检清理中被丢到一旁的文件杂物，走进里间。斯帕戈环顾四周，忽然瞥见地板上躺着一样东西，一个箭步猛地抓住了它。他刚把它攥牢、急急塞进口袋，布雷顿就回来了。

“我不知道这一切到底是怎么回事，斯帕戈。”他说，语气近乎疲惫，“我想你知道。听着，”他转向女工接着说，“别嚎了——哭嚎没有一点用，你也明白。我看卡德尔斯顿先生是匆忙出远门了。你最好——她最好怎么办，斯帕戈？”

“东西一概保持原样，把寓所锁好，既然你是卡德尔斯顿先生的朋友，让她把钥匙交给你保管。”斯帕戈答道，意味深长地看了一眼，“就这么办，这就走——我还有事要办。”

===Original===

Once outside, with the startled charwoman gone away, Spargo turned to Breton.

“I’ll tell you all I know, presently, Breton,” he said. “In the meantime, I want to find out if the lodge porter saw Mr. Elphick or Mr. Cardlestone leave. I must know where they’ve gone﻿—if I can only find out. I don’t suppose they went on foot.”

“All right,” responded Breton, gloomily. “We’ll go and ask. But this is all beyond me. You don’t mean to say﻿—”

“Wait a while,” answered Spargo. “One thing at once,” he continued, as they walked up Middle Temple Lane. “This is the first thing. You ask the porter if he’s seen anything of either of them﻿—he knows you.”

===Chinese===

出了门，等那惊魂未定的女工走开，斯帕戈转向布雷顿。

“我知道的情况，回头会原原本本告诉你，布雷顿。”他说，“眼下我想先弄清门房见没见埃尔菲克先生或卡德尔斯顿先生离开。我必须知道他们去哪儿了——只要能打听出来。我想他们总不至于步行走。”

“行。”布雷顿阴郁地应道，“我们去问。可这一切我全然摸不着头脑。你该不会是要说——”

“稍等。”斯帕戈答道，“一次只办一件事。”他们沿着中殿巷往上走，他接着说，“这是头一件。你去问门房见没见过他们两位当中的哪一个——他认识你。”

===Original===

The porter, duly interrogated, responded with alacrity.

“Anything of Mr. Elphick this morning, Mr. Breton?” he answered. “Certainly, sir. I got a taxi for Mr. Elphick and Mr. Cardlestone early this morning﻿—soon after seven. Mr. Elphick said they were going to Paris, and they’d breakfast at Charing Cross before the train left.”

“Say when they’d be back?” asked Breton, with an assumption of entire carelessness.

“No, sir, Mr. Elphick didn’t,” answered the porter. “But I should say they wouldn’t be long because they’d only got small suitcases with them﻿—such as they’d put a day or two’s things in, sir.”

===Chinese===

门房被正经一问，痛痛快快地答了话。

“今儿早上埃尔菲克先生有什么动静，布雷顿先生？”他答道，“当然有，先生。今天一大早——七点刚过——我给埃尔菲克先生和卡德尔斯顿先生叫了一辆出租汽车。埃尔菲克先生说他俩要去巴黎，要在火车开车前在查令十字（Charing Cross）吃早饭。”

“说没说什么时候回来？”布雷顿问，装出一副满不在乎的样子。

“没说，先生，埃尔菲克先生没提。”门房答道，“不过依我看他们去不了多久，因为两人只随身带了小提箱——就是装个一两天替换衣物的那种，先生。”

===Original===

“All right,” said Breton. He turned away towards Spargo who had already moved off. “What next?” he asked. “Charing Cross, I suppose!”

Spargo smiled and shook his head.

“No,” he answered. “I’ve no use for Charing Cross. They haven’t gone to Paris. That was all a blind. For the present let’s go back to your chambers. Then I’ll talk to you.”

Once within Breton’s inner room, with the door closed upon them, Spargo dropped into an easy-chair and looked at the young barrister with earnest attention.

===Chinese===

“好。”布雷顿说。他转身走向已经先行走开的斯帕戈。“下一步呢？”他问，“我猜是查令十字！”

斯帕戈微微一笑，摇了摇头。

“不去。”他答道，“查令十字对我没用。他们没有去巴黎。那全是障眼法。眼下我们先回你的寓所去，然后我再跟你细谈。”

一进布雷顿的里间，关上门，斯帕戈便一屁股坐进安乐椅，聚精会神地端详着这位年轻的出庭律师。

===Original===

“Breton!” he said. “I believe we’re coming in sight of land. You want to save your prospective father-in-law, don’t you?”

“Of course!” growled Breton. “That goes without saying. But﻿—”

“But you may have to make some sacrifices in order to do it,” said Spargo. “You see﻿—”

“Sacrifices!” exclaimed Breton. “What﻿—”

===Chinese===

“布雷顿！”他说，“我相信咱们快望见陆地了。你想救你那位准岳父，对不对？”

“那还用说！”布雷顿粗声粗气地说，“这是明摆着的。可是——”

“可是为了救他，你也许得作出一些牺牲。”斯帕戈说，“你要明白——”

“牺牲！”布雷顿嚷了起来，“什么——”

===Original===

“You may have to sacrifice some ideas﻿—you may find that you’ll not be able to think as well of some people in the future as you have thought of them in the past. For instance﻿—Mr. Elphick.”

Breton’s face grew dark.

“Speak plainly, Spargo!” he said. “It’s best with me.”

“Very well,” replied Spargo. “Mr. Elphick, then, is in some way connected with this affair.”

===Chinese===

“你也许得牺牲掉某些看法——你也许会发现，今后你再不能像过去那样高看某些人了。比如说——埃尔菲克先生。”

布雷顿的脸色阴沉下来。

“有话直说，斯帕戈！”他说，“跟我说话最好直来直去。”

“那好。”斯帕戈答道，“这么说吧：埃尔菲克先生同这件事有某种牵连。”

===Original===

“You mean the﻿—murder?”

“I mean the murder. So is Cardlestone. Of that I’m now dead certain. And that’s why they’re off. I startled Elphick last night. It’s evident that he immediately communicated with Cardlestone, and that they made a rapid exit. Why?”

“Why? That’s what I’m asking you! Why? Why? Why?”

===Chinese===

“你是说——那桩谋杀案？”

“我说的就是那桩谋杀案。卡德尔斯顿也一样。这一点我现在万分确定。所以他们才跑了。昨晚我把埃尔菲克吓着了。显然他立刻联系了卡德尔斯顿，两人随即迅速脱身。为什么？”

“为什么？这正是我在问你的！为什么？为什么？为什么？”

===Original===

“Because they’re afraid of something coming out. And being afraid, their first instinct is to﻿—run. They’ve run at the first alarm. Foolish﻿—but instinctive.”

Breton, who had flung himself into the elbow-chair at his desk, jumped to his feet and thumped his blotting-pad.

“Spargo!” he exclaimed. “Are you telling me that you accuse my guardian and his friend, Mr. Cardlestone, of being﻿—murderers?”

“Nothing of the sort. I am accusing Mr. Elphick and Mr. Cardlestone of knowing more about the murder than they care to tell or want to tell. I am also accusing them, and especially your guardian, of knowing all about Maitland, alias Marbury. I made him confess last night that he knew this dead man to be John Maitland.”

===Chinese===

“因为他们害怕某件事败露出来。人一害怕，第一本能就是——跑。头一阵警讯一响他们就跑了。愚蠢——但出于本能。”

布雷顿本已瘫坐进书桌旁的扶手椅里，此刻一跃而起，一拳捶在吸墨纸板上。

“斯帕戈！”他喊道，“你是在告诉我，你指控我的监护人及其朋友卡德尔斯顿先生是——凶手吗？”

“绝不是这个意思。我指控埃尔菲克先生和卡德尔斯顿先生的是：关于这桩谋杀案，他们知道的远比他们肯说的、愿意说的多。我还指控他们——尤其是你的监护人——对梅特兰，化名马伯里的底细一清二楚。昨晚我逼得他承认，他知道这个死去的男人就是约翰·梅特兰。”

===Original===

“You did!”

“I did. And now, Breton, since it’s got to come out, we’ll have the truth. Pull yourself together﻿—get your nerves ready, for you’ll have to stand a shock or two. But I know what I’m talking about﻿—I can prove every word I’m going to say to you. And first let me ask you a few questions. Do you know anything about your parentage?”

“Nothing﻿—beyond what Mr. Elphick has told me.”

“And what was that?”

===Chinese===

“你真逼他招了！”

“逼了。而现在，布雷顿，既然事情终究要见天日，咱们就把真相弄个水落石出。打起精神来——把神经绷紧，因为你还得经受一两记打击。不过我清楚自己在说什么——我接下来说的每一个字，我都能证明。先让我问你几个问题。关于你的出身父母，你知道些什么？”

“一无所知——只知道埃尔菲克先生告诉我的那些。”

“他都怎么说的？”

===Original===

“That my parents were old friends of his, who died young, leaving me unprovided for, and that he took me up and looked after me.”

“And he’s never given you any documentary evidence of any sort to prove the truth of that story?”

“Never! I never questioned his statement. Why should I?”

“You never remember anything of your childhood﻿—I mean of any person who was particularly near you in your childhood?”

===Chinese===

“说我父母是他多年的老朋友，年纪轻轻就去世了，撇下我无人照管，是他把我收留下来抚养照顾的。”

“他从来没给过你任何书面凭证，来证明这套说法确有其事？”

“从来没有！我从来没质疑过他的说法。我凭什么质疑呢？”

“你对童年毫无记忆——我是说，对童年时代格外亲近你的某个人，毫无印象？”

===Original===

“I remember the people who brought me up from the time I was three years old. And I have just a faint, shadowy recollection of some woman, a tall, dark woman, I think, before that.”

“Miss Baylis,” said Spargo to himself. “All right, Breton,” he went on aloud. “I’m going to tell you the truth. I’ll tell it to you straight out and give you all the explanations afterwards. Your real name is not Breton at all. Your real name is Maitland, and you’re the only child of the man who was found murdered at the foot of Cardlestone’s staircase!”

Spargo had been wondering how Breton would take this, and he gazed at him with some anxiety as he got out the last words. What would he do?﻿—what would he say?﻿—what﻿—

===Chinese===

“从三岁起带我长大的人，我都记得。在那之前，我只对某个女人有一点淡淡的、朦朦胧胧的记忆，我记得是个高个子、黑头发的女人。”

“贝利斯小姐。”斯帕戈暗自说道。“好，布雷顿。”他扬声接着说，“我要把真相告诉你了。我会直截了当地讲，之后再向你解释所有原委。你的真名根本不叫布雷顿。你的真名是梅特兰，你就是在卡德尔斯顿楼梯脚下被人发现遇害的那个男人唯一的孩子！”

斯帕戈一直在揣测布雷顿会怎样承受这个消息，吐出最后几个字时，他不无焦虑地凝视着他。他会怎么做？——他会怎么说？——会——

===Original===

Breton sat down quietly at his desk and looked Spargo hard between the eyes.

“Prove that to me, Spargo,” he said, in hard, matter-of-fact tones. “Prove it to me, every word. Every word, Spargo!”

Spargo nodded.

“I will﻿—every word,” he answered. “It’s the right thing. Listen, then.”

===Chinese===

布雷顿静静地坐到书桌前，目光直逼斯帕戈的双眼之间。

“向我证明这一点，斯帕戈。”他语调生硬、就事论事地说，“向我证明，每一个字都要证明。每一个字，斯帕戈！”

斯帕戈点了点头。

“我会的——每一个字。”他答道，“这是应该做的事。那就听着吧。”

===Original===

It was a quarter to twelve, Spargo noticed, throwing a glance at the clock outside, as he began his story; it was past one when he brought it to an end. And all that time Breton listened with the keenest attention, only asking a question now and then; now and then making a brief note on a sheet of paper which he had drawn to him.

“That’s all,” said Spargo at last.

“It’s plenty,” observed Breton laconically.

He sat staring at his notes for a moment; then he looked up at Spargo. “What do you really think?” he asked.

===Chinese===

斯帕戈开始讲述时朝外面的时钟瞥了一眼，注意到是十一点四十五分；讲完时已过一点。这一整段时间里，布雷顿始终全神贯注地听着，只是偶尔发问，间或在他拉到面前的一张纸上简要记上一笔。

“完了。”斯帕戈终于说道。

“够多的了。”布雷顿的话简洁干脆。

他坐着盯了自己的笔记片刻，然后抬头望着斯帕戈。“你到底是怎么想的？”他问。

===Original===

“About﻿—what?” said Spargo.

“This flight of Elphick’s and Cardlestone’s.”

“I think, as I said, that they knew something which they think may be forced upon them. I never saw a man in a greater fright than that I saw Elphick in last night. And it’s evident that Cardlestone shares in that fright, or they wouldn’t have gone off in this way together.”

“Do you think they know anything of the actual murder?”

Spargo shook his head.

===Chinese===

“关于——什么？”斯帕戈说。

“关于埃尔菲克和卡德尔斯顿这趟出逃。”

“我认为，正如我说过的，他们知道某件自认为可能被强行翻出来的事。我平生没见过比昨晚的埃尔菲克更惊恐的人。而卡德尔斯顿显然也分担着这份惊恐，否则他们不会这样双双出走。”

“你认为他们知道谋杀的实情吗？”

斯帕戈摇了摇头。

===Original===

“I don’t know. Probably. They know something. And﻿—look here!”

Spargo put his hand in his breast pocket and drew something out which he handed to Breton, who gazed at it curiously.

“What’s this?” he demanded. “Stamps?”

“That, from the description of Criedir, the stamp-dealer, is a sheet of those rare Australian stamps which Maitland had on him﻿—carried on him. I picked it up just now in Cardlestone’s room, when you were looking into his bedroom.”

===Chinese===

“我不知道。多半知道。他们是知道些什么的。还有——你瞧这个！”

斯帕戈把手伸进胸前口袋，掏出一样东西递给布雷顿，布雷顿好奇地端详着它。

“这是什么？”他问，“邮票？”

“照邮票商克里迪尔先生的描述，那是梅特兰随身带着的一整版珍稀澳大利亚邮票——贴身携带的。方才你查看他卧室的时候，我在卡德尔斯顿的房间里捡到的。”

===Original===

“But that, after all, proves nothing. Those mayn’t be the identical stamps. And whether they are or not﻿—”

“What are the probabilities?” interrupted Spargo sharply. “I believe that those are the stamps which Maitland﻿—your father!﻿—had on him, and I want to know how they came to be in Cardlestone’s rooms. And I will know.”

Breton handed the stamps back.

“But the general thing, Spargo?” he said. “If they didn’t murder﻿—I can’t realize the thing yet!﻿—my father﻿—”

===Chinese===

“可这终究证明不了什么。那些未必就是同一版邮票。再说不管是不是——”

“可能性有多大？”斯帕戈厉声打断他，“我相信那就是梅特兰——你的父亲！——随身带着的那版邮票，我要知道它们怎么会到了卡德尔斯顿的寓所里。而且我一定会知道。”

布雷顿把邮票递了回去。

“可是大处呢，斯帕戈？”他说，“如果他们没有杀害——这件事我到现在还没法想明白！——我的父亲——”

===Original===

“If they didn’t murder your father, they know who did!” exclaimed Spargo. “Now, then, it’s time for more action. Let Elphick and Cardlestone alone for the moment﻿—they’ll be tracked easily enough. I want to tackle something else for the moment. How do you get an authority from the Government to open a grave?”

“Order from the Home Secretary, which will have to be obtained by showing the very strongest reasons why it should be made.”

“Good! We’ll give the reasons. I want to have a grave opened.”

“A grave opened! Whose grave?”

===Chinese===

“如果他们没有杀害你的父亲，那他们就知道是谁下的手！”斯帕戈高声道，“好了，该再行动起来了。埃尔菲克和卡德尔斯顿暂且放一放——要追查他们易如反掌。眼下我要办的是另一件事。要从政府那里取得开挖坟墓的许可，该怎么办？”

“得由内政大臣下令，而要取得这道命令，必须摆出极其充分的理由。”

“好！理由我们会摆的。我要开一座坟。”

“开坟！谁的坟？”

===Original===

“The grave of the man Chamberlayne at Market Milcaster,” replied Spargo.

Breton started.

“His? In Heaven’s name, why?” he demanded.

Spargo laughed as he got up.

“Because I believe it’s empty,” he answered. “Because I believe that Chamberlayne is alive, and that his other name is﻿—Cardlestone!”

===Chinese===

“马基特·米尔卡斯特那个张伯莱恩的坟。”斯帕戈答道。

布雷顿猛地一惊。

“他的？老天爷在上，为什么？”他追问。

斯帕戈站起身，笑了起来。

“因为我相信那座坟是空的。”他答道，“因为我相信张伯莱恩还活着，而他的另一个名字是——卡德尔斯顿！”

## XXV Revelations / 第二十五章 揭秘

===Original===

Spargo turned on his disreputable and dissolute companion with all his journalistic energies and instincts roused. He had not been sure, since entering the “King of Madagascar,” that he was going to hear anything material to the Middle Temple Murder; he had more than once feared that this old gin-drinking harridan was deceiving him, for the purpose of extracting drink and money from him. But now, at the mere prospect of getting important information from her, he forgot all about Mother Gutch’s unfortunate propensities, evil eyes, and sodden face; he only saw in her somebody who could tell him something. He turned on her eagerly.

“You say that John Maitland’s son didn’t die!” he exclaimed.

“The boy did not die,” replied Mother Gutch.

“And that you know where he is?” asked Spargo.

Mother Gutch shook her head.

“I didn’t say that I know where he is, young man,” she replied. “I said I knew what she did with him.”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈把全部新闻从业的劲头和本能都调动了起来，转过身来对付他这位名声狼藉、落魄放荡的同伴。自打进了“马达加斯加国王”酒馆，他一直拿不准究竟能不能听到什么与中殿谋杀案实质相关的东西；他不止一次疑心，这个嗜金酒如命的老泼妇是在诓他，好从他身上榨点酒钱。可如今，一想到有望从她嘴里掏出重要消息，他就把格奇大妈那些不体面的癖好、那双邪恶的眼睛和那张浮肿的脸统统忘到了脑后；在他眼里，她只剩下一样身份——一个能告诉他些什么的人。他急切地转向她。

“你说约翰·梅特兰的儿子没有死！”他叫道。

“那孩子没有死，”格奇大妈答道。

“你还知道他如今在哪儿？”斯帕戈问。

格奇大妈摇了摇头。

“我可没说晓得他在哪儿，年轻人，”她答道，“我说的是，我晓得她把他怎么处置了。”

===Original===

“What, then?” demanded Spargo.

Mother Gutch drew herself up in a vast assumption of dignity, and favoured Spargo with a look.

“That’s the secret, young man,” she said. “I’m willing to sell that secret, but not for two half-sovereigns and two or three drops of cold gin. If Maitland left all that money you told Jane Baylis of, when I was listening to you from behind the hedge, my secret’s worth something.”

Spargo suddenly remembered his bit of bluff to Miss Baylis. Here was an unexpected result of it.

“Nobody but me can help you to trace Maitland’s boy,” continued Mother Gutch, “and I shall expect to be paid accordingly. That’s plain language, young man.”

Spargo considered the situation in silence for a minute or two. Could this wretched, bibulous old woman really be in possession of a secret which would lead to the solving of the mystery of the Middle Temple Murder? Well, it would be a fine thing for the *Watchman* if the clearing up of everything came through one of its men.
