And the *Watchman* was noted for being generous even to extravagance in laying out money on all sorts of objects: it had spent money like water on much less serious matters than this.

===Chinese===

“那么，怎么处置的？”斯帕戈追问。

格奇大妈挺直了身子，摆出一副煞有介事的威严架势，赏了斯帕戈一眼。

“那可是个秘密，年轻人，”她说，“这秘密我愿意卖，可两枚半索维林金币外加两三滴凉金酒，是买不动的。要是梅特兰真留下了你跟简·贝利斯说的那一大笔钱——你在树篱后头跟她说话那会儿，我可是一句句听着呢——那我这个秘密就值几个钱。”

斯帕戈猛然想起自己当初对贝利斯小姐那番虚张声势的诓话。不想它竟结出了这样一记意外的果子。

“除了我，谁也帮不了你去追查梅特兰那孩子的下落，”格奇大妈接着说，“所以嘛，我指望报酬也得照这个来。这是明摆着的话，年轻人。”

斯帕戈默默掂量了一两分钟的形势。这个穷愁潦倒、贪杯好酒的老太婆，手里当真攥着一个能解开中殿谋杀案之谜的秘密？也罢，倘若一切真相竟能经由《守望报》自家的人之手大白于天下，那可是件漂亮事。何况《守望报》花钱大方是出了名的，出手阔绰近乎挥霍：比这轻省得多的事，它都曾大把银子往水里扔。

===Original===

“How much do you want for your secret?” he suddenly asked, turning to his companion.

Mother Gutch began to smooth out a pleat in her gown. It was really wonderful to Spargo to find how very sober and normal this old harridan had become; he did not understand that her nerves had been all a-quiver and on edge when he first met her, and that a resort to her favourite form of alcohol in liberal quantity had calmed and quickened them; secretly he was regarding her with astonishment as the most extraordinary old person he had ever met, and he was almost afraid of her as he waited for her decision. At last Mother Gutch spoke.

“Well, young man,” she said, “having considered matters, and having a right to look well to myself, I think that what I should prefer to have would be one of those annuities. A nice, comfortable annuity, paid weekly﻿—none of your monthlies or quarterlies, but regular and punctual, every Saturday morning. Or Monday morning, as was convenient to the parties concerned﻿—but punctual and regular. I know a good many ladies in my sphere of life as enjoys annuities, and it’s a great comfort to have ’em paid weekly.”

It occurred to Spargo that Mrs. Gutch would probably get rid of her weekly dole on the day it was paid, whether that day happened to be Monday or Saturday, but that, after all, was no concern of his, so he came back to first principles.

“Even now you haven’t said how much,” he remarked.

===Chinese===

“你这秘密开价多少？”他忽然转身问同伴。

格奇大妈动手抚平裙子上一道褶子。斯帕戈眼见这老泼妇变得如此清醒、如此正常，心里着实称奇；他不明白，他初见她时，她的神经本还抖抖索索绷得紧紧的，全靠放量痛饮她偏爱的那种酒，才安定下来、活泛起来；他暗暗诧异地把她打量一番，认作生平所遇最不寻常的老太太，等她拿主意的那会儿，心里竟有几分怕她。格奇大妈终于开了口。

“嗳，年轻人，”她说，“我把事情掂量过了，我也有权替自己好好打算打算。想来想去，我最中意的还是那种年金。一份舒舒服服的好年金，按礼拜付——月付季付一概不要，要的是每礼拜六上午，准时又足额。或者礼拜一上午也成，只要两下里方便——但必定要准时、要按期。我这等人家的圈子里，吃年金的太太我认得好多位，那钱按礼拜付，才叫一个安心。”

斯帕戈心下明白，格奇太太领到钱的那一天，多半当天就把那份周钱打发干净，管它是礼拜一还是礼拜六——不过这终究与他无关，于是他回到正题上。

“到了这会儿，你还没说要多少呢。”他说。

===Original===

“Three pound a week,” replied Mother Gutch. “And cheap, too!”

Spargo thought hard for two minutes. The secret might﻿—might!﻿—lead to something big. This wretched old woman would probably drink herself to death within a year or two. Anyhow, a few hundreds of pounds was nothing to the *Watchman*. He glanced at his watch. At that hour﻿—for the next hour﻿—the great man of the *Watchman* would be at the office. He jumped to his feet, suddenly resolved and alert.

“Here, I’ll take you to see my principals,” he said. “We’ll run along in a taxicab.”

“With all the pleasure in the world, young man,” replied Mother Gutch; “when you’ve given me that other half-sovereign. As for principals, I’d far rather talk business with masters than with men﻿—though I mean no disrespect to you.”

Spargo, feeling that he was in for it, handed over the second half-sovereign, and busied himself in ordering a taxicab. But when that came round he had to wait while Mrs. Gutch consumed a third glass of gin and purchased a flask of the same beverage to put in her pocket. At last he got her off, and in due course to the *Watchman* office, where the hall-porter and the messenger boys stared at her in amazement, well used as they were to seeing strange folk, and he got her to his own room, and locked her in, and then he sought the presence of the mighty.

===Chinese===

“一礼拜三镑，”格奇大妈答道，“还是便宜的哩！”

斯帕戈苦苦盘算了两分钟。这秘密说不定——说不定！——能牵出天大的事。这个可怜的老太婆，要不了两年八成就会把自己喝死。再怎么说，几百镑在《守望报》眼里也算不得什么。他瞥了一眼表。这个钟点——往后这一个钟头里——《守望报》的大人物准在报馆。他一跃而起，当机立断，精神抖擞。

“这样吧，我带你去见我家东家，”他说，“咱们坐出租汽车去。”

“那可真是求之不得，年轻人，”格奇大妈答道，“不过先把你许下的那另一枚半索维林给我。说起东家嘛，谈生意我宁可同东家当面谈，也不同伙计打交道——不过我对你本人没有半点不敬的意思。”

斯帕戈自知骑虎难下，交出了第二枚半索维林，张罗着叫出租汽车。车转到门口，他还得等着，听凭格奇太太灌下第三杯金酒，又买上一小瓶同样的酒水揣进衣兜。他总算把她弄上了车，顺顺当当到了《守望报》报馆。门房和信童们虽是见惯了各路奇人，仍不免对她瞠目结舌；他把她带进自己的房间，锁上门，然后便去求见那位大人物。

===Original===

What Spargo said to his editor and to the great man who controlled the fortunes and workings of the *Watchman* he never knew. It was probably fortunate for him that they were both thoroughly conversant with the facts of the Middle Temple Murder, and saw that there might be an advantage in securing the revelations of which Spargo had got the conditional promise. At any rate, they accompanied Spargo to his room, intent on seeing, hearing and bargaining with the lady he had locked up there.

Spargo’s room smelt heavily of unsweetened gin, but Mother Gutch was soberer than ever. She insisted upon being introduced to proprietor and editor in due and proper form, and in discussing terms with them before going into any further particulars. The editor was all for temporizing with her until something could be done to find out what likelihood of truth there was in her, but the proprietor, after sizing her up in his own shrewd fashion, took his two companions out of the room.

“We’ll hear what the old woman has to say on her own terms,” he said. “She may have something to tell that is really of the greatest importance in this case: she certainly has something to tell. And, as Spargo says, she’ll probably drink herself to death in about as short a time as possible. Come back﻿—let’s hear her story.” So they returned to the gin-scented atmosphere, and a formal document was drawn out by which the proprietor of the *Watchman* bound himself to pay Mrs. Gutch the sum of three pounds a week for life (Mrs. Gutch insisting on the insertion of the words “every Saturday morning, punctual and regular”) and then Mrs. Gutch was invited to tell her tale. And Mrs. Gutch settled herself to do so, and Spargo prepared to take it down, word for word.

===Chinese===

斯帕戈到底对主编、对那位执掌着《守望报》气数与运转的大人物说了些什么，他自己始终也说不清。算他走运，那两位对中殿谋杀案的案情早已烂熟于胸，也看得出把斯帕戈拿到有条件许诺的这番隐情抓到自己手里，或许大有益处。总之，两人随斯帕戈来到他的房间，一心要见一见、听一听，再同那位被他锁在里面的太太讲讲价钱。

斯帕戈的房间里一股不加糖的金酒气味冲鼻，可格奇大妈比先前任何时候都清醒。她坚持要按规矩、按正套，把她引见给报馆老板和主编，还坚持先同他们把条件谈定，再往下细说详情。主编一心主张跟她软磨拖延，设法先查查她的话里有几成可信；老板却自有一副精明的眼光，把她上下打量一番，随即把两个同伴带出了房间。

“这老太婆开什么价，我们就听她说什么，”他说，“她要讲的东西，说不定当真与本案干系重大：反正她手里必定有点东西可讲。再说，照斯帕戈的说法，她多半用不了多久就会把自己喝死。回来吧——听听她的故事。”于是三人回到那满屋金酒味的空气里，当场拟出一份正式文书，由《守望报》老板立约，终身每礼拜付给格奇太太三镑之数（格奇太太坚持添上“每礼拜六上午，准时足额”字样），然后便请格奇太太讲述她的故事。格奇太太也当真安顿下来，摆开了讲的架势，斯帕戈则备好纸笔，预备一字不落地记录。

===Original===

“Which the story, as that young man called it, is not so long as a monkey’s tail nor so short as a Manx cat’s, gentlemen,” said Mrs. Gutch; “but full of meat as an egg. Now, you see, when that Maitland affair at Market Milcaster came off, I was housekeeper to Miss Jane Baylis at Brighton. She kept a boardinghouse there, in Kemp Town, and close to the seafront, and a very good thing she made out of it, and had saved a nice bit, and having, like her sister, Mrs. Maitland, had a little fortune left her by her father, as was at one time a publican here in London, she had a good lump of money. And all that money was in this here Maitland’s hands, every penny. I very well remember the day when the news came about that affair of Maitland robbing the bank. Miss Baylis, she was like a mad thing when she saw it in the paper, and before she’d seen it an hour she was off to Market Milcaster. I went up to the station with her, and she told me then before she got in the train that Maitland had all her fortune and her savings, and her sister’s, his wife’s, too, and that she feared all would be lost.”

===Chinese===

“诸位先生，这段故事，照那位年轻先生的叫法，长不过猴子的尾巴，短也不至于短到马恩岛猫那般没尾巴，可是满满当当全是干货，赛过一只鸡蛋。”格奇太太说，“你们听好，马基特·米尔卡斯特那桩梅特兰案子发作的时候，我正在布莱顿给简·贝利斯小姐当管家。她在肯普敦开着一所供膳寄宿公寓，离海滨不远，这买卖让她做得有声有色，也攒下了一小笔。又因为她跟她妹妹梅特兰太太一样，父亲曾留给她一小份私财——她们那位父亲早年在伦敦这儿做过酒馆老板——所以她手里是攥着一大注钱的。而那笔钱，一文不剩，全在这位梅特兰手里。那桩梅特兰挪用银行钱款的案子见报那天的光景，我记得清清楚楚。贝利斯小姐在报上一看到，人登时疯了似的，报到手还不到一个钟头，她就动身去了马基特·米尔卡斯特。我送她到车站，她上火车前亲口对我说：她的全部家当和积蓄，连她妹妹——也就是梅特兰太太——的钱，也都在梅特兰手里，她怕要全部泡汤。”

===Original===

“Mrs. Maitland was then dead,” observed Spargo without looking up from his writing-block.

“She was, young man, and a good thing, too,” continued Mrs. Gutch. “Well, away went Miss Baylis, and no more did I hear or see for nearly a week, and then back she comes, and brings a little boy with her﻿—which was Maitland’s. And she told me that night that she’d lost every penny she had in the world, and that her sister’s money, what ought to have been the child’s, was gone, too, and she said her say about Maitland. However, she saw well to that child; nobody could have seen better. And very soon after, when Maitland was sent to prison for ten years, her and me talked about things. ‘What’s the use,’ says I to her, ‘of your letting yourself get so fond of that child, and looking after it as you do, and educating it, and so on?’ I says. ‘Why not?’ says she. ‘ ’Tisn’t yours,’ I says, ‘you haven’t no right to it,’ I says. ‘As soon as ever its father comes out,’ says I,’ he’ll come and claim it, and you can’t do nothing to stop him.’ Well, gentlemen, if you’ll believe me, never did I see a woman look as she did when I says all that. And she up and swore that Maitland should never see or touch the child again﻿—not under no circumstances whatever.”

Mrs. Gutch paused to take a little refreshment from her pocket-flask, with an apologetic remark as to the state of her heart. She resumed, presently, apparently refreshed.

===Chinese===

“那时梅特兰太太已经过世了，”斯帕戈说道，眼睛没有离开记事簿。

“是没了，年轻人，而且还是件好事哩，”格奇太太接着说，“嗳，贝利斯小姐走了，将近一个礼拜音信全无，随后她回来了，还领回来一个小男孩——正是梅特兰的儿子。当天夜里她就告诉我，她在这世上的每一文钱都赔了个精光，她妹妹那笔本该归孩子的钱也打了水漂，她还把梅特兰痛痛快快骂了个够。不过，那孩子她是照看得再好没有的了，谁也照看不出更好的来。过后不久，梅特兰判了十年监禁，她跟我闲谈起来。‘你何苦，’我对她说，‘由着自己这么疼那孩子，像你这样伺候他、供他读书上进，图个什么呢？’我说。‘为什么不该？’她说。‘他又不是你的，’我说，‘你没有一点权利占着他，’我说。‘只等他爹一出狱，’我说，‘他准会来把孩子认走，你一点法子也没有。’唉，诸位先生，你们信不信，我把这话一说，从没见过哪个女人摆出她那副脸色。她当场就发起誓来：梅特兰今生今世休想再见那孩子一面、再碰那孩子一指头——无论如何也不成。”

格奇太太停下来，从随身携带的小酒瓶里呷了一口提神，还道了声不是，说是什么心脏的毛病。片刻之后她又讲下去，看样子缓过劲儿来了。

===Original===

“Well, gentlemen, that notion, about Maitland’s taking the child away from her seemed to get on her mind, and she used to talk to me at times about it, always saying the same thing﻿—that Maitland should never have him. And one day she told me she was going to London to see lawyers about it, and she went, and she came back, seeming more satisfied, and a day or two afterwards, there came a gentleman who looked like a lawyer, and he stopped a day or two, and he came again and again, until one day she came to me, and she says, ‘You don’t know who that gentleman is that’s come so much lately?’ she says. ‘Not I,’ I says, ‘unless he’s after you.’ ‘After me!’ she says, tossing her head: ‘That’s the gentleman that ought to have married my poor sister if that scoundrel Maitland hadn’t tricked her into throwing him over!’ ‘You don’t say so!’ I says. ‘Then by rights he ought to have been the child’s pa!’ ‘He’s going to be a father to the boy,’ she says. ‘He’s going to take him and educate him in the highest fashion, and make a gentleman of him,’ she says, ‘for his mother’s sake.’ ‘Mercy on us!’ says I. ‘What’ll Maitland say when he comes for him?’ ‘Maitland’ll never come for him,’ she says, ‘for I’m going to leave here, and the boy’ll be gone before then. This is all being done,’ she says, ‘so that the child’ll never know his father’s shame﻿—he’ll never know who his father was.’ And true enough, the boy was taken away, but Maitland came before she’d gone, and she told him the child was dead, and I never see a man so cut up. However, it wasn’t no concern of mine. And so there’s so much of the secret, gentlemen, and I would like to know if I ain’t giving good value.”

“Very good,” said the proprietor. “Go on.” But Spargo intervened.

“Did you ever hear the name of the gentleman who took the boy away?” he asked.

“Yes, I did,” replied Mrs. Gutch. “Of course I did. Which it was Elphick.”

===Chinese===

“唉，诸位先生，梅特兰要把孩子从她手里夺走这个念头，像是扎根在她心上了，她时不时就同我念叨，翻来覆去总是一句话——梅特兰休想得到他。有一天，她说要上伦敦为这事见律师，去了，回来了，看样子宽心了不少。过后一两天，来了位先生，瞧着像个律师，盘桓了一两天，此后一趟又一趟地来，直到有一天她走到我跟前说：‘那位近来常来的先生是谁，你可知道？’她说。‘我哪知道，’我说，‘莫不是看上你了。’‘看上我！’她说着把头一甩，‘要不是那个恶棍梅特兰哄得我可怜的妹妹把他甩了，那位先生本来才是该娶她的人！’‘竟有这般事！’我说，‘那论起理来，他才该是这孩子的爹呀！’‘他就要当这孩子的父亲了，’她说，‘他要把他领走，照最上等的路子教养他，把他栽培成一个体面人，’她说，‘看在他母亲的份上。’‘老天爷！’我说，‘等梅特兰来找孩子，可怎么好？’‘梅特兰永远也找不着他了，’她说，‘因为我马上要离开这儿，孩子在我走之前就没了踪影。这一切安排，’她说，‘为的就是叫这孩子永远不知道他父亲的耻辱——他永远不会知道自己的父亲是谁。’果不其然，孩子送走了，可她还没搬走，梅特兰就找上门来，她对他说孩子死了——我从没见过一个男人伤成那副样子。不过，这与我什么相干。诸位先生，秘密就先交代这么多，我倒想问一句，我这份货给得还算实在吧？”

“很好，”老板说，“往下讲。”斯帕戈却插了话。

“领走那孩子的那位先生尊姓大名，你可曾听说？”他问。

“听说了，怎么没听说，”格奇太太答道，“那人便是埃尔菲克。”

## V Spargo Wishes to Specialize / 第五章 斯帕戈意欲专攻此案

===Original===
The barrister and the journalist, left thus unceremoniously on a crowded pavement, looked at each other. Breton laughed.

“We don’t seem to have gained much information,” he remarked. “I’m about as wise as ever.”

“No﻿—wiser,” said Spargo. “At any rate, I am. I know now that this dead man called himself John Marbury; that he came from Australia; that he only landed at Southampton yesterday morning, and that he was in the company last night of a man whom we have had described to us﻿—a tall, grey-bearded, well-dressed man, presumably a gentleman.”

===Chinese===
出庭律师和记者就这样被毫不客气地撂在拥挤的人行道上，两人面面相觑。布雷顿笑了。

“咱们好像没打听到多少消息，”他说。“我还是跟先前一样糊涂。”

“不——更明白了，”斯帕戈说。“至少我是这样。我现在知道了：这个死人自称约翰·马伯里（John Marbury）；他从澳大利亚来；昨天上午才在南安普顿（Southampton）上岸；而且昨晚他同一个男人在一起，那人的模样人家已经给我们描述过了——高个子，灰白胡子，衣着讲究，多半是位绅士。”

===Original===
Breton shrugged his shoulders.

“I should say that description would fit a hundred thousand men in London,” he remarked.

“Exactly﻿—so it would,” answered Spargo. “But we know that it was one of the hundred thousand, or half-million, if you like. The thing is to find that one﻿—the one.”

“And you think you can do it?”

“I think I’m going to have a big try at it.”

===Chinese===
布雷顿耸了耸肩。

“要我说，那副模样跟伦敦十万个人都对得上，”他说。

“正是——就是对得上，”斯帕戈答道。“可我们知道，那人就是这十万人里的一个，说五十万人也行。要紧的是把那一个找出来——就是那一个。”

“那你觉得你办得到？”

“我想，我要为它大试一场。”

===Original===
Breton shrugged his shoulders again.

“What?﻿—by going up to every man who answers the description, and saying ‘Sir, are you the man who accompanied John Marbury to the Anglo﻿—’ ”

Spargo suddenly interrupted him.

“Look here!” he said. “Didn’t you say that you knew a man who lives in that block in the entry of which Marbury was found?”

“No, I didn’t,” answered Breton. “It was Mr. Elphick who said that. All the same, I do know that man﻿—he’s Mr. Cardlestone, another barrister. He and Mr. Elphick are friends﻿—they’re both enthusiastic philatelists﻿—stamp collectors, you know﻿—and I dare say Mr. Elphick was round there last night examining something new Cardlestone’s got hold of. Why?”

===Chinese===
布雷顿又耸了耸肩。

“怎么试？——挨个儿凑到每个符合那副模样的人跟前，说：‘先生，您就是陪约翰·马伯里去盎格鲁——’”

斯帕戈猛地打断了他。

“听着！”他说。“你不是说过，你认识一个住在那排楼里的人吗——马伯里就是在那排楼的门廊里被发现的。”

“不，我没说过，”布雷顿答道。“说这话的是埃尔菲克先生。不过，那人我的确认识——是卡德尔斯顿先生，也是位出庭律师。他和埃尔菲克先生是朋友——他们俩都是热心的集邮家——就是收藏邮票的，你知道——我敢说，昨晚埃尔菲克先生就是过去看卡德尔斯顿新弄到手的什么稀罕玩意儿了。怎么？”

===Original===
“I’d like to go round there and make some enquiries,” replied Spargo. “If you’d be kind enough to﻿—”

“Oh, I’ll go with you!” responded Breton, with alacrity. “I’m just as keen about this business as you are, Spargo! I want to know who this man Marbury is, and how he came to have my name and address on him. Now, if I had been a well-known man in my profession, you know, why﻿—”

“Yes,” said Spargo, as they got into a cab, “yes, that would have explained a lot. It seems to me that we’ll get at the murderer through that scrap of paper a lot quicker than through Rathbury’s line. Yes, that’s what I think.”

Breton looked at his companion with interest.

“But﻿—you don’t know what Rathbury’s line is,” he remarked.

===Chinese===
“我想过去那边打听打听，”斯帕戈答道。“要是您肯劳驾——”

“哦，我跟你一起去！”布雷顿爽快地应道。“这桩事我跟你一样上心，斯帕戈！我想知道这个马伯里是谁，他身上怎么会有我的姓名住址。话说回来，要是在我这行里我是个响当当的人物，你知道，那——”

“是啊，”两人上了出租汽车，斯帕戈说道，“是啊，那样就能解释许多了。依我看，顺着那张碎纸片查到凶手，要比顺着拉思伯里那条线查快得多。对，我就是这么想的。”

布雷顿饶有兴致地打量着自己的同伴。

“可是——你并不知道拉思伯里那条线是什么啊，”他说。

===Original===
“Yes, I do,” said Spargo. “Rathbury’s gone off to discover who the man is with whom Marbury left the Anglo-Orient Hotel last night. That’s his line.”

“And you want﻿—?”

“I want to find out the full significance of that bit of paper, and who wrote it,” answered Spargo. “I want to know why that old man was coming to you when he was murdered.”

Breton started.

“By Jove!” he exclaimed. “I﻿—I never thought of that. You﻿—you really think he was coming to me when he was struck down?”

===Chinese===
“我知道，”斯帕戈说。“拉思伯里已经去查昨晚马伯里离开盎格鲁-东方旅馆时，同他一道走的那个男人是什么人了。那就是他那条线。”

“那你想——？”

“我要弄清那张碎纸片的全部含义，还要弄清写它的人是谁，”斯帕戈答道。“我想知道，那位老人被杀的时候，正要上你这儿来，为的是什么。”

布雷顿猛地一怔。

“天哪！”他叫道。“我——我从没往这上头想过。你——你真觉得，他被打倒的时候是冲着你来的？”

===Original===
“Certain. Hadn’t he got an address in the Temple? Wasn’t he in the Temple? Of course, he was trying to find you.”

“But﻿—the late hour?”

“No matter. How else can you explain his presence in the Temple? I think he was asking his way. That’s why I want to make some enquiries in this block.”

===Chinese===
“十拿九稳。他身上不是带着律师学院里的一个地址吗？他人不是就在律师学院里吗？他当然是在找你。”

“可是——那么晚了？”

“晚不碍事。不然你怎么解释他会出现在律师学院里？依我看，他是在问路。所以我才想在这排楼里打听打听。”

===Original===
It appeared to Spargo that a considerable number of people, chiefly of the office-boy variety, were desirous of making enquiries about the dead man. Being luncheon-hour, that bit of Middle Temple Lane where the body was found, was thick with the inquisitive and the sensation-seeker, for the news of the murder had spread, and though there was nothing to see but the bare stones on which the body had lain, there were more open mouths and staring eyes around the entry than Spargo had seen for many a day. And the nuisance had become so great that the occupants of the adjacent chambers had sent for a policeman to move the curious away, and when Spargo and his companion presented themselves at the entry this policeman was being lectured as to his duties by a little weazen-faced gentleman, in very snuffy and old-fashioned garments, and an ancient silk hat, who was obviously greatly exercised by the unwonted commotion.

===Chinese===
在斯帕戈看来，为数不少的人——多半是听差少年之流——都很想来打听死人的事。时值午饭时分，中殿巷发现尸体的那一段，挤满了看热闹的和猎奇的人，凶杀的消息已经传开，虽然除了尸体躺过的光石板之外并没有什么可看的，可门廊四周张开的嘴、瞪圆的眼，比斯帕戈许多日子以来见过的都多。骚扰已经闹到不堪的地步，邻近寓所里的人便叫来一名警察驱赶好奇之徒；待斯帕戈和他的同伴来到门廊前，一个干瘪脸的小个子老先生正在训斥这名警察失职——他一身沾着鼻烟、老掉牙的衣裳，头顶一顶古旧的大礼帽，显然为这场非常的骚动大为动气。

===Original===
“Drive them all out into the street!” exclaimed this personage. “Drive them all away, constable﻿—into Fleet Street or upon the Embankment﻿—anywhere, so long as you rid this place of them. This is a disgrace, and an inconvenience, a nuisance, a﻿—”

“That’s old Cardlestone,” whispered Breton. “He’s always irascible, and I don’t suppose we’ll get anything out of him. Mr. Cardlestone,” he continued, making his way up to the old gentleman who was now retreating up the stone steps, brandishing an umbrella as ancient as himself. “I was just coming to see you, sir. This is Mr. Spargo, a journalist, who is much interested in this murder. He﻿—”

“I know nothing about the murder, my dear sir!” exclaimed Mr. Cardlestone. “And I never talk to journalists﻿—a pack of busybodies, sir, saving your presence. I am not aware that any murder has been committed, and I object to my doorway being filled by a pack of office boys and street loungers. Murder indeed! I suppose the man fell down these steps and broke his neck﻿—drunk, most likely.”

He opened his outer door as he spoke, and Breton, with a reassuring smile and a nod at Spargo, followed him into his chambers on the first landing, motioning the journalist to keep at their heels.

===Chinese===
“把他们统统赶到街上去！”这位人物喝道。“统统赶走，警察——赶到舰队街去，或者泰晤士河堤上去——随便哪儿都行，只要把这地方清干净。这简直是丢脸，是搅扰，是胡闹，是——”

“那就是老卡德尔斯顿，”布雷顿低声说。“他脾气一向火爆，我看咱们从他嘴里问不出什么东西来。卡德尔斯顿先生，”他接着说，一边朝正退上石阶的老先生迎去——老先生挥着一柄同他本人一样古旧的雨伞。“我正要去看您呢，先生。这位是斯帕戈先生，一位记者，对这桩凶案非常上心。他——”

“这桩凶案我一无所知，亲爱的先生！”卡德尔斯顿先生叫道。“我也从不跟记者打交道——一伙多管闲事的家伙，先生，恕我直言。我不认为这里发生过什么凶杀，我也不乐意我的门口挤满一群听差小子和街上闲逛的游民。凶杀，亏他们说得出！依我看，那人是自己从台阶上摔下去摔断了脖子——多半是喝醉了。”

他说话间打开了临街的外门，布雷顿朝斯帕戈宽慰地一笑、点了点头，跟着他走进楼梯第一层平台上那套寓所，又招手示意记者跟在他们身后。

===Original===
“Mr. Elphick tells me that he was with you until a late hour last evening, Mr. Cardlestone,” he said. “Of course, neither of you heard anything suspicious?”

“What should we hear that was suspicious in the Temple, sir?” demanded Mr. Cardlestone, angrily. “I hope the Temple is free from that sort of thing, young Mr. Breton. Your respected guardian and myself had a quiet evening on our usual peaceful pursuits, and when he went away all was as quiet as the grave, sir. What may have gone on in the chambers above and around me I know not! Fortunately, our walls are thick, sir﻿—substantial. I say, sir, the man probably fell down and broke his neck. What he was doing here, I do not presume to say.”

“Well, it’s guess, you know, Mr. Cardlestone,” remarked Breton, again winking at Spargo. “But all that was found on this man was a scrap of paper on which my name and address were written. That’s practically all that was known of him, except that he’d just arrived from Australia.”

Mr. Cardlestone suddenly turned on the young barrister with a sharp, acute glance.

===Chinese===
“埃尔菲克先生告诉我，昨晚他在您那儿一直待到很晚，卡德尔斯顿先生，”他说。“你们两位当然什么可疑的动静也没听见吧？”

“在律师学院里，我们该听见什么可疑的动静，先生？”卡德尔斯顿先生怒气冲冲地反问。“我希望律师学院里没有那种事，小布雷顿先生。您那位受人敬重的监护人和我，照着平素清静的消遣安安静静过了一晚；他走的时候，四下里静得像座坟，先生。我头顶上和四周那些寓所里发生过什么，我不知道！所幸我们的墙厚，先生——结实着呢。我说，先生，那人多半是自己摔下去摔断了脖子。他到这儿来干什么，我不便妄言。”

“唉，这不过是猜猜罢了，您知道，卡德尔斯顿先生，”布雷顿说，又朝斯帕戈眨了眨眼。“可这人身上搜出来的全部东西，只有一张写着我的姓名住址的碎纸片。人们对他知道的差不多也就这些了，只知道他刚从澳大利亚来。”

卡德尔斯顿先生猛地转过身，用尖利而敏锐的目光逼视这位年轻的出庭律师。

===Original===
“Eh?” he exclaimed. “What’s this? You say this man had your name and address on him, young Breton!﻿—yours? And that he came from﻿—Australia?”

“That’s so,” answered Breton. “That’s all that’s known.”

Mr. Cardlestone put aside his umbrella, produced a bandana handkerchief of strong colours, and blew his nose in a reflective fashion.

“That’s a mysterious thing,” he observed. “Um﻿—does Elphick know all that?”

===Chinese===
“嗯？”他叫道。“这是怎么说？你说这人身上带着你的姓名住址，小布雷顿！——你的？还说他是从——澳大利亚来的？”

“是这样，”布雷顿答道。“已知的就这么多。”

卡德尔斯顿先生放下雨伞，掏出一方花色浓艳的班丹纳大手帕，若有所思地擤了擤鼻子。

“这可是件蹊跷事，”他说。“嗯——这些埃尔菲克全知道吗？”

===Original===
Breton looked at Spargo as if he was asking him for an explanation of Mr. Cardlestone’s altered manner. And Spargo took up the conversation.

“No,” he said. “All that Mr. Elphick knows is that Mr. Ronald Breton’s name and address were on the scrap of paper found on the body. Mr. Elphick”﻿—here Spargo paused and looked at Breton﻿—“Mr. Elphick,” he presently continued, slowly transferring his glance to the old barrister, “spoke of going to view the body.”

“Ah!” exclaimed Mr. Cardlestone, eagerly. “It can be seen? Then I’ll go and see it. Where is it?”

Breton started.

===Chinese===
布雷顿望着斯帕戈，仿佛在请他解释卡德尔斯顿先生的态度为何陡然一变。于是斯帕戈接过了话头。

“不知道，”他说。“埃尔菲克先生知道的，无非是罗纳德·布雷顿先生的姓名住址写在尸体上找到的那张碎纸片上。埃尔菲克先生”——说到这里，斯帕戈顿住，看了布雷顿一眼——“埃尔菲克先生，”他随即接着说，目光缓缓移向那位年老的出庭律师，“说起过要去看一眼尸体。”

“啊！”卡德尔斯顿先生急切地叫道。“还能看？那我要去看看。在哪儿？”

布雷顿一怔。

===Original===
“But﻿—my dear sir!” he said. “Why?”

Mr. Cardlestone picked up his umbrella again.

“I feel a proper curiosity about a mystery which occurs at my very door,” he said. “Also, I have known more than one man who went to Australia. This might﻿—I say might, young gentlemen﻿—might be a man I had once known. Show me where this body is.”

Breton looked helplessly at Spargo: it was plain that he did not understand the turn that things were taking. But Spargo was quick to seize an opportunity. In another minute he was conducting Mr. Cardlestone through the ins and outs of the Temple towards Blackfriars. And as they turned into Tudor Street they encountered Mr. Elphick.

===Chinese===
“可是——亲爱的先生！”他说。“为什么呢？”

卡德尔斯顿先生又拿起了雨伞。

“谜案就出在我自家门口，我怀有一份正当的好奇，”他说。“再说，去澳大利亚的人我也认识不止一个。这说不定——我说的是说不定，年轻的先生们——说不定正是我从前认识的某个人。领我去看看尸体在哪儿。”

布雷顿一筹莫展地望着斯帕戈：事情走到这一步，他显然摸不着头脑。可斯帕戈眼疾手快，一把抓住了机会。转眼间，他已经领着卡德尔斯顿先生穿过律师学院曲曲折折的路径往黑衣修士方向去。他们拐进都铎街时，迎面遇上了埃尔菲克先生。

===Original===
“I am going to the mortuary,” he remarked. “So, I suppose, are you, Cardlestone? Has anything more been discovered, young man?”

Spargo tried a chance shot﻿—at what he did not know. “The man’s name was Marbury,” he said. “He was from Australia.”

He was keeping a keen eye on Mr. Elphick, but he failed to see that Mr. Elphick showed any of the surprise which Mr. Cardlestone had exhibited. Rather, he seemed indifferent.

“Oh?” he said﻿—“Marbury? And from Australia. Well﻿—I should like to see the body.”

===Chinese===
“我正要去停尸房，”他说。“卡德尔斯顿，我料想你也是吧？有什么新发现吗，年轻人？”

斯帕戈试着放了一记冷枪——冲着什么放，他自己也不知道。“这人名叫马伯里，”他说。“从澳大利亚来。”

他紧紧盯住埃尔菲克先生，却没看出埃尔菲克先生有半点卡德尔斯顿先生先前露出的惊讶。毋宁说，他显得漠不关心。

“哦？”他说——“马伯里？从澳大利亚来。好吧——我想去看看那具尸体。”

===Original===
Spargo and Breton had to wait outside the mortuary while the two elder gentlemen went in. There was nothing to be learnt from either when they reappeared.

“We don’t know the man,” said Mr. Elphick, calmly. “As Mr. Cardlestone, I understand, has said to you already﻿—we have known men who went to Australia, and as this man was evidently wandering about the Temple, we thought it might have been one of them, come back. But﻿—we don’t recognize him.”

“Couldn’t recognize him,” said Mr. Cardlestone. “No!”

They went away together arm in arm, and Breton looked at Spargo.

===Chinese===
两位老先生进去的时候，斯帕戈和布雷顿只得在停尸房外候着。两人再露面时，从谁嘴里也问不出个所以然。

“我们不认识这个人，”埃尔菲克先生平静地说。“据我所知，卡德尔斯顿先生已经跟你们说过了——我们认识一些去了澳大利亚的人，而这人显然是在律师学院里转悠，所以我们想，说不定是那些人里的哪一个回来了。可是——我们认不出他。”

“认不出他，”卡德尔斯顿先生说。“认不出！”

两人挽着胳膊一道走了，布雷顿看向斯帕戈。

===Original===
“As if anybody on earth ever fancied they’d recognize him!” he said. “Well﻿—what are you going to do now, Spargo? I must go.”

Spargo, who had been digging his walking-stick into a crack in the pavement, came out of a fit of abstraction.

“I?” he said. “Oh﻿—I’m going to the office.” And he turned abruptly away, and walking straight off to the editorial rooms at the *Watchman*, made for one in which sat the official guardian of the editor. “Try to get me a few minutes with the chief,” he said.

===Chinese===
“倒好像世上真有人会觉得自己认得出他似的！”他说。“唉——你现在打算做什么，斯帕戈？我该走了。”

斯帕戈一直拿手杖往人行道的裂缝里戳，这时才从出神中回过味来。

“我？”他说。“哦——我去报馆。”说着他霍然转身，径直走到《守望报》的编辑室，直奔其中一间——坐在里面的是替主编把守门户的私人秘书。“想法子给我匀出几分钟见见老总，”他说。

===Original===
The private secretary looked up.

“Really important?” he asked.

“Big!” answered Spargo. “Fix it.”

Once closeted with the great man, whose idiosyncrasies he knew pretty well by that time, Spargo lost no time.

===Chinese===
私人秘书抬起头来。

“当真要紧？”

“天大的事！”斯帕戈答道。“安排上。”

待到与那位大人物关起门来单独相谈——此公的脾气秉性，斯帕戈到那时已经摸得相当熟了——他一刻也不耽搁。

===Original===
“You’ve heard about this murder in Middle Temple Lane?” he suggested.

“The mere facts,” replied the editor, tersely.

“I was there when the body was found,” continued Spargo, and gave a brief résumé of his doings. “I’m certain this is a most unusual affair,” he went on. “It’s as full of mystery as﻿—as it could be. I want to give my attention to it. I want to specialize on it. I can make such a story of it as we haven’t had for some time﻿—ages. Let me have it. And to start with, let me have two columns for tomorrow morning. I’ll make it﻿—big!”

===Chinese===
“中殿巷这桩凶案，您听说了吧？”他先开了个头。

“光知道些干巴巴的事实，”主编言简意赅地答道。

“发现尸体的时候我就在场，”斯帕戈接着说，把自己一路的所作所为简要交代了一遍。“我敢肯定，这是一桩极不寻常的案子，”他继续说道。“它充满了神秘——神秘得无以复加。我要把心思放在它上面，专门采写它。我能就它写出这样一篇报道——这样的报道咱们已经好一阵子、好久好久没有过了。把它交给我。头一件，先给我明天早上留出两栏版面。我会把它做成——一篇大文章！”

===Original===
The editor looked across his desk at Spargo’s eager face.

“Your other work?” he said.

“Well in hand,” replied Spargo. “I’m ahead a whole week﻿—both articles and reviews. I can tackle both.”

The editor put his finger tips together.

===Chinese===
主编隔着办公桌，望着斯帕戈那张热切的脸。

“你手头别的活计呢？”他说。

“都把控得住，”斯帕戈答道。“我整整领先一周的进度——文章和书评都是。两边我顾得过来。”

主编把两手指尖对在了一起。

===Original===
“Have you got some idea about this, young man?” he asked.

“I’ve got a great idea,” answered Spargo. He faced the great man squarely, and stared at him until he had brought a smile to the editorial face. “That’s why I want to do it,” he added. “And﻿—it’s not mere boasting nor overconfidence﻿—I know I shall do it better than anybody else.”

The editor considered matters for a brief moment.

“You mean to find out who killed this man?” he said at last.

===Chinese===
“这事你有些想法了，年轻人？”他问。

“我有个了不起的想法，”斯帕戈答道。他端端正正地面朝这位大人物，直盯得那张主编的脸浮起了笑意。“所以我才要来做它，”他补充道。“而且——这不是吹牛，也不是过分自信——我知道我会做得比任何人都好。”

主编寻思了片刻。

“你是打算查出是谁杀了这个人？”他终于开口。

===Original===
Spargo nodded his head﻿—twice.

“I’ll find that out,” he said doggedly.

The editor picked up a pencil, and bent to his desk.

“All right,” he said. “Go ahead. You shall have your two columns.”

Spargo went quietly away to his own nook and corner. He got hold of a block of paper and began to write. He was going to show how to do things.

===Chinese===
斯帕戈点了点头——点了两下。

“我会把它查出来，”他执拗地说。

主编拿起一支铅笔，俯身向办公桌。

“好吧，”他说。“去干吧。你要的两栏版面归你了。”

斯帕戈悄没声儿地回到自己那个小角落。他抓过一本便笺簿，动笔写了起来。他要让人瞧瞧，事情该怎么办。

## XXVI Still Silent / 第二十六章 依旧沉默

===Original===

Spargo dropped his pen on the desk before him with a sharp clatter that made Mrs. Gutch jump. A steady devotion to the bottle had made her nerves to be none of the strongest, and she looked at the startler of them with angry malevolence.

“Don’t do that again, young man!” she exclaimed sharply. “I can’t a-bear to be jumped out of my skin, and it’s bad manners. I observed that the gentleman’s name was Elphick.”

Spargo contrived to get in a glance at his proprietor and his editor﻿—a glance which came near to being a wink.

“Just so﻿—Elphick,” he said. “A law gentleman I think you said, Mrs. Gutch?”

“I said,” answered Mrs. Gutch, “as how he looked like a lawyer gentleman. And since you’re so particular, young man, though I wasn’t addressing you but your principals, he was a lawyer gentleman. One of the sort that wears wigs and gowns﻿—ain’t I seen his picture in Jane Baylis’s room at the boardinghouse where you saw her this morning?”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈把手中的笔往面前的书桌上一摔，啪的一声脆响，惊得格奇太太一跳。长年贪杯使她的神经本就不算坚强，此刻她望着这个惊吓了她的人，眼里满是怨毒。

“别再来这一套，年轻人！”她厉声道，“我可受不了叫人吓得魂儿都出窍，这也没有规矩。我方才说的是，那位先生姓埃尔菲克。”

斯帕戈设法朝他的老板和主编递过去一眼——那一眼跟使个眼色相差无几。

“正是——埃尔菲克，”他说，“我想您方才说的是，有位法律界的绅士，格奇太太？”

“我说的是，”格奇太太答道，“他瞧着像位律师绅士。既然你这年轻人这般究根问底——我方才可不是冲你说，是冲你家两位主人说的——那他确实是位律师绅士。就是戴假发、穿法袍的那路人——我在简·贝利斯房里见过他的相片，就在你今天早上见她的那家寄宿公寓里，这还有假？”

===Original===

“Elderly man?” asked Spargo.

“Elderly he will be now,” replied the informant; “but when he took the boy away he was a middle-aged man. About his age,” she added, pointing to the editor in a fashion which made that worthy man wince and the proprietor desire to laugh unconsumedly; “and not so very unlike him neither, being one as had no hair on his face.”

“Ah!” said Spargo. “And where did this Mr. Elphick take the boy, Mrs. Gutch?”

But Mrs. Gutch shook her head.

“Ain’t no idea,” she said. “He took him. Then, as I told you, Maitland came, and Jane Baylis told him that the boy was dead. And after that she never even told me anything about the boy. She kept a tight tongue. Once or twice I asked her, and she says, ‘Never you mind,’ she says; ‘he’s all right for life, if he lives to be as old as Methusalem.’ And she never said more, and I never said more. But,” continued Mrs. Gutch, whose pocket-flask was empty, and who began to wipe tears away, “she’s treated me hard has Jane Baylis, never allowing me a little comfort such as a lady of my age should have, and when I hears the two of you a-talking this morning the other side of that privet hedge, thinks I, ‘Now’s the time to have my knife into you, my fine madam!’ And I hope I done it.”

===Chinese===

“上了年纪么？”斯帕戈问。

“如今他是上了年纪了，”这位报信人答道，“可他领走那孩子的时候，还是个中年人。年纪跟这位相仿，”她加了一句，手指着主编，那副模样叫这位可敬的人物直皱眉头，又叫老板把笑意硬生生憋在肚子里；“长相也跟他差不太多，都是脸上不长胡子的那路人。”

“啊！”斯帕戈说，“那么这位埃尔菲克先生把孩子领到哪儿去了，格奇太太？”

可格奇太太摇了摇头。

“一点儿也不知道，”她说，“他把他领走了。后来呢，就像我告诉过你们的，梅特兰来了，简·贝利斯对他说孩子死了。打那以后，她连关于那孩子的一个字都不曾对我提过。她嘴紧得很。我有一两回问起她，她就摆出那副腔调：‘不关你的事，’她说，‘他一辈子吃穿不愁，哪怕活到玛土撒拉那么老。’她再没多说，我也再没多问。可是，”格奇太太接着说，她的随身小酒壶已经空了，她开始抹起眼泪来，“简·贝利斯待我实在狠心，连我这个年纪的太太应有的一点儿宽慰都不肯给我；今天早上我听见你们两位在那道女贞树篱的那一头说话，我心里就想：‘这会儿可轮到我捅你一刀了，我的好太太！’但愿我捅成了。”

===Original===

Spargo looked at the editor and the proprietor, nodding his head slightly. He meant them to understand that he had got all he wanted from Mother Gutch.

“What are you going to do, Mrs. Gutch, when you leave here?” he asked. “You shall be driven straight back to Bayswater, if you like.”

“Which I shall be obliged for, young man,” said Mrs. Gutch, “and likewise for the first week of the annuity, and will call every Saturday for the same at eleven punctual, or can be posted to me on a Friday, whichever is agreeable to you gentlemen. And having my first week in my purse, and being driven to Bayswater, I shall take my boxes and go to a friend of mine where I shall be hearty welcome, shaking the dust of my feet off against Jane Baylis and where I’ve been living with her.”

“Yes, but, Mrs. Gutch,” said Spargo, with some anxiety, “if you go back there tonight, you’ll be very careful not to tell Miss Baylis that you’ve been here and told us all this?”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈望了望主编和老板，微微点头。他要他们明白：他从格奇大妈嘴里，想要的东西已经全拿到了。

“您离开这儿以后打算怎么办，格奇太太？”他问，“您要是愿意，可以坐车直接回贝斯沃特。”

“这个情我领了，年轻人，”格奇太太说，“头一个礼拜的年金我也一并领情。往后每个礼拜六上午十一点整，我准来取当周的，或者礼拜五寄给我也行，全凭你们几位先生高兴。等头一周的钱进了荷包，也坐车到了贝斯沃特，我就收拾我的箱子，上我的一个朋友那儿去，在那儿我保管大受欢迎——把脚上的尘土抖个干净，从此跟简·贝利斯一刀两断，也跟我陪她同住的那个地方一刀两断。”

“是，不过，格奇太太，”斯帕戈有些不放心地说，“您今晚回那儿去，千万小心，别告诉贝利斯小姐您来过这儿、把这些全都倒给我们了，可好？”

===Original===

Mrs. Gutch rose, dignified and composed.

“Young man,” she said, “you mean well, but you ain’t used to dealing with ladies. I can keep my tongue as still as anybody when I like. I wouldn’t tell Jane Baylis my affairs﻿—my new affairs, gentlemen, thanks to you﻿—not for two annuities, paid twice a week!”

“Take Mrs. Gutch downstairs, Spargo, and see her all right, and then come to my room,” said the editor. “And don’t you forget, Mrs. Gutch﻿—keep a quiet tongue in your head﻿—no more talk﻿—or there’ll be no annuities on Saturday mornings.”

So Spargo took Mother Gutch to the cashier’s department and paid her her first week’s money, and he got her a taxicab, and paid for it, and saw her depart, and then he went to the editor’s room, strangely thoughtful. The editor and the proprietor were talking, but they stopped when Spargo entered and looked at him eagerly. “I think we’ve done it,” said Spargo quietly.

===Chinese===

格奇太太站起身来，端庄而镇定。

“年轻人，”她说，“你是一片好意，可你不惯于同太太们打交道。我高兴的时候，嘴能跟任何人一样严实。想叫我把我的事告诉简·贝利斯——我的新差事，二位先生，托你们的福——就是给我两份年金、一个礼拜发两回，我也不说！”

“斯帕戈，送格奇太太下楼去，把她安顿妥当，然后到我房间来。”主编说，“您可别忘了，格奇太太——管住您那根舌头——别再多话——不然礼拜六早上可就没有年金了。”

于是斯帕戈领着格奇大妈到出纳部门，付了她头一周的钱，又替她叫了辆出租汽车，付了车钱，目送她离去，然后才进主编的房间，心里若有所思，异乎寻常。主编和老板正在交谈，斯帕戈一进门，两人便住了口，热切地望着他。“我想，我们成了。”斯帕戈平静地说。

===Original===

“What, precisely, have we found out?” asked the editor.

“A great deal more than I’d anticipated,” answered Spargo, “and I don’t know what fields it doesn’t open out. If you look back, you’ll remember that the only thing found on Marbury’s body was a scrap of grey paper on which was a name and address﻿—Ronald Breton, King’s Bench Walk.”

“Well?”

“Breton is a young barrister. Also he writes a bit﻿—I have accepted two or three articles of his for our literary page.”

===Chinese===

“我们究竟查出了什么？”主编问。

“比我预想的多得多，”斯帕戈答道，“而且我不知道还有什么天地是它开辟不出来的。诸位回想一下就记得，马伯里尸体上唯一找到的东西，是一小片灰纸，上面写着一个姓名住址——罗纳德·布雷顿，王座法庭步道。”

“嗯？”

“布雷顿是个年轻的出庭律师。他还写点文章——我采用了他的两三篇稿子，登在我们的文学版上。”

===Original===

“Well?”

“Further, he is engaged to Miss Aylmore, the eldest daughter of Aylmore, the Member of Parliament who has been charged at Bow Street today with the murder of Marbury.”

“I know. Well, what then, Spargo?”

“But the most important matter,” continued Spargo, speaking very deliberately, “is this﻿—that is, taking that old woman’s statement to be true, as I personally believe it is﻿—that Breton, as he has told me himself (I have seen a good deal of him) was brought up by a guardian. That guardian is Mr. Septimus Elphick, the barrister.”

The proprietor and the editor looked at each other. Their faces wore the expression of men thinking on the same lines and arriving at the same conclusion. And the proprietor suddenly turned on Spargo with a sharp interrogation: “You think then﻿—”

===Chinese===

“嗯？”

“再者，他同艾尔莫尔小姐订了婚——就是下议院议员艾尔莫尔的长女；这位议员今天已在弓街被控谋杀马伯里。”

“这我知道。那么后来呢，斯帕戈？”

“可是最要紧的一点，”斯帕戈接着说，说得字斟句酌，“是这个——也就是说，假定那老太婆的话属实，我个人是相信属实的——布雷顿，据他亲口对我讲（我跟他交往很多），是由一位监护人抚养长大的。这位监护人，就是出庭律师塞普蒂默斯·埃尔菲克先生。”

老板和主编面面相觑。两张脸上都是那种同循一条思路、同得一个结论的神情。老板忽然转过身来，向斯帕戈厉声发问：“那么你认为——”

===Original===

Spargo nodded.

“I think that Mr. Septimus Elphick is the Elphick, and that Breton is the young Maitland of whom Mrs. Gutch has been talking,” he answered.

The editor got up, thrust his hands in his pockets, and began to pace the room.

“If that’s so,” he said, “if that’s so, the mystery deepens. What do you propose to do, Spargo?”

“I think,” said Spargo, slowly, “I think that without telling him anything of what we have learnt, I should like to see young Breton and get an introduction from him to Mr. Elphick. I can make a good excuse for wanting an interview with him. If you will leave it in my hands﻿—”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈点了点头。

“我认为，塞普蒂默斯·埃尔菲克先生就是我们说的那个埃尔菲克，而布雷顿就是格奇太太讲到的那个小梅特兰。”他答道。

主编站起身来，双手插进衣袋，开始在屋里踱来踱去。

“倘若如此，”他说，“倘若如此，这谜团可就越发深了。你打算怎么办，斯帕戈？”

“我想，”斯帕戈慢慢地说，“我想，在不向他透露半点我们查到的东西的前提下，我要去见见小布雷顿，请他引见埃尔菲克先生。要采访他，我找得出一个像样的由头。这事诸位若肯交到我手上——”

===Original===

“Yes, yes!” said the proprietor, waving a hand. “Leave it entirely in Spargo’s hands.”

“Keep me informed,” said the editor. “Do what you think. It strikes me you’re on the track.”

Spargo left their presence, and going back to his own room, still faintly redolent of the personality of Mrs. Gutch, got hold of the reporter who had been present at Bow Street when Aylmore was brought up that morning. There was nothing new; the authorities had merely asked for another remand. So far as the reporter knew, Aylmore had said nothing fresh to anybody.

Spargo went round to the Temple and up to Ronald Breton’s chambers. He found the young barrister just preparing to leave, and looking unusually grave and thoughtful. At sight of Spargo he turned back from his outer door, beckoned the journalist to follow him, and led him into an inner room.

===Chinese===

“好，好！”老板挥了挥手说，“全交给斯帕戈去办。”

“随时向我通报，”主编说，“放手去做。我看你是摸上路子了。”

斯帕戈从他们跟前退出来，回到自己那间还隐隐留着格奇太太气息的屋里，找来了那天早上艾尔莫尔被提堂时在弓街旁听的记者。没有什么新鲜事；当局只是又申请了一次还押。据这记者所知，艾尔莫尔没有对任何人说过一句新的话。

斯帕戈绕到中殿律师学院，上楼去罗纳德·布雷顿的律师事务所。他发现这位年轻的出庭律师正准备出门，神色异乎寻常地凝重，若有所思。一见斯帕戈，他从外门口折回身来，招手叫记者跟上，把他领进了里间。

===Original===

“I say, Spargo!” he said, as he motioned his visitor to take a chair. “This is becoming something more than serious. You know what you told me to do yesterday as regards Aylmore?”

“To get him to tell all?﻿—Yes,” said Spargo.

Breton shook his head.

“Stratton﻿—his solicitor, you know﻿—and I saw him this morning before the police-court proceedings,” he continued. “I told him of my talk with you; I even went as far as to tell him that his daughters had been to the *Watchman* office. Stratton and I both begged him to take your advice and tell all, everything, no matter at what cost to his private feelings. We pointed out to him the serious nature of the evidence against him; how he had damaged himself by not telling the whole truth at once; how he had certainly done a great deal to excite suspicion against himself; how, as the evidence stands at present, any jury could scarcely do less than convict him. And it was all no good, Spargo!”

===Chinese===

“我说，斯帕戈！”他说着，示意来客落座，“这事可就不止是严重了。你总记得，你昨天叫我怎么办艾尔莫尔的事吧？”

“劝他把一切和盘托出？——记得。”斯帕戈说。

布雷顿摇了摇头。

“斯特拉顿——他的事务律师，你知道的——跟我，今天早上在治安法庭开审之前见了他，”他接着说，“我把跟你谈的话告诉了他；我甚至告诉他，他的两个女儿去过《守望报》报馆。斯特拉顿和我一齐恳求他听你的劝，把一切统统说出来，不管私人感情上要付出什么代价。我们向他指出，对他不利的证据何等严重；他不肯一下子把全部真话说尽，是何等损害自己；他无疑已经做了太多惹人疑心的事；就现有证据而论，任何一个陪审团几乎都免不了要定他的罪。可这一切全是白费，斯帕戈！”

===Original===

“He won’t say anything?”

“He’ll say no more. He was adamant. ‘I told the entire truth in respect to my dealings with Marbury on the night he met his death at the inquest,’ he said, over and over again, ‘and I shall say nothing further on any consideration. If the law likes to hang an innocent man on such evidence as that, let it!’ And he persisted in that until we left him. Spargo, I don’t know what’s to be done.”

“And nothing happened at the police-court?”

“Nothing﻿—another remand. Stratton and I saw Aylmore again before he was removed. He left us with a sort of sardonic remark﻿—‘If you all want to prove me innocent,’ he said, ‘find the guilty man.’ ”

===Chinese===

“他什么都不肯说了？”

“他再不肯多说了。他是铁打的心肠。‘在验尸审讯上，关于我在马伯里丧命那晚与他往来的情形，我已经把全部实话说尽了，’他翻来覆去地说，‘无论如何，我什么也不再说了。法律要是高兴凭那样的证据绞死一个无辜的人，那就由它去！’直到我们离开他，他一直是这个调子。斯帕戈，我不知道还有什么办法可想。”

“治安法庭上没有什么新情况？”

“没有——又还押了。艾尔莫尔被带走之前，斯特拉顿和我又见了他一面。他丢下我们走时说了一句冷嘲式的话——‘你们诸位要是都想证明我清白，’他说，‘那就去找出真凶来。’”

===Original===

“Well, there was a tremendous lot of common sense in that,” said Spargo.

“Yes, of course, but how, how, how is it going to be done?” exclaimed Breton. “Are you any nearer﻿—is Rathbury any nearer? Is there the slightest clue that will fasten the guilt on anybody else?”

Spargo gave no answer to these questions. He remained silent a while, apparently thinking.

“Was Rathbury in court?” he suddenly asked.

“He was,” replied Breton. “He was there with two or three other men who I suppose were detectives, and seemed to be greatly interested in Aylmore.”

===Chinese===

“嗯，这话里头的道理可实在得很。”斯帕戈说。

“是啊，这当然，可是怎么办、怎么办、到底怎么办呢？”布雷顿嚷道，“你有没有更接近一步——拉思伯里有没有更接近一步？有没有一丝一毫的线索，能把这罪责扣到别人头上？”

这些问题，斯帕戈一个也没有回答。他沉默了片刻，像是在思索。

“拉思伯里到庭了么？”他突然问。

“到了，”布雷顿答道，“他跟另外两三个人在一起，我猜都是侦探，而且似乎对艾尔莫尔大感兴趣。”

===Original===

“If I don’t see Rathbury tonight I’ll see him in the morning,” said Spargo. He rose as if to go, but after lingering a moment, sat down again. “Look here,” he continued, “I don’t know how this thing stands in law, but would it be a very weak case against Aylmore if the prosecution couldn’t show some motive for his killing Marbury?”

Breton smiled.

“There’s no necessity to prove motive in murder,” he said. “But I’ll tell you what, Spargo﻿—if the prosecution can show that Aylmore had a motive for getting rid of Marbury, if they could prove that it was to Aylmore’s advantage to silence him﻿—why, then, I don’t think he’s a chance.”

“I see. But so far no motive, no reason for his killing Marbury has been shown.”

“I know of none.”

===Chinese===

“今晚见不着拉思伯里，明天早上我也要见他。”斯帕戈说。他起身像是告辞，可又耽搁了片刻，重新坐下。“你听我说，”他接着道，“我不懂这事在法律上是什么章程，不过，倘若控方拿不出艾尔莫尔杀害马伯里的任何动机，这案子加在他身上是不是就很站不住脚？”

布雷顿微微一笑。

“谋杀案里，证明动机并非必要，”他说，“不过我告诉你吧，斯帕戈——要是控方能证明艾尔莫尔有除掉马伯里的动机，要是他们能证明让马伯里闭嘴对艾尔莫尔有利——那么，我看他就没什么指望了。”

“我明白。可是到目前为止，还没有提出他杀害马伯里的任何动机、任何理由。”

“我是半点也不知道。”

===Original===

Spargo rose and moved to the door.

“Well, I’m off,” he said. Then, as if he suddenly recollected something, he turned back. “Oh, by the by,” he said, “isn’t your guardian, Mr. Elphick, a big authority on philately?”

“One of the biggest. Awful enthusiast.”

“Do you think he’d tell me a bit about those Australian stamps which Marbury showed to Criedir, the dealer?”

“Certain, he would﻿—delighted. Here”﻿—and Breton scribbled a few words on a card﻿—“there’s his address and a word from me. I’ll tell you when you can always find him in, five nights out of seven﻿—at nine o’clock, after he’s dined. I’d go with you tonight, but I must go to Aylmore’s. The two girls are in terrible trouble.”

“Give them a message from me,” said Spargo as they went out together. “Tell them to keep up their hearts and their courage.”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈起身朝门口走去。

“好了，我走了。”他说。接着，像是突然想起什么，他又回过身来。“哦，对了，”他说，“你的监护人埃尔菲克先生，不就是集邮学方面的大权威么？”

“数一数二的权威。狂热的爱好者。”

“你想他肯不肯跟我讲讲，马伯里给那个邮票商克里迪尔看过的那些澳大利亚邮票？”

“准肯——他还巴不得呢。给”——布雷顿在一张卡片上潦草地写了几句——“这是他的地址，还有我的一句话。我告诉你什么时候准能找到他：一个礼拜七个晚上，倒有五个晚上他必在家——九点钟，他吃过晚饭以后。今晚我本想陪你同去，可我必须到艾尔莫尔家去。两个姑娘正伤心得了不得。”

“替我给她们捎句话，”两人一同出门时，斯帕戈说，“叫她们别灰心，鼓起勇气来。”

## IV The Anglo-Orient Hotel / 第四章 盎格鲁-东方旅馆

===Original===
The house at which Spargo and his companions presently drew up was an old-fashioned place in the immediate vicinity of Waterloo Railway Station﻿—a plain-fronted, foursquare erection, essentially mid-Victorian in appearance, and suggestive, somehow, of the very early days of railway travelling. Anything more in contrast with the modern ideas of a hotel it would have been difficult to find in London, and Ronald Breton said so as he and the others crossed the pavement.

“And yet a good many people used to favour this place on their way to and from Southampton in the old days,” remarked Rathbury. “And I daresay that old travellers, coming back from the East after a good many years’ absence, still rush in here. You see, it’s close to the station, and travellers have a knack of walking into the nearest place when they’ve a few thousand miles of steamboat and railway train behind them. Look there, now!” They had crossed the threshold as the detective spoke, and as they entered a square, heavily-furnished hall, he made a sidelong motion of his head towards a bar on the left, wherein stood or lounged a number of men who from their general appearance, their slouched hats, and their bronzed faces appeared to be Colonials, or at any rate to have spent a good part of their time beneath Oriental skies. There was a murmur of tongues that had a Colonial accent in it; an aroma of tobacco that suggested Sumatra and Trichinopoly, and Rathbury wagged his head sagely. “Lay you anything the dead man was a Colonial, Mr. Spargo,” he remarked. “Well, now, I suppose that’s the landlord and landlady.”
===Chinese===
斯帕戈（Spargo）和同伴们此刻停在那家旅店门前，它就在滑铁卢火车站（Waterloo Railway Station）近旁，是一处老式的房子——门面朴实，四四方方，一看便是维多利亚时代中期的建筑，不知怎的，让人联想到铁路旅行的极早期岁月。在整个伦敦，要找一家与现代旅馆概念反差更大的地方，怕是难了；罗纳德·布雷顿（Ronald Breton）一行人穿过人行道时，就说了这么一句。

“不过从前，好些人往来南安普顿（Southampton）的路上，都爱光顾这家店，”拉思伯里（Rathbury）说。“我还敢说，那些阔别多年、从东方回来的老旅行客，如今照样一头扎进这里。你瞧，它离车站近；人在几千英里的轮船火车颠簸之后，天生就会就近钻进头一家店。喏，你看那边！”警探说话间，众人已跨过门槛，走进一间方方正正、陈设厚重的接待厅；他朝左边一间酒吧侧头示意——里面或站或倚着好些人，看那做派、那压得软塌塌的帽檐、那古铜色的脸膛，多半是殖民地来的，至少也在东方的天空下消磨过大半光阴。一片嗡嗡的话音里带着殖民地口音；一股烟草味，闻着像是苏门答腊（Sumatra）和特里奇诺波利（Trichinopoly）的货色。拉思伯里一副内行的样子直点头。“跟你打什么赌都行，死者准是个殖民地人，斯帕戈先生，”他说。“好——我琢磨着，那两位该是老板和老板娘了。”

===Original===
There was an office facing them, at the rear of the hall, and a man and woman were regarding them from a box window which opened above a ledge on which lay a register book. They were middle-aged folk: the man, a fleshy, round-faced, somewhat pompous-looking individual, who might at some time have been a butler; the woman a tall, spare-figured, thin-featured, sharp-eyed person, who examined the newcomers with an enquiring gaze. Rathbury went up to them with easy confidence.

“You the landlord of this house, sir?” he asked. “Mr. Walters? Just so﻿—and Mrs. Walters, I presume?”

The landlord made a stiff bow and looked sharply at his questioner.

“What can I do for you, sir?” he enquired.

“A little matter of business, Mr. Walters,” replied Rathbury, pulling out a card. “You’ll see there who I am﻿—Detective-Sergeant Rathbury, of the Yard. This is Mr. Frank Spargo, a newspaper man; this is Mr. Ronald Breton, a barrister.”
===Chinese===
正对面、大厅后部有一间账房，一男一女正从一格凸窗里打量他们；窗开在一道台架上方，架上摊着住客登记簿。两人都是中年模样：男的是个肉头脸圆、派头十足的汉子，瞧那气派，早年兴许当过管家；女的高挑精瘦、面容削薄、目光尖利，正用审视的眼神打量这几个来客。拉思伯里从容自信地迎上前去。

“您是这店的老板吗，先生？”他问。“沃尔特斯（Walters）先生？这就对了——这位想必是沃尔特斯太太？”

老板僵硬地躬了躬身，锐利地打量着发问的人。

“先生，有何贵干？”他问道。

“一点小小的公事，沃尔特斯先生，”拉思伯里边说边掏出一张名片。“您一看便知我是谁——苏格兰场的侦探警长拉思伯里。这位是弗兰克·斯帕戈先生，新闻界人士；这位是罗纳德·布雷顿先生，出庭律师。”

===Original===
The landlady, hearing their names and description, pointed to a side door, and signed Rathbury and his companions to pass through. Obeying her pointed finger, they found themselves in a small private parlour. Walters closed the two doors which led into it and looked at his principal visitor.

“What is it, Mr. Rathbury?” he enquired. “Anything wrong?”

“We want a bit of information,” answered Rathbury, almost with indifference.

“Did anybody of the name of Marbury put up here yesterday﻿—elderly man, grey hair, fresh complexion?”

Mrs. Walters started, glancing at her husband.

“There!” she exclaimed. “I knew some enquiry would be made. Yes﻿—a Mr. Marbury took a room here yesterday morning, just after the noon train got in from Southampton. Number 20 he took. But﻿—he didn’t use it last night. He went out﻿—very late﻿—and he never came back.”
===Chinese===
老板娘听罢他们的姓名身份，便指了指一扇侧门，示意拉思伯里和同伴进去。顺着她指的方向，众人进了一间小巧的私人起居室。沃尔特斯关上通向这屋子的两扇门，望着为首的来客。

“什么事，拉思伯里先生？”他问。“出了岔子？”

“想打听点事儿，”拉思伯里答道，语气近乎淡漠。

“昨天可有位姓马伯里（Marbury）的客人投宿——上了年纪，头发花白，面色红润？”

沃尔特斯太太一惊，瞥了丈夫一眼。

“瞧！”她叫道。“我就知道会有人来查问。是这样——昨天上午，从南安普顿开来的正午班车刚进站，就有一位马伯里先生在敝店开了个房间，要的是20号。可是——昨夜他没回来睡。他出去了——很晚才出去——就再没回来。”

===Original===
Rathbury nodded. Answering a sign from the landlord, he took a chair and, sitting down, looked at Mrs. Walters.

“What made you think some enquiry would be made, ma’am?” he asked. “Had you noticed anything?”

Mrs. Walters seemed a little confused by this direct question. Her husband gave vent to a species of growl.

“Nothing to notice,” he muttered. “Her way of speaking﻿—that’s all.”

“Well﻿—why I said that was this,” said the landlady. “He happened to tell us, did Mr. Marbury, that he hadn’t been in London for over twenty years, and couldn’t remember anything about it, him, he said, never having known much about London at any time. And, of course, when he went out so late and never came back, why, naturally, I thought something had happened to him, and that there’d be enquiries made.”

“Just so﻿—just so!” said Rathbury. “So you would, ma’am﻿—so you would. Well, something has happened to him. He’s dead. What’s more, there’s strong reason to think he was murdered.”
===Chinese===
拉思伯里点点头。应着老板的示意，他拉过一把椅子坐下，望着沃尔特斯太太。

“您怎么会想到会有人来查问，太太？”他问。“您察觉到什么了？”

这直截了当的一问，让沃尔特斯太太有点慌神。她丈夫喉头发出一声低吼。

“没什么可察觉的，”他咕哝道。“她说话就这德行——没别的。”

“哎——我那么说，是这么回事，”老板娘道。“这位马伯里先生恰好同我们提起过，说他有二十多年没来过伦敦了，如今什么也记不得；他说自己从前就不大认得伦敦。那么，他深更半夜出去、又一去不回，我自然就寻思，他多半出了什么事，肯定要有人来查问的。”

“正是——正是！”拉思伯里说。“换了谁都会这么想，太太——都会的。唔，他确实出事了。他死了。而且，有充分的理由认为，他是被人谋害的。”

===Original===
Mr. and Mrs. Walters received this announcement with proper surprise and horror, and the landlord suggested a little refreshment to his visitors. Spargo and Breton declined, on the ground that they had work to do during the afternoon; Rathbury accepted it, evidently as a matter of course.

“My respects,” he said, lifting his glass. “Well, now, perhaps you’ll just tell me what you know of this man? I may as well tell you, Mr. and Mrs. Walters, that he was found dead in Middle Temple Lane this morning, at a quarter to three; that there wasn’t anything on him but his clothes and a scrap of paper which bore this gentleman’s name and address; that this gentleman knows nothing whatever of him, and that I traced him here because he bought a cap at a West End hatter’s yesterday, and had it sent to your hotel.”

“Yes,” said Mrs. Walters quickly, “that’s so. And he went out in that cap last night. Well﻿—we don’t know much about him. As I said, he came in here about a quarter past twelve yesterday morning, and booked Number 20. He had a porter with him that brought a trunk and a bag﻿—they’re in 20 now, of course. He told me that he had stayed at this house over twenty years ago, on his way to Australia﻿—that, of course, was long before we took it. And he signed his name in the book as John Marbury.”
===Chinese===
沃尔特斯先生和太太听到这一消息，适时地露出应有的惊愕与骇然，老板便提议请几位来客用些茶点。斯帕戈和布雷顿婉辞了，理由是下午还有事要办；拉思伯里却照单收下，显然觉得理所当然。

“敬您二位，”他举杯说道。“那么，现在，二位不妨把知道的这位先生的情况都说说？我也不妨直说，沃尔特斯先生、沃尔特斯太太：今天凌晨差一刻三点，他在中殿巷（Middle Temple Lane）被人发现死了；他身上除了衣服和一张写着这位先生姓名住址的小纸片，别无他物；这位先生对他一无所知；我之所以追到贵店，是因为他昨天在伦敦西区一家帽店买了顶便帽，让店里送到贵店来了。”

“是的，”沃尔特斯太太赶忙应道，“是有这么回事。昨晚他正是戴着那顶帽子出去的。这个——我们对他知道得不多。就像我说的，昨天中午十二点一刻左右他进的店，订了20号房。有个搬运工跟着，送来一只大衣箱和一只提包——如今自然都在20号房里。他告诉我，二十多年前他去澳大利亚的途中，在这家店住过——那当然早在我们盘下这店之前了。他在登记簿上签的名字是约翰·马伯里。”

===Original===
“We’ll look at that, if you please,” said Rathbury.

Walters fetched in the register and turned the leaf to the previous day’s entries. They all bent over the dead man’s writing.

“ ‘John Marbury, Coolumbidgee, New South Wales,’ ” said Rathbury. “Ah﻿—now I was wondering if that writing would be the same as that on the scrap of paper, Mr. Breton. But, you see, it isn’t﻿—it’s quite different.”

“Quite different,” said Breton. He, too, was regarding the handwriting with great interest. And Rathbury noticed his keen inspection of it, and asked another question.

“Ever seen that writing before?” he suggested.
===Chinese===
“劳驾，我们看看登记簿，”拉思伯里说。

沃尔特斯取来登记簿，翻到前一天那一页。众人都俯身去看死者的笔迹。

“‘约翰·马伯里，新南威尔士，库伦比奇（Coolumbidgee），’”拉思伯里念道。“啊——我方才还在琢磨，这笔迹会不会跟那小纸片上的一样呢，布雷顿先生。可您瞧——不一样，大不相同。”

“大不相同，”布雷顿说。他也在饶有兴味地端详那笔迹。拉思伯里注意到他看得仔细，便又发一问。

“从前见过这笔迹吗？”他问道。

===Original===
“Never,” answered Breton. “And yet﻿—there’s something very familiar about it.”

“Then the probability is that you have seen it before,” remarked Rathbury. “Well﻿—now we’ll hear a little more about Marbury’s doings here. Just tell me all you know, Mr. and Mrs. Walters.”

“My wife knows most,” said Walters. “I scarcely saw the man﻿—I don’t remember speaking with him.”

“No,” said Mrs. Walters. “You didn’t﻿—you weren’t much in his way. Well,” she continued, “I showed him up to his room. He talked a bit﻿—said he’d just landed at Southampton from Melbourne.”

“Did he mention his ship?” asked Rathbury. “But if he didn’t, it doesn’t matter, for we can find out.”
===Chinese===
“从没见过，”布雷顿答道。“可是——这字看着又眼熟得很。”

“那你多半是见过的，”拉思伯里说。“好——现在再讲讲马伯里在这儿的一举一动吧。把你们知道的都告诉我，沃尔特斯先生、沃尔特斯太太。”

“多半是我太太知道，”沃尔特斯说。“我几乎没见过这个人——不记得同他说过话。”

“是啊，”沃尔特斯太太说。“你没见过——你不大跟他打照面。是这样，”她接着说，“我领他上楼到他房间去。他说了几句——说他是刚从墨尔本（Melbourne）那边到南安普顿上岸的。”

“他提过坐的是哪条船吗？”拉思伯里问。“不过没提也不要紧，我们查得出来。”

===Original===
“I believe the name’s on his things,” answered the landlady. “There are some labels of that sort. Well, he asked for a chop to be cooked for him at once, as he was going out. He had his chop, and he went out at exactly one o’clock, saying to me that he expected he’d get lost, as he didn’t know London well at any time, and shouldn’t know it at all now. He went outside there﻿—I saw him﻿—looked about him and walked off towards Blackfriars way. During the afternoon the cap you spoke of came for him﻿—from Fiskie’s. So, of course, I judged he’d been Piccadilly way. But he himself never came in until ten o’clock. And then he brought a gentleman with him.”

“Aye?” said Rathbury. “A gentleman, now? Did you see him?”

“Just,” replied the landlady. “They went straight up to 20, and I just caught a mere glimpse of the gentleman as they turned up the stairs. A tall, well-built gentleman, with a grey beard, very well dressed as far as I could see, with a top hat and a white silk muffler round his throat, and carrying an umbrella.”

“And they went to Marbury’s room?” said Rathbury. “What then?”
===Chinese===
“我记得他行李上就有船名，”老板娘答道。“行李上贴着那类标签。是这样，他叫马上给他煎一块猪排，因为他要出门。他吃完猪排，一点整就出去了，对我说他估摸着自己会迷路，他从前就不大认得伦敦，如今更是一点儿也认不得了。他走到外头——我瞧见的——四下张望了一番，便朝黑衣修士（Blackfriars）那头去了。下午，您说的那顶帽子送来了——菲斯基帽店（Fiskie's）送来的。所以我自然就断定，他去的是皮卡迪利（Piccadilly）那一带。可他自己直到十点钟才回店。回来时还带了位先生。”

“哦？”拉思伯里说。“一位先生？你看清他了？”

“就瞅了一眼，”老板娘答道。“他们径直上20号房去，他们拐上楼梯那会儿，我恰好瞥了那位先生一眼。高个子，身板结实，留一把灰白胡须，就我看来穿着十分讲究，头戴大礼帽，脖子上围一条白绸围巾，手里拿着一把伞。”

“他们进了马伯里的房间？”拉思伯里说。“后来呢？”

===Original===
“Well, then, Mr. Marbury rang for some whiskey and soda,” continued Mrs. Walters. “He was particular to have a decanter of whiskey: that, and a syphon of soda were taken up there. I heard nothing more until nearly midnight; then the hall-porter told me that the gentleman in 20 had gone out, and had asked him if there was a night-porter﻿—as, of course, there is. He went out at half-past eleven.”

“And the other gentleman?” asked Rathbury.

“The other gentleman,” answered the landlady, “went out with him. The hall-porter said they turned towards the station. And that was the last anybody in this house saw of Mr. Marbury. He certainly never came back.”

“That,” observed Rathbury with a quiet smile, “that is quite certain, ma’am? Well﻿—I suppose we’d better see this Number 20 room, and have a look at what he left there.”

“Everything,” said Mrs. Walters, “is just as he left it. Nothing’s been touched.”
===Chinese===
“后来，马伯里先生按铃要威士忌和苏打水，”沃尔特斯太太接着说。“他特意嘱咐要一整瓶的威士忌：那一瓶酒，还有一只苏打水瓶，一并送了上去。直到将近午夜，我才又听到动静；门厅侍者告诉我，20号那位先生出去了，还问他店里有没有值夜班的门房——我们店里自然是有的。他是十一点半出去的。”

“另一位先生呢？”拉思伯里问。

“另一位先生，”老板娘答道，“是同他一起出去的。门厅侍者说，他们朝车站那头拐过去了。这店里的人，最后见到的马伯里先生就是那一眼。他确实再没回来过。”

“这一点，”拉思伯里带着淡淡一笑说，“这一点是千真万确的了，太太？好——我看我们最好去看看这间20号房，瞧瞧他留下了什么。”

“东西，”沃尔特斯太太说，“都照他离去时原样摆着。什么都没动过。”

===Original===
It seemed to two of the visitors that there was little to touch. On the dressing-table lay a few ordinary articles of toilet﻿—none of them of any quality or value: the dead man had evidently been satisfied with the plain necessities of life. An overcoat hung from a peg: Rathbury, without ceremony, went through its pockets; just as unceremoniously he proceeded to examine trunk and bag, and finding both unlocked, he laid out on the bed every article they contained and examined each separately and carefully. And he found nothing whereby he could gather any clue to the dead owner’s identity.

“There you are!” he said, making an end of his task. “You see, it’s just the same with these things as with the clothes he had on him. There are no papers﻿—there’s nothing to tell who he was, what he was after, where he’d come from﻿—though that we may find out in other ways. But it’s not often that a man travels without some clue to his identity. Beyond the fact that some of this linen was, you see, bought in Melbourne, we know nothing of him. Yet he must have had papers and money on him. Did you see anything of his money, now, ma’am?” he asked, suddenly turning to Mrs. Walters. “Did he pull out his purse in your presence, now?”

“Yes,” answered the landlady, with promptitude. “He came into the bar for a drink after he’d been up to his room. He pulled out a handful of gold when he paid for it﻿—a whole handful. There must have been some thirty to forty sovereigns and half-sovereigns.”

“And he hadn’t a penny piece on him﻿—when found,” muttered Rathbury.

“I noticed another thing, too,” remarked the landlady. “He was wearing a very fine gold watch and chain, and had a splendid ring on his left hand﻿—little finger﻿—gold, with a big diamond in it.”
===Chinese===
在两位来客看来，这里实在没什么可动的。梳妆台上搁着几样寻常的盥洗用品——没有一件讲究值钱：死者生前显然满足于生活上最朴素的必需。衣钩上挂着一件大衣：拉思伯里毫不客气地搜了一遍口袋；又同样不客气地动手检查衣箱和提包，发现两样都没上锁，便把里面的东西一件件摊在床上，逐件细细检视。结果一无所获，找不出任何能查明死者身份的线索。

“就是这样！”他说着，收拾停当。“瞧见了吧，这些东西跟他身上的衣服一个样。没有文件——没有任何东西能说明他是谁、图的是什么、从哪儿来——虽然这些我们还可以另想法子查。可一个人出门在外，身上连一点能证明身份的线索都没有，这可不常见。除了这几件亚麻衬衣、您瞧、是在墨尔本买的，我们对他就一无所知了。然而他身上先前必定带着文件和钱。您见过他的钱吗，太太？”他忽然转向沃尔特斯太太，问道。“他当着您的面掏过钱包没有？”

“见过，”老板娘立刻答道。“他上楼安顿好之后，到酒吧喝了一杯。付账时掏出一把金币——满满一把。怎么也得有三四十枚索维林和半索维林金币。”

“可他被发现的时候，身上连一枚便士都没有，”拉思伯里低声道。

“我还留意到一桩，”老板娘说。“他戴着一块上好的金表和金链子，左手上还戴着枚讲究的戒指——在小指上——金的，镶着一颗大钻石。”

===Original===
“Yes,” said the detective, thoughtfully, “I noticed that he’d worn a ring, and that it had been a bit tight for him. Well﻿—now there’s only one thing to ask about. Did your chambermaid notice if he left any torn paper around﻿—tore any letters up, or anything like that?”

But the chambermaid, produced, had not noticed anything of the sort; on the contrary, the gentleman of Number 20 had left his room very tidy indeed. So Rathbury intimated that he had no more to ask, and nothing further to say, just then, and he bade the landlord and landlady of the Anglo-Orient Hotel good morning, and went away, followed by the two young men.

“What next?” asked Spargo, as they gained the street.

“The next thing,” answered Rathbury, “is to find the man with whom Marbury left this hotel last night.”
===Chinese===
“是了，”警探若有所思地说，“我注意到他戴过戒指，而且那戒指戴着有点紧。好——现在只剩一桩要问了。你们的女服务员留意过没有，他有没有留下撕碎的纸片——撕碎过信件，或是这类的事情？”

可唤来一问，那女服务员却什么也没留意到；恰恰相反，20号那位先生把房间收拾得十分整齐。于是拉思伯里表示，他再没有别的要问，眼下也没有别的话说，便向盎格鲁-东方旅馆（Anglo-Orient Hotel）的老板和老板娘道了早安，径自离去，两个年轻人跟在后面。

“接下来呢？”走上大街，斯帕戈问道。

“接下来，”拉思伯里答道，“就是找到昨夜马伯里离开这家旅馆时同行的那个男人。”

===Original===
“And how’s that to be done?” asked Spargo.

“At present,” replied Rathbury, “I don’t know.”

And with a careless nod, he walked off, apparently desirous of being alone.
===Chinese===
“是了，”警探若有所思地说，“我注意到他戴过戒指，而且那戒指戴着有点紧。好——现在只剩一桩要问了。你们的女服务员留意过没有，他有没有留下撕碎的纸片——撕碎过信件，或是这类的事情？”

可唤来一问，那女服务员却什么也没留意到；恰恰相反，20号那位先生把房间收拾得十分整齐。于是拉思伯里表示，他再没有别的要问，眼下也没有别的话说，便向盎格鲁-东方旅馆（Anglo-Orient Hotel）的老板和老板娘道了早安，径自离去，两个年轻人跟在后面。

“接下来呢？”走上大街，斯帕戈问道。

“接下来，”拉思伯里答道，“就是找到昨夜马伯里离开这家旅馆时同行的那个男人。”

“那可怎么着手？”斯帕戈问。

“眼下，”拉思伯里答道，“我也不知道。”

说罢他漫不经心地点点头，自顾走了，像是不愿有人作伴。

## XXII The Blank Past / 第二十二章 空白的过去

===Original===

Jessie Aylmore came forward to meet Spargo with ready confidence; the elder girl hung back diffidently.

“May we speak to you?” said Jessie. “We have come on purpose to speak to you. Evelyn didn’t want to come, but I made her come.”

Spargo shook hands silently with Evelyn Aylmore and motioned them both to follow him. He took them straight upstairs to his room and bestowed them in his easiest chairs before he addressed them.

“I’ve only just got back to town,” he said abruptly. “I was sorry to hear the news about your father. That’s what’s brought you here, of course. But﻿—I’m afraid I can’t do much.”

===Chinese===

杰西·艾尔莫尔落落大方地迎上前来招呼斯帕戈；姐姐却怯生生地退在后面。

“我们可以跟您谈谈吗？”杰西说，“我们是特地来跟您谈的。伊夫琳本不想来，是我硬拉她来的。”

斯帕戈默然同伊夫琳·艾尔莫尔握了握手，示意两人跟他走。他把她们径直带上楼进了自己的房间，让她们在两把最舒适的椅子里坐定，这才开口说话。

“我刚刚回到城里，”他唐突地说，“令尊的事我听说了，很遗憾。你们自然是为这个来的。不过——恐怕我帮不上什么忙。”

===Original===

“I told you that we had no right to trouble Mr. Spargo, Jessie,” said Evelyn Aylmore. “What can he do to help us?”

Jessie shook her head impatiently.

“The *Watchman*’s about the most powerful paper in London, isn’t it?” she said. “And isn’t Mr. Spargo writing all these articles about the Marbury case? Mr. Spargo, you must help us!”

Spargo sat down at his desk and began turning over the letters and papers which had accumulated during his absence.

===Chinese===

“我早说过，我们无端去打扰斯帕戈先生是不对的，杰西，”伊夫琳·艾尔莫尔说，“他能帮我们什么呢？”

杰西不耐烦地摇了摇头。

“《守望报》差不多是伦敦最有势力的报纸，不是吗？”她说，“这些关于马伯里案的文章，不都是斯帕戈先生写的吗？斯帕戈先生，您一定要帮帮我们！”

斯帕戈在书桌前坐下，动手翻阅他不在期间积攒下来的信件和文稿。

===Original===

“To be absolutely frank with you,” he said, presently, “I don’t see how anybody’s going to help, so long as your father keeps up that mystery about the past.”

“That,” said Evelyn, quietly, “is exactly what Ronald says, Jessie. But we can’t make our father speak, Mr. Spargo. That he is as innocent as we are of this terrible crime we are certain, and we don’t know why he wouldn’t answer the questions put to him at the inquest. And﻿—we know no more than you know or anyone knows, and though I have begged my father to speak, he won’t say a word. We saw his danger: Ronald﻿—Mr. Breton﻿—told us, and we implored him to tell everything he knew about Mr. Marbury. But so far he has simply laughed at the idea that he had anything to do with the murder, or could be arrested for it, and now﻿—”

“And now he’s locked up,” said Spargo in his usual matter-of-fact fashion. “Well, there are people who have to be saved from themselves, you know. Perhaps you’ll have to save your father from the consequences of his own﻿—shall we say obstinacy? Now, look here, between ourselves, how much do you know about your father’s﻿—past?”

The two sisters looked at each other and then at Spargo.

===Chinese===

“跟你们实话实说吧，”他过了一会儿说道，“只要令尊还对过去的事遮遮掩掩，我看谁都帮不上忙。”

“这话，”伊夫琳平静地说，“正是罗纳德说的，杰西。可是我们没法让父亲开口，斯帕戈先生。我们确信，对这桩可怕的罪案，他跟我们一样清白无辜；我们不明白，他为什么不肯回答验尸审讯上问他的那些问题。我们——我们所知道的，不比您或任何外人知道得多；我恳求过父亲把话说出来，他就是一个字也不肯讲。我们看出他处境危险了：是罗纳德——布雷顿先生——告诉我们的，我们还苦苦哀求他把知道的关于马伯里先生的一切都讲出来。可他至今只当这是笑话，压根不信自己跟这桩凶案有什么牵连，更不信会因此被捕，可现在——”

“可现在他已经被关起来了，”斯帕戈用他惯常的务实口吻说道，“唉，要知道，有些人非得靠别人把他们从自己手里救出来不可。也许你们就得把令尊从他自己的——咱们就说是固执吧——后果里救出来。好吧，听着，咱们私下说说，关于令尊的——过去，你们知道多少？”

姐妹俩面面相觑，又看看斯帕戈。

===Original===

“Nothing,” said the elder.

“Absolutely nothing!” said the younger.

“Answer a few plain questions,” said Spargo. “I’m not going to print your replies, nor make use of them in any way: I’m only asking the questions with a desire to help you. Have you any relations in England?”

“None that we know of,” replied Evelyn.

“Nobody you could go to for information about the past?” asked Spargo.

“No﻿—nobody!”

===Chinese===

“一无所知，”姐姐说。

“彻底一无所知！”妹妹说。

“回答我几个直截了当的问题，”斯帕戈说，“你们的回答我不会拿去见报，也不会以任何方式加以利用：我问这些问题，只是想帮你们。你们在英格兰有亲戚吗？”

“就我们所知，一个也没有，”伊夫琳答道。

“没有一个可以打听过去情况的人？”斯帕戈问。

“没有——一个也没有！”

===Original===

Spargo drummed his fingers on his blotting-pad. He was thinking hard.

“How old is your father?” he asked suddenly.

“He was fifty-nine a few weeks ago,” answered Evelyn.

“And how old are you, and how old is your sister?” demanded Spargo.

“I am twenty, and Jessie is nearly nineteen.”

“Where were you born?”

===Chinese===

斯帕戈的手指在吸墨纸上敲个不停。他正在苦苦思索。

“令尊多大年纪？”他突然问道。

“几个星期前他刚满五十九岁，”伊夫琳答道。

“那你们俩多大？你妹妹多大？”斯帕戈追问。

“我二十岁，杰西快十九了。”

“你们生在哪儿？”

===Original===

“Both of us at San Gregorio, which is in the San José province of Argentina, north of Monte Video.”

“Your father was in business there?”

“He was in business in the export trade, Mr. Spargo. There’s no secret about that. He exported all sorts of things to England and to France﻿—skins, hides, wools, dried salts, fruit. That’s how he made his money.”

“You don’t know how long he’d been there when you were born?”

“No.”

“Was he married when he went out there?”

===Chinese===

“我们俩都生在圣格雷戈里奥，在阿根廷的圣何塞省，蒙得维的亚以北。”

“令尊是在那边经商？”

“他做的是出口买卖，斯帕戈先生。这没有什么秘密。他向英格兰和法国出口各种货物——皮张、皮革、羊毛、干盐、水果。他的钱就是这么挣来的。”

“你们出生时他在那边已经待了多久，这个你们不知道？”

“不知道。”

“他出国的时候成过家吗？”

===Original===

“No, he wasn’t. We do know that. He’s told us the circumstances of his marriage, because they were romantic. When he sailed from England to Buenos Aires, he met on the steamer a young lady who, he said, was like himself, relationless and nearly friendless. She was going out to Argentina as a governess. She and my father fell in love with each other, and they were married in Buenos Aires soon after the steamer arrived.”

“And your mother is dead?”

“My mother died before we came to England. I was eight years old, and Jessie six, then.”

“And you came to England﻿—how long after that?”

“Two years.”

===Chinese===

“没有。这个我们确实知道。他把结婚的经过讲给我们听过，因为那段经历很浪漫。他乘船从英格兰去布宜诺斯艾利斯的时候，在轮船上遇到一位年轻小姐——他说，她跟自己一样，无亲无故，几乎也没有朋友。她是去阿根廷当家庭教师的。她和我父亲彼此相爱，船到布宜诺斯艾利斯后不久，两人就成了亲。”

“那你们的母亲不在了？”

“我们母亲在我们来英格兰之前就去世了。那时我八岁，杰西六岁。”

“那你们来英格兰——是在那之后多久？”

“两年。”

===Original===

“So that you’ve been in England ten years. And you know nothing whatever of your father’s past beyond what you’ve told me?”

“Nothing﻿—absolutely nothing.”

“Never heard him talk of﻿—you see, according to your account, your father was a man of getting on to forty when he went out to Argentina. He must have had a career of some sort in this country. Have you never heard him speak of his boyhood? Did he never talk of old times, or that sort of thing?”

“I never remember hearing my father speak of any period antecedent to his marriage,” replied Evelyn.

“I once asked him a question about his childhood,” said Jessie. “He answered that his early days had not been very happy ones, and that he had done his best to forget them. So I never asked him anything again.”

“So that it really comes to this,” remarked Spargo. “You know nothing whatever about your father, his family, his fortunes, his life, beyond what you yourselves have observed since you were able to observe? That’s about it, isn’t it?”

===Chinese===

“这么说，你们来英格兰已经十年了。除了方才告诉我的这些，关于令尊的过去，你们就一无所知了？”

“一无所知——彻底一无所知。”

“从没听他谈起过——你们想想，照你们的说法，令尊去阿根廷的时候已经年近四十。他在这个国家必定有过一番经历。你们从没听他讲过自己的少年时代？他从没谈起过往昔岁月，或者诸如此类的事情？”

“我不记得听父亲谈起过他结婚之前的任何时期，”伊夫琳答道。

“我有一回问过他一个关于童年时代的问题，”杰西说，“他回答说，他的早年过得不大快活，他一直尽力把它们忘掉。所以我后来再也没问过他什么。”

“这么说，事情实际上就是这样，”斯帕戈评说道，“关于令尊——他的家世、他的财产、他的一生——除了你们自己懂事以来亲眼所见的一切，你们什么都不知道？差不多是这样吧？”

===Original===

“I should say that that is exactly it,” answered Evelyn.

“Just so,” said Spargo. “And therefore, as I told your sister the other day, the public will say that your father has some dark secret behind him, and that Marbury had possession of it, and that your father killed him in order to silence him. That isn’t my view. I not only believe your father to be absolutely innocent, but I believe that he knows no more than a child unborn of Marbury’s murder, and I’m doing my best to find out who that murderer was. By the by, since you’ll see all about it in tomorrow morning’s *Watchman*, I may as well tell you that I’ve found out who Marbury really was. He﻿—”

At this moment Spargo’s door was opened and in walked Ronald Breton. He shook his head at sight of the two sisters.

===Chinese===

“我应该说，正是这么回事，”伊夫琳答道。

“正是如此，”斯帕戈说，“因此，正如前几天我对你姐姐说的那样，公众会认定令尊背后藏着什么见不得人的秘密，认定马伯里掌握了这个秘密，认定令尊为了叫他永远闭口才杀了他。这不是我的看法。我不仅相信令尊绝对清白，还相信对于马伯里被杀一事，他知道得不比一个尚未出世的胎儿多；而且我正在竭尽全力，查出那真凶究竟是谁。对了，反正明天一早的《守望报》上你们都会看到，我不妨现在就告诉你们：我已经查明了马伯里的真实身份。他——”

就在这时，斯帕戈的房门开了，罗纳德·布雷顿走了进来。一见这姐妹俩，他便摇了摇头。

===Original===

“I thought I should find you here,” he said. “Jessie said she was coming to see you, Spargo. I don’t know what good you can do﻿—I don’t see what good the most powerful newspaper in the world can do. My God!﻿—everything’s about as black as ever it can be. Mr. Aylmore﻿—I’ve just come away from him; his solicitor, Stratton, and I have been with him for an hour﻿—is obstinate as ever﻿—he will not tell more than he has told. Whatever good can you do, Spargo, when he won’t speak about that knowledge of Marbury which he must have?”

“Oh, well!” said Spargo. “Perhaps we can give him some information about Marbury. Mr. Aylmore has forgotten that it’s not such a difficult thing to rake up the past as he seems to think it is. For example, as I was just telling these young ladies, I myself have discovered who Marbury really was.”

Breton started.

===Chinese===

“我就知道会在这儿找到你们，”他说，“杰西说她要来找你，斯帕戈。我不知道你能帮上什么忙——我看，就连全世界最有势力的报纸又能帮上什么。天哪！——事情已经黑得不能再黑了。艾尔莫尔先生——我刚从他那儿过来；他的事务律师斯特拉顿和我陪了他一个钟头——还是一如既往地固执——他决不肯比已经说过的再多讲一个字。斯帕戈，他明明肯定掌握着关于马伯里的情况却死不开口，你还能帮上什么忙呢？”

“哦，那可未必！”斯帕戈说，“说不定我们倒能给他提供一些关于马伯里的情况呢。艾尔莫尔先生忘了，把过去翻出来这件事，并不像他想的那样难。比方说，我刚才正跟这两位小姐说，我自己就已经查明了马伯里的真实身份。”

布雷顿猛地一怔。

===Original===

“You have? Without doubt?” he exclaimed.

“Without reasonable doubt. Marbury was an ex-convict.”

Spargo watched the effect of this sudden announcement. The two girls showed no sign of astonishment or of unusual curiosity; they received the news with as much unconcern as if Spargo had told them that Marbury was a famous musician. But Ronald Breton started, and it seemed to Spargo that he saw a sense of suspicion dawn in his eyes.

“Marbury﻿—an ex-convict!” he exclaimed. “You mean that?”

“Read your *Watchman* in the morning,” said Spargo. “You’ll find the whole story there﻿—I’m going to write it tonight when you people have gone. It’ll make good reading.”

Evelyn and Jessie Aylmore took Spargo’s hint and went away, Spargo seeing them to the door with another assurance of his belief in their father’s innocence and his determination to hunt down the real criminal. Ronald Breton went down with them to the street and saw them into a cab, but in another minute he was back in Spargo’s room as Spargo had expected. He shut the door carefully behind him and turned to Spargo with an eager face.

===Chinese===

“你查出来了？确定无疑？”他叫道。

“确凿无疑。马伯里是个坐过牢的人。”

斯帕戈注视着这句突如其来的宣告所产生的效果。两位姑娘既无惊讶之色，也没有异样的好奇；她们听到这个消息时满不在乎，仿佛斯帕戈告诉她们的只是马伯里是位知名音乐家。倒是罗纳德·布雷顿吃了一惊，而且在斯帕戈看来，他眼中似乎掠过了一丝疑忌之色。

“马伯里——坐过牢的人！”他叫道，“你说的是真的？”

“明天早上看你的《守望报》吧，”斯帕戈说，“来龙去脉都在上面——等你们各位走了，我今夜就把它写出来。会是很精彩的一篇报道。”

伊夫琳和杰西·艾尔莫尔听出了斯帕戈的逐客之意，起身告辞；斯帕戈送她们到门口，再次申明他相信她们的父亲清白无辜，并且决心追查出真正的罪犯。罗纳德·布雷顿陪她们下楼到街上，目送她们上了马车，可是不出一分钟，正如斯帕戈所料，他又回到了斯帕戈的房间里。他小心地随手关上身后的门，一脸急切地转向斯帕戈。

===Original===

“I say, Spargo, is that really so?” he asked. “About Marbury being an ex-convict?”

“That’s so, Breton. I’ve no more doubt about it than I have that I see you. Marbury was in reality one John Maitland, a bank manager, of Market Milcaster, who got ten years’ penal servitude in 1891 for embezzlement.”

“In 1891? Why﻿—that’s just about the time that Aylmore says he knew him!”

“Exactly. And﻿—it just strikes me,” said Spargo, sitting down at his desk and making a hurried note, “it just strikes me﻿—didn’t Aylmore say he knew Marbury in London?”

===Chinese===

“喂，斯帕戈，真是这样吗？”他问道，“说马伯里是个坐过牢的人这件事？”

“就是这样，布雷顿。对此我深信不疑，就像我确信眼前看见的是你一样。马伯里实际上是约翰·梅特兰，马基特·米尔卡斯特一家银行的经理，1891 年因挪用公款被判处十年苦役徒刑。”

“1891 年？咦——那不正是艾尔莫尔说自己认识他的那段时间吗！”

“一点不错。而且——我忽然想到，”斯帕戈说着，在书桌前坐下，匆匆记了一笔，“我忽然想到——艾尔莫尔不是说过，他是在伦敦认识马伯里的吗？”

===Original===

“Certainly,” replied Breton. “In London.”

“Um!” mused Spargo. “That’s queer, because Maitland had never been in London up to the time of his going to Dartmoor, whatever he may have done when he came out of Dartmoor, and, of course, Aylmore had gone to South America long before that. Look here, Breton,” he continued, aloud, “have you access to Aylmore? Will you, can you, see him before he’s brought up at Bow Street tomorrow?”

“Yes,” answered Breton. “I can see him with his solicitor.”

===Chinese===

“当然是伦敦，”布雷顿答道，“在伦敦。”

“唔！”斯帕戈沉吟道，“这可就怪了，因为直到去达特穆尔服刑之前，梅特兰从来没到过伦敦——至于他从达特穆尔出来之后做过什么，另当别论——而艾尔莫尔呢，当然，早在那之前很久就去南美洲了。听我说，布雷顿，”他接着扬声说道，“你能见到艾尔莫尔吗？你肯不肯、能不能在明天他被解上弓街法庭之前见他一面？”

“能，”布雷顿答道，“我可以和他的事务律师一同见他。”

===Original===

“Then listen,” said Spargo. “Tomorrow morning you’ll find the whole story of how I proved Marbury’s identity with Maitland in the *Watchman*. Read it as early as you can; get an interview with Aylmore as early as you can; make him read it, every word, before he’s brought up. Beg him if he values his own safety and his daughters’ peace of mind to throw away all that foolish reserve, and to tell all he knows about Maitland twenty years ago. He should have done that at first. Why, I was asking his daughters some questions before you came in﻿—they know absolutely nothing of their father’s history previous to the time when they began to understand things! Don’t you see that Aylmore’s career, previous to his return to England, is a blank past!”

“I know﻿—I know!” said Breton. “Yes﻿—although I’ve gone there a great deal, I never heard Aylmore speak of anything earlier than his Argentine experiences. And yet, he must have been getting on when he went out there.”

“Thirty-seven or eight, at least,” remarked Spargo. “Well, Aylmore’s more or less of a public man, and no public man can keep his life hidden nowadays. By the by, how did you get to know the Aylmores?”

===Chinese===

“那好，你听着，”斯帕戈说，“明天早上的《守望报》上，你会看到我如何证实马伯里就是梅特兰的全过程。尽早去读它；尽早设法见到艾尔莫尔；在他被解上法庭之前，让他一字不落地读完。恳求他——倘若他还珍惜自己的安危、珍惜女儿们心中的安宁——把那一套愚蠢的缄默统统抛开，把二十年前关于梅特兰他所知道的一切统统讲出来。他一开始就该这么做。咳，你进来之前，我正问他的女儿们一些问题——对于她们开始懂事之前父亲的历史，她们竟一无所知！你难道不明白吗，艾尔莫尔回英格兰之前的经历，是一段空白的过去！”

“我知道——我知道！”布雷顿说，“是的——尽管我常去他们家，却从没听艾尔莫尔谈起过比他的阿根廷经历更早的任何事情。可是，他出国的时候年纪肯定不小了。”

“至少三十七八岁，”斯帕戈说，“嗯，艾尔莫尔多少算个公众人物，如今没有哪个公众人物能把自己的一生遮得严严实实。对了，你是怎么结识艾尔莫尔一家的？”

===Original===

“My guardian, Mr. Elphick, and I met them in Switzerland,” answered Breton. “We kept up the acquaintance after our return.”

“Mr. Elphick still interesting himself in the Marbury case?” asked Spargo.

“Very much so. And so is old Cardlestone, at the foot of whose stairs the thing came off. I dined with them last night and they talked of little else,” said Breton.

“And their theory﻿—”

“Oh, still the murder for the sake of robbery!” replied Breton. “Old Cardlestone is furious that such a thing could have happened at his very door. He says that there ought to be a thorough enquiry into every tenant of the Temple.”

“Longish business that,” observed Spargo. “Well, run away now, Breton﻿—I must write.”

===Chinese===

“我的监护人埃尔菲克先生和我在瑞士结识了他们，”布雷顿答道，“回国之后我们一直保持来往。”

“埃尔菲克先生还在关心马伯里案吗？”斯帕戈问。

“非常关心。老卡德尔斯顿也一样，那件事就出在他的楼梯脚下。昨晚我同他们一起吃饭，他们谈的几乎全是这件事，”布雷顿说。

“那他们的看法是——”

“哦，还是谋财害命那一套！”布雷顿答道，“老卡德尔斯顿大为恼火，这种事居然就出在他自家门口。他说，应该把律师学院里的每个房客都彻底查一遍。”

“那可是个旷日持久的差事，”斯帕戈说，“好了，快走吧，布雷顿——我得写稿子了。”

===Original===

“Shall you be at Bow Street tomorrow morning?” asked Breton as he moved to the door. “It’s to be at ten-thirty.”

“No, I shan’t!” replied Spargo. “It’ll only be a remand, and I know already just as much as I should hear there. I’ve got something much more important to do. But you’ll remember what I asked of you﻿—get Aylmore to read my story in the *Watchman*, and beg him to speak out and tell all he knows﻿—all!”

And when Breton had gone, Spargo again murmured those last words: “All he knows﻿—all!”

===Chinese===

“明天上午你去弓街吗？”布雷顿朝门口走去时问道，“定的是十点半。”

“不，我不去！”斯帕戈答道，“那不过是还押提审，在那里会听到的东西我早已一清二楚。我有重要得多的事情要做。不过你要记住我拜托你的事——让艾尔莫尔读我在《守望报》上的报道，求他坦率直言，把知道的一切都讲出来——全部讲出来！”

布雷顿走后，斯帕戈又低声念叨起那最后几个字：“他所知道的一切——全部！”

## XIX The Chamberlayne Story / 第十九章 张伯莱恩的故事

===Original===

“I perceive, sir,” said Mr. Quarterpage, as Spargo entered the library, “that you have read the account of the Maitland trial.”

“Twice,” replied Spargo.

“And you have come to the conclusion that—but what conclusion have you come to?” asked Mr. Quarterpage.

“That the silver ticket in my purse was Maitland’s property,” said Spargo, who was not going to give all his conclusions at once.

“Just so,” agreed the old gentleman. “I think so—I can’t think anything else. But I was under the impression that I could have accounted for that ticket, just as I am sure I can account for the other forty-nine.”

“Yes—and how?” asked Spargo.

===Chinese===

“我看得出，先生，”斯帕戈（Spargo）走进书房时，夸特佩奇（Quarterpage）先生说道，“您已经读过梅特兰（Maitland）一案的庭审报道了。”

“读了两遍。”斯帕戈答道。

“那么您已经得出了结论，说——您究竟得出了什么结论呢？”夸特佩奇先生问。

“我皮夹里那枚银质票牌，本是梅特兰的东西。”斯帕戈说，他不打算一下子把全部结论都倒出来。

“正是如此。”老绅士表示赞同，“我也这么想——实在想不出别的可能。可我原先的印象是，那枚票牌的去向我本该能交代清楚，就像我敢肯定其余四十九枚我都能交代明白一样。”

“是吗——那怎么个交代法？”斯帕戈问。

===Original===

Mr. Quarterpage turned to a corner cupboard and in silence produced a decanter and two curiously-shaped old wineglasses. He carefully polished the glasses with a cloth which he took from a drawer, and set glasses and decanter on a table in the window, motioning Spargo to take a chair in proximity thereto. He himself pulled up his own elbow-chair.

“We’ll take a glass of my old brown sherry,” he said. “Though I say it as shouldn’t, as the saying goes, I don’t think you could find better brown sherry than that from Land’s End to Berwick-upon-Tweed, Mr. Spargo—no, nor further north either, where they used to have good taste in liquor in my young days! Well, here’s your good health, sir, and I’ll tell you about Maitland.”

“I’m curious,” said Spargo. “And about more than Maitland. I want to know about a lot of things arising out of that newspaper report. I want to know something about the man referred to so much—the stockbroker, Chamberlayne.”

===Chinese===

夸特佩奇先生转身走向墙角的小橱，一言不发地取出一瓶酒和两只形状古雅的老酒杯。他从抽屉里拿出一块布，细心地把杯子擦拭干净，然后将杯子和酒瓶安放在窗边一张桌上，示意斯帕戈在近旁落座，自己则拖过了他那把扶手椅。

“咱们来喝一杯我的陈年棕雪利。”他说，“照老话讲，自夸总归不该，话虽如此，依我看，从兰兹角（Land’s End）到特威德河畔贝里克（Berwick-upon-Tweed），您再找不出更好的棕雪利了，斯帕戈先生——再往北去也找不出，我年轻那会儿，北方人对酒还是很有讲究的！来，祝您康健，先生，我这就给您讲讲梅特兰的事。”

“我正好奇着呢。”斯帕戈说，“好奇的还不止梅特兰一个人。这份报纸的报道里引出的一大堆事，我都想知道。我还想了解那个被反复提到的人——股票经纪人张伯莱恩（Chamberlayne）。”

===Original===

“Just so,” observed Mr. Quarterpage, smiling. “I thought that would touch your sense of the inquisitive. But Maitland first. Now, when Maitland went to prison, he left behind him a child, a boy, just then about two years old. The child’s mother was dead. Her sister, a Miss Baylis, appeared on the scene—Maitland had married his wife from a distance—and took possession of the child and of Maitland’s personal effects. He had been made bankrupt while he was awaiting his trial, and all his household goods were sold. But this Miss Baylis took some small personal things, and I always believed that she took the silver ticket. And she may have done, for anything I know to the contrary. Anyway, she took the child away, and there was an end of the Maitland family in Market Milcaster. Maitland, of course, was in due procedure of things removed to Dartmoor, and there he served his term. There were people who were very anxious to get hold of him when he came out—the bank people, for they believed that he knew more about the disposition of that money than he’d ever told, and they wanted to induce him to tell what they hoped he knew—between ourselves, Mr. Spargo, they were going to make it worth his while to tell.”

Spargo tapped the newspaper, which he had retained while the old gentleman talked.

“Then they didn’t believe what his counsel said—that Chamberlayne got all the money?” he asked.

Mr. Quarterpage laughed.

===Chinese===

“正是。”夸特佩奇先生含笑说道，“我就知道这准会勾动您那爱刨根问底的天性。不过先说梅特兰。喏，梅特兰入狱时，留下一个孩子，是个男孩，那时才两岁上下。孩子的母亲已经去世，她的姐姐——贝利斯（Baylis）小姐——随之露面——梅特兰的妻子本是外地娶来的——这位姨妈便接管了孩子和梅特兰的随身财物。他在候审期间已被宣告破产，全部家什都被变卖了，但这位贝利斯小姐取走了一些细小的私人物品，而我一直相信，那枚银质票牌就是她拿走的。就我所知，也没有任何相反的凭据。总之，她把孩子带走了，梅特兰一家人在马基特·米尔卡斯特（Market Milcaster）从此绝迹。至于梅特兰，当然，照例行的程序被转到达特穆尔（Dartmoor），在那儿服满了刑期。有一班人急等着他出狱——银行方面的人，因为他们相信，那笔钱的下落他知道得远比他招认的多，他们想诱他说出他们指望他知道的那些事——咱们私下讲，斯帕戈先生，他们原打算让他觉得开口大有好处呢。”

斯帕戈拍了拍手里的报纸——老绅士讲话的当儿，他一直把它拿在手中。
