“这么说，他们并不相信他的辩护律师所讲的话——说钱全落进了张伯莱恩手里？”他问。

夸特佩奇先生笑了。

===Original===

“No—nor anybody else!” he answered. “There was a strong idea in the town—you’ll see why afterwards—that it was all a put-up job, and that Maitland cheerfully underwent his punishment knowing that there was a nice fortune waiting for him when he came out. And as I say, the bank people meant to get hold of him. But though they sent a special agent to meet him on his release, they never did get hold of him. Some mistake arose—when Maitland was released, he got clear away. Nobody’s ever heard a word of him from that day to this. Unless Miss Baylis has.”

“Where does this Miss Baylis live?” asked Spargo.

“Well, I don’t know,” replied Mr. Quarterpage. “She did live in Brighton when she took the child away, and her address was known, and I have it somewhere. But when the bank people sought her out after Maitland’s release, she, too, had clean disappeared, and all efforts to trace her failed. In fact, according to the folks who lived near her in Brighton, she’d completely disappeared, with the child, five years before. So there wasn’t a clue to Maitland. He served his time—made a model prisoner—they did find that much out!—earned the maximum remission, was released, and vanished. And for that very reason there’s a theory about him in this very town to this very day!”

“What?” asked Spargo.

“This. That he’s now living comfortably, luxuriously abroad on what he got from the bank,” replied Mr. Quarterpage. “They say that the sister-in-law was in at the game; that when she disappeared with the child, she went abroad somewhere and made a home ready for Maitland, and that he went off to them as soon as he came out. Do you see?”

===Chinese===

“不信——别人也全不信！”他答道，“镇上倒有一种很强烈的看法——过后您就知道缘故了——认为整件事是一出串通好的把戏，梅特兰是欢欢喜喜领受惩罚的，因为他知道出狱那天有一笔可观的家财等着他。正像我方才说的，银行方面一心要抓到他。可是，虽然他们派了一名专差去迎他出狱，却始终没能找到他。中间不知哪里出了岔子——梅特兰一获释，便走得无影无踪。从那天起到今天，谁也没再听到过他半个字的音信。除非贝利斯小姐听到过。”

“这位贝利斯小姐住在哪儿？”斯帕戈问。

“这个嘛，我不知道。”夸特佩奇先生答道，“她带走孩子那阵子确实住在布莱顿（Brighton），当时她的地址是清楚的，我这儿什么地方还记着呢。可是梅特兰获释之后，银行的人去寻她，她也已消失得干干净净，一切查访都落了空。其实，据布莱顿一带住在她左近的人说，她带着孩子，早在五年前就彻底不见了。于是梅特兰便无线索可寻。他服满了刑——做了个模范囚犯——这一点他们倒是打听出来了！——挣得了最大限度的减刑，一获释，人便无影无踪。也正因如此，直到今天，就在这个镇上，还流传着一套关于他的说法！”

“什么说法？”斯帕戈问。

“就是说他如今正在国外，靠着从银行弄到手的那笔钱，过着舒坦奢侈的日子。”夸特佩奇先生答道，“他们说，他那位大姨子也是局中之人；说她带着孩子消失的时候，是到国外什么地方去了，替梅特兰安顿好一个家；说他一出狱就径直投奔她们去了。您明白了么？”

===Original===

“I suppose that was possible,” said Spargo.

“Quite possible, sir. But now,” continued the old gentleman, replenishing the glasses, “now we come on to the Chamberlayne story. It’s a good deal more to do with the Maitland story than appears at first sight, I’ll tell it to you and you can form your own conclusions. Chamberlayne was a man who came to Market Milcaster—I don’t know from where—in 1886—five years before the Maitland smash-up. He was then about Maitland’s age—a man of thirty-seven or eight. He came as clerk to old Mr. Vallas, the rope and twine manufacturer: Vallas’s place is still there, at the bottom of the High Street, near the river, though old Vallas is dead. He was a smart, cute, pushing chap, this Chamberlayne; he made himself indispensable to old Vallas, and old Vallas paid him a rare good salary. He settled down in the town, and he married a town girl, one of the Corkindales, the saddlers, when he’d been here three years. Unfortunately she died in childbirth within a year of their marriage. It was very soon after that that Chamberlayne threw up his post at Vallas’s, and started business as a stock-and-share broker. He’d been a saving man; he’d got a nice bit of money with his wife; he always let it be known that he had money of his own, and he started in a good way. He was a man of the most plausible manners: he’d have coaxed butter out of a dog’s throat if he’d wanted to. The moneyed men of the town believed in him—I believed in him myself, Mr. Spargo—I’d many a transaction with him, and I never lost aught by him—on the contrary, he did very well for me. He did well for most of his clients—there were, of course, ups and downs, but on the whole he satisfied his clients uncommonly well. But, naturally, nobody ever knew what was going on between him and Maitland.”

“I gather from this report,” said Spargo, “that everything came out suddenly—unexpectedly?”

===Chinese===

“我想这倒有可能。”斯帕戈说。

“大有可能，先生。不过现在，”老绅士说着，给两只杯子续上酒，“现在该讲张伯莱恩的故事了。它同梅特兰这桩案子的干系，比初看上去要深得多，我原原本本讲给您听，结论您自己下。张伯莱恩是1886年来到马基特·米尔卡斯特的——打哪儿来的，我不清楚——那是梅特兰垮台的五年前。他当时同梅特兰年纪相仿——三十七八岁的样子。他来时给制绳商老瓦拉斯（Vallas）先生当职员：瓦拉斯的铺子如今还在高街尽头、河的近旁，虽说老瓦拉斯本人已经过世。这个张伯莱恩是个精明机灵、劲头十足的家伙；他把自己弄得叫老瓦拉斯须臾离他不得，老瓦拉斯也给他一份相当优厚的薪水。他在镇上安顿下来，住满三年后娶了本地一位姑娘——马具商科金代尔（Corkindale）家的一位小姐。不幸的是，婚后不到一年，她便因难产去世了。也就是在那之后没多久，张伯莱恩辞去了瓦拉斯铺子里的差事，自己开业做起了股票证券经纪人。他一向省吃俭用；娶妻时又得了一笔可观的钱财；他还总让人知道自己手头有私财，起点便很不坏。这人有一副最能哄人的好做派：只要他愿意，他连狗喉咙里的黄油都哄得出来。镇上的有钱人都信他——我本人就信过他，斯帕戈先生——我同他打过不知多少回交道，从没在他手上吃过一分亏——恰恰相反，他替我办得极好。他的主顾们大多得了实惠——行情当然有起有落，但总的说来，他把主顾们伺候得极其周到。可是，他和梅特兰之间究竟是怎么回事，自然，谁也不曾知晓。”

“我从这份报道里得到的印象是，”斯帕戈说，“一切都是突然败露的——出乎意料？”

===Original===

“That was so, sir,” replied Mr. Quarterpage. “Sudden? Unexpected? Aye, as a crack of thunder on a fine winter’s day. Nobody had the ghost of a notion that anything was wrong. John Maitland was much respected in the town; much thought of by everybody; well known to everybody. I can assure you, Mr. Spargo, that it was no pleasant thing to have to sit on that grand jury as I did—I was its foreman, sir—and hear a man sentenced that you’d regarded as a bosom friend. But there it was!”

“How was the thing discovered?” asked Spargo, anxious to get at facts.

“In this way,” replied Mr. Quarterpage. “The Market Milcaster Bank is in reality almost entirely the property of two old families in the town, the Gutchbys and the Hostables. Owing to the death of his father, a young Hostable, fresh from college, came into the business. He was a shrewd, keen young fellow; he got some suspicion, somehow, about Maitland, and he insisted on the other partners consenting to a special investigation, and on their making it suddenly. And Maitland was caught before he had a chance. But we’re talking about Chamberlayne.”

===Chinese===

“正是如此，先生。”夸特佩奇先生答道，“突然？出乎意料？可不是嘛，就像晴朗冬日里打了个炸雷。谁都不曾起过半点疑心，觉出有什么不对。约翰·梅特兰（John Maitland）在镇上深受敬重；人人都高看他；人人都熟识他。我可以向您担保，斯帕戈先生，像我那样坐在大陪审团里——我还是陪审长呢，先生——听着一个你素来当作至交的人被宣判，那滋味绝不好受。可事实就是如此！”

“这桩事是怎么败露的？”斯帕戈问，急于摸到实处。

“是这样败露的。”夸特佩奇先生答道，“马基特·米尔卡斯特银行实际上几乎全部是镇上两户老门第的产业——格奇比（Gutchby）家和霍斯塔布尔（Hostable）家。由于父亲去世，霍斯塔布尔家一位刚出大学门的年轻人进了银行当差。他是个精明厉害的年轻人；不知怎么的，他对梅特兰起了疑心，便非要其他合伙人点头同意进行一次特别查账不可，而且要出其不意地进行。于是梅特兰还没来得及有任何补救，就被抓住了。不过咱们这会儿谈的是张伯莱恩。”

===Original===

“Yes, about Chamberlayne,” agreed Spargo.

“Well, now, Maitland was arrested one evening,” continued Mr. Quarterpage. “Of course, the news of his arrest ran through the town like wildfire. Everybody was astonished; he was at that time—aye, and had been for years—a churchwarden at the Parish Church, and I don’t think there could have been more surprise if we’d heard that the Vicar had been arrested for bigamy. In a little town like this, news is all over the place in a few minutes. Of course, Chamberlayne would hear that news like everybody else. But it was remembered, and often remarked upon afterwards, that from the moment of Maitland’s arrest nobody in Market Milcaster ever had speech with Chamberlayne again. After his wife’s death he’d taken to spending an hour or so of an evening across there at the ‘Dragon,’ where you saw me and my friends last night, but on that night he didn’t go to the ‘Dragon.’ And next morning he caught the eight o’clock train to London. He happened to remark to the stationmaster as he got into the train that he expected to be back late that night, and that he should have a tiring day of it. But Chamberlayne didn’t come back that night, Mr. Spargo. He didn’t come back to Market Milcaster for four days, and when he did come back it was in a coffin!”

“Dead?” exclaimed Spargo. “That was sudden!”

===Chinese===

“对，谈张伯莱恩。”斯帕戈表示同意。

“好吧，话说梅特兰是在一天傍晚被捕的。”夸特佩奇先生接着讲，“他被捕的消息当然像野火一样传遍了全镇。人人惊愕不已；他那时——是呀，而且多年来一直是——教区教堂的执事，依我看，即便是听说牧师因重婚罪被抓，人们惊愕的程度也高不过去了。在这么个小镇子上，消息几分钟便传得满街都是。张伯莱恩自然同旁人一样听到了这消息。可是后来大家记得清清楚楚，也常常提起：从梅特兰被捕那一刻起，全马基特·米尔卡斯特再没有一个人同张伯莱恩说过一句话。自他妻子死后，他惯于每晚到对面那家‘龙旅店’——昨儿晚上您看见我和我那几位老朋友的地方——消磨个把钟头，可那天晚上他没去‘龙旅店’。第二天一早，他赶八点钟的火车去了伦敦。上车时他恰巧对站长提了一句，说预计当天深夜返回，还说这一天要奔波劳顿。可是那天夜里张伯莱恩没有回来，斯帕戈先生。他一连四天没有回马基特·米尔卡斯特，而当他回来的时候——是躺在棺材里回来的！”

“死了？”斯帕戈失声道，“这可真突然！”

===Original===

“Very sudden,” agreed Mr. Quarterpage. “Yes, sir, he came back in his coffin, did Chamberlayne. On the very evening on which he’d spoken of being back, there came a telegram here to say that he’d died very suddenly at the Cosmopolitan Hotel. That telegram came to his brother-in-law, Corkindale, the saddler—you’ll find him down the street, opposite the Town Hall. It was sent to Corkindale by a nephew of Chamberlayne’s, another Chamberlayne, Stephen, who lived in London, and was understood to be on the Stock Exchange there. I saw that telegram, Mr. Spargo, and it was a long one. It said that Chamberlayne had had a sudden seizure, and though a doctor had been got to him he’d died shortly afterwards. Now, as Chamberlayne had his nephew and friends in London, his brother-in-law, Tom Corkindale, didn’t feel that there was any necessity for him to go up to town, so he just sent off a wire to Stephen Chamberlayne asking if there was aught he could do. And next morning came another wire from Stephen saying that no inquest would be necessary, as the doctor had been present and able to certify the cause of death, and would Corkindale make all arrangements for the funeral two days later. You see, Chamberlayne had bought a vault in our cemetery when he buried his wife, so naturally they wished to bury him in it, with her.”

Spargo nodded. He was beginning to imagine all sorts of things and theories; he was taking everything in.

“Well,” continued Mr. Quarterpage, “on the second day after that, they brought Chamberlayne’s body down. Three of ’em came with it—Stephen Chamberlayne, the doctor who’d been called in, and a solicitor. Everything was done according to proper form and usage. As Chamberlayne had been well known in the town, a good number of townsfolk met the body at the station and followed it to the cemetery. Of course, many of us who had been clients of Chamberlayne’s were anxious to know how he had come to such a sudden end. According to Stephen Chamberlayne’s account, our Chamberlayne had wired to him and to his solicitor to meet him at the Cosmopolitan to do some business. They were awaiting him there when he arrived, and they had lunch together. After that, they got to their business in a private room. Towards the end of the afternoon, Chamberlayne was taken suddenly ill, and though they got a doctor to him at once, he died before evening. The doctor said he’d a diseased heart. Anyhow, he was able to certify the cause of his death, so there was no inquest and they buried him, as I have told you.”

===Chinese===

“突然得很。”夸特佩奇先生附和道，“是啊，先生，张伯莱恩是躺在棺材里回来的。就在他说过当天返回的那个傍晚，镇上收到一封电报，说他在大都会旅馆（Cosmopolitan Hotel）暴病身亡。电报发给他妻舅——马具商科金代尔——就在街那头，市政厅对面，您找得着他。电报是张伯莱恩的一个侄子拍给科金代尔的，那人也姓张伯莱恩，叫斯蒂芬（Stephen），住在伦敦，据说是伦敦证券交易所里的人。那封电报我亲眼见过，斯帕戈先生，写得挺长。电文说张伯莱恩突发急症，虽然请到了医生，不久之后他还是死了。如今，张伯莱恩在伦敦既然有侄子和朋友，他妻舅汤姆·科金代尔（Tom Corkindale）觉得自己没有必要进城跑一趟，便只给斯蒂芬·张伯莱恩回了一封电报，问有没有什么用得着他的地方。第二天早上，斯蒂芬又来了一封电报，说无需验尸审讯，因为医生当时在场，能够出具死因证明，并请科金代尔料理一切，两天后下葬。您知道，张伯莱恩安葬妻子时，曾在我们墓园里置下一处墓穴，所以他们自然愿意把他葬进那穴里，同她作伴。”

斯帕戈点了点头。他脑子里已经开始设想出种种事由和推断；他把这一切一一收进心里。

“喏，”夸特佩奇先生继续说道，“在那之后的第二天，他们把张伯莱恩的遗体运了下来。随棺来了三个人——斯蒂芬·张伯莱恩、那位被请去的医生，还有一位事务律师。一切都办得合乎规矩礼数。张伯莱恩在镇上素来是有名望的，好些镇民到车站迎灵，随棺一路送到墓园。当然，我们许多当过张伯莱恩主顾的人，都急于知道他怎么落得个如此突然的结局。据斯蒂芬·张伯莱恩的说法，我们这位张伯莱恩事先拍电报要他和他的律师到大都会旅馆去同他办事。他到的时候，两人正候着他，于是三人共进了午餐。之后，他们便进一间专用房间谈正事。临近下午尾声，张伯莱恩突然病倒，虽然他们立刻请来医生，他还是在入夜前死了。医生说他心脏有宿疾。总之，医生能够出具死因证明，因此没有验尸审讯，人便下葬了，就是我方才告诉您的那样。”

===Original===

The old gentleman paused and, taking a sip at his sherry, smiled at some reminiscence which occurred to him.

“Well,” he said, presently going on, “of course, on that came all the Maitland revelations, and Maitland vowed and declared that Chamberlayne had not only had nearly all the money, but that he was absolutely certain that most of it was in his hands in hard cash. But Chamberlayne, Mr. Spargo, had left practically nothing. All that could be traced was about three or four thousand pounds. He’d left everything to his nephew, Stephen. There wasn’t a trace, a clue to the vast sums with which Maitland had entrusted him. And then people began to talk, and they said what some of them say to this very day!”

“What’s that?” asked Spargo.

Mr. Quarterpage leaned forward and tapped his guest on the arm.

“That Chamberlayne never did die, and that that coffin was weighted with lead!” he answered.

===Chinese===

老绅士顿了顿，呷了一口雪利酒，想起什么往事，自顾自地微笑起来。

“喏，”过了片刻他又接着说，“当然，紧跟着便是梅特兰案的全部败露。梅特兰一口咬定，张伯莱恩不但拿走了几乎全部的钱，而且他绝对确信，其中大半当时就在张伯莱恩手里，是现款。可是张伯莱恩呢，斯帕戈先生，身后几乎什么也没留下。能查到的，不过三四千镑。他把一切都留给了侄子斯蒂芬。梅特兰托付给他的那些巨款，没有留下一丝踪迹、一条线索。于是人们开始议论，说出了直到今天还有些人在说的话！”

“说什么？”斯帕戈问。

夸特佩奇先生俯身向前，在客人臂上轻轻一拍。

“说张伯莱恩根本就没有死，说那口棺材里装的是压分量的铅块！”他答道。

## XXIX The Closed Doors / 第二十九章 紧闭的门

===Original===
Alone of all the London morning newspapers, the *Watchman* appeared next day destitute of sensationalism in respect to the Middle Temple Murder. The other daily journals published more or less vivid accounts of the identification of Mr. Stephen Aylmore, M.P. for the Brookminster Division, as the ci-devant Stephen Ainsworth, ex-convict, once upon a time founder and secretary of the Hearth and Home Mutual Benefit Society, the headquarters of which had been at Cloudhampton, in Daleshire; the fall of which had involved thousands of honest working folk in terrible distress if not in absolute ruin. Most of them had raked up Ainsworth’s past to considerable journalistic purpose: it had been an easy matter to turn up old files, to recount the fall of the Hearth and Home, to tell anew the story of the privations of the humble investors whose small hoards had gone in the crash; it had been easy, too, to set out again the history of Ainsworth’s arrest, trial, and fate. There was plenty of romance in the story: it was that of a man who by his financial ability had built up a great industrial insurance society; had﻿—as was alleged﻿—converted the large sums entrusted to him to his own purposes; had been detected and punished; had disappeared, after his punishment, so effectually that no one knew where he had gone; had come back, comparatively a few years later, under another name, a very rich man, and had entered Parliament and been, in a modest way, a public character without any of those who knew him in his new career suspecting that he had once worn a dress liberally ornamented with the broad arrow. Fine copy, excellent copy: some of the morning newspapers made a couple of columns of it.

But the *Watchman*, up to then easily ahead of all its contemporaries in keeping the public informed of all the latest news in connection with the Marbury affair, contented itself with a brief announcement. For after Rathbury had left him, Spargo had sought his proprietor and his editor, and had sat long in consultation with them, and the result of their talk had been that all the *Watchman* thought fit to tell its readers next morning was contained in a curt paragraph:

> “We understand that Mr. Stephen Aylmore, M.P., who is charged with the murder of John Marbury, or Maitland, in the Temple on June 21st last, was yesterday afternoon identified by certain officials as Stephen Ainsworth, who was sentenced to a term of penal servitude in connection with the Hearth and Home Mutual Benefit Society funds nearly thirty years ago.”

===Chinese===
伦敦各家晨报当中，唯独《守望报》第二天关于中殿谋杀案的报道不带半点耸人听闻的气息。其他各家日报则多多少少以生动的笔触报道说：布鲁克敏斯特选区下院议员斯蒂芬·艾尔莫尔先生，已被认出就是当年的斯蒂芬·艾恩斯沃斯——此人有前科、蹲过监牢，早年曾是炉边家园互助会的创办人兼秘书；该会总部设在达尔郡的克劳德汉普顿，其倒闭曾把成千上万诚实的劳工拖入可怕的困顿，即便不至彻底倾家荡产，也已够凄惨了。这些报纸大多把艾恩斯沃斯的旧事翻出来大做文章：翻检旧档案本是轻而易举的事，于是它们重述炉边家园互助会垮台的始末，重新讲述那些小投资者积蓄尽丧后的困苦遭遇；把艾恩斯沃斯被捕、受审直到下场的经过重铺一遍，同样不费吹灰之力。这故事里有的是传奇色彩：一个凭金融手腕缔造出一家大型产业保险会的人；据称——把托付给他的巨款挪作了私用；事情败露，受到惩处；刑满之后消失得如此彻底，谁也不知道他去了哪里；相隔不过几年，他改名换姓、腰缠万贯地归来，进了议会，还低低调调当起了公众人物，而在新生活中结识他的人，没有一个疑心他从前曾穿过一件缀满宽箭标记（英国囚服的记号）的衣裳。好材料，上等的材料：有几家晨报为此写足了整整两栏。

而此前在向公众通报马伯里案各项最新消息方面，《守望报》一直遥遥领先于各家同行，这一次却只满足于发一则简短通告。原来拉思伯里走后，斯帕戈便去寻了报社的老板和主编，同他们长谈良久；这番商谈的结果是，《守望报》第二天早晨认为可以告诉读者的全部内容，尽在下面这段寥寥数语的短讯之中：

> “据悉：因本年六月二十一日在中殿杀害约翰·马伯里（又名梅特兰）而受控的下院议员斯蒂芬·艾尔莫尔先生，昨日下午经若干官员确认，即近三十年前因炉边家园互助会款项一案被判处苦役的斯蒂芬·艾恩斯沃斯。”

===Original===
Coming down to Fleet Street that morning, Spargo, strolling jauntily along the front of the Law Courts, encountered a fellow-journalist, a man on an opposition newspaper, who grinned at him in a fashion which indicated derision.

“Left behind a bit, that rag of yours, this morning, Spargo, my boy!” he remarked elegantly. “Why, you’ve missed one of the finest opportunities I ever heard of in connection with that Aylmore affair. A miserable paragraph!﻿—why, I worked off a column and a half in ours! What were you doing last night, old man?”

“Sleeping,” said Spargo and went by with a nod. “Sleeping!”

===Chinese===
那天早晨斯帕戈来到舰队街，正脚步轻快地踱过皇家法院门前，迎面遇上一位同行——对立阵营一份报纸的记者，冲他咧嘴一笑，那笑法里分明透着讥诮。

“今儿早上你们那份破报落伍喽，斯帕戈老弟！”他的话说得可真够风雅的。“嘿，艾尔莫尔那桩案子，你错过了我平生闻所未闻的好机会之一。可怜巴巴一小段新闻！——嘿，我在我们报上足足写掉了一栏半！你昨晚干什么去了，老兄？”

“睡觉来着。”斯帕戈说，点个头便走过去了。“睡觉！”

===Original===
He left the other staring at him, and crossed the road to Middle Temple Lane. It was just on the stroke of eleven as he walked up the stairs to Mr. Elphick’s chambers; precisely eleven as he knocked at the outer door. It is seldom that outer doors are closed in the Temple at that hour, but Elphick’s door was closed fast enough. The night before it had been promptly opened, but there was no response to Spargo’s first knock, nor to his second, nor to his third. And half-unconsciously he murmured aloud: “Elphick’s door is closed!”

It never occurred to Spargo to knock again: instinct told him that Elphick’s door was closed because Elphick was not there; closed because Elphick was not going to keep the appointment. He turned and walked slowly back along the corridor. And just as he reached the head of the stairs Ronald Breton, pale and anxious, came running up them, and at sight of Spargo paused, staring questioningly at him. As if with a mutual sympathy the two young men shook hands.

“I’m glad you didn’t print more than those two or three lines in the *Watchman* this morning,” said Breton. “It was﻿—considerate. As for the other papers!﻿—Aylmore assured me last night, Spargo, that though he did serve that term at Dartmoor he was innocent enough! He was scapegoat for another man who disappeared.”

Then, as Spargo merely nodded, he added, awkwardly:

===Chinese===
他把对方撂在原地干瞪眼，自己穿过马路，进了中殿巷。他走上楼梯去埃尔菲克先生的寓所时，时钟正敲十一点；他叩响外层房门时，恰是十一点整。在这个钟点，中殿里的外层门难得有关着的，可埃尔菲克的门却关得严严实实。头天晚上这扇门一叩即开，今天却任凭斯帕戈叩了第一遍没人应、第二遍没人应、第三遍还是没人应。他半是不自觉地出了声，喃喃道：“埃尔菲克的门关着！”

斯帕戈压根儿没想再敲下去：本能告诉他，埃尔菲克的门之所以关着，是因为埃尔菲克人不在；之所以关着，是因为埃尔菲克不打算来赴约了。他转身沿走廊缓缓往回走。刚走到楼梯口，罗纳德·布雷顿正脸色发白、神色焦灼地一路跑上楼来，一见斯帕戈便收住脚，用探询的目光盯着他。两个年轻人像是心有灵犀，握了握手。

“今早《守望报》只登了那么两三行，我很高兴，”布雷顿说，“这真是——体贴。至于别的报纸！——斯帕戈，艾尔莫尔昨晚向我担保，他在达特穆尔虽然蹲过那一趟苦役，人却是清白得很！他是替另一个消失无踪的人顶了罪。”

见斯帕戈只点了点头，他又讪讪地补了一句：“还有，也多谢你了，老伙计，昨晚给两位姑娘拍去那封电报——你想得真周到。可怜的人儿，如今什么安慰都用得着！可是——你在这儿做什么，斯帕戈？”

===Original===
“And I’m obliged to you, too, old chap, for sending that wire to the two girls last night﻿—it was good of you. They want all the comfort they can get, poor things! But﻿—what are you doing here, Spargo?”

Spargo leant against the head of the stairs and folded his hands.

“I came here,” he said, “to keep an appointment with Mr. Elphick﻿—an appointment which he made when I called on him, as you suggested, at nine o’clock. The appointment﻿—a most important one﻿—was for eleven o’clock.”

Breton glanced at his watch.

“Come on, then,” he said. “It’s well past that now, and my guardian’s a very martinet in the matter of punctuality.”

But Spargo did not move. Instead, he shook his head, regarding Breton with troubled eyes.

===Chinese===
斯帕戈倚在楼梯口，抱起了双臂。

“我到这儿来，”他说，“是来赴埃尔菲克先生的约——我照你的主意九点钟去拜访他，是他当面定下的。这个约会——要紧得很——定在十一点。”

布雷顿看了一眼表。

“那就走吧，”他说，“现在早过十一点了，我这位监护人在守时上是出了名的一丝不苟。”

可斯帕戈没挪步，反而摇了摇头，用不安的眼光望着布雷顿。

“我也一样，”他答道，“我受的就是这种训练。你的监护人不在，布雷顿。”

===Original===
“So am I,” he answered. “I was trained to it. Your guardian isn’t there, Breton.”

“Not there? If he made an appointment for eleven? Nonsense﻿—I never knew him miss an appointment!”

“I knocked three times﻿—three separate times,” answered Spargo.

“You should have knocked half a dozen times﻿—he may have overslept himself. He sits up late﻿—he and old Cardlestone often sit up half the night, talking stamps or playing piquet,” said Breton. “Come on﻿—you’ll see!”

Spargo shook his head again.

“He’s not there, Breton,” he said. “He’s gone!”

===Chinese===
“不在？定了十一点的约还会不在？胡扯——我从没见他误过一次约！”

“我敲了三遍——实实在在敲了三遍。”斯帕戈答道。

“那你该敲上六遍——他兴许睡过头了。他睡得晚——他跟老卡德尔斯顿常常半宿半宿地熬着，不是谈邮票就是打皮克牌，”布雷顿说，“走吧——你亲眼瞧去！”

斯帕戈又摇了摇头。

“他不在，布雷顿，”他说，“他走了！”

布雷顿直勾勾地瞪着这位记者，仿佛他刚才是说亲眼看见塞普蒂默斯·埃尔菲克先生骑着一头单峰骆驼沿舰队街而下。他一把攥住斯帕戈的胳膊肘。

===Original===
Breton stared at the journalist as if he had just announced that he had seen Mr. Septimus Elphick riding down Fleet Street on a dromedary. He seized Spargo’s elbow.

“Come on!” he said. “I have a key to Mr. Elphick’s door, so that I can go in and out as I like. I’ll soon show you whether he’s gone or not.”

Spargo followed the young barrister down the corridor.

“All the same,” he said meditatively as Breton fitted a key to the latch, “he’s not there, Breton. He’s﻿—off!”

===Chinese===
“走！”他说，“我有埃尔菲克先生这扇门的钥匙，进出向来随心。他走没走，我这就让你看个分明。”

斯帕戈跟着这位年轻的出庭律师沿走廊走去。

“不过，”布雷顿往锁眼上插钥匙时，他若有所思地说，“他不在里面，布雷顿。他——跑啦！”

“老天爷，伙计，我简直不知道你在说些什么！”布雷顿叫道，一边开门走进门厅。“跑啦！他跟你约好十一点，还能往哪儿跑？——咦！”

===Original===
“Good heavens, man, I don’t know what you’re talking about!” exclaimed Breton, opening the door and walking into the lobby. “Off! Where on earth should he be off to, when he’s made an appointment with you for eleven, and﻿—Hullo!”

He had opened the door of the room in which Spargo had met Elphick and Miss Baylis the night before, and was walking in when he pulled himself up on the threshold with a sharp exclamation.

“Good God!” he cried. “What﻿—what’s all this?”

===Chinese===
他推开那间屋子的门——头天晚上斯帕戈就是在那屋里见到埃尔菲克和贝利斯小姐的——正往里走，却在门槛上猛地煞住脚，失声叫了出来。

“天哪！”他喊道，“这——这都是怎么回事？”

斯帕戈从布雷顿肩后静静地望进去。只消飞快一眼他便看清，自他昨夜离开之后，这间安静的屋子里出了大事。安乐椅还立在原处，他昨夜离开时埃尔菲克就坐在里面；紧挨着它的小桌被推在一边，像被一只仓促的手推开的，桌上是酒具匣、苏打水瓶和酒杯，杯里还立着走了气的残酒；那本小说脸朝下扣着；小说上搁着埃尔菲克的烟斗。可屋里其余的地方却乱作一团。写字台的抽屉拉开后就没再推回去；各式各样的文件、样子像旧法律文书的文书、旧信件，撒满了屋子中央的大桌和地板；屋角一只黑漆箱被打开，里面的东西翻得四散，箱盖大敞着口。炉膛里、炉挡上，到处是一堆堆烧过焦透的纸；再明白不过的是：这寓所的主人不管已遁往何处，临走之前曾花费不少工夫销毁掉一大批文件纸张，而且慌急慌忙，连把东西归置整齐都顾不上。

===Original===
Spargo quietly looked over Breton’s shoulder. It needed but one quick glance to show him that much had happened in that quiet room since he had quitted it the night before. There stood the easy-chair in which he had left Elphick; there, close by it, but pushed aside, as if by a hurried hand, was the little table with its spirit case, its syphon, its glass, in which stale liquid still stood; there was the novel, turned face downwards; there, upon the novel, was Elphick’s pipe. But the rest of the room was in dire confusion. The drawers of a bureau had been pulled open and never put back; papers of all descriptions, old legal-looking documents, old letters, littered the centre-table and the floor; in one corner of the room a black japanned box had been opened, its contents strewn about, and the lid left yawning. And in the grate, and all over the fender there were masses of burned and charred paper; it was only too evident that the occupant of the chambers, wherever he might have disappeared to, had spent some time before his disappearance in destroying a considerable heap of documents and papers, and in such haste that he had not troubled to put matters straight before he went.

Breton stared at this scene for a moment in utter consternation. Then he made one step towards an inner door, and Spargo followed him. Together they entered an inner room﻿—a sleeping apartment. There was no one in it, but there were evidences that Elphick had just as hastily packed a bag as he had destroyed his papers. The clothes which Spargo had seen him wearing the previous evening were flung here, there, everywhere: the gorgeous smoking-jacket was tossed unceremoniously in one corner, a dress-shirt, in the bosom of which valuable studs still glistened, in another. One or two suitcases lay about, as if they had been examined and discarded in favour of something more portable; here, too, drawers, revealing stocks of linen and underclothing, had been torn open and left open; open, too, swung the door of a wardrobe, revealing a quantity of expensive clothing. And Spargo, looking around him, seemed to see all that had happened﻿—the hasty, almost frantic search for and tearing up and burning of papers; the hurried change of clothing, of packing necessaries into a bag that could be carried, and then the flight, the getting away, the﻿—

“What on earth does all this mean?” exclaimed Breton. “What is it, Spargo?”

“I mean exactly what I told you,” answered Spargo. “He’s off! Off!”

===Chinese===
布雷顿望着眼前这副光景，一时间惊得目瞪口呆。随后他朝一扇内门跨出一步，斯帕戈跟了上去。两人一同进了里间——一间卧室。屋里没有人，却处处可见埃尔菲克收拾行装时同他烧毁文件一样仓促的痕迹。斯帕戈前一天傍晚见他穿在身上的衣服，被东一件西一件扔得满屋都是：那件华丽的吸烟服被随随便便抛在一个墙角，一件前襟上贵重袖扣犹自闪亮的礼服衬衫扔在另一个墙角。一两口提箱丢在一旁，像是被翻检过又弃置不用，另换了更便于携带的物件；这里的抽屉也同样被扯开就那么敞着，露出一摞摞衬衣和内衣；衣柜的门也开着晃荡，露出一批价钱不菲的衣裳。斯帕戈环顾四周，仿佛亲眼看见了这一切经过——那急切得近乎发狂的翻寻、撕毁和焚烧文件；那匆忙的换装，把必需品塞进一只提得动的提包，然后便是出逃，是远走高飞，是——

“这一切究竟是什么意思？”布雷顿叫道，“到底怎么回事，斯帕戈？”

“就是我方才对你说的那句话，”斯帕戈答道，“他跑啦！跑啦！”

“跑啦！可为什么要跑？什么——我的监护人！——全中殿数得着的安静老先生——跑啦！”布雷顿嚷道，“为的什么，嗯？该不会是——天哪，斯帕戈，该不会是因为你昨晚对他说了什么吧！”

===Original===
“Off! But why off? What﻿—my guardian!﻿—as quiet an old gentleman as there is in the Temple﻿—off!” cried Breton. “For what reason, eh? It isn’t﻿—good God, Spargo, it isn’t because of anything you said to him last night!”

“I should say it is precisely because of something that I said to him last night,” replied Spargo. “I was a fool ever to let him out of my sight.”

Breton turned on his companion and gasped.

“Out﻿—of﻿—your﻿—sight!” he exclaimed. “Why﻿—why﻿—you don’t mean to say that Mr. Elphick has anything to do with this Marbury affair? For God’s sake, Spargo﻿—”

Spargo laid a hand on the young barrister’s shoulder.

===Chinese===
“依我看，恰恰就是因为我昨晚对他说了些什么，”斯帕戈答道，“我真是个傻子，竟会让他在我眼皮底下走脱。”

布雷顿猛地转向同伴，倒抽了一口气。

“离——开——你的——眼皮底下！”他失声道，“什么——什么——你莫不是要说，埃尔菲克先生同这桩马伯里案子有什么牵连？看在上帝分上，斯帕戈——”

斯帕戈把一只手搭在这位年轻出庭律师的肩上。

“恐怕你有一大堆事情非听不可了，布雷顿，”他说，“反正我今天本来就要找你谈的。是这样——”

===Original===
“I’m afraid you’ll have to hear a good deal, Breton,” he said. “I was going to talk to you today in any case. You see﻿—”

Before Spargo could say more a woman, bearing the implements which denote the charwoman’s profession, entered the room and immediately cried out at what she saw. Breton turned on her almost savagely.

“Here, you!” he said. “Have you seen anything of Mr. Elphick this morning?”

The charwoman rolled her eyes and lifted her hands.

===Chinese===
斯帕戈话音未落，一个女人提着清洁女工那一行当的家什走进屋来，一眼看见屋里的光景，当即惊叫出声。布雷顿几乎是凶巴巴地转向她。

“喂，你！”他说，“今儿早上你见过埃尔菲克先生没有？”

女工转了转眼珠，举起双手。

“俺吗，老爷！连他的影儿也没见着，老爷。俺向来不大会儿在十一点半以前到这儿来，老爷，因为那会儿埃尔菲克先生已经出门吃早饭去了。俺昨儿早上倒是见着他来着，老爷，那会儿他身子骨跟平常一样好，老爷——要是他这会儿当真出了什么事的话。没有了，老爷，俺压根儿没瞧见他。”

===Original===
“Me, sir! Not a sign of him, sir. Which I never comes here much before half-past eleven, sir, Mr. Elphick being then gone out to his breakfast. I see him yesterday morning, sir, which he was then in his usual state of good health, sir, if anything’s the matter with him now. No, sir, I ain’t seen nothing of him.”

Breton let out another exclamation of impatience.

“You’d better leave all this,” he said. “Mr. Elphick’s evidently gone away in a hurry, and you mustn’t touch anything here until he comes back. I’m going to lock up the chambers: if you’ve a key of them give it to me.”

The charwoman handed over a key, gave another astonished look at the rooms, and vanished, muttering, and Breton turned to Spargo.

===Chinese===
布雷顿又不耐烦地叫了一声。

“这些你最好都先别动，”他说，“埃尔菲克先生显然走得匆忙，他回来之前，这里的东西你一样也不许碰。我要把寓所锁上：你手里若有这儿的钥匙，交给我。”

女工交出一把钥匙，又把这几间屋子惊愕地打量了一眼，嘴里咕哝着走了。布雷顿转身向着斯帕戈。

“你怎么说？”他追问道，“我必须听——原原本本都听！那就快说吧，伙计，千万看在老天爷的分上。”

===Original===
“What do you say?” he demanded. “I must hear﻿—a good deal! Out with it, then, man, for Heaven’s sake.”

But Spargo shook his head.

“Not now, Breton,” he answered. “Presently, I tell you, for Miss Aylmore’s sake, and your own, the first thing to do is to get on your guardian’s track. We must﻿—must, I say!﻿—and at once.”

Breton stood staring at Spargo for a moment as if he could not credit his own senses. Then he suddenly motioned Spargo out of the room.

“Come on!” he said. “I know who’ll know where he is, if anybody does.”

“Who, then?” asked Spargo, as they hurried out.

===Chinese===
斯帕戈却摇了摇头。

“现在不行，布雷顿，”他答道，“回头再说。我告诉你，为了艾尔莫尔小姐，也为了你自己，眼下头一件大事，是追上你监护人的踪迹。我们必须——我说必须！——刻不容缓。”

布雷顿站在那里，盯着斯帕戈望了片刻，仿佛不敢相信自己的耳目。随即他猛一抬手，示意斯帕戈出屋。

“走！”他说，“要是还有人知道他在哪儿，我知道那一位是谁。”

“谁？”两人匆匆出门时，斯帕戈问道。

“卡德尔斯顿，”布雷顿咬着牙答道，“卡德尔斯顿！”

## III The Clue of the Cap / 第三章 便帽的线索

===Original===
Having no clear conception of what had led him to these scenes of litigation, Spargo went wandering aimlessly about in the great hall and the adjacent corridors until an official, who took him to be lost, asked him if there was any particular part of the building he wanted. For a moment Spargo stared at the man as if he did not comprehend his question. Then his mental powers reasserted themselves.

“Isn’t Mr. Justice Borrow sitting in one of the courts this morning?” he suddenly asked.

“Number seven,” replied the official. “What’s your case﻿—when’s it down?”

“I haven’t got a case,” said Spargo. “I’m a pressman﻿—reporter, you know.”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈（Spargo）并不清楚究竟是什么把自己引到这片对簿公堂之地来的，只管在大厅和四周的走廊里漫无目的地转悠，直到一名官员看他像是迷了路，上来问他是不是要找楼里的哪个去处。斯帕戈盯着那人望了片刻，仿佛没听懂他的问话。随后他的心思才回到正轨。

“博罗法官（Mr. Justice Borrow）今天上午不是有一个庭要开吗？”他突然问道。

“第七法庭，”官员答道。“您的案子是哪桩——排在什么时候？”

“我没有案子，”斯帕戈说。“我是报馆的人——记者，您知道。”

===Original===
The official stuck out a finger.

“Round the corner﻿—first to your right﻿—second on the left,” he said automatically. “You’ll find plenty of room﻿—nothing much doing there this morning.”

He turned away, and Spargo recommenced his apparently aimless perambulation of the dreary, depressing corridors.

“Upon my honour!” he muttered. “Upon my honour, I really don’t know what I’ve come up here for. I’ve no business here.”

Just then he turned a corner and came face to face with Ronald Breton. The young barrister was now in his wig and gown and carried a bundle of papers tied up with pink tape; he was escorting two young ladies, who were laughing and chattering as they tripped along at his side. And Spargo, glancing at them meditatively, instinctively told himself which of them it was that he and Rathbury had overheard as she made her burlesque speech: it was not the elder one, who walked by Ronald Breton with something of an air of proprietorship, but the younger, the girl with the laughing eyes and the vivacious smile, and it suddenly dawned upon him that somewhere, deep within him, there had been a notion, a hope of seeing this girl again﻿—why, he could not then think.

===Chinese===
官员伸出一根手指。

“拐过弯——右手第一间——左手第二间，”他不假思索地顺口背道。“那儿座位有的是——今天上午没什么热闹。”

他转身走开，斯帕戈便又继续在那阴沉压抑的走廊里看似漫无目的地兜起圈来。

“我敢发誓！”他喃喃自语。“我敢发誓，我实在不知道自己上这儿来干什么。我在这儿无事可办。”

正在这时，他拐过一个弯，迎面遇上罗纳德·布雷顿（Ronald Breton）。这位年轻的出庭律师已戴好假发、披上法袍，手里拿着一捆用粉红纸带扎起的文件，正陪伴着两位年轻小姐；两人傍着他款款而行，一路笑语不断。斯帕戈若有所思地朝她们望了一眼，本能地认定了她们之中哪一个正是他和拉思伯里（Rathbury）偷听到她说那番戏言的人：不是年长的那位——她走在罗纳德·布雷顿身旁，神气里透着几分主人的做派——而是年轻的那位，那个眉眼含笑、笑靥活泼的姑娘。他蓦然醒悟，在自己内心深处的某个角落，原来一直存着一个念头、一份想再见这姑娘一面的期望——至于为什么，他一时还想不明白。

===Original===
Spargo, thus coming face to face with these three, mechanically lifted his hat. Breton stopped, half inquisitive. His eyes seemed to ask a question.

“Yes,” said Spargo. “I﻿—the fact is, I remembered that you said you were coming up here, and I came after you. I want﻿—when you’ve time﻿—to have a talk, to ask you a few questions. About﻿—this affair of the dead man, you know.”

Breton nodded. He tapped Spargo on the arm.

“Look here,” he said. “When this case of mine is over, I can give you as much time as you like. Can you wait a bit? Yes? Well, I say, do me a favour. I was taking these ladies round to the gallery﻿—round there, and up the stairs﻿—and I’m a bit pressed for time﻿—I’ve a solicitor waiting for me. You take them﻿—there’s a good fellow; then, when the case is over, bring them down here, and you and I will talk. Here﻿—I’ll introduce you all﻿—no ceremony. Miss Aylmore﻿—Miss Jessie Aylmore. Mr. Spargo﻿—of the *Watchman*. Now, I’m off!” Breton turned on the instant; his gown whisked round a corner, and Spargo found himself staring at two smiling girls. He saw then that both were pretty and attractive, and that one seemed to be the elder by some three or four years.

===Chinese===
就这样同这三位打了个照面，斯帕戈机械地举了举帽。布雷顿停住脚步，半带好奇，一双眼睛似乎在发问。

“是这样，”斯帕戈说。“我——不瞒你说，我想起你说过要上这儿来，就跟过来了。我想——等你得空——同你谈谈，问你几个问题。关于——那个死人的案子，你知道的。”

布雷顿点点头，伸手拍了拍斯帕戈的手臂。

“听我说，”他道。“等我这件案子完事，你要多少时间我都奉陪。你能稍等一会儿吗？能？那好，我说，帮我个忙。我正要陪这两位小姐去旁听席——那边拐过去，上楼梯——我这儿时间有点紧——还有一位事务律师等着我。你陪她们去——好伙计，就这么办；等案子完了，把她们带回这儿下楼来，你我再谈。来——我给你们互相引见一下——不必客套。艾尔莫尔（Aylmore）小姐——杰西·艾尔莫尔（Jessie Aylmore）小姐。斯帕戈先生——《守望报》（the *Watchman*）的。好了，我走啦！”布雷顿当即转身，法袍一闪，掠过屋角，斯帕戈发觉自己正对着两位笑盈盈的姑娘。他这才看清，两人都生得俏丽动人，一个看来比另一个年长着三四岁。

===Original===
“That is very cool of Ronald,” observed the elder young lady. “Perhaps his scheme doesn’t fit in with yours, Mr. Spargo? Pray don’t﻿—”

“Oh, it’s all right!” said Spargo, feeling himself uncommonly stupid. “I’ve nothing to do. But﻿—where did Mr. Breton say you wished to be taken?”

“Into the gallery of number seven court,” said the younger girl promptly. “Round this corner﻿—I think I know the way.”

Spargo, still marvelling at the rapidity with which affairs were moving that morning, bestirred himself to act as cicerone, and presently led the two young ladies to the very front of one of those public galleries from which idlers and specially-interested spectators may see and hear the proceedings which obtain in the badly-ventilated, ill-lighted tanks wherein justice is dispensed at the Law Courts. There was no one else in that gallery; the attendant in the corridor outside seemed to be vastly amazed that anyone should wish to enter it, and he presently opened the door, beckoned to Spargo, and came halfway down the stairs to meet him.

===Chinese===
“罗纳德这可真会自作主张，”年长的那位小姐说。“兴许他的安排跟您的打算对不上呢，斯帕戈先生？请您千万别——”

“哦，没关系！”斯帕戈说，只觉得自己笨得可以。“我没什么事。不过——布雷顿先生说你们想去哪儿来着？”

“第七法庭的旁听席，”年纪小的姑娘应声答道。“拐过这个弯——我想我认得路。”

那天上午诸事推进之神速，仍令斯帕戈暗自称奇；他打起精神充任向导，不多时便把两位小姐领到了一张公众旁听席的最前排。闲人与怀有特殊兴致的看客，正是从这类旁听席上，望着、听着皇家法院（the Law Courts）那些通风不良、光线昏暗的“池子”里进行的种种审理——司法公道就是在那种地方发落的。旁听席里空无一人；外间走廊上的侍者似乎大为诧异，居然有人肯进来，他随即开了门，朝斯帕戈招手，自己迎下楼梯一半来接他。

===Original===
“Nothing much going on here this morning,” he whispered behind a raised hand. “But there’s a nice breach case in number five﻿—get you three good seats there if you like.”

Spargo declined this tempting offer, and went back to his charges. He had decided by that time that Miss Aylmore was about twenty-three, and her sister about eighteen; he also thought that young Breton was a lucky dog to be in possession of such a charming future wife and an equally charming sister-in-law. And he dropped into a seat at Miss Jessie Aylmore’s side, and looked around him as if he were much awed by his surroundings.

“I suppose one can talk until the judge enters?” he whispered. “Is this really Mr. Breton’s first case?”

===Chinese===
“今天上午这儿没什么可看的，”他抬手掩着嘴悄声道。“不过第五法庭有桩精彩的违约案子——你们要是愿意，我给你们弄三个好座位。”

斯帕戈婉谢了这份好意，回到他受托照看的两位小姐身边。到这时他已断定，艾尔莫尔小姐约莫二十三岁，她妹妹约莫十八岁；他还暗想，小布雷顿真是个走运的家伙，有这样一位迷人的未婚妻，还外带一位同样迷人的未来妻妹。他在杰西·艾尔莫尔小姐身旁落了座，四下张望，一副被这环境镇住了的样子。

“我想，法官进来之前总还能说话吧？”他低声问。“这真是布雷顿先生头一回办案子？”

===Original===
“His very first﻿—all on his own responsibility, anyway,” replied Spargo’s companion, smiling. “And he’s very nervous﻿—and so’s my sister. Aren’t you, now, Evelyn?”

Evelyn Aylmore looked at Spargo, and smiled quietly.

“I suppose one’s always nervous about first appearances,” she said. “However, I think Ronald’s got plenty of confidence, and, as he says, it’s not much of a case: it isn’t even a jury case. I’m afraid you’ll find it dull, Mr. Spargo﻿—it’s only something about a promissory note.”

“Oh, I’m all right, thank you,” replied Spargo, unconsciously falling back on a favourite formula. “I always like to hear lawyers﻿—they manage to say such a lot about﻿—about﻿—”

===Chinese===
“千真万确是头一回——反正全要他自己担着了，”斯帕戈身旁的同伴笑着答道。“他紧张得很——我姐姐也一样。你说是不是，伊夫琳（Evelyn）？”

伊夫琳·艾尔莫尔望了望斯帕戈，浅浅一笑。

“我想，人初次露面总免不了紧张，”她说。“不过依我看，罗纳德信心足得很，况且正如他自己说的，这案子算不得什么：连陪审团审理的案子都够不上。恐怕您会觉得乏味的，斯帕戈先生——不过是桩本票（promissory note）上的纠纷。”

“哦，我没关系，多谢，”斯帕戈答道，不知不觉又搬出他心爱的一句套话。“我一向爱听律师们说话——他们总能就一件事讲上一大通——一大通——”

===Original===
“About nothing,” said Jessie Aylmore. “But there﻿—so do gentlemen who write for the papers, don’t they?”

Spargo was about to admit that there was a good deal to be said on that point when Miss Aylmore suddenly drew her sister’s attention to a man who had just entered the well of the court.

“Look, Jessie!” she observed. “There’s Mr. Elphick!”

Spargo looked down at the person indicated: an elderly, large-faced, smooth-shaven man, a little inclined to stoutness, who, wigged and gowned, was slowly making his way to a corner seat just outside that charmed inner sanctum wherein only King’s Counsel are permitted to sit. He dropped into this in a fashion which showed that he was one of those men who loved personal comfort; he bestowed his plump person at the most convenient angle and fitting a monocle in his right eye, glanced around him. There were a few of his professional brethren in his vicinity; there were half a dozen solicitors and their clerks in conversation with one or other of them; there were court officials. But the gentleman of the monocle swept all these with an indifferent look and cast his eyes upward until he caught sight of the two girls. Thereupon he made a most gracious bow in their direction; his broad face beamed in a genial smile, and he waved a white hand.

===Chinese===
“就‘什么都没有’讲上一大通，”杰西·艾尔莫尔说。“不过嘛——给报馆写稿的先生们，不也是一路么？”

斯帕戈正要承认这一点确有可说，艾尔莫尔小姐忽然引妹妹去看一个刚走进法庭井区的人。

“瞧，杰西！”她说。“埃尔菲克（Elphick）先生来了！”

斯帕戈朝所指的人望去：一位上了年纪、脸盘宽大、胡须刮得精光的先生，身子微微发福；他戴着假发、披着法袍，正缓缓走向一个角落座位——就在那圈只有王室法律顾问（King's Counsel）才许落座的内堂圣地外侧。他一屁股坐了下去，那副做派表明他是顶讲究个人安逸的人；他把富态的身躯安置到最舒坦的角度，往右眼嵌上一片单片眼镜，环顾起四周来。他近旁有几位同行；有五六位事务律师带着文书，正同这一位或那一位交谈；还有法庭的官员们。可是这位单片眼镜先生只淡淡一扫，便抬眼向上望去，直到望见那两位姑娘。于是他朝她们那边极其雍容地鞠了一躬；宽大的脸上绽出和蔼的笑容，挥了挥一只白皙的手。

===Original===
“Do you know Mr. Elphick, Mr. Spargo?” enquired the younger Miss Aylmore.

“I rather think I’ve seen him, somewhere about the Temple,” answered Spargo. “In fact, I’m sure I have.”

“His chambers are in Paper Buildings,” said Jessie. “Sometimes he gives tea-parties in them. He is Ronald’s guardian, and preceptor, and mentor, and all that, and I suppose he’s dropped into this court to hear how his pupil goes on.”

“Here is Ronald,” whispered Miss Aylmore.

===Chinese===
“您认识埃尔菲克先生吗，斯帕戈先生？”年纪小的艾尔莫尔小姐问道。

“我想我见过他，就在律师学院一带的什么地方，”斯帕戈答道。“其实，我肯定见过。”

“他的事务室在纸楼（Paper Buildings），”杰西说。“他有时还在那儿开茶会呢。他是罗纳德的监护人，又是启蒙师，又是领路人，一身兼任，诸如此类；我猜他是顺脚拐进这个法庭，来听听他弟子进行得如何了。”

“罗纳德来了，”艾尔莫尔小姐低声道。

===Original===
“And here,” said her sister, “is his lordship, looking very cross. Now, Mr. Spargo, you’re in for it.”

Spargo, to tell the truth, paid little attention to what went on beneath him. The case which young Breton presently opened was a commercial one, involving certain rights and properties in a promissory note; it seemed to the journalist that Breton dealt with it very well, showing himself master of the financial details, and speaking with readiness and assurance. He was much more interested in his companions, and especially in the younger one, and he was meditating on how he could improve his further acquaintance when he awoke to the fact that the defence, realizing that it stood no chance, had agreed to withdraw, and that Mr. Justice Borrow was already giving judgment in Ronald Breton’s favour.

In another minute he was walking out of the gallery in rear of the two sisters.

“Very good﻿—very good, indeed,” he said, absentmindedly. “I thought he put his facts very clearly and concisely.”

===Chinese===
“这位呢，”她妹妹说，“是法官大人，瞧那脸色，凶着呢。这下好了，斯帕戈先生，您可有的受了。”

说实在的，斯帕戈对脚下法庭里进行的一切并未怎么留心。小布雷顿随即开庭陈述的案子是件商事案子，涉及一张本票上的某些权利和利益；在这位记者看来，布雷顿办得相当出色，对财务细节烂熟于胸，谈吐敏捷而自信。他更感兴趣的是身旁的同伴，尤其是年轻的那一位；他正盘算着如何把这段新结识再往前推进一步，忽然回过神来，才发现辩方自知毫无胜算、业已同意撤诉，而博罗法官已经在宣判，判罗纳德·布雷顿胜诉。

不消一分钟，他已经跟在两姐妹身后走出了旁听席。

“很好——实在很好，”他心不在焉地说。“我觉得他把事实摆得非常清楚扼要。”

===Original===
Downstairs, in the corridor, Ronald Breton was talking to Mr. Elphick. He pointed a finger at Spargo as the latter came up with the girls: Spargo gathered that Breton was speaking of the murder and of his, Spargo’s, connection with it. And directly they approached, he spoke.

“This is Mr. Spargo, subeditor of the *Watchman*.” Breton said. “Mr. Elphick﻿—Mr. Spargo. I was just telling Mr. Elphick, Spargo, that you saw this poor man soon after he was found.”

Spargo, glancing at Mr. Elphick, saw that he was deeply interested. The elderly barrister took him﻿—literally﻿—by the buttonhole.

“My dear sir!” he said. “You﻿—saw this poor fellow? Lying dead﻿—in the third entry down Middle Temple Lane? The third entry, eh?”

===Chinese===
下楼来到走廊，罗纳德·布雷顿正同埃尔菲克先生说话。斯帕戈陪着两位姑娘走近时，布雷顿朝他一指：斯帕戈听出来，布雷顿讲的正是那桩凶案，以及他斯帕戈同这案子的干系。两人刚一走近，布雷顿便开了口。

“这位是斯帕戈先生，《守望报》的助理编辑。”布雷顿说。“埃尔菲克先生——斯帕戈先生。斯帕戈，我正跟埃尔菲克先生说来着：这可怜人被发现之后不久，是你见着了他。”

斯帕戈瞥了埃尔菲克先生一眼，看出他兴致极浓。这位上了年纪的出庭律师一把揪住了他——货真价实地揪住了他的纽扣眼。

“亲爱的先生！”他说。“您——亲眼见了这个可怜人？直挺挺死了——在中殿巷（Middle Temple Lane）往里数第三个门洞里？第三个门洞，嗯？”

===Original===
“Yes,” replied Spargo, simply. “I saw him. It was the third entry.”

“Singular!” said Mr. Elphick, musingly. “I know a man who lives in that house. In fact, I visited him last night, and did not leave until nearly midnight. And this unfortunate man had Mr. Ronald Breton’s name and address in his pocket?”

Spargo nodded. He looked at Breton, and pulled out his watch. Just then he had no idea of playing the part of informant to Mr. Elphick.

“Yes, that’s so,” he answered shortly. Then, looking at Breton significantly, he added, “If you can give me those few minutes, now﻿—?”

===Chinese===
“是的，”斯帕戈简简单单地答道。“我见到了。就是第三个门洞。”

“真怪！”埃尔菲克先生若有所思地说。“我认得一个人，就住在那栋房子里。昨晚我还去看他，快到半夜才走。而这位不幸的人，口袋里竟装着罗纳德·布雷顿先生的名姓和地址？”

斯帕戈点点头。他看看布雷顿，掏出怀表。这会儿他全无心思给埃尔菲克先生充当消息来源。

“是的，是这么回事，”他简短地答道。接着他意味深长地望了望布雷顿，补上一句：“您现在能腾出那几分钟的话——？”

===Original===
“Yes﻿—yes!” responded Ronald Breton, nodding. “I understand. Evelyn﻿—I’ll leave you and Jessie to Mr. Elphick; I must go.”

Mr. Elphick seized Spargo once more.

“My dear sir!” he said, eagerly. “Do you﻿—do you think I could possibly see﻿—the body?”

“It’s at the mortuary,” answered Spargo. “I don’t know what their regulations are.”

===Chinese===
“能——能！”罗纳德·布雷顿连连点头。“我明白。伊夫琳——你和杰西就托付给埃尔菲克先生了；我得走了。”

埃尔菲克先生又一把抓住了斯帕戈。

“亲爱的先生！”他急切地说。“您——您觉得我有没有可能见一见——那具遗体？”

“遗体在停尸房，”斯帕戈答道。“他们有什么规矩，我不知道。”

===Original===
Then he escaped with Breton. They had crossed Fleet Street and were in the quieter shades of the Temple before Spargo spoke.

“About what I wanted to say to you,” he said at last. “It was﻿—this. I﻿—well, I’ve always wanted, as a journalist, to have a real big murder case. I think this is one. I want to go right into it﻿—thoroughly, first and last. And﻿—I think you can help me.”

“How do you know that it is a murder case?” asked Breton quietly.

“It’s a murder case,” answered Spargo, stolidly. “I feel it. Instinct, perhaps. I’m going to ferret out the truth. And it seems to me﻿—”

===Chinese===
随后他便同布雷顿脱身走开。两人已经穿过舰队街（Fleet Street），走进律师学院较为清幽的浓荫，斯帕戈这才开口。

“关于我要跟你说的话，”他终于说道。“是——这么回事。我——唉，当记者的，我一直巴望能摊上一桩真正的大凶杀案。我看这就是一桩。我要一头扎进去——彻头彻尾、一查到底。而且——我觉得你帮得上我的忙。”

“你怎么知道这是桩凶杀案？”布雷顿平静地问。

“就是凶杀案，”斯帕戈斩钉截铁地答道。“我感觉得到。也许是直觉。我非把真相挖出来不可。而依我看——”

===Original===
He paused and gave his companion a sharp glance.

“It seems to me,” he presently continued, “that the clue lies in that scrap of paper. That paper and that man are connecting links between you and﻿—somebody else.”

“Possibly,” agreed Breton. “You want to find the somebody else?”

“I want you to help me to find the somebody else,” answered Spargo. “I believe this is a big, very big affair: I want to do it. I don’t believe in police methods﻿—much. By the by, I’m just going to meet Rathbury. He may have heard of something. Would you like to come?”

===Chinese===
他顿住话头，锐利地瞥了同伴一眼。

“依我看，”他随即接着说，“线索就在那片碎纸上。那片纸和那个人，是把你和——另外某个人——联结起来的链环。”

“也许吧，”布雷顿表示同意。“你想找到那另外的某个人？”

“我要你帮我找到那另外的某个人，”斯帕戈答道。“我相信这是件大案、非常大的案子：我要拿下它。警方那套办法——我不大信得过。对了，我这就去见拉思伯里。他也许听到了什么风声。你愿意同去吗？”

===Original===
Breton ran into his chambers in King’s Bench Walk, left his gown and wig, and walked round with Spargo to the police office. Rathbury came out as they were stepping in.

“Oh!” he said. “Ah!﻿—I’ve got what may be helpful, Mr. Spargo. I told you I’d sent a man to Fiskie’s, the hatter! Well, he’s just returned. The cap which the dead man was wearing was bought at Fiskie’s yesterday afternoon, and it was sent to Mr. Marbury, Room 20, at the Anglo-Orient Hotel.”

“Where is that?” asked Spargo.

“Waterloo district,” answered Rathbury. “A small house, I believe. Well, I’m going there. Are you coming?”

===Chinese===
布雷顿奔回王座法庭步道（King's Bench Walk）的事务室，撂下法袍和假发，便同斯帕戈一路走到警察局。两人正进门，拉思伯里走了出来。

“哦！”他说。“啊！——我弄到一样东西，兴许管用，斯帕戈先生。我说过派人去了帽商菲斯基帽店（Fiskie's）！喏，那人刚回来。死人头上那顶便帽，是昨天下午在菲斯基帽店买的，店家把它送到了马伯里（Marbury）先生手里，地址是盎格鲁-东方旅馆（Anglo-Orient Hotel）20 号房间。”

“那地方在哪儿？”斯帕戈问。

“滑铁卢区（Waterloo district），”拉思伯里答道。“我想是家小客栈。这样吧，我正要去那儿。你们去不去？”

===Original===
“Yes,” replied Spargo. “Of course. And Mr. Breton wants to come, too.”

“If I’m not in the way,” said Breton.

Rathbury laughed.

“Well, we may find out something about this scrap of paper,” he observed. And he waved a signal to the nearest taxicab driver.

===Chinese===
“去，”斯帕戈答道。“当然去。布雷顿先生也想去。”

“只要不碍事，”布雷顿说。

拉思伯里笑了。

“也好，那片碎纸头，咱们兴许能查出点眉目，”他说。说着，他朝离得最近的那辆出租汽车的车夫扬手打了个招呼。

## XXXII The Contents of the Coffin / 第三十二章 棺中之物

===Original===
There travelled down together to Market Milcaster late that afternoon, Spargo, Breton, the officials from the Home Office, entrusted with the order for the opening of the Chamberlayne grave, and a solicitor acting on behalf of the proprietor of the *Watchman*. It was late in the evening when they reached the little town, but Spargo, having looked in at the parlour of the “Yellow Dragon” and ascertained that Mr. Quarterpage had only just gone home, took Breton across the street to the old gentleman’s house. Mr. Quarterpage himself came to the door, and recognized Spargo immediately. Nothing would satisfy him but that the two should go in; his family, he said, had just retired, but he himself was going to take a final nightcap and a cigar, and they must share it.

“For a few minutes only then, Mr. Quarterpage,” said Spargo as they followed the old man into his dining-room. “We have to be up at daybreak. And—possibly—you, too, would like to be up just as early.”

Mr. Quarterpage looked an enquiry over the top of a decanter which he was handling.

“At daybreak?” he exclaimed.

“The fact is,” said Spargo, “that grave of Chamberlayne’s is going to be opened at daybreak. We have managed to get an order from the Home Secretary for the exhumation of Chamberlayne’s body: the officials in charge of it have come down in the same train with us; we’re all staying across there at the ‘Dragon.’ The officials have gone to make the proper arrangements with your authorities. It will be at daybreak, or as near it as can conveniently be managed. And I suppose, now that you know of it, you’ll be there?”

===Chinese===
那天下午晚些时候，斯帕戈、布雷顿、奉命持开启张伯莱恩坟墓之令而来的内政部官员，以及代表《守望报》东主的事务律师，一行人同车南下马基特·米尔卡斯特。他们抵达这座小镇时已是入夜时分，但斯帕戈到“黄龙”旅店的客厅里探看了一下，查明夸特佩奇先生刚刚回家，便带着布雷顿过街去了这位老绅士的宅邸。夸特佩奇先生亲自出来开门，一眼就认出了斯帕戈。他非拉着两人进屋不可；他说，家里人刚刚歇下，不过他自己正要喝睡前的最后一杯酒、抽一支雪茄，两人务必奉陪。

“那么就只坐几分钟，夸特佩奇先生，”斯帕戈跟着老人走进饭厅，说道，“我们天亮前就得起床。而且——说不定——您自己也愿意起得一样早。”

夸特佩奇先生正摆弄着一只细颈酒瓶，从瓶口上方投来探询的一瞥。

“天亮前？”他失声道。

“实情是，”斯帕戈说，“张伯莱恩那座坟天亮时就要开挖了。我们设法从内政大臣那里拿到了掘出张伯莱恩遗体的许可令：负责此事的官员与我们同车而至；我们都住在对面的‘龙旅店’。官员们已去同贵镇当局接洽妥善安排。时间就在天亮时分，或视方便尽早。我想，您既已知情，也会到场的吧？”

===Original===
“God bless me!” exclaimed Mr. Quarterpage. “You’ve really done that! Well, well, so we shall know the truth at last, after all these years. You’re a very wonderful young man, Mr. Spargo, upon my word. And this other young gentleman?”

Spargo looked at Breton, who had already given him permission to speak. “Mr. Quarterpage,” he said, “this young gentleman is, without doubt, John Maitland’s son. He’s the young barrister, Mr. Ronald Breton, that I told you of, but there’s no doubt about his parentage. And I’m sure you’ll shake hands with him and wish him well.”

Mr. Quarterpage set down decanter and glass and hastened to give Breton his hand.

“My dear young sir!” he exclaimed. “That I will indeed! And as to wishing you well—ah, I never wished anything but well to your poor father. He was led away, sir, led away by Chamberlayne. God bless me, what a night of surprises! Why, Mr. Spargo, supposing that coffin is found empty—what then?”

“Then,” answered Spargo, “then I think we shall be able to put our hands on the man who is supposed to be in it.”

===Chinese===
“老天爷！”夸特佩奇先生失声道，“你们当真办成了！好哇，好哇，过了这么多年，我们总算要知道真相了。斯帕戈先生，说句实话，您真是个了不起的年轻人。这位年轻先生又是哪位？”

斯帕戈看了看布雷顿——后者早已允许他开口。“夸特佩奇先生，”他说，“这位年轻先生毫无疑问是约翰·梅特兰的儿子。就是我向您提起过的那位年轻出庭律师罗纳德·布雷顿先生，而他的出身血脉，如今已无可置疑。我相信您定会与他握手，祝他前程顺遂。”

夸特佩奇先生放下细颈酒瓶和酒杯，赶忙向布雷顿伸出手去。

“我亲爱的年轻先生！”他失声道，“那是自然！至于祝您好运——唉，对您那位可怜的父亲，我从来只盼他好，没盼过他半点不好。他是被人带坏的，先生，被张伯莱恩带坏的。老天爷，今晚真是奇事连连！哎，斯帕戈先生，倘若那口棺材被发现是空的——那可怎么好？”

“那样的话，”斯帕戈答道，“那我想，我们就能把那个本该躺在里面的人抓到手了。”

===Original===
“You think my father was worked upon by this man Chamberlayne, sir?” observed Breton a few minutes later when they had all sat down round Mr. Quarterpage’s hospitable hearth. “You think he was unduly influenced by him?”

Mr. Quarterpage shook his head sadly.

“Chamberlayne, my dear young sir,” he answered. “Chamberlayne was a plausible and a clever fellow. Nobody knew anything about him until he came to this town, and yet before he had been here very long he had contrived to ingratiate himself with everybody—of course, to his own advantage. I firmly believe that he twisted your father round his little finger. As I told Mr. Spargo there when he was making his enquiries of me a short while back, it would never have been any surprise to me to hear—definitely, I mean, young gentlemen—that all this money that was in question went into Chamberlayne’s pockets. Dear me—dear me!—and you really believe that Chamberlayne is actually alive, Mr. Spargo?”

Spargo pulled out his watch. “We shall all know whether he was buried in that grave before another six hours are over, Mr. Quarterpage,” he said.

===Chinese===
“先生，您认为我父亲是受了张伯莱恩这个人的摆布？”片刻之后，众人围着夸特佩奇先生好客的炉火坐定，布雷顿说道，“您认为他是被他左右得没了主见？”

夸特佩奇先生黯然摇头。

“张伯莱恩，我亲爱的年轻先生，”他答道，“张伯莱恩是个花言巧语的聪明角色。他来本镇之前，没人知道他的半点底细，可到这儿还没多久，他便设法讨得了人人的欢心——当然，图的是他自己的好处。我坚信，他把你父亲玩弄于股掌之上。正如不久前斯帕戈先生向我查证时我对他说过的，倘若听说——我是说确凿地听说，两位年轻先生——那笔有争议的款项尽数落进了张伯莱恩的腰包，我一点也不会吃惊。哎呀——哎呀！——斯帕戈先生，您当真相信张伯莱恩还活着？”

斯帕戈掏出怀表。“不出六个钟头，我们就都知道他究竟下没下葬在那座坟里了，夸特佩奇先生。”他说。

===Original===
He might well have spoken of four hours instead of six, for it was then nearly midnight, and before three o’clock Spargo and Breton, with the other men who had accompanied them from London were out of the “Yellow Dragon” and on their way to the cemetery just outside the little town. Over the hills to the eastward the grey dawn was slowly breaking: the long stretch of marshland which lies between Market Milcaster and the sea was white with fog: on the cypresses and acacias of the cemetery hung veils and webs of gossamer: everything around them was quiet as the dead folk who lay beneath their feet. And the people actively concerned went quietly to work, and those who could do nothing but watch stood around in silence.

“In all my long life of over ninety years,” whispered old Quarterpage, who had met them at the cemetery gates, looking fresh and brisk in spite of his shortened rest, “I have never seen this done before. It seems a strange, strange thing to interfere with a dead man’s last resting-place—a dreadful thing.”

“If there is a dead man there,” said Spargo.

===Chinese===
他满可以说四个钟头而非六个，因为那时已近午夜，还不到三点，斯帕戈和布雷顿便已带着从伦敦同来的其他人出了“黄龙”旅店，动身前往镇外不远的墓园。东边山岭上方，灰白的曙光正缓缓破开：马基特·米尔卡斯特与大海之间绵延的那片沼泽地白雾茫茫：墓园里的柏树和金合欢树上挂满了游丝织成的轻纱罗网：四周万籁俱寂，一如他们脚下长眠的死者。办事的人们悄然开工，无事可做、只能旁观的人则默然环立。

“我活了九十多个年头，”在墓园门口迎候他们的老夸特佩奇悄声说道——尽管睡眠打了折扣，他看上去仍精神矍铄、神采奕奕，“还从未见过这种事。惊扰亡人最后的安息之所，似乎是天大的怪事——是可怕的事。”

“假如里面真有亡人的话。”斯帕戈说。

===Original===
He himself was mainly curious about the details of this exhumation; he had no scruples, sentimental or otherwise, about the breaking in upon the dead. He watched all that was done. The men employed by the local authorities, instructed overnight, had fenced in the grave with canvas; the proceedings were accordingly conducted in strict privacy; a man was posted to keep away any very early passersby, who might be attracted by the unusual proceedings. At first there was nothing to do but wait, and Spargo occupied himself by reflecting that every spadeful of earth thrown out of that grave was bringing him nearer to the truth; he had an unconquerable intuition that the truth of at any rate one phase of the Marbury case was going to be revealed to them. If the coffin to which they were digging down contained a body, and that the body of the stockbroker, Chamberlayne, then a good deal of his, Spargo’s, latest theory, would be dissolved to nothingness. But if that coffin contained no body at all, then—

“They’re down to it!” whispered Breton.

===Chinese===
他自己主要是好奇这次掘尸的种种细节；至于惊扰亡者这件事，他并无情感上或其他任何方面的顾忌。他注视着每一道工序。当地当局雇来的工人昨夜已领了指示，用帆布把坟场围了起来；于是整个过程在严格保密中进行；还派了一个人守在外围，把可能被这不寻常的动静引来的极早过路人挡开。起初无事可做，只能等待，斯帕戈便一心琢磨着：从那座坟里挖出的每一锹土，都在把他向真相推近一步；他有一种不可动摇的直觉——马伯里案至少一个阶段的真相，即将在他们面前揭开。如果他们正向下挖去的那口棺材里装着一具尸体，而且是股票经纪人张伯莱恩的尸体，那么他斯帕戈最新的那套推想，便有相当一部分要化作泡影。可倘若那口棺材里根本没有任何尸体，那么——

“他们挖到棺材了！”布雷顿低声道。

===Original===
Presently they all went and looked down into the grave. The workmen had uncovered the coffin preparatory to lifting it to the surface; one of them was brushing the earth away from the nameplate. And in the now strong light they could all read the lettering on it.

> **James Cartwright Chamberlayne**

> Born 1852

> Died 1891

Spargo turned away as the men began to lift the coffin out of the grave.

===Chinese===
不多时，众人都走过去朝坟坑里张望。工人们已扒开棺材上的覆土，准备把它吊上地面；其中一个正拂去铭牌上的泥土。在此时已很明亮的晨光里，众人都能认出上面的刻字。

> **詹姆斯·卡特赖特·张伯莱恩**

> 生于 1852 年

> 卒于 1891 年

工人们开始把棺材从坟坑里吊出时，斯帕戈转过身去。

===Original===
“We shall know now!” he whispered to Breton. “And yet—what is it we shall know if—”

“If what?” said Breton. “If—what?”

But Spargo shook his head. This was one of the great moments he had lately been working for, and the issues were tremendous.

“Now for it!” said the *Watchman*’s solicitor in an undertone. “Come, Mr. Spargo, now we shall see.”

===Chinese===
“这下我们就知道了！”他悄声对布雷顿说，“可是——倘若……我们知道的又究竟是什么？”

“倘若什么？”布雷顿说，“倘若——什么？”

斯帕戈却只是摇头。这是他近来苦心经营所等来的重大时刻之一，而其间利害，非同小可。

“就要见分晓了！”《守望报》的律师低声说，“来吧，斯帕戈先生，这就见真章了。”

===Original===
They all gathered round the coffin, set on low trestles at the graveside, as the workmen silently went to work on the screws. The screws were rusted in their sockets; they grated as the men slowly worked them out. It seemed to Spargo that each man grew slower and slower in his movements; he felt that he himself was getting fidgety. Then he heard a voice of authority.

“Lift the lid off!”

A man at the head of the coffin, a man at the foot suddenly and swiftly raised the lid: the men gathered round craned their necks with a quick movement.

Sawdust!

===Chinese===
棺材已架在坟边的低矮支架上，工人们默默动手起螺丝，众人围拢过去。螺丝锈死在螺孔里，被慢慢旋出来时嘎吱作响。在斯帕戈看来，每个人的动作都越来越迟缓；他觉得自己也烦躁起来。这时他听见一个发号施令的声音。

“揭棺盖！”

棺首一人，棺尾一人，猛地麻利地掀起了棺盖：围拢的众人齐刷刷伸长了脖子。

锯末！

===Original===
The coffin was packed to the brim with sawdust, tightly pressed down. The surface lay smooth, undisturbed, levelled as some hand had levelled it long years before. They were not in the presence of death, but of deceit.

Somebody laughed faintly. The sound of the laughter broke the spell. The chief official present looked round him with a smile.

“It is evident that there were good grounds for suspicion,” he remarked. “Here is no dead body, gentlemen. See if anything lies beneath the sawdust,” he added, turning to the workmen. “Turn it out!”

===Chinese===
棺材里满满当当塞的全是锯末，压得严严实实。表层平整光洁，纹丝未动，一如多年前某只手抹平它时的模样。此刻他们面对的不是死亡，而是欺诈。

有人低低笑了一声。笑声打破了这道魔咒。在场的主事官员微笑着环顾四周。

“显然，当初的怀疑是有充分根据的，”他说，“诸位，这里面并没有尸体。看看锯末底下有没有别的东西，”他转向工人们，又加了一句，“把它翻出来！”

===Original===
The workmen began to scoop out the sawdust with their hands; one of them, evidently desirous of making sure that no body was in the coffin, thrust down his fingers at various places along its length. He, too, laughed.

“The coffin’s weighted with lead!” he remarked. “See!”

And tearing the sawdust aside, he showed those around him that at three intervals bars of lead had been tightly wedged into the coffin where the head, the middle, and the feet of a corpse would have rested.

“Done it cleverly,” he remarked, looking round. “You see how these weights have been adjusted. When a body’s laid out in a coffin, you know, all the weight’s in the end where the head and trunk rest. Here you see the heaviest bar of lead is in the middle; the lightest at the feet. Clever!”

===Chinese===
工人们开始动手把锯末往外捧；其中一个显然想确认棺材里确实没有尸体，沿着棺身各处把手指插了下去。他也笑了。

“这棺材是用铅块压的分量！”他说，“看！”

他把锯末扒开，让周围的人看：棺内三处分别紧紧楔入了铅条，位置恰在尸身的头部、腰身和脚部本该搁放的地方。

“干得真巧妙，”他环顾四周，说道，“你们瞧这些配重安排得多讲究。要知道，尸体入殓的时候，分量全落在头颅和躯干搁置的那一头。而这儿你们看，最重的铅条在中间；最轻的在脚头。高明！”

===Original===
“Clear out all the sawdust,” said someone. “Let’s see if there’s anything else.”

There was something else. At the bottom of the coffin two bundles of papers, tied up with pink tape. The legal gentlemen present immediately manifested great interest in these. So did Spargo, who, pulling Breton along with him, forced his way to where the officials from the Home Office and the solicitor sent by the *Watchman* were hastily examining their discoveries.

===Chinese===
“把锯末全清出来，”有人说，“看看还有没有别的。”

还真有别的。棺底放着两捆文件，用粉色绑带扎着。在场的法律界人士立刻对这些东西表现出极大的兴趣。斯帕戈也不例外，他拉着布雷顿，挤到内政部官员和《守望报》所派律师正匆匆检视其发现物的地方。

===Original===
The first bundle of papers opened evidently related to transactions at Market Milcaster: Spargo caught glimpses of names that were familiar to him, Mr. Quarterpage’s amongst them. He was not at all astonished to see these things. But he was something more than astonished when, on the second parcel being opened, a quantity of papers relating to Cloudhampton and the Hearth and Home Mutual Benefit Society were revealed. He gave a hasty glance at these and drew Breton aside.

“It strikes me we’ve found a good deal more than we ever bargained for!” he exclaimed. “Didn’t Aylmore say that the real culprit at Cloudhampton was another man—his clerk or something of that sort?”

“He did,” agreed Breton. “He insists on it.”

===Chinese===
打开的第一捆文件显然与马基特·米尔卡斯特的交易有关：斯帕戈瞥见几个熟悉的名字，其中就有夸特佩奇先生的。见到这些东西他毫不惊讶。可当第二捆打开，露出一批与克劳德汉普顿及炉边家园互助会相关的文件时，他可就不止是惊讶了。他匆匆扫了这些文件一眼，便把布雷顿拉到一旁。

“依我看，我们找到的东西远远超出了当初的预想！”他失声道，“艾尔莫尔不是说过，克劳德汉普顿案的真凶另有其人——是他手下的办事员或诸如此类的人吗？”

“他说过，”布雷顿附和道，“而且一口咬定。”

===Original===
“Then this fellow Chamberlayne must have been the man,” said Spargo. “He came to Market Milcaster from the north. What’ll be done with those papers?” he asked, turning to the officials.

“We are going to seal them up at once, and take them to London,” replied the principal person in authority. “They will be quite safe, Mr. Spargo; have no fear. We don’t know what they may reveal.”

“You don’t, indeed!” said Spargo. “But I may as well tell you that I have a strong belief that they’ll reveal a good deal that nobody dreams of, so take the greatest care of them.”

Then, without waiting for further talk with anyone, Spargo hurried Breton out of the cemetery. At the gate, he seized him by the arm.

“Now, then, Breton!” he commanded. “Out with it!”

===Chinese===
“那么这个张伯莱恩必定就是那个人了，”斯帕戈说，“他是从北方来马基特·米尔卡斯特的。这些文件会怎么处置？”他转向官员们问道。

“我们马上把它们封存起来，带回伦敦，”主事的官员答道，“它们万无一失，斯帕戈先生；不必担心。我们还不知道它们会揭示些什么。”

“你们的确不知道！”斯帕戈说，“不过我不妨告诉您，我坚信它们会揭示出许多无人梦想到的东西，所以务必好生保管。”

说罢，斯帕戈不再与任何人多谈，拉着布雷顿匆匆出了墓园。到了门口，他一把抓住布雷顿的手臂。

“好了，布雷顿！”他催促道，“快说！”

===Original===
“With what?”

“You promised to tell me something—a great deal, you said—if we found that coffin empty. It is empty. Come on—quick!”

“All right. I believe I know where Elphick and Cardlestone can be found. That’s all.”

“All! It’s enough. Where, then, in heaven’s name?”

“Elphick has a queer little place where he and Cardlestone sometimes go fishing—right away up in one of the wildest parts of the Yorkshire moors. I expect they’ve gone there. Nobody knows even their names there—they could go and lie quiet there for—ages.”

===Chinese===
“说什么？”

“你答应过，倘若我们发现那口棺材是空的，就告诉我一件事——你说过，是很要紧的事。棺材如今空了。快说——快！”

“好吧。我想我知道埃尔菲克和卡德尔斯顿能在哪儿找到。就这些。”

“就这些！这就够了。究竟在哪儿，看在老天的份上？”

“埃尔菲克有一处古怪的小地方，他和卡德尔斯顿有时去那儿钓鱼——远在约克郡荒原最荒僻的地带之一。我料想他们去那儿了。当地连他们的名字都没人知道——他们尽可以躲到那儿悄无声息地待上——天知道多久。”

===Original===
“Do you know the way to it?”

“I do—I’ve been there.”

Spargo motioned him to hurry.

“Come on, then,” he said. “We’re going there by the very first train out of this. I know the train, too—we’ve just time to snatch a mouthful of breakfast and to send a wire to the *Watchman*, and then we’ll be off. Yorkshire!—Gad, Breton, that’s over three hundred miles away!”

===Chinese===
“你认得去那儿的路吗？”

“认得——我去过。”

斯帕戈示意他加快脚步。

“那就走吧，”他说，“我们搭从这里开出的头一班火车去。那趟车我也知道——时间刚够抓紧啃口早饭、给《守望报》发封电报，然后我们就动身。约克郡！——天哪，布雷顿，那可远在三百多英里之外！”

## IX The Dealer in Rare Stamps / 第九章 珍稀邮票商

===Original===
“Buried﻿—and deep-down, too﻿—for many and many a year,” repeated Mr. Myerst, eyeing his companions with keen glances. “I consider that, gentlemen, a very remarkable remark﻿—very remarkable!”

Rathbury stuck his thumbs in the armholes of his waistcoat again and began swaying backwards and forwards in his chair. He looked at Spargo. And with his knowledge of men, he knew that all Spargo’s journalistic instincts had been aroused, and that he was keen as mustard to be off on a new scent.

“Remarkable﻿—remarkable, Mr. Myerst!” he assented. “What do you say, Mr. Spargo?”

Spargo turned slowly, and for the first time since Myerst had entered made a careful inspection of him. The inspection lasted several seconds; then Spargo spoke.

“And what did you say to that?” he asked quietly.

Myerst looked from his questioner to Rathbury. And Rathbury thought it time to enlighten the caller.

“I may as well tell you, Mr. Myerst,” he said smilingly, “that this is Mr. Spargo, of the *Watchman*. Mr. Spargo wrote the article about the Marbury case of which you spoke when you came in. Mr. Spargo, you’ll gather, is deeply interested in this matter﻿—and he and I, in our different capacities, are working together. So﻿—you understand?” Myerst regarded Spargo in a new light. And while he was so looking at him. Spargo repeated the question he had just put.
===Chinese===
“埋了——而且埋得深——埋了许多许多年。”迈尔斯特先生重复道，锐利的目光扫视着两位听众。“二位，我认为这句话非同小可——非同小可！”

拉思伯里又把两个拇指插进马甲袖孔，开始在椅子里前后摇晃起来。他望着斯帕戈。凭他阅人的经验，他知道斯帕戈的新闻本能已经全部调动起来，正热切得很，只待循着新线索一扑而上。

“非同小可——非同小可，迈尔斯特先生！”他表示赞同。“斯帕戈先生，您怎么说？”

斯帕戈缓缓转过身来，自迈尔斯特进门以来头一回把他细细打量了一番。这一打量持续了好几秒；然后斯帕戈开口了。

“那么您听了这话是怎么答的？”他轻声问。

迈尔斯特把目光从问话人移向拉思伯里。拉思伯里觉得，该向这位来客点明底细了。

“我不妨告诉您，迈尔斯特先生，”他笑吟吟地说，“这位是《守望报》的斯帕戈先生。您进门时提到的那篇谈马伯里案的文章，就是斯帕戈先生写的。您想得到，斯帕戈先生对这件事极其关心——他跟我，各司其职，是在一起办案的。所以——您明白吗？”迈尔斯特换了一副眼光重新审视斯帕戈。他正这么打量着，斯帕戈把刚才的问题又问了一遍。

===Original===
“I said﻿—What did you say to that?”

Myerst hesitated.

“Well﻿—er﻿—I don’t think I said anything,” he replied. “Nothing that one might call material, you know.”

“Didn’t ask him what he meant?” suggested Spargo.

“Oh, no﻿—not at all,” replied Myerst.

Spargo got up abruptly from his chair.

“Then you missed one of the finest opportunities I ever heard of!” he said, half-sneeringly. “You might have heard such a story﻿—”
===Chinese===
“我问的是——您听了这话是怎么答的？”

迈尔斯特迟疑了。

“这个——呃——我想我什么也没说，”他答道，“您知道，没有一句称得上有分量的。”

“没问问他这话是什么意思？”斯帕戈提示道。

“哦，没有——完全没有。”迈尔斯特答道。

斯帕戈霍地从椅子上站起身。

“那您可错失了我平生所闻数一数二的好机会！”他半带讥诮地说。“您本可以听到那样一个故事——”

===Original===
He paused, as if it were not worth while to continue, and turned to Rathbury, who was regarding him with amusement.

“Look here, Rathbury,” he said. “Is it possible to get that box opened?”

“It’ll have to be opened,” answered Rathbury, rising. “It’s got to be opened. It probably contains the clue we want. I’m going to ask Mr. Myerst here to go with me just now to take the first steps about having it opened. I shall have to get an order. We may get the matter through today, but at any rate we’ll have it done tomorrow morning.”

“Can you arrange for me to be present when that comes off?” asked Spargo. “You can﻿—certain? That’s all right, Rathbury. Now I’m off, and you’ll ring me up or come round if you hear anything, and I’ll do the same by you.”

And without further word, Spargo went quickly away, and just as quickly returned to the *Watchman* office. There the assistant who had been told off to wait upon his orders during this new crusade met him with a business card.

“This gentleman came in to see you about an hour ago, Mr. Spargo,” he said. “He thinks he can tell you something about the Marbury affair, and he said that as he couldn’t wait, perhaps you’d step round to his place when you came in.”

Spargo took the card and read:
===Chinese===
他顿住了，仿佛再说下去不值得，便转向拉思伯里；拉思伯里正饶有兴味地望着他。

“我说，拉思伯里，”他开了口，“那只箱子有可能打开吗？”

“非打开不可。”拉思伯里起身答道。“一定得打开。我们要的线索多半就在里面。我这就请这位迈尔斯特先生陪我一道去，把开箱的头几步手续办起来。我得去领一道许可。顺利的话今天就能办下来，但无论如何，明天上午一定办妥。”

“开箱的时候，您能安排我在场吗？”斯帕戈问。“能——准成？那就好，拉思伯里。我这就走了；您听到什么风声，就打电话或直接过来，我得了消息也会照样奉告。”

斯帕戈不再多言，快步离去，又同样快地回到了《守望报》报馆。馆里有一名助理，是这场新的征战期间专被指派来听候他调遣的；此刻他迎上斯帕戈，递来一张名片。

“这位先生大约一个钟头前来找过您，斯帕戈先生，”他说，“他觉得能向您提供一些马伯里案的情况；他说自己等不及，也许您回来以后可以到他那儿走一趟。”

斯帕戈接过名片，只见上面写着：

===Original===
> Mr. James Criedir, 

 Dealer in Philatelic Rarities, 

 2,021, Strand.

Spargo put the card in his waistcoat pocket and went out again, wondering why Mr. James Criedir could not, would not, or did not call himself a dealer in rare postage stamps, and so use plain English. He went up Fleet Street and soon found the shop indicated on the card, and his first glance at its exterior showed that whatever business might have been done by Mr. Criedir in the past at that establishment there was to be none done there in the future by him, for there were newly-printed bills in the window announcing that the place was to let. And inside he found a short, portly, elderly man who was superintending the packing-up and removal of the last of his stock. He turned a bright, enquiring eye on the journalist.
===Chinese===
> 詹姆斯·克里迪尔先生，

 集邮珍品经销商，

 斯特兰德街 2,021 号。

斯帕戈把名片揣进马甲口袋，又出了门，心里纳闷：这位詹姆斯·克里迪尔先生为什么不能、不肯、或者压根没有自称“珍稀邮票商”，说句平实的英语。他沿舰队街往前走，很快找到了名片上指明的那家铺子；朝铺面外头一眼扫过去，他就看明白了：不论克里迪尔（Criedir）先生过去在这家字号做过什么生意，往后是再不会在此做了——橱窗里贴着新印的招贴，写明此屋招租。铺子里头，他见到一个矮胖的老者，正督着人把最后一批存货打包运走。老者把一双亮闪闪、打探似的眼睛转向这位记者。

===Original===
“Mr. Criedir?” said Spargo.

“The same, sir,” answered the philatelist. “You are﻿—?”

“Mr. Spargo, of the *Watchman*. You called on me.”

Mr. Criedir opened the door of a tiny apartment at the rear of the very little shop and motioned his caller to enter. He followed him in and carefully closed the door.

“Glad to see you, Mr. Spargo,” he said genially. “Take a seat, sir﻿—I’m all in confusion here﻿—giving up business, you see. Yes, I called on you. I think, having read the *Watchman* account of that Marbury affair, and having seen the murdered man’s photograph in your columns, that I can give you a bit of information.”

“Material?” asked Spargo, tersely.

Mr. Criedir cocked one of his bright eyes at his visitor. He coughed drily.

“That’s for you to decide﻿—when you’ve heard it,” he said. “I should say, considering everything, that it was material. Well, it’s this﻿—I kept open until yesterday﻿—everything as usual, you know﻿—stock in the window and so on﻿—so that anybody who was passing would naturally have thought that the business was going on, though as a matter of fact, I’m retiring﻿—retired,” added Mr. Criedir with a laugh, “last night. Now﻿—but won’t you take down what I’ve got to tell you?”
===Chinese===
“是克里迪尔先生吗？”斯帕戈说。

“正是在下，先生。”这位集邮家答道。“您是——？”

“《守望报》的斯帕戈。您刚才去找过我。”

克里迪尔先生打开店面后部一间小隔间的门，示意来客进去，自己跟着进来，又仔细地掩上了门。

“很高兴见到您，斯帕戈先生，”他和气地说，“请坐，先生——我这里乱成一团——您瞧，正在歇业。是的，我去找过您。我读了《守望报》上那篇关于马伯里案的报道，又看到贵报登的被谋杀者的照片，我想，我能给您提供一点情况。”

“有分量吗？”斯帕戈言简意赅地问。

克里迪尔先生把他那只机灵的眼睛朝来客一挑，干咳了一声。

“这得由您来判断——等您听完之后。”他说。“不过通盘掂量，我该说，是有分量的。是这样——铺子我一直开到昨天——一切照旧，您知道——橱窗里照样陈列着货色，等等——路过的任何人自然都会以为这买卖还在做下去；可实际上，我已经在收摊——收完了，”克里迪尔先生笑着补了一句，“昨晚收的。那么——您不把我讲的话记下来吗？”

===Original===
“I am taking it down,” answered Spargo. “Every word. In my head.”

Mr. Criedir laughed and rubbed his hands.

“Oh!” he said. “Ah, well, in my young days journalists used to pull out pencil and notebook at the first opportunity. But you modern young men﻿—”

“Just so,” agreed Spargo. “This information, now?”

“Well,” said Mr. Criedir, “we’ll go on then. Yesterday afternoon the man described as Marbury came into my shop. He﻿—”

“What time﻿—exact time?” asked Spargo.

“Two﻿—to the very minute by St. Clement Danes clock,” answered Mr. Criedir. “I’d swear twenty affidavits on that point. He was precisely as you’ve described him﻿—dress, everything﻿—I tell you I knew his photo as soon as I saw it. He was carrying a little box﻿—”
===Chinese===
“我正在记，”斯帕戈答道，“一字不落。记在脑子里。”

克里迪尔先生笑了，搓着两只手。

“哦！”他说。“唉，是啊，我年轻那会儿，记者们一有机会就掏出铅笔和本子。可你们这些新派年轻人——”

“正是如此。”斯帕戈表示同意。“那么，现在就说说这件事？”

“好吧，”克里迪尔先生说，“那咱们就接着讲。昨天下午，那个被称作马伯里的人进了我的铺子。他——”

“什么时间——确切的时间？”斯帕戈问。

“两点——按圣克莱门特·丹斯教堂的钟，分毫不差。”克里迪尔先生答道。“这一点我可以立二十份宣誓书。他的模样跟您笔下描写的一丝不差——衣着、样样都对——我告诉您，他的照片我一眼就认了出来。他随身带着一只小箱子——”

===Original===
“What sort of box?” said Spargo.

“A queer, old-fashioned, much-worn leather box﻿—a very miniature trunk, in fact,” replied Mr. Criedir. “About a foot square; the sort of thing you never see nowadays. It was very much worn; it attracted me for that very reason. He set it on the counter and looked at me. ‘You’re a dealer in stamps﻿—rare stamps?’ he said. ‘I am,’ I replied. ‘I’ve something here I’d like to show you,’ he said, unlocking the box. ‘It’s﻿—’ ”

“Stop a bit,” said Spargo. “Where did he take the key from with which he unlocked the box?”

“It was one of several which he carried on a split ring, and he took the bunch out of his left-hand trousers pocket,” replied Mr. Criedir. “Oh, I keep my eyes open, young gentleman! Well﻿—he opened his box. It seemed to me to be full of papers﻿—at any rate there were a lot of legal-looking documents on the top, tied up with red tape. To show you how I notice things I saw that the papers were stained with age, and that the red tape was faded to a mere washed-out pink.”

“Good﻿—good!” murmured Spargo. “Excellent! Proceed, sir.”
===Chinese===
“什么样的箱子？”斯帕戈说。

“一只古里古怪、老式的、磨得很旧的皮箱——其实就是个袖珍小行李箱。”克里迪尔先生答道。“约莫一英尺见方；如今根本见不着的那种东西。箱子磨得很厉害；恰恰因为这个，它才引我注意。他把箱子搁在柜台上，望着我。‘您是做邮票生意的——珍稀邮票？’他说。‘正是。’我答道。‘我这儿有样东西想给您瞧瞧，’他一面开箱一面说。‘是——’”

“且慢，”斯帕戈说。“他开箱的钥匙是从哪儿掏出来的？”

“他带着好几把钥匙，都穿在一个开口钥匙环上；那一串，他是从左边裤袋里掏出来的。”克里迪尔先生说。“哦，我这双眼睛可是随时睁着的，年轻人！总之——他打开了箱子。依我看，那箱子塞满了文件——至少最上头是一大摞法律文书模样的东西，用红扎带捆着。要说我的眼睛有多尖：我瞧出那些纸因年深日久已渍迹斑斑，红扎带也褪得只剩一抹洗淡了的粉色。”

“好——好！”斯帕戈喃喃道。“妙极！请讲下去，先生。”

===Original===
“He put his hand under the topmost papers and drew out an envelope,” continued Mr. Criedir. “From the envelope he produced an exceedingly rare, exceedingly valuable set of Colonial stamps﻿—the very-first ever issued. ‘I’ve just come from Australia,’ he said. ‘I promised a young friend of mine out there to sell these stamps for him in London, and as I was passing this way I caught sight of your shop. Will you buy ’em, and how much will you give for ’em?’ ”

“Prompt,” muttered Spargo.

“He seemed to me the sort of man who doesn’t waste words,” agreed Mr. Criedir. “Well, there was no doubt about the stamps, nor about their great value. But I had to explain to him that I was retiring from business that very day, and did not wish to enter into even a single deal, and that, therefore, I couldn’t do anything. ‘No matter,’ he says, ‘I daresay there are lots of men in your line of trade﻿—perhaps you can recommend me to a good firm?’ ‘I could recommend you to a dozen extra-good firms,’ I answered. ‘But I can do better for you. I’ll give you the name and address of a private buyer who, I haven’t the least doubt, will be very glad to buy that set from you and will give you a big price.’ ‘Write it down,’ he says, ‘and thank you for your trouble.’ So I gave him a bit of advice as to the price he ought to get, and I wrote the name and address of the man I referred to on the back of one of my cards.”
===Chinese===
“他把手伸到最上层的纸底下，抽出一只信封。”克里迪尔先生继续道。“从信封里，他取出一套极其罕见、极其值钱的殖民地邮票——头一批发行的那种。‘我刚从澳大利亚来，’他说，‘我答应那边一位年轻朋友，替他在伦敦把这套邮票出手；路过这里时，我瞧见了您的铺子。您收不收？肯出多少价？’”

“痛快。”斯帕戈低声道。

“在我看，他就是那种不肯浪费字句的人。”克里迪尔先生表示有同感。“唉，邮票是真真切切的，其巨大价值也毋庸置疑。可我只好向他说明：我当天正在歇业，连一桩买卖也不打算做了，因此实在无能为力。‘不要紧，’他说，‘我想干您这一行的有的是——也许您能给我推荐一家靠得住的商号？’‘靠得住的商号，我能给您数出一打来，’我答道，‘可我能帮您帮得更到位。我给您一位私人买主的姓名住址，这位买主，我半点也不怀疑，会非常乐意买下您那套邮票，而且肯出大价钱。’‘写下来吧，’他说，‘劳您驾了。’于是我给他提了一点建议，告诉他该要什么价钱，又把我说的那个人的姓名住址写在了我一张名片的背面。”

===Original===
“Whose name and address?” asked Spargo.

“Mr. Nicholas Cardlestone, 2, Pilcox Buildings, Middle Temple Lane,” replied Mr. Criedir. “Mr. Cardlestone is one of the most enthusiastic and accomplished philatelists in Europe. And I knew he didn’t possess that set of stamps.”

“I know Mr. Cardlestone,” remarked Spargo. “It was at the foot of his stairs that Marbury was found murdered.”

“Just so,” said Mr. Criedir. “Which makes me think that he was going to see Mr. Cardlestone when he was set upon, murdered, and robbed.”
===Chinese===
“谁的姓名住址？”斯帕戈问。

“尼古拉斯·卡德尔斯顿先生，中殿巷皮尔科克斯楼 2 号。”克里迪尔先生答道。“卡德尔斯顿先生是欧洲最热心、最在行的集邮家之一。而且我知道，他手里没有那套邮票。”

“我认得卡德尔斯顿先生。”斯帕戈说。“马伯里正是在他楼梯脚下被人发现遇害的。”

“正是。”克里迪尔先生说。“这就让我想到：他是在正要去见卡德尔斯顿先生的当口，遭人袭击、谋杀、劫掠的。”

===Original===
Spargo looked fixedly at the retired stamp-dealer.

“What, going to see an elderly gentleman in his rooms in the Temple, to offer to sell him philatelic rarities at﻿—past midnight?” he said. “I think﻿—not much!”

“All right,” replied Mr. Criedir. “You think and argue on modern lines﻿—which are, of course, highly superior. But﻿—how do you account for my having given Marbury Mr. Cardlestone’s address and for his having been found dead﻿—murdered﻿—at the foot of Cardlestone’s stairs a few hours later?”

“I don’t account for it,” said Spargo. “I’m trying to.”
===Chinese===
斯帕戈目不转睛地望着这位歇业的邮票商。

“什么，在半夜——过了半夜的时辰——去见一位住在律师学院寓所里的老先生，向他兜售集邮珍品？”他说。“我看——不见得！”

“好吧。”克里迪尔先生答道。“您思考和论证用的是现代路数——那自然高明得多。可是——我把卡德尔斯顿先生的地址给了马伯里，几个钟头之后他就被人发现死在卡德尔斯顿的楼梯脚下——死了，被谋杀了——这您又怎么解释？”

“我现在还解释不了，”斯帕戈说，“我正在设法解释。”

===Original===
Mr. Criedir made no comment on this. He looked his visitor up and down for a moment; gathered some idea of his capabilities, and suddenly offered him a cigarette. Spargo accepted it with a laconic word of thanks, and smoked halfway through it before he spoke again.

“Yes,” he said. “I’m trying to account. And I shall account. And I’m much obliged to you, Mr. Criedir, for what you’ve told me. Now, then, may I ask you a question or two?”

“A thousand!” responded Mr. Criedir with great geniality.

“Very well. Did Marbury say he’d call on Cardlestone?”

“He did. Said he’d call as soon as he could﻿—that day.”
===Chinese===
克里迪尔先生对此不置一词。他把来客上下打量了片刻，对这位的能耐有了几分掂量，忽然递上一支香烟。斯帕戈简短地道了声谢便接过来，抽到半截才重新开口。

“不错，”他说。“我正在设法解释。而且我一定会解释清楚。克里迪尔先生，您告诉我的这些情况，我非常感激。那么，我可以问您一两个问题吗？”

“一千个都行！”克里迪尔先生极为和气地应道。

“很好。马伯里说过他要去拜访卡德尔斯顿吗？”

“说过。他说一有机会就去——当天就去。”

===Original===
“Have you told Cardlestone what you’ve just told me?”

“I have. But not until an hour ago﻿—on my way back from your office, in fact. I met him in Fleet Street and told him.”

“Had he received a call from Marbury?”

“No! Never heard of or seen the man. At least, never heard of him until he heard of the murder. He told me he and his friend, Mr. Elphick, another philatelist, went to see the body, wondering if they could recognize it as any man they’d ever known, but they couldn’t.”
===Chinese===
“您把刚才告诉我的这些，对卡德尔斯顿说了吗？”

“说了。不过直到一个钟头前才说——其实是打您报馆回来的路上。我在舰队街碰见他，就告诉了他。”

“马伯里去拜访过他吗？”

“没有！这个人他从未听说过，也从未见过。至少，在听说这桩凶案之前从未听说过。他告诉我，他和他的朋友埃尔菲克先生——另一位集邮家——去看过尸体，想认认是不是哪位相识的人，结果没能认出来。”

===Original===
“I know they did,” said Spargo. “I saw ’em at the mortuary. Um! Well﻿—one more question. When Marbury left you, did he put those stamps in his box again, as before?”

“No,” replied Mr. Criedir. “He put them in his right-hand breast pocket, and he locked up his old box, and went off swinging it in his left hand.”

Spargo went away down Fleet Street, seeing nobody. He muttered to himself, and he was still muttering when he got into his room at the office. And what he muttered was the same thing, repeated over and over again:

“Six hours﻿—six hours﻿—six hours! Those six hours!”

Next morning the *Watchman* came out with four leaded columns of up-to-date news about the Marbury Case, and right across the top of the four ran a heavy double line of great capitals, black and staring:﻿—
===Chinese===
“我知道他们去过，”斯帕戈说。“我在停尸房见过他们俩。嗯！好——再问一个问题。马伯里离开您的时候，那些邮票还照原先那样放回箱子里了吗？”

“没有。”克里迪尔先生答道。“他把邮票放进了右胸口袋，锁好那只旧箱子，拎在左手里，一晃一晃地走了。”

斯帕戈顺着舰队街走回去，一路上目中无人。他自言自语地嘟哝着，回到报馆自己房间里时还在嘟哝。他嘟哝的翻来覆去只有同一句：

“六个钟头——六个钟头——六个钟头！那六个钟头！”

第二天早晨，《守望报》刊出整整四栏以铅条隔行排印的马伯里案最新报道，四栏顶上横贯着一道加粗的双行大写标题，黑得刺眼：

===Original===
> Who Saw John Marbury Between 3:15 p.m. and 9:15 p.m. on the Day Preceding His Murder?
===Chinese===
> 谁在凶案发生前一日的下午 3:15 至 9:15 之间见过约翰·马伯里？

## XXXVI The Final Telegram / 第三十六章 最后一封电报

===Original===
Myerst paused, to take a pull at his glass, and to look at the two amazed listeners with a smile of conscious triumph.

“In the hands of Cardlestone,” he repeated. “Now, what did I argue from that? Why, of course, that Maitland had been to Cardlestone’s rooms that night. Wasn’t he found lying dead at the foot of Cardlestone’s stairs? Aye—but who found him? Not the porter—not the police—not you, Master Spargo, with all your cleverness. The man who found Maitland lying dead there that night was—I!”

In the silence that followed, Spargo, who had been making notes of what Myerst said, suddenly dropped his pencil and thrusting his hands in his pockets sat bolt upright with a look which Breton, who was watching him seriously, could not make out. It was the look of a man whose ideas and conceptions are being rudely upset. And Myerst, too, saw it and he laughed, more sneeringly than ever.

===Chinese===
迈尔斯特停了下来，抿了一口杯中的酒，带着自觉稳操胜券的微笑，望着那两个听得目瞪口呆的人。

“落在卡德尔斯顿手里，”他重复道，“现在想想，我由这一点推出了什么？嘿，当然是：那天夜里，梅特兰到过卡德尔斯顿的房间。他不正是死在卡德尔斯顿的楼梯脚下才被人发现的吗？没错——可又是谁发现他的？不是门房——不是警察——也不是你，斯帕戈少爷，任凭你天大的聪明。那天夜里发现梅特兰倒毙在那儿的人，正是——我！”

接下来是一阵沉默。斯帕戈本一直在把迈尔斯特的话记录下来，此刻忽然丢下铅笔，双手往口袋里一插，腰板笔直地坐着，脸上露出一副神情——正郑重注视着他的布雷顿也捉摸不透。那是一个人的种种想法和成见正被粗暴颠覆时的神情。迈尔斯特也看出来了，他笑了起来，比先前更加讥诮。

===Original===
“That’s one for you, Spargo!” he said. “That surprises you—that makes you think. Now what do you think?—if one may ask.”

“I think,” said Spargo, “that you are either a consummate liar, or that this mystery is bigger than before.”

“I can lie when it’s necessary,” retorted Myerst. “Just now it isn’t necessary. I’m telling you the plain truth: there’s no reason why I shouldn’t. As I’ve said before, although you two young bullies have tied me up in this fashion, you can’t do anything against me. I’ve a power of attorney from those two old men in there, and that’s enough to satisfy anybody as to my possession of their cheques and securities. I’ve the whip hand of you, my sons, in all ways. And that’s why I’m telling you the truth—to amuse myself during this period of waiting. The plain truth, my sons!”

“In pursuance of which,” observed Breton, drily, “I think you mentioned that you were the first person to find my father lying dead?”

===Chinese===
“这下算我赢了你一分，斯帕戈！”他说，“这让你吃惊了——这让你动起脑子来了。那么你是怎么想的？——如果不介意我打听的话。”

“我嘛，”斯帕戈说，“认为你要么是个撒谎撒到了家的大骗子，要么这桩谜案比先前更加深不可测。”

“需要的时候，我自然撒得成谎，”迈尔斯特反唇相讥，“可眼下没有必要。我告诉你们的是不折不扣的实话：我没有理由不说。我早就说过，你们两个小恶霸虽然把我捆成了这副样子，却奈何不得我分毫。我手里有里头那两位老人的授权委托书，单凭这个，就足以向任何人证明，我持有他们的支票和有价证券是名正言顺的。方方面面，我都稳稳骑在你们头上，我的小子们。所以我才肯对你们说实话——也好在这等候的当口给自己解个闷。不折不扣的实话，我的小子们！”

“照这话往下推，”布雷顿冷冷地说，“我记得你方才提到，第一个发现我父亲惨死的人，是你？”

===Original===
“I was. That is—as far as I can gather. I’ll tell you all about it. As I said, I live over Cardlestone. That night I came home very late—it was well past one o’clock. There was nobody about—as a matter of fact, no one has residential chambers in that building but Cardlestone and myself. I found the body of a man lying in the entry. I struck a match and immediately recognized my visitor of the afternoon—John Marbury.
