He raised a hand, pointing down the lane. The three men followed his outstretched finger. And Spargo then saw a man’s foot, booted, grey-socked, protruding from an entry on the left hand.

“Sticking out there, just as you see it now,” said the porter. “I ain’t touched it. And so﻿—”

===Chinese===
门房朝他们身后正走出的那扇门摆了摆头。

“我听见那扇门砰的一声关上了，”他烦躁地答道，仿佛他提到的这桩事本身就叫他着恼。“我敢肯定听见了！于是我起来四处查看。接着——得，我就瞧见了那个！”

他抬起一只手，指向巷子深处。三个人顺着他那伸出的手指望去。这时斯帕戈看见一只男人的脚——穿着靴子、灰色袜子——从左手边的一个门洞里探了出来。

“就那么支棱在那儿，跟你们现在瞧见的一模一样，”门房说。“我碰都没碰。于是——”

===Original===
He paused and made a grimace as if at the memory of some unpleasant thing. Driscoll nodded comprehendingly.

“And so you went along and looked?” he suggested. “Just so﻿—just to see who it belonged to, as it might be.”

“Just to see﻿—what there was to see,” agreed the porter. “Then I saw there was blood. And then﻿—well, I made up the lane to tell one of you chaps.”

“Best thing you could have done,” said Driscoll. “Well, now then﻿—”

===Chinese===
他顿住，做了个苦脸，像是想起了什么不痛快的事。德里斯科尔会意地点了点头。

“于是你就走过去瞧了瞧？”他提示道。“正是——比方说，就想看看那是谁。”

“就是想看看——有什么可看的呗，”门房表示同意。“这一看就瞧见血了。再后来——得，我就一路跑上巷子来，找你们弟兄中的哪一个报个信。”

“你能这么办，再妥当不过了，”德里斯科尔说。“好，那么现在——”

===Original===
The little procession came to a halt at the entry. The entry was a cold and formal thing of itself; not a nice place to lie dead in, having glazed white tiles for its walls and concrete for its flooring; something about its appearance in that grey morning air suggested to Spargo the idea of a mortuary. And that the man whose foot projected over the step was dead he had no doubt: the limpness of his pose certified to it.

For a moment none of the four men moved or spoke. The two policemen unconsciously stuck their thumbs in their belts and made play with their fingers; the porter rubbed his chin thoughtfully﻿—Spargo remembered afterwards the rasping sound of this action; he himself put his hands in his pockets and began to jingle his money and his keys. Each man had his own thoughts as he contemplated the piece of human wreckage which lay before him.

===Chinese===
这一小队人马在那个门洞前停了下来。门洞本身就是个冷冰冰、一本正经的所在：墙壁贴着上釉的白瓷砖，地面铺着水泥，实在不是个死后躺倒的好地方；在那灰蒙蒙的晨气里，它那副样子让斯帕戈想到了停尸房。至于那只脚翘过了台阶的人已经死了，他毫不怀疑：那一副瘫软的姿势便是明证。

一时间，四个人谁也不动，谁也不说话。两名警察不约而同地把大拇指勾进腰带，手指头拨弄个不停；门房若有所思地搓着下巴——斯帕戈事后还记得这动作发出的锉磨声；他自己则双手插进衣袋，把口袋里的钱币和钥匙拨弄得叮当作响。每个人望着眼前这具人的残骸，都转着各自的心思。

===Original===
“You’ll notice,” suddenly observed Driscoll, speaking in a hushed voice, “You’ll notice that he’s lying there in a queer way﻿—same as if﻿—as if he’d been put there. Sort of propped up against that wall, at first, and had slid down, like.”

Spargo was taking in all the details with a professional eye. He saw at his feet the body of an elderly man; the face was turned away from him, crushed in against the glaze of the wall, but he judged the man to be elderly because of grey hair and whitening whisker; it was clothed in a good, well-made suit of grey check cloth﻿—tweed﻿—and the boots were good: so, too, was the linen cuff which projected from the sleeve that hung so limply. One leg was half doubled under the body; the other was stretched straight out across the threshold; the trunk was twisted to the wall. Over the white glaze of the tiles against which it and the shoulder towards which it had sunk were crushed there were gouts and stains of blood. And Driscoll, taking a hand out of his belt, pointed a finger at them.

“Seems to me,” he said, slowly, “seems to me as how he’s been struck down from behind as he came out of here. That blood’s from his nose﻿—gushed out as he fell. What do you say, Jim?” The other policeman coughed.

===Chinese===
“你们注意到没有，”德里斯科尔忽然开口，声音压得很低，“你们注意到没有，他躺在那儿的姿势挺蹊跷——就像——就像是被什么人搁在那儿的。起先是斜倚着那面墙撑住的架势，后来才出溜下去的。”

斯帕戈正以职业的眼光把种种细节尽收眼底。他看见自己脚边是一具上了年纪的男人的尸体；那张脸背朝着他，挤压在墙面的釉砖上，但凭着一头灰发和渐渐花白的颊须，他断定死者上了年纪；身上是一套灰色格子呢的衣服，料子上好、做工考究——粗花呢的——靴子也是好货色：从那条软软垂下的袖管里露出的亚麻袖口，同样讲究。一条腿半蜷在身子底下，另一条腿直挺挺伸出去，横在门槛上；躯干朝墙壁拧着。那张脸连同它陷靠过去的那一侧肩膀都挤压在白瓷砖上，釉面上凝着一摊摊、一斑斑的血迹。德里斯科尔从腰带里抽出一只手，伸出一根手指朝血迹点了点。

“照我看呐，”他慢吞吞地说，“照我看，他是打这儿出来的时候，叫人从背后撂倒的。那血是鼻子里的——倒下去的时候涌出来的。你说呢，吉姆（Jim）？”另一名警察咳了一声。

===Original===
“Better get the inspector here,” he said. “And the doctor and the ambulance. Dead﻿—ain’t he?”

Driscoll bent down and put a thumb on the hand which lay on the pavement.

“As ever they make ’em,” he remarked laconically. “And stiff, too. Well, hurry up, Jim!”

===Chinese===
“最好把督察请来，”他说。“还有大夫和救护车。人已经死了——对吧？”

德里斯科尔俯下身，把大拇指按在躺在人行道上的那只手上。

“死得不能再透了，”他言简意赅地说。“还僵着呢。好啦，快去，吉姆！”

===Original===
Spargo waited until the inspector arrived; waited until the hand-ambulance came. More policemen came with it; they moved the body for transference to the mortuary, and Spargo then saw the dead man’s face. He looked long and steadily at it while the police arranged the limbs, wondering all the time who it was that he gazed at, how he came to that end, what was the object of his murderer, and many other things. There was some professionalism in Spargo’s curiosity, but there was also a natural dislike that a fellow-being should have been so unceremoniously smitten out of the world.

There was nothing very remarkable about the dead man’s face. It was that of a man of apparently sixty to sixty-five years of age; plain, even homely of feature, clean-shaven, except for a fringe of white whisker, trimmed, after an old-fashioned pattern, between the ear and the point of the jaw. The only remarkable thing about it was that it was much lined and seamed; the wrinkles were many and deep around the corners of the lips and the angles of the eyes; this man, you would have said to yourself, has led a hard life and weathered storm, mental as well as physical.

===Chinese===
斯帕戈一直等到督察到来；等到手抬担架来了。跟着担架又来了几名警察；他们挪动尸体，准备转送停尸房，这时斯帕戈才看见死者的脸。警察们替尸体整理四肢的时候，他久久地、一动不动地端详着那张脸，心里始终在琢磨：自己盯着看的这个人究竟是谁，他怎么落到这般下场，凶手所图何在，还有许多别的问题。斯帕戈这份好奇里带着几分职业味道，但也有一种出于天性的不快：一个人，竟就这么不由分说地被人从世上打发了出去。

死者的脸并没有什么十分出奇之处。那是一张看上去年约六十到六十五岁之间的男人的脸；五官平淡，甚至可以说不起眼，脸刮得干干净净，只留一圈白色颊须，照老式的样式修剪在耳畔与下颌尖之间。唯一出奇的地方是那张脸满布褶痕沟壑；嘴角和眼角的皱纹又多又深；这个人，你会暗自说，过的是艰难的日子，闯过大风大浪——精神上的和肉体上的都经历过。

===Original===
Driscoll nudged Spargo with a turn of his elbow. He gave him a wink. “Better come down to the dead-house,” he muttered confidentially.

“Why?” asked Spargo.

“They’ll go through him,” whispered Driscoll. “Search him, d’ye see? Then you’ll get to know all about him, and so on. Help to write that piece in the paper, eh?”

Spargo hesitated. He had had a stiff night’s work, and until his encounter with Driscoll he had cherished warm anticipation of the meal which would be laid out for him at his rooms, and of the bed into which he would subsequently tumble. Besides, a telephone message would send a man from the *Watchman* to the mortuary. This sort of thing was not in his line now, now﻿—

===Chinese===
德里斯科尔用胳膊肘拐了拐斯帕戈，又朝他递了个眼色。“最好跟我到停尸房去一趟，”他凑近了低声说。

“为什么？”斯帕戈问。

“他们要把他浑身上下过一遍，”德里斯科尔悄声道。“搜身，明白吗？这样他的底细你就全知道了，还有种种别的。你那篇报纸文章不就好写了，嗯？”

斯帕戈犹豫了。他熬了一宿的苦工，在撞上德里斯科尔之前，满心惦记的是寓所里会给他备下的那顿饭，和饭后一骨碌滚进去的那张床。再说，一个电话就能从《守望报》派个人到停尸房去。这种事如今已经不归他管了，如今——

===Original===
“You’ll be for getting one o’ them big play-cards out with something about a mystery on it,” suggested Driscoll. “You never know what lies at the bottom o’ these affairs, no more you don’t.”

That last observation decided Spargo; moreover, the old instinct for getting news began to assert itself.

“All right,” he said. “I’ll go along with you.”

===Chinese===
“回头你们准要出那种大字招贴，登一条什么‘疑案’的，”德里斯科尔怂恿道。“这种事情的底下藏着什么，你永远说不准，谁也说不准。”

这最后一句说动了斯帕戈；况且，挖新闻的老本能也开始抬头了。

“好吧，”他说。“我跟你去。”

===Original===
And re-lighting his pipe he followed the little cortège through the streets, still deserted and quiet, and as he walked behind he reflected on the unobtrusive fashion in which murder could stalk about. Here was the work of murder, no doubt, and it was being quietly carried along a principal London thoroughfare, without fuss or noise, by officials to whom the dealing with it was all a matter of routine. Surely﻿—

“My opinion,” said a voice at Spargo’s elbow, “my opinion is that it was done elsewhere. Not there! He was put there. That’s what I say.” Spargo turned and saw that the porter was at his side. He, too, was accompanying the body.

===Chinese===
于是他重新点上烟斗，跟在这一小队送尸的人马后面，穿过依然空寂安静的街道；走在后头，他不禁寻思起凶案竟能这样不动声色地四处游走。眼前这无疑是凶杀的产物，而它正被不声不响地抬着走过伦敦一条通衢大道，无忙乱，无喧哗，抬它的官员们办起这种事来不过是例行公事。想必——

“依我看呐，”斯帕戈肘边响起一个声音，“依我看，这是在别处干的。不是在那儿！他是被人搁到那儿的。我就是这么说。”斯帕戈一转头，看见门房就在自己身边。他也是一路跟着尸体来的。

===Original===
“Oh!” said Spargo. “You think﻿—”

“I think he was struck down elsewhere and carried there,” said the porter. “In somebody’s chambers, maybe. I’ve known of some queer games in our bit of London! Well!﻿—he never came in at my lodge last night﻿—I’ll stand to that. And who is he, I should like to know? From what I see of him, not the sort to be about our place.”

“That’s what we shall hear presently,” said Spargo. “They’re going to search him.”

But Spargo was presently made aware that the searchers had found nothing. The police-surgeon said that the dead man had, without doubt, been struck down from behind by a terrible blow which had fractured the skull and caused death almost instantaneously. In Driscoll’s opinion, the murder had been committed for the sake of plunder. For there was nothing whatever on the body. It was reasonable to suppose that a man who is well dressed would possess a watch and chain, and have money in his pockets, and possibly rings on his fingers. But there was nothing valuable to be found; in fact there was nothing at all to be found that could lead to identification﻿—no letters, no papers, nothing. It was plain that whoever had struck the dead man down had subsequently stripped him of whatever was on him. The only clue to possible identity lay in the fact that a soft cap of grey cloth appeared to have been newly purchased at a fashionable shop in the West End.

===Chinese===
“哦！”斯帕戈说。“你是说——”

“我是说，他是在别处被撂倒，再弄到那儿去的，”门房说。“说不定就在哪家律师事务所里。咱们伦敦这一角出过的蹊跷勾当，我见得多了！哼！——昨晚他压根没打我的门房进来——这话我敢作保。还有，他是谁？我倒想弄明白。照我看，他不是咱们这地界走动的那路人。”

“这个我们马上就会听到，”斯帕戈说。“他们要搜他的身了。”

可是不多时，斯帕戈便获悉：搜身的人什么也没找到。法医说，死者毫无疑问是被人从背后一记凶狠的重击撂倒的，那一击击碎了颅骨，几乎当场毙命。在德里斯科尔看来，这桩凶杀是谋财害命。因为尸体上什么都没有。按常理推想，一个衣着体面的人，总该有块怀表带根表链，口袋里总该有些钱，指头上说不定还戴着戒指。可值钱的东西一样也没搜到；事实上，凡能查明身份的东西压根一样也没有——没有信，没有文件，什么都没有。显然，那个把死者撂倒的人，事后把他身上的东西剥了个精光。唯一可能指明身份的线索是：死者那顶灰呢软帽，看样子是刚在伦敦西区（West End）一家时髦铺子里买的。

===Original===
Spargo went home; there seemed to be nothing to stop for. He ate his food and he went to bed, only to do poor things in the way of sleeping. He was not the sort to be impressed by horrors, but he recognized at last that the morning’s event had destroyed his chance of rest; he accordingly rose, took a cold bath, drank a cup of coffee, and went out. He was not sure of any particular idea when he strolled away from Bloomsbury, but it did not surprise him when, half an hour later he found that he had walked down to the police station near which the unknown man’s body lay in the mortuary. And there he met Driscoll, just going off duty. Driscoll grinned at sight of him.

“You’re in luck,” he said. “ ’Tisn’t five minutes since they found a bit of grey writing paper crumpled up in the poor man’s waistcoat pocket﻿—it had slipped into a crack. Come in, and you’ll see it.”

Spargo went into the inspector’s office. In another minute he found himself staring at the scrap of paper. There was nothing on it but an address, scrawled in pencil:﻿—Ronald Breton, Barrister, King’s Bench Walk, Temple, London.

===Chinese===
斯帕戈回了家；似乎没有什么再值得逗留。他吃了饭，上了床，结果却睡得很不像样。他不是那种容易被惨事骇住的人，但到头来他也不得不承认，清晨那桩事已经断送了他的休息；于是他起身，洗了个凉水澡，喝下一杯咖啡，出了门。离开布卢姆斯伯里信步闲逛时，他心里并没有什么一定的念头；可半小时之后，他发觉自己竟一路走到了那座警察局跟前——无名男子的尸体就停在近旁的停尸房里——对此他并不意外。就在那儿，他碰上了刚要下班的德里斯科尔。德里斯科尔一见他就咧嘴笑了。

“您来得真巧，”他说。“还不到五分钟呢，他们刚在那可怜人的马甲口袋里发现一小张揉皱的灰纸——它滑进了一道夹缝里。进来吧，您亲眼瞧瞧。”

斯帕戈走进督察的办公室。不消一分钟，他已经直勾勾地盯住了那张纸片。上面除了一个用铅笔潦草写下的地址之外，什么也没有：罗纳德·布雷顿（Ronald Breton），出庭律师，王座法庭步道（King's Bench Walk），律师学院，伦敦。

## XIV The Silver Ticket / 第十四章 银质票牌

===Original===

With a sudden instinct of protection, Spargo quickly drew the girl aside from the struggling crowd, and within a moment had led her into a quiet by-street. He looked down at her as she stood recovering her breath.

“Yes?” he said quietly.

Jessie Aylmore looked up at him, smiling faintly.

“I want to speak to you,” she said. “I must speak to you.”

“Yes,” said Spargo. “But—the others? Your sister?—Breton?”

“I left them on purpose to speak to you,” she answered. “They knew I did. I am well accustomed to looking after myself.”

===Chinese===

出于一种油然而生的保护本能，斯帕戈迅速把姑娘从推挤的人群中拉到一旁，不多时便领着她走进一条僻静的小街。他低头看着她，她正站在那儿平复呼吸。

“嗯？”他轻声问道。

杰西·艾尔莫尔抬起头望着他，脸上浮着淡淡的一丝微笑。

“我想跟您谈谈，”她说，“我必须跟您谈谈。”

“好啊，”斯帕戈说，“不过——其他人呢？你姐姐呢？——布雷顿呢？”

“我是特意离开他们来找您说话的，”她答道，“他们都知道。我早就习惯自己照顾自己了。”

===Original===

Spargo moved down the by-street, motioning his companion to move with him.

“Tea,” he said, “is what you want. I know a queer, old-fashioned place close by here where you can get the best China tea in London. Come and have some.”

Jessie Aylmore smiled and followed her guide obediently. And Spargo said nothing, marching stolidly along with his thumbs in his waistcoat pockets, his fingers playing soundless tunes outside, until he had installed himself and his companion in a quiet nook in the old teahouse he had told her of, and had given an order for tea and hot teacakes to a waitress who evidently knew him. Then he turned to her.

===Chinese===

斯帕戈沿小街往前走，示意同伴跟上。

“你现在需要的是，”他说，“喝杯茶。我知道这附近有一家古怪的老式茶馆，能喝到全伦敦最好的中国茶。走吧，去喝一点。”

杰西·艾尔莫尔笑了笑，乖乖地跟着引路人走。斯帕戈一言不发，只顾闷头大步向前，两个拇指插在马甲口袋里，手指在外面无声地弹着曲子，直到把自己和同伴安顿在他所说的那家老茶馆里一个清静的角落，向一位显然认得他的女侍点了茶和热茶饼，他才转过身来面对她。

===Original===

“You want,” he said, “to talk to me about your father.”

“Yes,” she answered. “I do.”

“Why?” asked Spargo.

The girl gave him a searching look.

“Ronald Breton says you’re the man who’s written all those special articles in the *Watchman* about the Marbury case,” she answered. “Are you?”

===Chinese===

“你想跟我谈的，”他说，“是你父亲的事吧。”

“是的，”她答道，“正是。”

“为什么找我？”斯帕戈问。

姑娘用审视的目光打量了他一眼。

“罗纳德·布雷顿说，《守望报》上关于马伯里案的那些特别报道都是您写的，”她答道，“是吗？”

===Original===

“I am,” said Spargo.

“Then you’re a man of great influence,” she went on. “You can stir the public mind. Mr. Spargo—what are you going to write about my father and today’s proceedings?”

Spargo signed to her to pour out the tea which had just arrived. He seized, without ceremony, upon a piece of the hot buttered teacake, and bit a great lump out of it.

“Frankly,” he mumbled, speaking with his mouth full, “frankly, I don’t know. I don’t know—yet. But I’ll tell you this—it’s best to be candid—I shouldn’t allow myself to be prejudiced or biased in making up my conclusions by anything that you may say to me. Understand?”

Jessie Aylmore took a sudden liking to Spargo because of the unconventionality and brusqueness of his manners.

===Chinese===

“是我写的。”斯帕戈说。

“那您就是个很有影响力的人，”她接着说，“您能搅动公众的心。斯帕戈先生——关于我父亲和今天的庭审，您打算怎么写？”

斯帕戈示意她斟刚端上来的茶。他自己也不客气，抓起一块热乎乎的黄油茶饼，大大地咬下一口。

“老实说，”他嘴里塞得满满的，含混地说，“老实说，我不知道。现在还不知道。不过有一点我要告诉你——还是坦白说了好——无论你对我说什么，我都不会让自己在形成结论时受其左右、心存偏见。明白吗？”

斯帕戈这副不拘礼数、直来直去的做派，反倒让杰西·艾尔莫尔一下子对他生出好感。

===Original===

“I’m not wanting to prejudice or bias you,” she said. “All I want is that you should be very sure before you say—anything.”

“I’ll be sure,” said Spargo. “Don’t bother. Is the tea all right?”

“Beautiful!” she answered, with a smile that made Spargo look at her again. “Delightful! Mr. Spargo, tell me!—what did you think about—about what has just happened?”

Spargo, regardless of the fact that his fingers were liberally ornamented with butter, lifted a hand and rubbed his always untidy hair. Then he ate more teacake and gulped more tea.

“Look here!” he said suddenly. “I’m no great hand at talking. I can write pretty decently when I’ve a good story to tell, but I don’t talk an awful lot, because I never can express what I mean unless I’ve got a pen in my hand. Frankly, I find it hard to tell you what I think. When I write my article this evening, I’ll get all these things marshalled in proper form, and I shall write clearly about ’em. But I’ll tell you one thing I do think—I wish your father had made a clean breast of things to me at first, when he gave me that interview, or had told everything when he first went into that box.”

===Chinese===

“我不是想左右您、给您添偏见，”她说，“我只希望您在说出——任何话之前，先弄个确凿无疑。”

“我会弄确凿的，”斯帕戈说，“不用操心。茶还行吗？”

“好极了！”她答道，那微笑让斯帕戈又看了她一眼。“真舒服！斯帕戈先生，告诉我！——您怎么看——怎么看刚才发生的事？”

斯帕戈全然不顾自己的手指上沾满黄油，抬起一只手揉了揉他那向来乱糟糟的头发。接着他又吃了些茶饼，灌下几口茶。

“听我说！”他突然开口，“我这个人不大擅长讲话。手头有好故事的时候，我文章写得还算像样，可我不爱多说话，因为只要手里没有笔，我就没法把自己的意思讲明白。老实说，我觉得很难对你讲清我的想法。等今晚写文章的时候，我会把这些事都理出个章法，写得清清楚楚。不过有一件事我不妨告诉你——我倒希望你父亲当初接受我采访的时候，就把事情和盘托出，或者头一回坐进证人席时就把一切原原本本讲出来。”

===Original===

“Why?” she asked.

“Because he’s now set up an atmosphere of doubt and suspicion around himself. People’ll think—Heaven knows what they’ll think! They already know that he knows more about Marbury than he’ll tell, that—”

“But does he?” she interrupted quickly. “Do you think he does?”

“Yes!” replied Spargo, with emphasis. “I do. A lot more! If he had only been explicit at first—however, he wasn’t. Now it’s done. As things stand—look here, does it strike you that your father is in a very serious position?”

“Serious?” she exclaimed.

===Chinese===

“为什么？”她问。

“因为他如今在自己周身聚起了一团疑云。人们会想——天知道他们会怎么想！他们已经知道，关于马伯里，他知道的远比肯说的多，还有——”

“可他真的知道吗？”她急忙打断，“您觉得他真知道吗？”

“真的！”斯帕戈斩钉截铁地答道，“我是这么想的。他知道的多着呢！他当初要是把话说透——唉，他没有。如今木已成舟。就眼下的局面——我说，你有没有觉得，你父亲的处境相当不妙？”

“不妙？”她叫了起来。

===Original===

“Dangerous! Here’s the fact—he’s admitted that he took Marbury to his rooms in the Temple that midnight. Well, next morning Marbury’s found robbed and murdered in an entry, not fifty yards off!”

“Does anybody suppose that my father would murder him for the sake of robbing him of whatever he had on him?” she laughed scornfully. “My father is a very wealthy man, Mr. Spargo.”

“May be,” answered Spargo. “But millionaires have been known to murder men who held secrets.”

“Secrets!” she exclaimed.

“Have some more tea,” said Spargo, nodding at the teapot. “Look here—this way it is. The theory that people—some people—will build up (I won’t say that it hasn’t suggested itself to me) is this:—There’s some mystery about the relationship, acquaintanceship, connection, call it what you like, of your father and Marbury twenty odd years ago. Must be. There’s some mystery about your father’s life, twenty odd years ago. Must be—or else he’d have answered those questions. Very well. ‘Ha, ha!’ says the general public. ‘Now we have it!’ ‘Marbury,’ says the general public, ‘was a man who had a hold on Aylmore. He turned up. Aylmore trapped him into the Temple, killed him to preserve his own secret, and robbed him of all he had on him as a blind.’ Eh?”

===Chinese===

“是危险！事实摆在这儿——他自己承认，那天午夜是他把马伯里带到律师学院他的寓所去的。好，第二天早上，马伯里就在离那儿不到五十码的一条过道里，被人劫财害了命！”

“难道有人会以为，我父亲为了抢他身上那点东西就去杀他？”她讥诮地笑了，“我父亲是个非常有钱的人，斯帕戈先生。”

“也许吧，”斯帕戈答道，“可百万富翁为了除掉掌握自己秘密的人而行凶杀人，这种事也不是没有过。”

“秘密！”她惊呼。

“再来点茶吧，”斯帕戈朝茶壶点点头说，“听我说——事情是这样的。人们——某些人——将会编排出这么一套说法（我不敢说自己没想到过它）：二十多年前，你父亲和马伯里之间，不论你叫它关系也好、交情也好、瓜葛也好，总归有些蹊跷。准有。二十多年前，你父亲自己的生活里也有些蹊跷。准有——否则他早就回答那些问题了。好。公众就要说了：‘哈，哈！这下我们可逮着了！’‘马伯里，’公众说，‘是个拿捏着艾尔莫尔把柄的人。他冒出来了。艾尔莫尔把他诱进律师学院，为了保住自己的秘密杀了他，还把他身上的东西抢个精光，好遮人耳目。’怎么样？”

===Original===

“You think—people will say that?” she exclaimed.

“Cocksure! They’re saying it. Heard half a dozen of ’em say it, in more or less elegant fashion as I came out of that court. Of course, they’ll say it. Why, what else could they say?”

For a moment Jessie Aylmore sat looking silently into her teacup. Then she turned her eyes on Spargo, who immediately manifested a new interest in what remained of the teacakes.

“Is that what you’re going to say in your article tonight?” she asked, quietly.

“No!” replied Spargo, promptly. “It isn’t. I’m going to sit on the fence tonight. Besides, the case is sub judice. All I’m going to do is to tell, in my way, what took place at the inquest.”

===Chinese===

“您觉得——人们会这么说？”她失声道。

“笃定的！他们已经在这么说了。我走出法庭的时候，就听见五六个人在这么说，无非说法雅俗不一罢了。他们当然要这么说。不然呢，他们还能说什么？”

杰西·艾尔莫尔有一会儿坐着默默凝视自己的茶杯。随后她把目光转向斯帕戈，而斯帕戈立刻对盘中所剩的茶饼表现出新的兴趣。

“这就是您今晚文章里要写的吗？”她静静地问。

“不！”斯帕戈立刻答道，“不会的。今晚我要先骑墙观望。再说，这案子还在审理之中。我要做的，只是按我自己的方式，把验尸审讯上发生的事讲出来。”

===Original===

The girl impulsively put her hand across the table and laid it on Spargo’s big fist.

“Is it what you think?” she asked in a low voice.

“Honour bright, no!” exclaimed Spargo. “It isn’t—it isn’t! I don’t think it. I think there’s a most extraordinary mystery at the bottom of Marbury’s death, and I think your father knows an enormous lot about Marbury that he won’t tell, but I’m certain sure that he neither killed Marbury nor knows anything whatever about his death. And as I’m out to clear this mystery up, and mean to do it, nothing’ll make me more glad than to clear your father. I say, do have some more teacake? We’ll have fresh ones—and fresh tea.”

“No, thank you,” she said smiling. “And thank you for what you’ve just said. I’m going now, Mr. Spargo. You’ve done me good.”

“Oh, rot!” exclaimed Spargo. “Nothing—nothing! I’ve just told you what I’m thinking. You must go? …”

===Chinese===

姑娘一时情不自禁，把手伸过桌面，按在斯帕戈的大拳头上。

“那这是您心里的想法吗？”她低声问。

“说句真格的，不是！”斯帕戈高声道，“不是——绝不是！我不这么想。我认为马伯里的死背后藏着一桩极其离奇的谜，我也认为你父亲对马伯里知道得极多却不肯说，但我敢肯定，他既没有杀害马伯里，对他的死也毫不知情。我既然立志要解开这个谜，也非解开不可，那么再没有比还你父亲清白更让我高兴的了。我说，再来块茶饼吧？咱们叫新的——再沏壶新茶。”

“不用了，谢谢，”她微笑着说，“也谢谢您方才说的这些话。我该走了，斯帕戈先生。您让我心里好受多了。”

“嗐，胡说什么！”斯帕戈嚷道，“没什么——没什么！我不过是把心里想的告诉你罢了。你一定要走？……”

===Original===

He saw her into a taxicab presently, and when she had gone stood vacantly staring after the cab until a hand clapped him smartly on the shoulder. Turning, he found Rathbury grinning at him.

“All right, Mr. Spargo, I saw you!” he said. “Well, it’s a pleasant change to squire young ladies after being all day in that court. Look here, are you going to start your writing just now?”

“I’m not going to start my writing as you call it, until after I’ve dined at seven o’clock and given myself time to digest my modest dinner,” answered Spargo. “What is it?”

“Come back with me and have another look at that blessed leather box,” said Rathbury. “I’ve got it in my room, and I’d like to examine it for myself. Come on!”

“The thing’s empty,” said Spargo.

===Chinese===

不久他便送她上了一辆出租汽车，车走之后，他仍站在原地失神地望着车影远去，直到一只手在他肩上利落地一拍。他回过头，发现拉思伯里正冲他咧嘴笑。

“好哇，斯帕戈先生，我全看见了！”他说，“嗯，在法庭里蹲了一整天之后，换换口味陪陪年轻小姐，倒也不错。我说，你这就动笔写你的大作吗？”

“我那不叫什么‘动笔写作’，起码得等我七点钟吃过晚饭，再留点工夫消化我那顿便饭，才轮得到它，”斯帕戈答道，“什么事？”

“跟我回去，再瞧瞧那个要命的皮盒，”拉思伯里说，“它在我屋里，我想亲自查验一番。走吧！”

“那东西是空的。”斯帕戈说。

===Original===

“There might be a false bottom in it,” remarked Rathbury. “One never knows. Here, jump into this!”

He pushed Spargo into a passing taxicab, and following, bade the driver go straight to the Yard. Arrived there, he locked Spargo and himself into the drab-visaged room in which the journalist had seen him before.

“What d’ye think of today’s doings, Spargo?” he asked, as he proceeded to unlock a cupboard.

“I think,” said Spargo, “that some of you fellows must have had your ears set to tingling.”

“That’s so,” assented Rathbury. “Of course, the next thing’ll be to find out all about the Mr. Aylmore of twenty years since. When a man won’t tell you where he lived twenty years ago, what he was exactly doing, what his precise relationship with another man was—why, then, you’ve just got to find out, eh? Oh, some of our fellows are at work on the life history of Stephen Aylmore, Esq., M.P., already—you bet! Well, now, Spargo, here’s the famous box.”

===Chinese===

“说不定有夹层底呢，”拉思伯里说，“谁说得准。来，上这辆！”

他把斯帕戈推进一辆驶过的出租汽车，自己跟着上去，吩咐司机直奔苏格兰场。到了那儿，他把斯帕戈和自己一同锁进那间灰扑扑的屋子——记者从前就是在这间屋里见到他的。

“你觉得今天这一出怎么样，斯帕戈？”他一边动手开一只橱柜的锁，一边问。

“我觉得，”斯帕戈说，“你们这帮人里有的，耳朵这会儿只怕都烧得发烫了。”

“正是，”拉思伯里附和道，“当然，下一步就是把那位二十年前的艾尔莫尔先生的底细查个水落石出。一个人不肯告诉你他二十年前住在哪里、具体干的是什么营生、跟另一个人究竟是什么关系——那么，你就只好自己去查明，对吧？哦，我们有些同僚已经在着手查这位议员斯蒂芬·艾尔莫尔先生的生平了——这还用说！好，喏，斯帕戈，这就是那个大名鼎鼎的盒子。”

===Original===

The detective brought the old leather case out of the cupboard in which he had been searching, and placed it on his desk. Spargo threw back the lid and looked inside, measuring the inner capacity against the exterior lines.

“No false bottom in that, Rathbury,” he said. “There’s just the outer leather case, and the inner lining, of this old bed-hanging stuff, and that’s all. There’s no room for any false bottom or anything of that sort, d’you see?”

Rathbury also sized up the box’s capacity.

“Looks like it,” he said disappointedly. “Well, what about the lid, then? I remember there was an old box like this in my grandmother’s farmhouse, where I was reared—there was a pocket in the lid. Let’s see if there’s anything of the sort here?”

He threw the lid back and began to poke about the lining of it with the tips of his fingers, and presently he turned to his companion with a sharp exclamation.

===Chinese===

警探从他方才翻找着的橱柜里取出那只古旧的皮盒，摆在自己桌上。斯帕戈掀开盒盖往里看，把内里的容积与外部的轮廓比量了一番。

“这盒子里没有夹层底，拉思伯里，”他说，“只有外头这层皮壳，里头这层衬里——就是这种做老式床幔的料子——如此而已。哪儿还有地方塞什么夹层底之类的名堂，明白吗？”

拉思伯里也估量了一下盒子的容量。

“看来的确如此，”他失望地说，“那么，盒盖呢？我记得我祖母的农舍里——我就是在那儿长大的——也有这么一只旧盒子，盒盖里有个暗袋。看看这只是不是也有这一手？”

他把盒盖掀开，用指尖在盖子的衬里上四处摸索，不一会儿便低呼一声，转向同伴。

===Original===

“By George, Spargo!” he said. “I don’t know about any pocket, but there’s something under this lining. Feels like—here, you feel. There—and there.”

Spargo put a finger on the places indicated.

“Yes, that’s so,” he agreed. “Feels like two cards—a large and a small one. And the small one’s harder than the other. Better cut that lining out, Rathbury.”

“That,” remarked Rathbury, producing a penknife, “is just what I’m going to do. We’ll cut along this seam.”

He ripped the lining carefully open along the upper part of the lining of the lid, and looking into the pocket thus made, drew out two objects which he dropped on his blotting pad.

===Chinese===

“天哪，斯帕戈！”他说，“暗不暗袋的我说不准，可这层衬里底下有东西。摸着像——来，你摸摸。这儿——还有这儿。”

斯帕戈把手指按在他指的那两处。

“对，没错，”他表示同意，“摸着像两张卡片——一张大，一张小。小的那张比另一张硬。最好把衬里裁开，拉思伯里。”

“这个嘛，”拉思伯里掏出一把铅笔刀说道，“我正打算这么办。咱们就顺着这道缝裁。”

他顺着盒盖衬里的上半段小心裁开，朝由此形成的暗袋里一望，取出两样东西，丢在自己的吸墨纸板上。

===Original===

“A child’s photograph,” he said, glancing at one of them. “But what on earth is that?”

The object to which he pointed was a small, oblong piece of thin, much-worn silver, about the size of a railway ticket. On one side of it was what seemed to be a heraldic device or coat-of-arms, almost obliterated by rubbing; on the other, similarly worn down by friction, was the figure of a horse.

“That’s a curious object,” remarked Spargo, picking it up. “I never saw anything like that before. What can it be?”

“Don’t know—I never saw anything of the sort either,” said Rathbury. “Some old token, I should say. Now this photo. Ah—you see, the photographer’s name and address have been torn away or broken off—there’s nothing left but just two letters of what’s apparently been the name of the town—see. Er—that’s all there is. Portrait of a baby, eh?”

Spargo gave, what might have been called in anybody else but him, a casual glance at the baby’s portrait. He picked up the silver ticket again and turned it over and over.

===Chinese===

“一张孩子的照片，”他瞥了其中一样说，“可这到底是个什么玩意儿？”

他指的是一件小小的长方形物件：一片薄薄的、磨损得很厉害的银片，大小约莫跟火车票差不多。一面像是某种纹章图案或盾徽，几乎被摩挲得漫漶不清；另一面同样磨得模糊，是一匹马的图形。

“这物件可真稀奇，”斯帕戈把它拾起来说，“我从没见过这样的东西。这能是什么呢？”

“不知道——这种东西我也从没见过，”拉思伯里说，“我估摸着，是个什么旧信物吧。再看这张照片。啊——你瞧，照相师的名号和地址被撕掉或者折掉了——只剩下两个字母，看样子原先是城镇名字的一部分——瞧见没。呃——就剩这么点了。是个娃娃的相片，嗯？”

斯帕戈朝那娃娃的相片瞥了一眼——这要换了旁人，尽可称之为漫不经心的一瞥。他又拿起那枚银质票牌，翻来覆去地看。

===Original===

“Look here, Rathbury,” he said. “Let me take this silver thing. I know where I can find out what it is. At least, I think I do.”

“All right,” agreed the detective, “but take the greatest care of it, and don’t tell a soul that we found it in this box, you know. No connection with the Marbury case, Spargo, remember.”

“Oh, all right,” said Spargo. “Trust me.”

He put the silver ticket in his pocket, and went back to the office, wondering about this singular find. And when he had written his article that evening, and seen a proof of it, Spargo went into Fleet Street intent on seeking peculiar information.

===Chinese===

“我说，拉思伯里，”他说，“让我把这银玩意儿带走。我知道上哪儿能查明它是什么。至少，我想我知道。”

“行，”警探表示同意，“可你得把它当宝贝似的收好，而且千万别跟任何人说我们是从这盒子里发现的，知道吗。这事跟马伯里案毫无干系，斯帕戈，记住喽。”

“哦，放心，”斯帕戈说，“信我吧。”

他把银质票牌放进口袋，回到报馆，一路上琢磨着这件奇特的发现。当晚他写完文章、看过清样之后，便走进舰队街，一心要去寻访些特别的消息。

## XXXIV The Whip Hand / 第三十四章 占尽上风

===Original===
Spargo, almost irritable from desire to get at close grips with the objects of his long journey, shook off Breton’s hand with a growl of resentment.
“And how on earth can I waste time guessing?” he exclaimed. “Who is he?”
Breton laughed softly.
“Steady, Spargo, steady!” he said. “It’s Myerst﻿—the Safe Deposit man. Myerst!”
Spargo started as if something had bitten him.
“Myerst!” he almost shouted. “Myerst! Good Lord!﻿—why did I never think of him? Myerst! Then﻿—”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈一心只想同他长途跋涉所追寻的人正面交锋，急得几乎按捺不住，一把甩开布雷顿的手，愠怒地低吼了一声。
“难道我能把工夫白白耗在瞎猜上不成？”他嚷道，“他到底是谁？”
布雷顿轻轻笑了。
“稳住，斯帕戈，稳住！”他说，“是迈尔斯特——保险寄存库的那个人。迈尔斯特！”
斯帕戈猛然一惊，仿佛被什么东西咬了一口。
“迈尔斯特！”他几乎是喊出来的，“迈尔斯特！我的天！——我怎么从来没想过他？迈尔斯特！这么说——”

===Original===
“I don’t know why you should have thought of him,” said Breton. “But﻿—he’s there.”
Spargo took a step towards the cottage: Breton pulled him back.
“Wait!” he said. “We’ve got to discuss this. I’d better tell you what they’re doing.”
“What are they doing, then?” demanded Spargo impatiently.
“Well,” answered Breton. “They’re going through a quantity of papers. The two old gentlemen look very ill and very miserable. Myerst is evidently laying down the law to them in some fashion or other. I’ve formed a notion, Spargo.”

===Chinese===
“我不明白你凭什么就该想到他。”布雷顿说，“不过——他确实在那儿。”
斯帕戈朝小屋跨出一步，布雷顿把他拉了回来。
“等等！”他说，“这事咱们得先合计合计。我最好先告诉你他们在干什么。”
“那他们在干什么？”斯帕戈不耐烦地问。
“是这样，”布雷顿答道，“他们正在翻检一大堆文件。两位老先生看上去病恹恹的，一副愁苦不堪的样子。迈尔斯特分明是在摆出某种腔调对他们发号施令。我心里有个念头了，斯帕戈。”

===Original===
“What notion?”
“Myerst is in possession of whatever secret they have, and he’s followed them down here to blackmail them. That’s my notion.”
Spargo thought awhile, pacing up and down the river bank.
“I daresay you’re right,” he said. “Now, what’s to be done?”
Breton, too, considered matters.
“I wish,” he said at last, “I wish we could get in there and overhear what’s going on. But that’s impossible﻿—I know that cottage. The only thing we can do is this﻿—we must catch Myerst unawares. He’s here for no good. Look here!”

===Chinese===
“什么念头？”
“他们俩有什么秘密，全叫迈尔斯特抓在了手里，他一路尾随到这儿，就是要敲诈他们。这就是我的念头。”
斯帕戈在河岸上来回踱步，思量了一阵。
“恐怕你说得对。”他说，“那么，眼下该怎么办？”
布雷顿也把眼前的事盘算了一番。
“我真希望，”他终于说道，“真希望咱们能进到里面，听听他们在搞什么名堂。可那办不到——那座小屋我了解。咱们唯一的办法是这样——必须出其不意地逮住迈尔斯特。他到这儿来绝无好事。你瞧！”

===Original===
And reaching round to his hip-pocket Breton drew out a Browning revolver and wagged it in his hand with a smile.
“That’s a useful thing to have, Spargo,” he remarked. “I slipped it into my pocket the other day, wondering why on earth I did it. Now it’ll come in handy. For anything we know Myerst may be armed.”
“Well?” said Spargo.
“Come up to the cottage. If things turn out as I think they will, Myerst, when he’s got what he wants, will be off. Now, you shall get where I did just now, behind that bush, and I’ll station myself in the doorway. You can report to me, and when Myerst comes out I’ll cover him. Come on, Spargo; it’s beginning to get light already.”

===Chinese===
说着，布雷顿把手绕到后腰的裤袋里，掏出一支勃朗宁左轮手枪，笑吟吟地在手里晃了晃。
“带上这东西自有妙用，斯帕戈。”他说，“前几天我顺手把它塞进了口袋，还直纳闷自己为什么要这么干。如今可要派上用场了。谁知道呢，迈尔斯特身上说不定也带着枪。”
“然后呢？”斯帕戈说。
“到小屋跟前来。要是事情照我料想的发展，迈尔斯特一到手他要的东西，立马就会开溜。这样，你先到我方才待的地方去，躲到那丛灌木后面，我就在门口占住位置。你随时向我报告，等迈尔斯特一出来，我就用枪逼住他。来吧，斯帕戈，天已经渐渐亮了。”

===Original===
Breton cautiously led the way along the river bank, making use of such cover as the willows and alders afforded. Together, he and Spargo made their way to the front of the cottage. Arrived at the door, Breton posted himself in the porch, motioning to Spargo to creep in behind the bushes and to look through the window. And Spargo noiselessly followed his directions and slightly parting the branches which concealed him looked in through the uncurtained glass.
The interior into which he looked was rough and comfortless in the extreme. There were the bare accessories of a moorland cottage; rough chairs and tables, plastered walls, a fishing rod or two piled in a corner; some food set out on a side table. At the table in the middle of the floor the three men sat. Cardlestone’s face was in the shadow; Myerst had his back to the window; old Elphick bending over the table was laboriously writing with shaking fingers. And Spargo twisted his head round to his companion.
“Elphick,” he said, “is writing a cheque. Myerst has another cheque in his hand. Be ready!﻿—when he gets that second cheque I guess he’ll be off.”
Breton smiled grimly and nodded. A moment later Spargo whispered again.
“Look out, Breton! He’s coming.”

===Chinese===
布雷顿沿着河岸小心翼翼地在头里带路，凡是柳树赤杨能遮掩的地方都加以利用。他和斯帕戈一道摸到了小屋正面。到得门口，布雷顿在门廊里站定，示意斯帕戈爬到灌木丛后面，从窗口往里张望。斯帕戈悄没声儿地照他的吩咐行事，轻轻拨开遮掩身形的枝条，透过没挂窗帘的玻璃向屋里望去。
他望进去的这间屋子，粗陋寒碜到了极点。屋里只有荒原小屋的那几样光秃秃的家什：粗糙的桌椅，抹灰的墙面，一两根钓竿堆在角落；一张边桌上摆着些吃食。屋子正中的桌旁坐着那三个人。卡德尔斯顿的脸隐在暗影里；迈尔斯特背朝窗户；老埃尔菲克俯身桌上，正用颤抖的手指一笔一划、费力地写着什么。斯帕戈扭过头去看他的同伴。
“埃尔菲克在开支票。”他说，“迈尔斯特手里已经捏着另一张支票了。准备好！——等他拿到第二张，我估摸他就要动身了。”
布雷顿冷冷一笑，点了点头。片刻之后，斯帕戈又耳语起来。
“当心，布雷顿！他出来了。”

===Original===
Breton drew back into the angle of the porch; Spargo quitted his protecting bush and took the other angle. The door opened. And they heard Myerst’s voice, threatening, commanding in tone.
“Now, remember all I’ve said! And don’t you forget﻿—I’ve the whip hand of both of you﻿—the whip hand!”
Then Myerst turned and stepped out into the grey light﻿—to find himself confronted by an athletic young man who held the muzzle of an ugly revolver within two inches of the bridge of his nose and in a remarkably firm and steady grip. Another glance showed him the figure of a second businesslike looking young man at his side, whose attitude showed a desire to grapple with him.
“Good morning, Mr. Myerst,” said Breton with cold and ironic politeness. “We are glad to meet you so unexpectedly. And﻿—I must trouble you to put up your hands. Quick!”
Myerst made one hurried movement of his right hand towards his hip, but a sudden growl from Breton made him shift it just as quickly above his head, whither the left followed it. Breton laughed softly.

===Chinese===
布雷顿缩进门廊的一角；斯帕戈离开那丛遮蔽他的灌木，占了另一角。门开了。他们听见迈尔斯特的声音，话里带着威胁，一派发号施令的口气。
“都记着我说的话！还有，你们别忘了——如今占尽上风的是我——占尽上风！”
随后迈尔斯特转过身，一脚跨进灰蒙蒙的晨光里——却发现自己对面站着一个身手矫健的年轻人，一支凶相毕露的左轮手枪，枪口离他的鼻梁不过两英寸，而且那只手握得异常坚定沉稳。再一瞥眼，他看见身旁还有第二个模样干练的年轻人，那副架势分明是要扑上来跟他扭打。
“早安，迈尔斯特先生。”布雷顿说道，礼貌里透着冷淡与讥诮，“这样与您不期而遇，真是幸会。还有——劳驾您把手举起来。快！”
迈尔斯特的右手慌忙往腰际一探，可布雷顿陡然一声低吼，吓得他立刻把那只手改举到头顶，左手也跟着举了上去。布雷顿轻轻笑了。

===Original===
“That’s wise, Mr. Myerst,” he said, keeping his revolver steadily pointed at his prisoner’s nose. “Discretion will certainly be the better part of your valour on this occasion. Spargo﻿—may I trouble you to see what Mr. Myerst carries in his pockets? Go through them carefully. Not for papers or documents﻿—just now. We can leave that matter﻿—we’ve plenty of time. See if he’s got a weapon of any sort on him, Spargo﻿—that’s the important thing.”
Considering that Spargo had never gone through the experience of searching a man before, he made sharp and creditable work of seeing what the prisoner carried. And he forthwith drew out and exhibited a revolver, while Myerst, finding his tongue, cursed them both, heartily and with profusion.
“Excellent!” said Breton, laughing again. “Sure he’s got nothing else on him that’s dangerous, Spargo? All right. Now, Mr. Myerst, right about face! Walk into the cottage, hands up, and remember there are two revolvers behind your back. March!”
Myerst obeyed this peremptory order with more curses. The three walked into the cottage. Breton kept his eye on his captive; Spargo gave a glance at the two old men. Cardlestone, white and shaking, was lying back in his chair; Elphick, scarcely less alarmed, had risen, and was coming forward with trembling limbs.

===Chinese===
“这就明智了，迈尔斯特先生。”他说，手枪始终稳稳指着俘虏的鼻子，“眼下这情形，谨慎无疑才是您勇气的上策。斯帕戈——有劳你去看看迈尔斯特先生口袋里都装了些什么？仔仔细细搜一遍。先不用找文件文书——这会儿不急。那一桩可以搁一搁——咱们有的是时间。看看他身上带没带什么家伙，斯帕戈——这才是顶要紧的。”
考虑到斯帕戈从来没干过搜身的营生，他把查看俘虏随身之物这桩事办得干净利落，颇可称道。他随即掏出一支左轮手枪亮了出来，而迈尔斯特这时也找回了舌头，把两人劈头盖脸痛骂一顿，骂得又起劲又花哨。
“好极了！”布雷顿又笑道，“确定他身上再没别的危险玩意儿了吧，斯帕戈？很好。那么，迈尔斯特先生，向后转！举着手走进屋里去，记住你背后有两支枪。开步走！”
迈尔斯特又骂骂咧咧地服从了这道不容分说的命令。三个人走进小屋。布雷顿目不转睛地盯住俘虏；斯帕戈朝两位老人瞥了一眼。卡德尔斯顿面如死灰、浑身乱颤，瘫在椅子里；埃尔菲克的惊惶也不相上下，已经起了身，四肢发抖地迎上前来。

===Original===
“Wait a moment,” said Breton, soothingly. “Don’t alarm yourself. We’ll deal with Mr. Myerst here first. Now, Myerst, my man, sit down in that chair﻿—it’s the heaviest the place affords. Into it, now! Spargo, you see that coil of rope there. Tie Myerst up﻿—hand and foot﻿—to that chair. And tie him well. All the knots to be double, Spargo, and behind him.”
Myerst suddenly laughed. “You damned young bully!” he exclaimed. “If you put a rope round me, you’re only putting ropes round the necks of these two old villains. Mark that, my fine fellows!”
“We’ll see about that later,” answered Breton. He kept Myerst covered while Spargo made play with the rope. “Don’t be afraid of hurting him, Spargo,” he said. “Tie him well and strong. He won’t shift that chair in a hurry.”

===Chinese===
“稍等片刻。”布雷顿宽慰道，“别慌。我们得先在这儿料理迈尔斯特先生。来，迈尔斯特老兄，坐到那把椅子上去——这是全屋分量最沉的一把。坐进去！斯帕戈，你看见那卷绳子了。把迈尔斯特捆起来——连手带脚——捆在那把椅子上。捆结实些。所有的结一律打成双结，斯帕戈，都结到他背后去。”
迈尔斯特忽然大笑起来。“你们这两个该死的小恶霸！”他嚷道，“你们要是拿绳子捆我，那就是把绳索套上这两个老恶棍的脖子。给我记牢了，两位漂亮的小伙子！”
“那个回头再算。”布雷顿答道。斯帕戈摆弄绳子的时候，他的枪口始终罩着迈尔斯特。“别怕弄疼了他，斯帕戈。”他说，“捆得又牢又紧。这样一来，他就休想轻易挪动那把椅子了。”

===Original===
Spargo spliced his man to the chair in a fashion that would have done credit to a sailor. He left Myerst literally unable to move either hand or foot, and Myerst cursed him from crown to heel for his pains. “That’ll do,” said Breton at last. He dropped his revolver into his pocket and turned to the two old men. Elphick averted his eyes and sank into a chair in the darkest corner of the room: old Cardlestone shook as with palsy and muttered words which the two young men could not catch. “Guardian,” continued Breton, “don’t be frightened! And don’t you be frightened, either, Mr. Cardlestone. There’s nothing to be afraid of, just yet, whatever there may be later on. It seems to me that Mr. Spargo and I came just in time. Now, guardian, what was this fellow after?”
Old Elphick lifted his head and shook it; he was plainly on the verge of tears; as for Cardlestone, it was evident that his nerve was completely gone. And Breton pointed Spargo to an old corner cupboard.
“Spargo,” he said, “I’m pretty sure you’ll find whisky in there. Give them both a stiff dose: they’ve broken up. Now, guardian,” he continued, when Spargo had carried out this order, “what was he after? Shall I suggest it? Was it﻿—blackmail?”
Cardlestone began to whimper; Elphick nodded his head. “Yes, yes!” he muttered. “Blackmail! That was it﻿—blackmail. He﻿—he got money﻿—papers﻿—from us. They’re on him.”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈把他的俘虏往椅子上捆扎，那手法连水手见了也要称道。他捆得迈尔斯特当真手脚都动弹不得，迈尔斯特为报答他这番辛苦，把他从头到脚骂了个通透。“行了。”布雷顿终于说道。他把手枪放回口袋，转身面对两位老人。埃尔菲克把目光避开，颓然坐进屋里最暗角落的一把椅子；老卡德尔斯顿抖得如同中了麻痹，嘴里咕哝着两个年轻人听不真切的话。“监护人，”布雷顿接着说，“别害怕！您也别害怕，卡德尔斯顿先生。眼下还没有什么可怕的，往后的事往后再说。依我看，斯帕戈先生和我来得正是时候。好了，监护人，这家伙要的是什么？”
老埃尔菲克抬起头，又摇了摇头；他分明已经到了落泪的边缘；至于卡德尔斯顿，他的胆气显然已丧失殆尽。布雷顿指了指墙角一只旧橱，示意斯帕戈过去。
“斯帕戈，”他说，“我敢说那里面就有威士忌。给他们两位各斟上一大杯：他们已经垮了。好了，监护人，”斯帕戈照办之后，他接着说，“他要的是什么？要我替您说出来吗？是不是——敲诈？”
卡德尔斯顿呜咽起来；埃尔菲克点了点头。“是的，是的！”他喃喃道，“敲诈！就是这么回事——敲诈。他——他从我们这儿弄走了钱——还有文件。都在他身上。”

===Original===
Breton turned on the captive with a look of contempt.
“I thought as much, Mr. Myerst,” he said. “Spargo, let’s see what he has on him.”
Spargo began to search the prisoner’s pockets. He laid out everything on the table as he found it. It was plain that Myerst had contemplated some sort of flight or a long, long journey. There was a quantity of loose gold; a number of banknotes of the more easily negotiated denominations; various foreign securities, realizable in Paris. And there was an open cheque, signed by Cardlestone for ten thousand pounds, and another, with Elphick’s name at the foot, also open, for half that amount. Breton examined all these matters as Spargo handed them out. He turned to old Elphick.
“Guardian,” he said, “why have you or Mr. Cardlestone given this man these cheques and securities? What hold has he on you?”

===Chinese===
布雷顿带着一脸轻蔑转向那俘虏。
“我料想也就是这么回事，迈尔斯特先生。”他说，“斯帕戈，来看看他身上都有些什么。”
斯帕戈开始搜查俘虏的口袋，把寻到的东西逐样摆在桌上。显而易见，迈尔斯特盘算过要潜逃，或是来一场漫漫的长途远行。有一批散金；若干最容易脱手的面额的钞票；各式可在巴黎兑现的外国有价证券。还有一张不记名支票，卡德尔斯顿签发，票面一万英镑；另有一张，末尾签着埃尔菲克的名字，同样不记名，数目是那张的一半。斯帕戈逐样递过来，布雷顿逐件查验。他转向老埃尔菲克。
“监护人，”他说，“您或者卡德尔斯顿先生，为什么把支票和证券给了这个人？他拿住了你们什么把柄？”

===Original===
Old Cardlestone began to whimper afresh; Elphick turned a troubled face on his ward.
“He﻿—he threatened to accuse us of the murder of Marbury!” he faltered. “We﻿—we didn’t see that we had a chance.”
“What does he know of the murder of Marbury and of you in connection with it?” demanded Breton. “Come﻿—tell me the truth now.”
“He’s been investigating﻿—so he says,” answered Elphick. “He lives in that house in Middle Temple Lane, you know, in the top-floor rooms above Cardlestone’s. And﻿—and he says he’s the fullest evidence against Cardlestone﻿—and against me as an accessory after the fact.”

===Chinese===
老卡德尔斯顿又呜咽起来；埃尔菲克满脸愁苦地望着自己的被监护人。
“他——他威胁说，要告发我们谋杀马伯里！”他结结巴巴地说，“我们——我们实在想不出还有什么路可走。”
“关于马伯里谋杀案，关于你们同这案子的干系，他都知道些什么？”布雷顿追问道，“来吧——现在就把真相告诉我。”
“他一直在查——他自己是这么说的。”埃尔菲克答道，“你知道，他就住在中殿巷那所房子里，在卡德尔斯顿楼上的顶层房间。他——他说他掌握着对付卡德尔斯顿的最齐全的证据——还说我是事后从犯。”

===Original===
“And﻿—it’s a lie?” asked Breton.
“A lie!” answered Elphick. “Of course, it’s a lie. But﻿—he’s so clever that﻿—that﻿—”
“That you don’t know how you could prove it otherwise,” said Breton. “Ah! And so this fellow lives over Mr. Cardlestone there, does he? That may account for a good many things. Now we must have the police here.” He sat down at the table and drew the writing materials to him. “Look here, Spargo,” he continued. “I’m going to write a note to the superintendent of police at Hawes﻿—there’s a farm half a mile from here where I can get a man to ride down to Hawes with the note. Now, if you want to send a wire to the *Watchman*, draft it out, and he’ll take it with him.”

===Chinese===
“那么——这是谎话？”布雷顿问。
“谎话！”埃尔菲克答道，“当然是谎话。可是——他太精明了，精明得——精明得——”
“精明得你们不知道怎样才能证明他在撒谎。”布雷顿说，“啊！这么说，这家伙就住在卡德尔斯顿先生的楼上，是吗？这样一来，许多事情就说得通了。现在必须把警察叫来。”他在桌边坐下，把纸笔拉到面前。“听我说，斯帕戈，”他接着说，“我要给霍伊斯的警察局长写张便条——离这儿半英里有座农场，我在那儿能找个人骑马把条子捎到霍伊斯去。你要是想给《守望报》发电报，就把电文拟好，让他一并带去。”

===Original===
Elphick began to move in his corner.
“Must the police come?” he said. “Must﻿—”
“The police must come,” answered Breton firmly. “Go ahead with your wire, Spargo, while I write this note.”
Three quarters of an hour later, when Breton came back from the farm, he sat down at Elphick’s side and laid his hand on the old man’s.
“Now, guardian,” he said, quietly, “you’ve got to tell us the truth.”

===Chinese===
埃尔菲克在他的角落里挪动了一下。
“非得叫警察来吗？”他说，“非得——”
“警察非来不可。”布雷顿斩钉截铁地答道，“你只管拟你的电报，斯帕戈，我来写条子。”
三刻钟后，布雷顿从农场回来，在埃尔菲克身旁坐下，把手搁在老人手上。
“好了，监护人，”他平静地说，“您得把真相告诉我们了。”

## XVI The “Yellow Dragon” / 第十六章 “黄龙”旅店

===Original===
Spargo, changing his clothes, washing away the dust of his journey, in that old-fashioned lavender-scented bedroom, busied his mind in further speculations on his plan of campaign in Market Milcaster. He had no particularly clear plan. The one thing he was certain of was that in the old leather box which the man whom he knew as John Marbury had deposited with the London and Universal Safe Deposit Company, he and Rathbury had discovered one of the old silver tickets of Market Milcaster racecourse, and that he, Spargo, had come to Market Milcaster, with the full approval of his editor, in an endeavour to trace it. How was he going to set about this difficult task?

“The first thing,” said Spargo to himself as he tied a new tie, “is to have a look round. That’ll be no long job.”

For he had already seen as he approached the town, and as he drove from the station to the “Yellow Dragon” Hotel, that Market Milcaster was a very small place. It chiefly consisted of one long, wide thoroughfare﻿—the High Street﻿—with smaller streets leading from it on either side. In the High Street seemed to be everything that the town could show﻿—the ancient parish church, the town hall, the market cross, the principal houses and shops, the bridge, beneath which ran the river whereon ships had once come up to the town before its mouth, four miles away, became impassably silted up. It was a bright, clean, little town, but there were few signs of trade in it, and Spargo had been quick to notice that in the “Yellow Dragon,” a big, rambling old hostelry, reminiscent of the old coaching days, there seemed to be little doing. He had eaten a bit of lunch in the coffee-room immediately on his arrival; the coffee-room was big enough to accommodate a hundred and fifty people, but beyond himself, an old gentleman and his daughter, evidently tourists, two young men talking golf, a man who looked like an artist, and an unmistakable honeymooning couple, there was no one in it. There was little traffic in the wide street beneath Spargo’s windows; little passage of people to and fro on the sidewalks; here a countryman drove a lazy cow as lazily along; there a farmer in his light cart sat idly chatting with an aproned tradesman, who had come out of his shop to talk to him. Over everything lay the quiet of the sunlight of the summer afternoon, and through the open windows stole a faint, sweet scent of the new-mown hay lying in the meadows outside the old houses.

“A veritable Sleepy Hollow,” mused Spargo. “Let’s go down and see if there’s anybody to talk to. Great Scott!﻿—to think that I was in the poisonous atmosphere of the Octoneumenoi only sixteen hours ago!”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈在那间老式而透着薰衣草香的卧房里换衣服、洗去旅途风尘，脑子则在进一步盘算他在马基特·米尔卡斯特的作战方略。他并没有什么特别明确的计划。他唯一拿得准的是：在他所认识的那个名叫约翰·马伯里的人存放在伦敦万国保险寄存库的那只古旧皮盒里，他和拉思伯里发现了一枚马基特·米尔卡斯特赛马场的老银质票牌；而他斯帕戈此番前来马基特·米尔卡斯特——这是得了主编完全首肯的——就是要设法追查这枚票牌的来历。这件棘手的差事，他究竟该从哪儿下手呢？

“头一件事，”斯帕戈一面往颈间系一条新领带，一面对自己说，“是四处转一转、看一看。这费不了多大工夫。”

因为他临进镇时、从车站坐车来“黄龙”旅店的路上早已看清，马基特·米尔卡斯特是个很小的去处。它主要就是一条又长又宽的大街——高街——两侧岔出些小街小巷。镇上拿得出手的东西似乎全在高街上：古老的教区教堂、市政厅、市场十字碑、主要的宅第和商铺，还有一座桥——桥下流过一条河，昔日海船曾溯河而上直抵镇边，后来河口在四英里外淤塞得无法通航，这般光景才成了过去。这是一座明净鲜亮的小镇，却几乎看不出什么买卖营生。斯帕戈早已留意到，“黄龙”旅店这家宽宽散散的大老客栈，还留着几分驿马车时代的旧风貌，里头似乎也无甚生意。他一到便在咖啡室里匆匆吃了些午餐；那咖啡室大得能容一百五十人就餐，可是除了他自己、一对一看便知是游客的父女、两个聊高尔夫的年轻人、一个模样像画家的男子，还有一对一望而知的蜜月夫妻，再没有旁人。斯帕戈窗下那条宽阔的街道上车马寥寥；人行道上行人不来也不往；这边一个乡下人懒洋洋地赶着一头同样懒洋洋的母牛；那边一个农夫坐在轻便马车里，正跟一个系围裙的店主有一搭没一搭地闲磕牙——店主是从铺子里出来跟他说话的。一切都浸在夏日午后阳光的静谧里，敞开的窗间悄悄渗进新割干草的淡淡甜香，草就堆在老宅子外头的草场上。

“好一个名副其实的睡谷（Sleepy Hollow），”斯帕戈暗自沉吟，“下楼去瞧瞧有没有人可以攀谈。我的老天！——想想十六个钟头之前，我还泡在奥克托努梅诺伊那乌烟瘴气的浊气里呢！”

===Original===
Spargo, after losing himself in various corridors and passages, finally landed in the wide, stone-paved hall of the old hotel, and with a sure instinct turned into the bar-parlour which he had noticed when he entered the place. This was a roomy, comfortable, bow-windowed apartment, looking out upon the High Street, and was furnished and ornamented with the usual appurtenances of country-town hotels. There were old chairs and tables and sideboards and cupboards, which had certainly been made a century before, and seemed likely to endure for a century or two longer; there were old prints of the road and the chase, and an old oil-painting or two of red-faced gentlemen in pink coats; there were foxes’ masks on the wall, and a monster pike in a glass case on a side-table; there were ancient candlesticks on the mantelpiece and an antique snuffbox set between them. Also there was a small, old-fashioned bar in a corner of the room, and a new-fashioned young woman seated behind it, who was yawning over a piece of fancy needlework, and looked at Spargo when he entered as Andromeda may have looked at Perseus when he made arrival at her rock. And Spargo, treating himself to a suitable drink and choosing a cigar to accompany it, noted the look, and dropped into the nearest chair.

“This,” he remarked, eyeing the damsel with enquiry, “appears to me to be a very quiet place.”

“Quiet!” exclaimed the lady. “Quiet?”

“That,” continued Spargo, “is precisely what I observed. Quiet. I see that you agree with me. You expressed your agreement with two shades of emphasis, the surprised and the scornful. We may conclude, thus far, that the place is undoubtedly quiet.”

===Chinese===
斯帕戈在几条走廊过道里转得七荤八素，末了总算来到老旅馆那石板铺地的宽敞门厅，又凭着一股笃定的直觉，拐进了进门时就留意到的那间酒吧雅座。这是一间宽敞舒适、带凸窗的屋子，俯临高街，陈设装点则是乡野镇集旅馆惯有的那一套：老桌老椅、老餐具柜老碗橱，一准是一个世纪前的做工，瞧那结实劲儿，再撑个一两百年也不在话下；墙上挂着驿路与围猎的老版画，还有一两幅油画，画的是穿粉红猎装的红脸膛绅士；壁上悬着几张狐狸头标本，边桌上一只玻璃罩里陈着一条大得吓人的狗鱼；壁炉台上立着几支古旧烛台，当中搁一只古董鼻烟盒。屋子一角还有一座小巧的老式酒柜，柜台后头坐着个新派打扮的年轻姑娘，正对着一幅花式针线活打呵欠；斯帕戈进门时她抬眼望他，那副眼神，想必安德洛墨达（Andromeda）当年在礁石上望见珀尔修斯（Perseus）驾到时也正是这般。斯帕戈给自己要了杯对路的酒，又拣了支雪茄作陪，把那眼色收进眼里，一屁股坐进最近的一把椅子。

“这地方，”他打量着那姑娘，语带探询，“依我看可真是个清静所在。”

“清静！”这位小姐失声道，“清静？”

“正是，”斯帕戈接着说，“我方才说的正是这两个字——清静。我看您是赞同我的。您用了两种口气表达赞同，一是惊讶的，一是轻蔑的。至此我们不妨断定：此地无疑是个清静地方。”

===Original===
The damsel looked at Spargo as if she considered him in the light of a new specimen, and picking up her needlework she quitted the bar and coming out into the room took a chair near his own.

“It makes you thankful to see a funeral go by here,” she remarked. “It’s about all that one ever does see.”

“Are there many?” asked Spargo. “Do the inhabitants die much of inanition?”

The damsel gave Spargo another critical inspection.

“Oh, you’re joking!” she said. “It’s well you can. Nothing ever happens here. This place is a back number.”

“Even the back numbers make pleasant reading at times,” murmured Spargo. “And the backwaters of life are refreshing. Nothing doing in this town, then?” he added in a louder voice.

===Chinese===
那姑娘瞧着斯帕戈，仿佛把他当作一件新标本端详；随后她拿起针线活，走出柜台，进了屋子，在他近旁的一把椅子上落了座。

“在这儿，能望见一支出殡的队伍走过，你就该谢天谢地了，”她说，“这差不多是人们所能见着的全部光景了。”

“这样的多吗？”斯帕戈问，“镇上的居民可会因日子太寡淡而大批凋零？”

那姑娘又把斯帕戈挑剔地打量了一番。

“哦，您是说着玩呢！”她说，“您还说得起玩话，倒也不坏。这儿什么事都不曾有过。这地方就是一份过了期的旧刊。”

“过期的旧刊有时读来也蛮有滋味，”斯帕戈喃喃道，“生活的死水回湾也自有它清凉提神的地方。这么说，这镇上是一点动静也没有了？”他提高了些嗓门，又补上一句。

===Original===
“Nothing!” replied his companion. “It’s fast asleep. I came here from Birmingham, and I didn’t know what I was coming to. In Birmingham you see as many people in ten minutes as you see here in ten months.”

“Ah!” said Spargo. “What you are suffering from is dullness. You must have an antidote.”

“Dullness!” exclaimed the damsel. “That’s the right word for Market Milcaster. There’s just a few regular old customers drop in here of a morning, between eleven and one. A stray caller looks in﻿—perhaps during the afternoon. Then, at night, a lot of old fogies sit round that end of the room and talk about old times. Old times, indeed!﻿—what they want in Market Milcaster is new times.”

Spargo pricked up his ears.

“Well, but it’s rather interesting to hear old fogies talk about old times,” he said. “I love it!”

“Then you can get as much of it as ever you want here,” remarked the barmaid. “Look in tonight any time after eight o’clock, and if you don’t know more about the history of Market Milcaster by ten than you did when you sat down, you must be deaf. There are some old gentlemen drop in here every night, regular as clockwork, who seem to feel that they couldn’t go to bed unless they’ve told each other stories about old days which I should think they’ve heard a thousand times already!”

===Chinese===
“一点动静也没有！”他的同伴答道，“这镇子睡得死死的。我是从伯明翰（Birmingham）来的，来之前压根不知道自己要落到个什么地步。在伯明翰，十分钟里见到的人，比这儿十个月见到的还多。”

“啊！”斯帕戈说，“您这是害了烦闷症。那得有一剂解药才是。”

“烦闷！”姑娘失声道，“这词儿正配马基特·米尔卡斯特。每天上午十一点到一点，有那么几位老主顾熟客进来坐坐。偶尔有个把闲人进来张一张——多半在下午。到了晚上，就有一帮老古板围着屋子那一头坐下，谈说往昔。往昔，哼！——马基特·米尔卡斯特要的是新气象！”

斯帕戈竖起了耳朵。

“不过，听听老古板们谈说往昔，倒也蛮有意思，”他说，“我就爱听这个！”

“那您上这儿来，管保管够，”女招待说，“今晚八点过后的随便什么工夫进来，要是到十点钟您对马基特·米尔卡斯特的历史不曾比落座时多知道几分，那您准是个聋子。有几位老绅士每晚必到，跟钟表一样准，仿佛不当着彼此把那些老日子的故事讲上一遍就上不了床——那些故事我估摸他们早已听过一千遍了！”

===Original===
“Very old men?” asked Spargo.

“Methuselahs,” replied the lady. “There’s old Mr. Quarterpage, across the way there, the auctioneer, though he doesn’t do any business now﻿—they say he’s ninety, though I’m sure you wouldn’t take him for more than seventy. And there’s Mr. Lummis, further down the street﻿—he’s eighty-one. And Mr. Skene, and Mr. Kaye﻿—they’re regular patriarchs. I’ve sat here and listened to them till I believe I could write a history of Market Milcaster since the year One.”

“I can conceive of that as a pleasant and profitable occupation,” said Spargo.

He chatted a while longer in a fashion calculated to cheer the barmaid’s spirits, after which he went out and strolled around the town until seven o’clock, the “Dragon’s” hour for dinner. There were no more people in the big coffee-room than there had been at lunch and Spargo was glad, when his solitary meal was over, to escape to the bar-parlour, where he took his coffee in a corner near to that sacred part in which the old townsmen had been reported to him to sit.

“And mind you don’t sit in one of their chairs,” said the barmaid, warningly. “They all have their own special chairs and their special pipes there on that rack, and I suppose the ceiling would fall in if anybody touched pipe or chair. But you’re all right there, and you’ll hear all they’ve got to say.”

===Chinese===
“很老了吗？”斯帕戈问。

“一个个都是玛土撒拉，”这位小姐答道，“对面街上那位夸特佩奇老先生，拍卖师，虽说如今不开张了——据说他九十了，不过我敢保您看他顶多当他七十。还有再往街那头去的拉米斯先生——他八十一。还有斯基恩先生和凯伊先生——全是不折不扣的老族长。我坐在这儿听他们聊，听得只怕连一部打公元元年写起的马基特·米尔卡斯特史都能写出来了。”

“这倒不失为一桩又惬意又有进项的营生，”斯帕戈说。

他又陪她说了一阵子话，那口吻颇能给女招待解闷提神；随后他出了门，在镇上信步逛到七点——“龙旅店”开晚饭的钟点。大咖啡室里的人并不比午饭时多，一顿独食用完，斯帕戈乐得抽身遁进酒吧雅座，在紧挨着那一方圣地的角落里喝起咖啡——据女招待报称，镇上的老先生们就惯坐在那里。

“留神别坐了他们的椅子，”女招待告诫道，“他们各有各的专座，烟斗也各是各的，全搁在那架子上；我看呀，谁要是碰了一杆烟斗或一把椅子，天花板都得塌下来。不过您坐那儿稳当，他们说些什么，您全能听见。”

===Original===
To Spargo, who had never seen anything of the sort before, and who, twenty-four hours previously, would have believed the thing impossible, the proceedings of that evening in the bar-parlour of the “Yellow Dragon” at Market Milcaster were like a sudden transference to the eighteenth century. Precisely as the clock struck eight and a bell began to toll somewhere in the recesses of the High Street, an old gentleman walked in, and the barmaid, catching Spargo’s eye, gave him a glance which showed that the play was about to begin.

“Good evening, Mr. Kaye,” said the barmaid. “You’re first tonight.”

“Evening,” said Mr. Kaye and took a seat, scowled around him, and became silent. He was a tall, lank old gentleman, clad in rusty black clothes, with a pointed collar sticking up on both sides of his fringe of grey whisker and a voluminous black neckcloth folded several times round his neck, and by the expression of his countenance was inclined to look on life severely. “Nobody been in yet?” asked Mr. Kaye.

“No, but here’s Mr. Lummis and Mr. Skene,” replied the barmaid.

Two more old gentlemen entered the bar-parlour. Of these, one was a little, dapper-figured man, clad in clothes of an eminently sporting cut, and of very loud pattern; he sported a bright blue necktie, a flower in his lapel, and a tall white hat, which he wore at a rakish angle. The other was a big, portly, bearded man with a Falstaffian swagger and a rakish eye, who chaffed the barmaid as he entered, and gave her a good-humoured chuck under the chin as he passed her. These two also sank into chairs which seemed to have been specially designed to meet them, and the stout man slapped the arms of his as familiarly as he had greeted the barmaid. He looked at his two cronies.

===Chinese===
对斯帕戈来说——这般场面他生平未见，二十四小时之前若说与他听，他必斥为绝无可能——那天晚上在马基特·米尔卡斯特“黄龙”旅店酒吧雅座里上演的一幕，恍如一步跨回了十八世纪。时钟正敲八下，高街深处什么地方的钟声叮当响起，一位老绅士应声而入；女招待接住斯帕戈的目光，递来一记眼色：好戏就要开场了。

“晚上好，凯伊先生，”女招待说，“今晚您是头一位。”

“晚上好，”凯伊先生说着落了座，朝四下里绷着脸扫了一圈，便不再言语。他是位瘦高挑的老绅士，一身乌黑衣裳已磨出锈色，花白络腮胡两边竖着尖角硬领，脖子上一道道缠着宽大的黑色颈巾；单看那副神情，便知他素来板着严峻的面孔看待人生。“还没人来？”凯伊先生问。

“还没有，不过拉米斯先生和斯基恩先生这就到了，”女招待答道。

又有两位老绅士走进酒吧雅座。这两人，一个是矮小利落的身量，穿一身极够运动派头的衣裳，花样也花哨得可以；他系一条亮蓝领带，翻领上别一朵花，头上那顶白色高帽歪戴出一副斜挑的神气。另一个是位高大壮硕、蓄着胡须的胖子，一派福斯塔夫（Falstaff）式的昂然步态和一双顾盼有神的眼，进门时跟女招待打趣，走过她身边时又笑呵呵地在她下巴上轻轻一托。这两位也各自陷进椅子，那椅子仿佛是专为他们量身打造的；胖子拍打自己椅子的扶手，那份熟络劲儿不亚于他方才招呼女招待。他看了看他的两位老伙计。

===Original===
“Well?” he said, “Here’s three of us. And there’s a symposium.”

“Wait a bit, wait a bit,” said the dapper little man. “Grandpa’ll be here in a minute. We’ll start fair.”

The barmaid glanced out of the window.

“There’s Mr. Quarterpage coming across the street now,” she announced. “Shall I put the things on the table?”

“Aye, put them on, my dear, put them on!” commanded the fat man. “Have all in readiness.”

===Chinese===
“怎么着？”他说，“咱们到齐三个了。这会饮便可开场了。”

“慢着点儿，慢着点儿，”那利落的小个子说，“老祖宗这就到。咱们要开场，就等齐了人公平开场。”

女招待朝窗外望了一眼。

“夸特佩奇先生正穿过街来呢，”她通报道，“这就把东西摆上桌吗？”

“摆上，亲爱的，摆上！”胖子吩咐道，“各样都备齐喽。”

===Original===
The barmaid thereupon placed a round table before the sacred chairs, set out upon it a fine old punch-bowl and the various ingredients for making punch, a box of cigars, and an old leaden tobacco-box, and she had just completed this interesting prelude to the evening’s discourse when the door opened again and in walked one of the most remarkable old men Spargo had ever seen. And by this time, knowing that this was the venerable Mr. Benjamin Quarterpage, of whom Crowfoot had told him, he took good stock of the newcomer as he took his place amongst his friends, who on their part received him with ebullitions of delight which were positively boyish.

Mr. Quarterpage was a youthful buck of ninety﻿—a middle-sized, sturdily-built man, straight as a dart, still active of limb, clear-eyed, and strong of voice. His clean-shaven old countenance was ruddy as a sun-warmed pippin; his hair was still only silvered; his hand was steady as a rock. His clothes of buff-coloured whipcord were smart and jaunty, his neckerchief as gay as if he had been going to a fair. It seemed to Spargo that Mr. Quarterpage had a pretty long lease of life before him even at his age.

Spargo, in his corner, sat fascinated while the old gentlemen began their symposium. Another, making five, came in and joined them﻿—the five had the end of the bar-parlour to themselves. Mr. Quarterpage made the punch with all due solemnity and ceremony; when it was ladled out each man lighted his pipe or took a cigar, and the tongues began to wag. Other folk came and went; the old gentlemen were oblivious of anything but their own talk. Now and then a young gentleman of the town dropped in to take his modest half-pint of bitter beer and to dally in the presence of the barmaid; such looked with awe at the patriarchs: as for the patriarchs themselves they were lost in the past.

Spargo began to understand what the damsel behind the bar meant when she said that she believed she could write a history of Market Milcaster since the year One. After discussing the weather, the local events of the day, and various personal matters, the old fellows got to reminiscences of the past, telling tale after tale, recalling incident upon incident of long years before. At last they turned to memories of racing days at Market Milcaster. And at that Spargo determined on a bold stroke. Now was the time to get some information. Taking the silver ticket from his purse, he laid it, the heraldic device uppermost, on the palm of his hand, and approaching the group with a polite bow, said quietly:

“Gentlemen, can any of you tell me anything about that?”

===Chinese===
女招待随即在那些神圣的椅子跟前摆开一张圆桌，端上一只精美的老潘趣酒钵和调制潘趣酒的各样材料、一盒雪茄，还有一只老旧的铅皮烟草盒；她刚把晚间清谈这出戏的有趣序曲布置停当，门又开了，走进一位斯帕戈生平所见最不同凡响的老人。这时他已知道，来者便是克劳富特向他提起过的那位德高望重的本杰明·夸特佩奇先生，于是趁老人在朋友们中间落座，把他细细打量了一番；而那几位朋友迎接他时那阵欢腾雀跃，简直孩子气到了家。

夸特佩奇先生是位九十岁的翩翩少年郎——中等身量，筋骨结实，身板笔直如镖，手脚仍健，目光清明，嗓音洪亮。他那张修得干干净净的老脸红润得如同晒足了太阳的绯苹果；头发也不过刚刚泛银；手掌稳如磐石。一身米黄色马裤呢衣裳穿得利落潇洒，颈巾扎得花哨，仿佛正要去赶集赴会。在斯帕戈看来，夸特佩奇先生纵到了这般年纪，这条命的租期往后的年头还长着呢。

斯帕戈坐在角落里，出神地看老先生们开始他们的会饮。又进来一位，凑足五个——酒吧雅座那一头归他们五人独享。夸特佩奇先生满合规矩、郑重其事地调起潘趣酒；酒一分舀定，人人点起烟斗或取过雪茄，舌头便滚将起来。旁人来了又去；老先生们浑然忘我，只顾自家的谈话。偶尔有镇上的年轻绅士踱进来，喝他那客谦虚的半品脱苦啤酒，顺便在女招待跟前厮磨片刻；这类后生望那几位老族长，目光里满是敬畏：至于老族长们自己，则早已迷失在旧时光里了。

斯帕戈这才开始明白，柜台后那位姑娘说她相信自己能写一部打公元元年起的马基特·米尔卡斯特史，是什么意思。老先生们谈罢天气、当日镇上的新闻和种种私事，便转入对往昔的追忆，一个故事接一个故事，一桩旧事叠一桩旧事，全是多年前的光景。末了，话头转到了马基特·米尔卡斯特赛马年月的记忆上。斯帕戈一听，决意行一着大胆的棋。眼下正是套取情报的时候。他从钱夹里取出那枚银质票牌，纹章一面朝上，托在掌心，向那群人客气地一躬身，轻声说道：

“诸位绅士，可有一位能告诉我这件东西的来历？”

## XIII Under Suspicion / 第十三章 嫌疑之下

===Original===

A distinct, uncontrollable murmur of surprise ran round the packed court as this man in the witness-box gave this answer. It signified many things—that there were people present who had expected some such dramatic development; that there were others present who had not; that the answer itself was only a prelude to further developments. And Spargo, looking narrowly about him, saw that the answer had aroused different feelings in Aylmore’s two daughters. The elder one had dropped her face until it was quite hidden; the younger was sitting bolt upright, staring at her father in utter and genuine bewilderment. And for the first time, Aylmore made no response to her.

But the course of things was going steadily forward. There was no stopping the Treasury Counsel now; he was going to get at some truth in his own merciless fashion. He had exchanged one glance with the Coroner, had whispered a word to the solicitor who sat close by him, and now he turned again to the witness.

“So you know that gentleman—make sure now—as Mr. Anderson, an inmate of the Temple?”

===Chinese===

证人席上这个人一给出这个回答，满庭拥挤的听众间顿时掠过一阵清晰而无法遏制的惊愕低语。这阵低语意味着许多事情——在场有人早已料到会有这样一场戏剧性的进展；也有另一些人全然未曾料到；而这回答本身，不过是后续种种进展的序曲。斯帕戈细细打量四周，看出这个回答在艾尔莫尔的两个女儿身上激起了迥然不同的情绪。姐姐垂下了头，直到面庞完全隐没不见；妹妹则直挺挺地坐着，直瞪着父亲，满脸是全然真切、发自肺腑的惶惑。而艾尔莫尔破天荒没有回应她。

但事情的进程仍在稳步向前推进。此刻谁也拦不住财政部律师了；他要以他自己那套毫不留情的手段挖出某些真相来。他同验尸官交换了一个眼色，又同紧挨着他坐下的事务律师低语了一句，随即再度转向证人。

“这么说，你认得那位先生——现在可要想准了——他是律师学院的住户，叫安德森先生？”

===Original===

“Yes, sir.”

“You don’t know him by any other name?”

“No, sir, I don’t.”

“How long have you known him by that name?”

===Chinese===

“是的，先生。”

“你不知道他还有别的名字吗？”

“不知道，先生，我不知道。”

“你以这个名字认识他有多久了？”

===Original===

“I should say two or three years, sir.”

“See him go in and out regularly?”

“No, sir—not regularly.”

“How often, then?”

“Now and then, sir—perhaps once a week.”

===Chinese===

“我估摸有两三年了，先生。”

“常见他进出吗？”

“不常见，先生——并不规律。”

“那么，多久一回？”

“时来时往的，先生——大概一星期一回。”

===Original===

“Tell us what you know of Mr. Anderson’s goings-in-and-out.”

“Well, sir, I might see him two nights running; then I mightn’t see him again for perhaps a week or two. Irregular, as you might say, sir.”

“You say ‘nights.’ Do I understand that you never see Mr. Anderson except at night?”

“Yes, sir. I’ve never seen him except at night. Always about the same time, sir.”

===Chinese===

“把你所知道的安德森先生进出往来的情形，讲给我们听听。”

“是这样，先生，我兴许连着两晚都见着他；接下来兴许一两个星期都见不着。可以说是没个准儿的，先生。”

“你说‘晚上’。我是否可以这样理解：除了夜里，你从来没有见过安德森先生？”

“是的，先生。我从没在夜里以外的时间见过他。而且总在差不多的钟点，先生。”

===Original===

“What time?”

“Just about midnight, sir.”

“Very well. Do you remember the midnight of June 21st–22nd?”

“I do, sir.”

===Chinese===

“什么钟点？”

“就快半夜的时候，先生。”

“很好。你可记得6月21日到22日之间的那个午夜？”

“记得，先生。”

===Original===

“Did you see Mr. Anderson enter then?”

“Yes, sir, just after twelve.”

“Was he alone?”

“No, sir; there was another gentleman with him.”

“Remember anything about that other gentleman?”

===Chinese===

“那天夜里你见着安德森先生进来了？”

“见着了，先生，刚过十二点。”

“他是一个人吗？”

“不是，先生；还有另一位先生同他在一起。”

“关于那另一位先生，你可记得些什么？”

===Original===

“Nothing, sir, except that I noticed as they walked through, that the other gentleman had grey clothes on.”

“Had grey clothes on. You didn’t see his face?”

“Not to remember it, sir. I don’t remember anything but what I’ve told you, sir.”

“That is that the other gentleman wore a grey suit. Where did Mr. Anderson and this gentleman in the grey suit go when they’d passed through?”

===Chinese===

“没什么，先生，只除了他们走过去的时候，我留意到那另一位先生身上穿的是灰色衣裳。”

“穿灰色衣裳。你没看见他的脸？”

“看见了也记不真了，先生。除了我已经告诉您的，别的我什么都不记得了，先生。”

“也就是说，那另一位先生穿的是一套灰衣裳。安德森先生和这位灰衣先生穿过门房之后，往哪儿去了？”

===Original===

“Straight up the Lane, sir.”

“Do you know where Mr. Anderson’s rooms in the Temple are?”

“Not exactly, sir, but I understood in Fountain Court.”

“Now, on that night in question, did Mr. Anderson leave again by your lodge?”

===Chinese===

“径直沿中殿巷走上去，先生。”

“你知道安德森先生在律师学院里的住处在哪里吗？”

“说不很准，先生，不过我听人说是泉水庭院。”

“那么，在所说的那个夜里，安德森先生后来可曾再从你的门房出去？”

===Original===

“No, sir.”

“You heard of the discovery of the body of a dead man in Middle Temple Lane next morning?”

“I did, sir.”

“Did you connect that man with the gentleman in the grey suit?”

===Chinese===

“没有，先生。”

“第二天早上中殿巷里发现一名男尸的事，你听说了？”

“听说了，先生。”

“你可曾把那个人同那位灰衣先生联想到一处？”

===Original===

“No, sir, I didn’t. It never occurred to me. A lot of the gentlemen who live in the Temple bring friends in late of nights; I never gave the matter any particular thought.”

“Never mentioned it to anybody until now, when you were sent for to come here?”

“No, sir, never, to anybody.”

“And you have never known the gentleman standing there as anybody but Mr. Anderson?”

“No, sir, never heard any other name but Anderson.”

===Chinese===

“没有，先生，没有。我压根儿就没往那上头想过。律师学院里住的许多先生，深更半夜带朋友进来的多着呢；这事我从没特别放在心上。”

“直到这回人家来传你到堂，之前你从没对任何人提起过？”

“没有，先生，从来没有，对谁都没提起过。”

“而站在那里的那位先生，你从头到尾只当他是安德森先生，此外一无所知？”

“是的，先生，除了安德森，从没听见过别的名字。”

===Original===

The Coroner glanced at the Counsel.

“I think this may be a convenient opportunity for Mr. Aylmore to give the explanation he offered a few minutes ago,” he said. “Do you suggest anything?”

“I suggest, sir, that if Mr. Aylmore desires to give any explanation he should return to the witness-box and submit himself to examination again on his oath,” replied the Counsel. “The matter is in your hands.”

The Coroner turned to Aylmore.

===Chinese===

验尸官朝财政部律师瞥了一眼。

“我想，这或许正是让艾尔莫尔先生作他几分钟前主动提出的那番解释的合适时机，”他说，“你有什么提议？”

“我的提议是，先生，倘若艾尔莫尔先生想作什么解释，他应当回到证人席上，再度宣誓、重新接受盘问，”财政部律师答道，“事情全凭您裁夺。”

验尸官转向艾尔莫尔。

===Original===

“Do you object to that?” he asked.

Aylmore stepped boldly forward and into the box.

“I object to nothing,” he said in clear tones, “except to being asked to reply to questions about matters of the past which have not and cannot have anything to do with this case. Ask me what questions you like, arising out of the evidence of the last two witnesses, and I will answer them so far as I see myself justified in doing so. Ask me questions about matters of twenty years ago, and I shall answer them or not as I see fit. And I may as well say that I will take all the consequences of my silence or my speech.”

===Chinese===

“你反对这样办吗？”他问。

艾尔莫尔毅然举步上前，走进证人席。

“我什么都不反对，”他声音清朗地说道，“唯独反对要我回答那些关于往事的问题——它们同本案没有、也不可能有任何干系。围绕刚才两位证人的证词，你们要问什么尽管问，凡是我认为自己有权回答的，我都会作答。至于二十年前的事，我答与不答，全由我自己斟酌。我不妨把话说在前头：无论我沉默还是开口，一切后果概由我承担。”

===Original===

The Treasury Counsel rose again.

“Very well, Mr. Aylmore,” he said. “I will put certain questions to you. You heard the evidence of David Lyell?”

“I did.”

“Was that quite true as regards yourself?”

“Quite true—absolutely true.”

===Chinese===

财政部律师再度起身。

“很好，艾尔莫尔先生，”他说，“我要向你提出几个问题。你听到了大卫·莱尔的证词？”

“听到了。”

“就你本人而言，那番证词属实？”

“属实——千真万确。”

===Original===

“And you heard that of the last witness. Was that also true!”

“Equally true.”

“Then you admit that the evidence you gave this morning, before these witnesses came on the scene, was not true?”

“No, I do not! Most emphatically I do not. It was true.”

===Chinese===

“刚才那位证人的证词你也听到了。那番话也同样属实？”

“同样属实。”

“那么你承认，今天上午在这两位证人出场之前，你所给出的证词是不实的了？”

“不，我不承认！我断然、断然不承认。那证词是实话。”

===Original===

“True? You told me, on oath, that you parted from John Marbury on Waterloo Bridge!”

“Pardon me, I said nothing of the sort. I said that from the Anglo-Orient Hotel we strolled across Waterloo Bridge, and that shortly afterwards we parted—I did not say where we parted. I see there is a shorthand writer here who is taking everything down—ask him if that is not exactly what I said?”

A reference to the stenographer proved Aylmore to be right, and the Treasury Counsel showed plain annoyance.

“Well, at any rate, you so phrased your answer that nine persons out of ten would have understood that you parted from Marbury in the open streets after crossing Waterloo Bridge,” he said. “Now—?”

===Chinese===

“实话？你曾在宣誓之下告诉我，你是在滑铁卢桥上同约翰·马伯里分手的！”

“恕我直言，我没说过这样的话。我说的是，我们从盎格鲁-东方旅馆出来，信步走过滑铁卢桥，随后不久便分手了——我并没有说我们在何处分手。我看见庭上有位速记员，一字一句全都记录在案——问问他，我方才说的是不是正是这几句话？”

一查速记员的记录，证明艾尔莫尔所言不虚，财政部律师面露明显的不豫。

“好吧，无论如何，你把答话措辞成那个样子，十个人里有九个都会以为，你是过了滑铁卢桥之后、在露天大街上同马伯里分手的，”他说，“那么——？”

===Original===

Aylmore smiled.

“I am not responsible for the understanding of nine people out of ten any more than I am for your understanding,” he said, with a sneer. “I said what I now repeat—Marbury and I walked across Waterloo Bridge, and shortly afterwards we parted. I told you the truth.”

“Indeed! Perhaps you will continue to tell us the truth. Since you have admitted that the evidence of the last two witnesses is absolutely correct, perhaps you will tell us exactly where you and Marbury did part?”

“I will—willingly. We parted at the door of my chambers in Fountain Court.”

===Chinese===

艾尔莫尔微微一笑。

“十个人里有九个怎么理解，我不负责，正如你怎么理解我也不负责，”他语带讥诮地说，“我说过的话，我如今再复述一遍——马伯里和我一道步行走过滑铁卢桥，随后不久我们便分手了。我对你们说的都是实情。”

“是吗！那么，或许你还愿意继续对我们说实话。既然你已承认刚才两位证人的证词千真万确，或许你可以确切地告诉我们，你同马伯里究竟是在什么地方分手的？”

“我愿意——非常愿意。我们是在泉水庭院我那套事务室的门口分手的。”

===Original===

“Then—to reiterate—it was you who took Marbury into the Temple that night?”

“It was certainly I who took Marbury into the Temple that night.”

There was another murmur amongst the crowded benches. Here at any rate was fact—solid, substantial fact. And Spargo began to see a possible course of events which he had not anticipated.

“That is a candid admission, Mr. Aylmore. I suppose you see a certain danger to yourself in making it.”

===Chinese===

“那么——我再问一遍——那天夜里，把马伯里带进律师学院的就是你？”

“那天夜里把马伯里带进律师学院的，确实就是我。”

满庭的长凳间又是一阵低语。这下总算是有了事实——确凿、扎实的事实。斯帕戈由此开始看出一种自己先前未曾逆料的可能的情事走向。

“这可是个坦白的供认，艾尔莫尔先生。我想你也明白，作出这样的供认，于你自己不无几分危险。”

===Original===

“I need not say whether I do or I do not. I have made it.”

“Very good. Why did you not make it before?”

“For my own reasons. I told you as much as I considered necessary for the purpose of this enquiry. I have virtually altered nothing now. I asked to be allowed to make a statement, to give an explanation, as soon as Mr. Lyell had left this box: I was not allowed to do so. I am willing to make it now.”

“Make it then.”

“It is simply this,” said Aylmore, turning to the Coroner. “I have found it convenient, during the past three years, to rent a simple set of chambers in the Temple, where I could occasionally—very occasionally, as a rule—go late at night. I also found it convenient, for my own reasons—with which, I think, no one has anything to do—to rent those chambers under the name of Mr. Anderson. It was to my chambers that Marbury accompanied me for a few moments on the midnight with which we are dealing. He was not in them more than five minutes at the very outside: I parted from him at my outer door, and I understood that he would leave the Temple by the way we had entered and would drive or walk straight back to his hotel. That is the whole truth. I wish to add that I ought perhaps to have told all this at first. I had reasons for not doing so. I told what I considered necessary, that I parted from Marbury, leaving him well and alive, soon after midnight.”

===Chinese===

“我明白不明白，无须说出口。供认我已经作了。”

“很好。你先前为什么不作？”

“出于我自己的考虑。我把自认为本次审讯所必需的内容，都已经告诉了你们。如今我实际上并没有更动任何东西。莱尔先生一离开证人席，我便请求获准作一项陈述、作一番解释：未获许可。我现在愿意作了。”

“那就作吧。”

“事情很简单，”艾尔莫尔转向验尸官说道。“过去三年间，我觉得在律师学院里租一套简便的事务室颇为合宜，我可以偶尔——照例是极偶尔地——深夜到那里去。出于我自己的考虑——我想，这些考虑同任何人都毫不相干——我同样觉得，以安德森先生的名义租下那套事务室颇为合宜。在我们此刻所议的那个午夜，马伯里随我到那套事务室里待了片刻。他在里面待的时间，往宽里说也绝不超过五分钟：我在外门同他分手，而据我了解，他会顺着我们来时的原路离开律师学院，然后乘车或步行径直回他的旅馆去。全部实情就是如此。我愿意补上一句：或许我一开头就该把这些统统讲出来。我自有不那么做的理由。我把自认为必要的内容讲了出来——那就是：午夜刚过我便同马伯里分了手，分手时他安然无恙、活得好好的。”

===Original===

“What reasons were or are they which prevented you from telling all this at first?” asked the Treasury Counsel.

“Reasons which are private to me.”

“Will you tell them to the court?”

“No!”

===Chinese===

“当初妨碍你把这些和盘托出的，究竟是什么理由——如今依然如此吗？”财政部律师问道。

“是我个人的隐衷。”

“你肯把它们告诉本庭吗？”

“不肯！”

===Original===

“Then will you tell us why Marbury went with you to the chambers in Fountain Court which you tenant under the name of Anderson?”

“Yes. To fetch a document which I had in my keeping, and had kept for him for twenty years or more.”

“A document of importance?”

“Of very great importance.”

===Chinese===

“那么你可愿意告诉我们，马伯里为什么随你去了那套你以安德森的名义租下的泉水庭院事务室？”

“愿意。他去取一份由我保管着、已替他保管了二十年开外的文件。”

“一份要紧的文件？”

“一份极其要紧的文件。”

===Original===

“He would have it on him when he was—as we believe he was—murdered and robbed?”

“He had it on him when he left me.”

“Will you tell us what it was?”

“Certainly not!”

===Chinese===

“那么，当他——正如我们所认定的那样——遇害遭劫的时候，这文件应当就在他身上吧？”

“他离开我的时候，文件在他身上。”

“你可愿意告诉我们，那是一份什么文件？”

“当然不愿意！”

===Original===

“In fact, you won’t tell us any more than you choose to tell?”

“I have told you all I can tell of the events of that night.”

“Then I am going to ask you a very pertinent question. Is it not a fact that you know a great deal more about John Marbury than you have told this court?”

“That I shall not answer.”

===Chinese===

“也就是说，除了你愿意说的，你不会再多告诉我们一个字？”

“关于那天夜里的事情，我能说的都已经告诉你们了。”

“那么我要问你一个非常切中要害的问题。关于约翰·马伯里，你知道的远比你向本庭讲出的多得多——这是不是事实？”

“这个问题我不作答。”

===Original===

“Is it not a fact that you could, if you would, tell this court more about John Marbury and your acquaintanceship with him twenty years ago?”

“I also decline to answer that.”

The Treasury Counsel made a little movement of his shoulders and turned to the Coroner.

“I should suggest, sir, that you adjourn this enquiry,” he said quietly.

“For a week,” assented the Coroner, turning to the jury.

The crowd surged out of the court, chattering, murmuring, exclaiming—spectators, witnesses, jurymen, reporters, legal folk, police folk, all mixed up together. And Spargo, elbowing his own way out, and busily reckoning up the value of the new complexions put on everything by the day’s work, suddenly felt a hand laid on his arm. Turning he found himself gazing at Jessie Aylmore.

===Chinese===

“只要你愿意，你本可以向本庭多讲一讲约翰·马伯里其人，以及二十年前你同他的交往——这是不是事实？”

“这个问题我同样拒绝回答。”

财政部律师微微耸了耸肩，转向验尸官。

“我提议，先生，您将本次审讯延期，”他不动声色地说。

“延期一周。”验尸官表示同意，随即转向陪审团。

人群涌出法庭，叽叽喳喳、低语纷纷、惊叹连连——看客、证人、陪审员、记者、法律界人物、警界人物，全都混杂在一处。斯帕戈侧着膀子往外挤，脑子里正忙着掂量今天这场庭讯给一切事情涂上的新面目分量几何，忽然觉出一只手搭上了他的手臂。他一转身，发现自己正望着杰西·艾尔莫尔。

## VI Witness to a Meeting / 第六章 会面的见证人

===Original===
Ronald Breton walked into the *Watchman* office and into Spargo’s room next morning holding a copy of the current issue in his hand. He waved it at Spargo with an enthusiasm which was almost boyish.

“I say!” he exclaimed. “That’s the way to do it, Spargo! I congratulate you. Yes, that’s the way﻿—certain!”

Spargo, idly turning over a pile of exchanges, yawned.

“What way?” he asked indifferently.

“The way you’ve written this thing up,” said Breton. “It’s a hundred thousand times better than the usual cut-and-dried account of a murder. It’s﻿—it’s like a﻿—a romance!”

“Merely a new method of giving news,” said Spargo. He picked up a copy of the *Watchman*, and glanced at his two columns, which had somehow managed to make themselves into three, viewing the displayed lettering, the photograph of the dead man, the line drawing of the entry in Middle Temple Lane, and the facsimile of the scrap of grey paper, with a critical eye. “Yes﻿—merely a new method,” he continued. “The question is﻿—will it achieve its object?”

===Chinese===
第二天早晨，罗纳德·布雷顿（Ronald Breton）手里拿着一份当天刚出的报纸，走进《守望报》（the *Watchman*）报馆，径直来到斯帕戈（Spargo）的房间。他朝斯帕戈挥着那份报纸，那股热乎劲儿简直像个大孩子。

“我说！”他嚷道。“这才叫写法，斯帕戈！我恭喜你。对，就这么写——准没错！”

斯帕戈正有气无力地翻着一摞同业交换来的报纸，打了个哈欠。

“什么就这么写？”他淡淡地问。

“你这篇东西的写法呀，”布雷顿说。“比平常那种干巴巴的凶案报道强十万倍。这——这简直像——像一部传奇小说！”

“不过是发新闻的一个新法子罢了。”斯帕戈说。他拿起一份《守望报》，扫了一眼自己那两栏文字——不知怎么竟膨胀成了三栏——用挑剔的眼光端详着那些醒目的大字标题、死者的照片、中殿巷（Middle Temple Lane）入口的线描图，还有那张灰色小纸片的摹真件。“对——不过是个新法子，”他接着说。“问题是——它能不能达到目的？”

===Original===
“What’s the object?” asked Breton.

Spargo fished out a box of cigarettes from an untidy drawer, pushed it over to his visitor, helped himself, and tilting back his chair, put his feet on his desk.

“The object?” he said, drily. “Oh, well, the object is the ultimate detection of the murderer.”

“You’re after that?”

“I’m after that﻿—just that.”

“And not﻿—not simply out to make effective news?”

===Chinese===
“什么目的？”布雷顿问。

斯帕戈从一个乱糟糟的抽屉里摸出一盒香烟，推到客人面前，自己也抽出一支，然后把椅子往后一仰，双脚搁到办公桌上。

“目的？”他干巴巴地说。“噢，要说目的嘛，就是最终查出凶手。”

“你图的是这个？”

“我图的就是这个——只图这个。”

“而不是——不单单是为了做出一条轰动的新闻？”

===Original===
“I’m out to find the murderer of John Marbury,” said Spargo deliberately slow in his speech. “And I’ll find him.”

“Well, there doesn’t seem to be much in the way of clues, so far,” remarked Breton. “I see﻿—nothing. Do you?”

Spargo sent a spiral of scented smoke into the air.

“I want to know an awful lot,” he said. “I’m hungering for news. I want to know who John Marbury is. I want to know what he did with himself between the time when he walked out of the Anglo-Orient Hotel, alive and well, and the time when he was found in Middle Temple Lane, with his skull beaten in and dead. I want to know where he got that scrap of paper. Above everything, Breton, I want to know what he’d got to do with you!”

He gave the young barrister a keen look, and Breton nodded.

“Yes,” he said. “I confess that’s a corker. But I think﻿—”

===Chinese===
“我要办的，是查出杀害约翰·马伯里（John Marbury）的凶手，”斯帕戈说，字字都说得从容缓慢。“而且我一定查得到他。”

“唔，至少到现在为止，线索好像还谈不上多，”布雷顿说。“依我看——什么也没有。你呢？”

斯帕戈朝空中吐出一缕打着旋儿的香烟。

“我想要知道的事太多了，”他说。“我正眼馋着等新闻呢。我要知道约翰·马伯里是谁。我要知道他走出盎格鲁-东方旅馆（Anglo-Orient Hotel）时还活蹦乱跳，到被人发现在中殿巷、脑壳被打得粉碎、一命呜呼，这中间他自己干了些什么。我要知道那张小纸片他是从哪儿弄来的。而压倒一切的，布雷顿，我要知道他跟你有什么干系！”

他锐利地看了这位年轻的出庭律师（barrister）一眼，布雷顿点了点头。

“是啊，”他说。“我承认这一问真把我难住了。不过我想——”

===Original===
“Well?” said Spargo.

“I think he may have been a man who had some legal business in hand, or in prospect, and had been recommended to﻿—me,” said Breton.

Spargo smiled﻿—a little sardonically.

“That’s good!” he said. “You had your very first brief﻿—yesterday. Come﻿—your fame isn’t blown abroad through all the heights yet, my friend! Besides﻿—don’t intending clients approach﻿—isn’t it strict etiquette for them to approach?﻿—barristers through solicitors?”

“Quite right﻿—in both your remarks,” replied Breton, good-humouredly. “Of course, I’m not known a bit, but all the same I’ve known several cases where a barrister has been approached in the first instance and asked to recommend a solicitor. Somebody who wanted to do me a good turn may have given this man my address.”

“Possible,” said Spargo. “But he wouldn’t have come to consult you at midnight. Breton!﻿—the more I think of it, the more I’m certain there’s a tremendous mystery in this affair! That’s why I got the chief to let me write it up as I have done﻿—here. I’m hoping that this photograph﻿—though to be sure, it’s of a dead face﻿—and this facsimile of the scrap of paper will lead to somebody coming forward who can﻿—”

===Chinese===
“想什么？”斯帕戈说。

“我想，他也许是个手头正办着、或者打算要办什么法律事务的人，有人把他的事举荐到了——我名下。”布雷顿说。

斯帕戈微微一笑——笑意里带着几分讥诮。

“想得倒美！”他说。“你昨天才接到平生第一份讼案委托。得了——你的大名还没吹遍四面八方的山头呢，我的朋友！再说——有意委托的当事人，不是理应——按严格的规矩，不是理应——先经事务律师（solicitor），再来找出庭律师吗？”

“你说得全对——两句话都在理，”布雷顿好脾气地答道。“当然，我一点名气也没有；不过话虽如此，我也见过好几桩这样的事：当事人头一个找到的便是出庭律师，再请他举荐一位事务律师。许是哪位有心抬举我一把的人，把我的地址给了这个人。”

“有可能，”斯帕戈说。“可他总不至于半夜里跑来向你讨教吧。布雷顿！——这件事我越琢磨，越肯定其中藏着一桩天大的谜！正因如此，我才说动主编，容我照现在这个样子把它写出来——喏，就在这儿。我正指望这张照片——尽管它拍的的确是张死人的脸——还有这张纸片的摹真件，能让什么知情人站出来，他可以——”

===Original===
Just then one of the uniformed youths who hang about the marble pillared vestibule of the *Watchman* office came into the room with the unmistakable look and air of one who carries news of moment.

“I dare lay a sovereign to a cent that I know what this is,” muttered Spargo in an aside. “Well?” he said to the boy. “What is it?”

The messenger came up to the desk.

“Mr. Spargo,” he said, “there’s a man downstairs who says that he wants to see somebody about that murder case that’s in the paper this morning, sir. Mr. Barrett said I was to come to you.”

“Who is the man?” asked Spargo.

===Chinese===
正在这时，一个在《守望报》报馆大理石柱门厅里伺候差事的制服小厮走进屋来，那副神情气派，一望便知是带来了要紧的动静。

“我敢拿一枚索维林金币打赌，我知道这是什么事，”斯帕戈低声旁白了一句。“怎么样？”他对那孩子说。“什么事？”

小厮走到桌前。

“斯帕戈先生，”他说，“楼下有位先生，说想找人谈谈今早报上登的那桩凶杀案，先生。巴雷特（Barrett）先生吩咐我来找您。”

“那人是谁？”斯帕戈问。

===Original===
“Won’t say, sir,” replied the boy. “I gave him a form to fill up, but he said he wouldn’t write anything﻿—said all he wanted was to see the man who wrote the piece in the paper.”

“Bring him here,” commanded Spargo. He turned to Breton when the boy had gone, and he smiled. “I knew we should have somebody here sooner or later,” he said. “That’s why I hurried over my breakfast and came down at ten o’clock. Now then, what will you bet on the chances of this chap’s information proving valuable?”

“Nothing,” replied Breton. “He’s probably some crank or faddist who’s got some theory that he wants to ventilate.”

The man who was presently ushered in by the messenger seemed from preliminary and outward appearance to justify Breton’s prognostication.
